### [1] [TreeGraft: Adaptive Multi-Drafter Grafting for Tree-Based Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26112)
*Jiaming Fan,Daming Cao,Canchen Huang,Jiale Fu,Jin Zhang,Junjie Gao,Kai Yang,Xiangzhong Luo,Xu Yang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: TreeGraft is a multi-drafter, tree-structured speculative decoding framework that uses both weak and strong drafters plus a learned scheduler to build a higher-quality shared draft tree, improving LLM inference efficiency and acceptance length over single-drafter baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing tree-structured speculative decoding methods rely on a single drafter model. This creates a trade-off: small drafters are fast but generate low-quality candidate trees, while large drafters improve quality at the cost of high latency. There is a need for an approach that can flexibly combine fast and strong drafters to obtain better draft trees without incurring excessive cost.

Method: TreeGraft introduces a multi-drafter speculative decoding framework where a weaker (cheaper) and a stronger (more accurate) drafter jointly construct a shared draft tree. The stronger drafter periodically rescoring candidates to update scores, reselect grafting positions, and recover promising paths that a weaker drafter missed. Its expansions are integrated non-destructively, so existing branches that may still be accepted by the target model are preserved. A lightweight scheduler, distilled from an offline value system, learns when it is beneficial to invoke the stronger drafter to balance cost and quality.

Result: On 10 target/drafter model pairs and 6 benchmarks, TreeGraft consistently outperforms the better of two single-drafter baselines (always using only the weak or only the strong drafter). It achieves an average improvement of 15.1% and up to 26.6% maximum gain in the evaluated speculative decoding efficiency/quality metrics.

Conclusion: By coordinating multiple drafters with different cost-quality trade-offs through a shared draft tree and a learned scheduler, TreeGraft substantially improves the effectiveness of tree-structured speculative decoding. It demonstrates that multi-drafter designs can overcome the limitations of fixed single-drafter strategies, offering better speed–quality trade-offs for large language model inference.

Abstract: Speculative decoding accelerates large language model inference through a draft-then-verify paradigm. Building on this, tree-structured methods improve inference by organizing proposals into multiple candidate paths, increasing the accepted length. However, existing tree-structured methods use a single drafter for all drafting steps, creating a dilemma: a smaller drafter is fast but yields lower-quality trees, whereas a larger drafter improves tree quality but suffers from high latency. To address this, we propose TreeGraft, a multi-drafter framework in which drafters of different costs jointly construct a shared draft tree. TreeGraft uses the stronger drafter to rescore candidates by updating scores assigned by the weaker drafter, reselect grafting positions, and recover promising paths left unexplored. It also integrates stronger drafter expansions non-destructively, preserving existing branches that may still be accepted by the target model. Together, these designs improve the quality of the shared draft tree. To control the drafting cost, TreeGraft introduces a lightweight scheduler distilled from an offline value system to decide when to call the stronger drafter. Across 10 model pairs and 6 benchmarks, TreeGraft outperforms the better of the two fixed single-drafter endpoint strategies by 15.1% on average, reaching a maximum gain of 26.6%. Our code is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/TreeGraft-E983.

</details>


### [2] [ElementCheck: Complexity-Aware Long-Form Text Factuality Evaluation via Sentence Elements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26118)
*Xinming Wang,Haoran Du,Yi Chen,Jian Xu,Hongming Yang,Han Hu,Yulong Chen,Cheng-Lin Liu,Xu-Yao Zhang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: ElementCheck is a complexity-aware framework that evaluates factuality of long-form text by verifying sentence elements via an element graph, improving reliability and cost-efficiency over traditional decompose-retrieve-verify pipelines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current long-form factuality evaluation methods rely on decomposing text into sub-claims, retrieving evidence, and verifying each claim. This pipeline introduces noise from imperfect claim decomposition and uses a fixed verification granularity, which can lead to unreliable factuality judgments and unnecessary verification cost. There is a need for a more precise, structure-aware way to evaluate factuality that adapts verification effort to sentence complexity and reduces noise.

Method: The paper proposes ElementCheck, a framework that operates at the level of sentence elements rather than uniformly decomposed atomic claims. It extracts entity pairs connected by explicit, verifiable relations in each sentence and organizes them as nodes and edges in an element graph. The topology of this graph is used to estimate sentence complexity: simple sentences are directly verified as a whole, while complex ones undergo targeted refinement and element-level verification. To enable fine-grained evaluation, the authors construct FastFact-Sent by mapping isolated claims from the existing FastFact-Bench dataset back to their original source sentences, providing sentence-level factuality annotations.

Result: Across FastFact-Sent and two additional domain-specific factuality benchmarks, ElementCheck is tested with five different backbone models. It consistently achieves better factuality verification performance compared with standard decompose-retrieve-verify baselines, while also offering a better trade-off between accuracy and computational cost. The complexity-aware design reduces redundant re-verification operations and yields more stable performance across different backbone choices.

Conclusion: ElementCheck demonstrates that representing sentences as element graphs and adapting verification to sentence complexity improves the reliability and efficiency of long-form factuality evaluation. Complexity-aware, element-level verification mitigates noise from claim decomposition, decreases unnecessary verification steps, and generalizes well across models and domains. The newly introduced FastFact-Sent benchmark further enables more fine-grained and realistic assessment of sentence-level factuality checking approaches.

Abstract: Existing long-form factuality evaluation relies on the decompose-retrieve-verify pipeline. However, the pipeline suffers from noise from claim decomposition and fixed verification granularity, resulting in unreliable results. We propose ElementCheck, a complexity-aware framework that verifies long-form outputs via sentence elements. Instead of uniformly decomposing sentences into atomic sub-claims, ElementCheck extracts entity pairs that are explicitly linked through verifiable connections in the original sentence as elements, and organizes these into an element graph. The graph topology provides a structural signal for estimating sentence complexity, enabling direct verification for simple sentences and targeted element-level refinement and verification for complex ones. To support fine-grained evaluation, we construct a new benchmark FastFact-Sent by mapping isolated claims from FastFact-Bench back to their source sentences. Experiments on FastFact-Sent and two domain-specific benchmarks show ElementCheck consistently improves factuality verification across five backbone models while maintaining a favorable accuracy-cost trade-off. Further analyses demonstrate that complexity-aware verification reduces unnecessary re-verification and maintains stability across different backbones.

</details>


### [3] [DeflectBench: A Benchmark for Evaluating Rhetorical Fallacy Generation in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26119)
*Art Kanke*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces DeflectBench, a benchmark to test how easily large language models can be prompted to produce rhetorical fallacies, showing that prompt framing and fallacy type affect refusal rates far more than the content of the claims.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most prior work focuses on detecting rhetorical fallacies in text, not on whether LLMs can be induced to generate such fallacies despite safety training. The authors want to understand how current safety mechanisms behave when models are asked to produce manipulative argumentative tactics, and how different prompt structures and claim controversies affect refusal and compliance.

Method: They build DeflectBench, a benchmark containing 80 claims with four controversy levels and prompts that request three types of rhetorical fallacies (whataboutism, ad hominem, red herring) under seven different prompt framings. They query four frontier LLMs and collect 23,990 generations. They then categorize responses into refusal, labeled compliance (model acknowledges the manipulation while performing it), soft refusal, and clean compliance, and analyze how refusal/compliance rates vary with prompt frame, fallacy type, and claim content.

Result: Refusal behavior is driven much more by how the request is framed and which fallacy type is requested than by what the claim is about or how controversial it is. Refusal rates per claim vary by only about 11 percentage points, while changing the prompt framing can nearly flip refusal rates (up to ~100 percentage point shifts) and switching fallacy type can cause >80 percentage point swings. An "educational debate coach" framing nearly eliminates refusals across all models, but most resulting outputs are labeled compliance rather than safe, clean compliance. The four models show distinct profiles across refusal, labeled compliance, soft refusal, and clean compliance.

Conclusion: Safety post-training in current LLMs appears to be highly sensitive to superficial aspects of the prompt rather than the underlying content or controversy of claims. Models can be easily steered into producing rhetorical fallacies, especially under educational-style framings, and often respond with labeled compliance that still carries the harmful manipulation. Evaluating and improving safety thus requires benchmarks like DeflectBench that probe generative behavior under varied prompt structures, not just detection of fallacies in existing text.

Abstract: Whether large language models can be prompted to generate rhetorical fallacies on demand, and whether current safety post-training constrains this behavior, has received less attention than the related question of detecting fallacies in existing text. We close this gap with DeflectBench, evaluating 23,990 generations from four frontier models across three deflection strategies (whataboutism, ad hominem, red herring), seven prompt framings, and 80 claims spanning four controversy levels. Refusal is governed primarily by request structure rather than claim content. Per claim refusal varies by only 11 percentage points across the 80 claims, while a single prompt frame change can swing within model refusal by nearly 100 percentage points and switching the requested fallacy type can swing it by over 80 percentage points within explicit framings. An educational debate coach prompt framing collapses refusal to near zero across all four model families, but the bypassed behavior is not clean compliance. Models typically produce labeled compliance, naming the requested manipulation in the same response that contains it. The four models distribute differently across refusal, labeled compliance, soft refusal, and clean compliance. The code and dataset are released at https://github.com/ArtKanke/DeflectBench.

</details>


### [4] [Recipes for Steering and Scaling LLMs via Sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26120)
*Jiajun He,Zongyu Guo,José Miguel Hernández-Lobato,Yuanqi Du*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes a general sampling-based framework to more effectively steer and scale autoregressive LLMs toward modified target distributions, improving generation quality without external supervision.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLMs are trained as autoregressive probabilistic models, but practical use often requires steering them toward richer or modified target distributions (e.g., more focused, diverse, or higher-quality outputs). Current approaches for such steering, like Best-of-N sampling or simple MCMC, are computationally inefficient and lack a unified probabilistic foundation. There is a need for a principled, scalable way to perform probabilistic inference over LLMs’ output space when the target distribution differs from the base model.

Method: The authors introduce a general probabilistic framework for steering LLMs via sampling when the desired target distribution is a powered, product, or tilted version of the base model distribution. Within this framework, they develop two concrete algorithms: (1) a Sequential Monte Carlo (SMC)-based method that propagates and reweights multiple partial generations (particles) as tokens are generated, and (2) a Replica Exchange (RE)-based method that runs multiple Markov chains at different “temperatures” or modified distributions and swaps states between them. Both methods allow sampling from complex target distributions defined over the LLM’s autoregressive structure, while remaining compatible with standard LLM APIs.

Result: Through experiments, the authors show that using SMC and RE to steer sampling can significantly improve the quality of generated text compared with naive strategies such as Best-of-N sampling and standard MCMC-based sampling. Their methods scale more favorably in terms of sample efficiency and computational cost, especially as desired generation quality increases. They demonstrate that these gains can be achieved without external reward models or supervised signals, purely by exploiting the probabilistic structure of the LLM and the modified target distributions.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that probabilistic inference over LLM outputs can be done more effectively by leveraging structured sampling algorithms like SMC and Replica Exchange within a unified framework. This provides a systematic way to steer and scale autoregressive LLMs toward richer target distributions, enabling higher-quality generations without extra supervision. The proposed approach serves as a general recipe for sampling-based control of LLM behavior and can replace ad hoc methods like large Best-of-N sampling with more principled, efficient algorithms.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are probabilistic models, typically defined by an autoregressive factorization. While recent work has begun to study richer target distributions beyond the base model, the sampling strategies remain highly inefficient. In this paper, we present a flexible and theoretically grounded framework for steering and scaling autoregressive LLMs with sampling. Within this framework, we describe two algorithms -- one based on Sequential Monte Carlo (SMC) and one based on Replica Exchange (RE) -- that steer generation toward powering, product or tilting of the base model distribution. We illustrate this framework through scaling the generation quality of LLMs without external supervision or reward models. Experimental results demonstrate our methods scale more favorably than Best-of-N and standard MCMC baselines. Overall, this paper offers a systematic recipe for probabilistic inference with LLMs via sampling.

</details>


### [5] [Can a Model Catch Its Own Hallucinations for Free?: Label-Free Doubt Signals Hold Their Own Against a Labelled Dataset for Abstention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26121)
*Ali Asaria,Tony Salomone,Deep Gandhi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They show you can train language models to abstain (“I’m not sure”) using only the model’s own confidence, without any correctness labels, and it works as well as label-supervised methods on factual QA.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language models often hallucinate: they output false facts confidently and fluently. Prior approaches to reducing this rely on labeled datasets marking which answers are correct, which are expensive to obtain. Yet models internally have a usable confidence signal—token probabilities—which correlate with correctness. The authors want to know whether this internal, label-free confidence can be turned into an effective abstention mechanism, avoiding the need for human-labeled right/wrong data.

Method: They take several open-weight LLMs (1B–8B parameters, from two model families) and leave their base parameters frozen. Using LoRA fine-tuning, they train a small additional module so that the model answers normally when its *pre-fine-tuning* (frozen) confidence in its answer is high, and outputs “I’m not sure” when that confidence is low. The key is that the training signal uses only the model’s own confidence scores—no labels of correctness are used. They evaluate on short-form factual question answering, using an external judge model to decide whether answers are correct. They compare against a standard supervised abstention-tuning baseline that *does* use correctness labels, and run a control variant that instead focuses training on difficult examples rather than abstention behavior.

Result: Label-free abstention training using the model’s own confidence works about as well as supervised abstention-tuning that relies on correctness labels. At matched coverage rates (i.e., when both systems answer the same proportion of questions), there is no statistically significant difference in accuracy between label-free and supervised approaches. The hard-example drilling control does not improve performance, implying the observed gains are due to better calibration of confidence to abstention rather than rote memorization of specific hard cases. They also observe a limitation: the confidence signal fails for “confidently wrong” facts, which the method cannot detect or abstain on.

Conclusion: A model’s internal doubt, derived from its own confidence scores, can be converted—via lightweight LoRA fine-tuning—into an effective abstention behavior that matches the performance of methods trained with explicit correctness labels, at least for short-form factual QA in the tested models. This suggests practitioners can cheaply obtain robust “I’m not sure” behavior without assembling labeled right/wrong datasets, though confidently wrong outputs remain a key failure mode that this approach cannot fix.

Abstract: Large language models state false facts as fluently as true ones, yet a model often "knows" internally when it is on shaky ground: the probability it assigns to its own answer tends to dip on the facts it gets wrong. The usual way to act on this, teaching a model to abstain rather than guess, requires a labelled dataset of right and wrong answers. We ask whether the model's own confidence, which is free and needs no labels, can do that job instead. We fine-tune each model (with LoRA) to answer when its frozen confidence is high and to say "I'm not sure" when it is low, using the signal alone and no correctness labels. Across six open-weights models (1B-8B, two families) on short-form factual question answering, with correctness adjudicated by an independent judge model, this label-free recipe holds its own against label-supervised abstention-tuning: at matched coverage we find no statistically detectable difference between the two. A control that drills hard examples instead of abstaining does not help, indicating the gain comes from calibration, not rote memorization. The signal's one blind spot is confidently wrong facts, which it cannot flag. A model's own doubt is thus a near-free substitute for a labelled dataset when teaching it when to abstain. Code and artifacts are available on request.

</details>


### [6] [Which India Survives Translation? Narrative Homogenisation Across Indian Oral Traditions in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26123)
*Paarth Singh Rathore*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Pilot computational study showing that LLM-generated stories for three Indian traditions are partially homogenized and less faithful when prompted in regional languages than in English.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are mostly trained on English web text that over-represents certain Western cultural narratives. There is concern that this leads to homogenization, where rich, distinct non-Western storytelling traditions are flattened into a single generic style. The authors want to quantify whether this happens for specific Indian oral and literary traditions and how prompting language affects fidelity to those traditions.

Method: The authors select three culturally and stylistically distinct Indian traditions: the Rajasthani Pabuji epic, classical Tamil Sangam poetry, and Bengali folk tales. They build small authentic reference corpora for each tradition (11, 21, and 10 passages). They then create 54 prompts, covering three prompt types per tradition: generic prompts, culturally specific prompts, and prompts written in the regional languages (Hindi, Tamil, Bengali). They query two LLMs (Claude Sonnet and Gemini) with these prompts to generate stories. Using Sentence-BERT embeddings and cosine similarity, they compute: (1) reference drift, i.e., how similar a model’s outputs for a given tradition are to that tradition’s reference corpus relative to the other two traditions; and (2) cross-tradition convergence, i.e., how similar the outputs for different traditions are to each other. They compare similarity scores to the distances among the authentic corpora to detect homogenization effects and analyze how prompting language changes these metrics.

Result: The generated stories generally remain closer to their own tradition’s reference corpus than to the other two, suggesting some preservation of tradition-specific features. However, cross-tradition similarity of the generated texts is relatively high (cosine similarity 0.52–0.66) compared to the larger distances among the authentic corpora, indicating partial homogenization across traditions. A key unexpected finding is that prompts in the regional languages (Hindi, Tamil, Bengali) consistently reduce fidelity to the authentic tradition compared with English prompts, with drops in similarity of up to 27 percentage points for the Rajasthani and Bengali traditions. This suggests that regional-language prompting in current LLMs may not reliably elicit narrow, under-documented oral traditions.

Conclusion: The study concludes that LLMs partially homogenize distinct Indian storytelling traditions, producing outputs that are more similar across traditions than the authentic texts would suggest, even though some tradition-specific alignment is retained. Regional-language prompting, contrary to some prior work on multilingual prompting and cultural representation, decreases fidelity to the authentic traditions for these less-documented oral and literary corpora. The authors argue this difference arises between eliciting broad cultural diversity and accurately simulating a specific, narrow tradition that is under-represented in training data. They present their approach as a lightweight, computational complement to large human-annotation studies on Indian cultural misrepresentation, and as an early component of a broader doctoral research agenda on LLMs and cultural storytelling.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are trained predominantly on English-language internet text that over-represents certain cultural narratives, raising concerns that models flatten the diversity of non-Western storytelling traditions into a single homogenized archetype. We present a pilot computational study examining this across three maximally distinct Indian regional oral and literary traditions: the Rajasthani Pabuji epic, classical Tamil Sangam poetry, and Bengali folk tales. We collected authentic reference corpora for each tradition (11, 21, and 10 passages respectively) and prompted two LLMs (Claude Sonnet and Gemini) with 54 generation requests spanning three prompt types per tradition - generic, culturally specific, and regional-language. Using Sentence-BERT embeddings and cosine similarity, we measure reference drift (how closely outputs track their own tradition's authentic texts relative to the other two) and cross-tradition convergence (how similar outputs are across traditions). We find that while outputs remain closer to their own tradition's reference than to others, cross-tradition similarity is high (0.52-0.66) relative to what the traditions' genuine distance would predict, indicating partial homogenisation. Unexpectedly, prompting in the regional language (Hindi, Tamil, or Bengali) consistently reduced fidelity to the authentic tradition relative to English prompting, by as much as 27 percentage points for Rajasthani and Bengali traditions. We discuss this against conflicting prior results on multilingual prompting and argue it reflects a difference between eliciting general cultural diversity and simulating one narrow, lesser-documented oral tradition. We position this pilot as a lightweight, scalable complement to recent large-scale human-annotation studies of Indian cultural misrepresentation in LLM-generated stories, as part of a broader doctoral research program.

</details>


### [7] [Natural-Language Policies to Executable Decisions: An Interpretable Large Language Model Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26124)
*Ziqiang Zhang,Jing Ma,Zilong Wang,Jiayuan Chen,Yi Qiao,Yu He,Wei Zhang,Dai Cheng,Xiaoyu Shen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper presents a production-ready, LLM-assisted but numerically deterministic pricing system for large-scale tourism, achieving major efficiency gains while keeping pricing auditable and reliable.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tourism pricing is difficult to automate because orders are unstructured and policies are complex, dynamic, and open-ended. Traditional rule engines are hard to maintain, and fully autonomous LLM agents are unreliable and non-auditable for financial decisions. There is a need for a middle-ground system that leverages LLM flexibility without sacrificing determinism and governance.

Method: The authors design a hybrid architecture where LLMs are restricted to structured information extraction from unstructured orders and to selecting bounded policy paths, while all actual numeric computations are done by deterministic components. Pricing policies are converted into human-readable condition trees that can accommodate new clauses and rule changes without code modification. The system also uses periodic fine-tuning on operational traces to improve the induction of these trees and the accuracy of matching policies to orders.

Result: The system was deployed in a state-owned municipal tourism enterprise covering 7 scenic sites and 12 business lines, interacting with over 1,500 operators and more than 1,000 active pricing policies. Over six months it processed 3,960 orders, reduced the staffing requirement for order management from 15–20 people down to 3, and decreased average handling time per order from 10 minutes to under 2 minutes.

Conclusion: A constrained, auditable LLM-in-the-loop architecture can successfully automate complex tourism pricing at scale, striking a balance between flexibility and control. By limiting LLMs to structured tasks and keeping numerical pricing deterministic under interpretable policy trees, organizations can safely adopt AI for high-stakes financial workflows while gaining substantial efficiency improvements.

Abstract: Pricing automation in large-scale tourism is challenging because travel orders are highly unstructured, while pricing policies are complex, rapidly evolving, and inherently open-ended. Traditional rule engines are brittle and costly to maintain, whereas unconstrained LLM agents lack the reliability and auditability required for financial decisions. We present a production-grade LLM-powered pricing system with a strict decision boundary: LLMs perform structured extraction and bounded policy/path selection, while all numeric pricing, including total-price computation, is executed deterministically. Policies are compiled into interpretable condition trees, enabling open-ended support for new clauses and evolving rules without code changes, while exposing auditable artifacts for human-in-the-loop control. Periodic fine-tuning on logged traces further improves tree induction and path matching. Deployed at a municipal state-owned tourism enterprise across 7 scenic sites and 12 business categories with 1,500+ operators and 1,000+ active policies, the system processed 3,960 orders in six months, reduced the order management team from 15-20 to 3, and cut per-order handling time from 10 minutes to <2 minutes.

</details>


### [8] [Training-Time Explainability for Multilingual Hate Speech Detection: Aligning Model Reasoning with Human Rationales](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26125)
*Muhammad Deedahwar Mazhar Qureshi,Sannaan Khan,Muhammad Atif Qureshi,Wael Rashwan*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces a training-time explainability framework that aligns a hate-speech classifier’s internal reasoning with human-annotated rationales, improving both performance and interpretability across English and Hinglish anti-Muslim hate datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Online anti-Muslim hate speech is often culturally coded, multilingual, and implicit, making it hard for standard AI moderation systems to detect. Existing models can be accurate but opaque, potentially biased, and disconnected from the sociocultural context, which risks both over- and under-moderation. There is a need for moderation models that not only perform well but also provide interpretable, culturally grounded explanations that match human understanding.

Method: The authors propose a training-time explainability framework in which model reasoning is explicitly regularized to align with human-provided rationales. They implement and compare several explanation-based regularization strategies using gradient-based and attention-based signals, deploying them on two datasets: HateXplain (English) and BullySent (Hinglish). They then evaluate models using multiple explanation methods—LIME, Integrated Gradients, Grad × Input, and attention—to measure classification accuracy, explanation plausibility, faithfulness, and agreement across explanation methods.

Result: Models trained with gradient- and attention-based regularization achieve higher F-scores than baseline models without such regularization. These models also produce explanations that are more plausible to humans and more faithful to the model’s actual decision process. Furthermore, they better capture culturally specific linguistic cues that signal implicit anti-Muslim hate in both English and Hinglish content.

Conclusion: Aligning model explanations with human rationales during training can simultaneously enhance detection performance and interpretability for anti-Muslim hate speech, particularly in multilingual and culturally nuanced settings. The proposed framework offers a promising route toward more transparent, culturally aware content moderation systems that are better suited to handling implicit and coded forms of online hate.

Abstract: Online hate against Muslim communities often appears in culturally coded, multilingual forms that evade conventional AI moderation. Such systems, though accurate, remain opaque and risk bias, over-censorship, or under-moderation, particularly when detached from sociocultural context. We propose a \emph{training-time} explainability framework that aligns model reasoning with human-annotated rationales, improving both classification performance and interpretability. Our approach is evaluated on HateXplain (English) and BullySent (Hinglish), reflecting the prevalence of anti-Muslim hate across both languages. Using LIME, Integrated Gradients, Grad X Input, and attention, we assess accuracy, explanation quality, and cross-method agreement. Results show that gradient- and attention-based regularization improve F-scores, enhance plausibility and faithfulness, and capture culturally specific cues for detecting implicit anti-Muslim hate, offering a path toward multilingual, culturally aware content moderation.

</details>


### [9] [TelecomGPT-R1: A Unified Open-Source Reasoner for the Telecom Stack](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26126)
*Bohao Wang,Chenwei Wu,Haoyu Li,Hang Zou,Yu Tian,Lina Bariah,Li Wei,Chongwen Huang,Yongliang Shen,Zhaoyang Zhang,Merouane Debbah*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper presents TelecomGPT-R1-9B, an open-source 9B-parameter large language model specialized for telecom reasoning that achieves top performance on GSMA’s open telco leaderboard and is competitive with closed-source frontier models across multiple telecom benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Telecom engineering workflows require complex reasoning that jointly uses formal standards, real-time telemetry, vendor logs, and precise RF/network calculations. Existing generic LLMs lack deep telecom grounding, while existing telecom-specific LLMs are weak at structured, multi-step reasoning. This two-sided gap limits the effective use of LLMs in telecommunications. The paper aims to build a unified model that combines strong domain grounding with robust reasoning ability and that can serve as an open foundation for telecom automation.

Method: The authors start from the Qwen3.5-9B base model and design both a specialized training corpus and a two-stage post-training pipeline. (1) Data: They construct a 67,427-example supervised fine-tuning (SFT) dataset organized along four reasoning axes: protocol reasoning, knowledge recall/application, quantitative/network modeling, and fault diagnosis. Data comes from aligned public web sources and is augmented via axis-specific chain-of-thought (CoT) generation and a prefix-continuation self-validation process to improve reasoning quality. (2) Training: First, they perform multi-teacher LoRA-based SFT to inject telecom domain knowledge and enforce distinct reasoning formats per axis. Second, they apply a reinforcement-learning-style stage using group relative policy optimization (GRPO). This is stabilized with decoupled clip and dynamic sampling policy optimization (DAPO) and uses four axis-aligned binary verifiers as rewards, one per reasoning axis, to optimize the model’s policy toward high-quality domain reasoning.

Result: TelecomGPT-R1-9B reaches the top position among open-source telecom LLMs on the GSMA open telco leaderboard. Evaluated across seven public telecom benchmarks spanning multiple reasoning dimensions, it consistently outperforms other open telecom models and achieves a seven-axis average score comparable to state-of-the-art closed-source frontier LLMs specialized for reasoning. This demonstrates that the proposed data curation and two-stage post-training strategy substantially improves telecom-specific reasoning and factual correctness.

Conclusion: A targeted combination of axis-structured data, multi-teacher LoRA SFT, and GRPO-based RL with verifier rewards can turn a general 9B model into a strong telecom-specialized reasoner. TelecomGPT-R1-9B narrows the gap between open-source and closed-source systems in telecom reasoning and offers a practical open foundation for future research and deployment in telco automation, diagnostics, and planning. The work suggests that domain-grounded, axis-based training with explicit verifiers is an effective recipe for building high-performance vertical LLMs.

Abstract: Telecommunications is a high-leverage domain for large language model (LLM)-based reasoning because routine engineering workflows require joint grounding in normative specifications, operational telemetry, vendor-specific fault evidence, and exact RF/network calculations. However, current LLM integration in telecom remains bottlenecked by a two-sided capability gap: generic reasoners often lack telecom-specific grounding, while domain-specific telecom LLMs remain limited in structured, multi-step reasoning. To bridge this gap, we release TelecomGPT-R1-9B, a unified open-source telecom reasoner that ranks top-performing on the GSMA open telco leaderboard. Specifically, we curate a 67,427-example supervised fine-tuning (SFT) corpus organized around four complementary reasoning axes: protocol, knowledge, modeling, and fault. The corpus is built from axis-matched public web sources and enhanced through axis-specific chain-of-thought (CoT) generation and prefix-continuation self-validation. Starting from Qwen3.5-9B, we further develop a two-stage post-training recipe. First, multi-teacher low-rank adaptation (LoRA)-based SFT injects telecom knowledge and induces axis-specific reasoning formats. Second, group relative policy optimization (GRPO), stabilized by decoupled clip and dynamic sampling policy optimization (DAPO), optimizes the policy using four axis-aligned binary verifier rewards. Across seven public telecom benchmarks, TelecomGPT-R1-9B ranks first among open-source telecom LLMs and achieves a seven-axis mean comparable to state-of-the-art closed-source frontier reasoners.

</details>


### [10] [FIRSTPASS: A Multi-Domain, Multi-Round Peer Review Dataset Grounded in Real Editorial Outcomes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26129)
*Prabhjot Singh,Somnath Luitel,Manmeet Singh,Josh Durkee*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: FIRSTPASS is a large, multidisciplinary dataset of full peer-review dialogues from Nature Communications, enabling benchmarking and training of AI systems for scientific review beyond computer science and machine learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing AI models for peer review are almost entirely trained on datasets from computer science and machine learning conferences. These models have limited exposure to the norms, expectations, and domain-specific concerns of other scientific fields (e.g., experimental controls in biology, spectral validation in chemistry). Additionally, current datasets mostly contain single-round reviews without complete dialogue or reliable outcome labels, which constrains research on realistic scientific assessment and editorial decision-making. The authors aim to fill this gap with a richer, more diverse, and better-labeled corpus.

Method: The authors construct FIRSTPASS from Nature Communications’ mandatory transparent peer review, instituted in November 2022. They automatically collect and parse complete multi-round editorial dialogues: initial referee reports, detailed author point-by-point replies, and follow-up reviewer assessments. They categorize papers into five scientific domains (biology, chemistry, neuroscience, physics, earth science), annotate each record with an outcome label based on the number of review rounds (STANDARD for two rounds, EXTENDED for three or more), and conduct an automated audit to verify content integrity. They also compute basic statistics (e.g., average review length) and release all data, parsing pipelines, and evaluation scripts for public use.

Result: The resulting FIRSTPASS dataset contains 3,668 complete review-dialogue records across five scientific domains, all derived from a high-impact multidisciplinary journal. Each record preserves the full iterative structure of peer review and is labeled with editorial outcome type (STANDARD vs. EXTENDED). The dataset is verified to have 100% content integrity through automated checks. Reviews are long and detailed, averaging 2,155 words, which is substantially more than typical conference reviews. The authors also provide code and scripts to reproduce parsing and to support standardized benchmarking of AI models on this corpus.

Conclusion: FIRSTPASS establishes the first large-scale, multi-round, multidisciplinary peer review dataset built from a high-impact journal’s transparent review process. By including full dialogues, outcome-based labels, and richer domain diversity, it provides a more realistic foundation for training and evaluating AI systems that aim to emulate or assist scientific peer review. The resource enables research on AI scientific judgment that generalizes beyond computer science and supports reproducible benchmarking across multiple fields.

Abstract: Scientific peer review datasets have trained AI systems exclusively on Computer Science and Machine Learning venues, producing models that critique ablation studies yet have never seen a biology reviewer demand contamination controls or a chemist question Nuclear Magnetic Resonance (NMR) spectral assignments. We introduce FIRSTPASS, the first large-scale peer review dataset built on complete multi-round editorial dialogues from a multidisciplinary high-impact journal. Curated from Nature Communications mandatory transparent peer review (instituted November 2022), FIRSTPASS comprises 3,668 records spanning five scientific domains (biology, chemistry, neuroscience, physics, and earth science), capturing the full iterative structure of scientific validation: initial referee reports, author point-by-point responses, and updated reviewer assessments. Each record carries an outcome label derived directly from editorial decisions (STANDARD for two-round review; EXTENDED for three or more rounds), providing ground truth absent in all prior corpora. An automated audit confirms 100% content integrity. Expert reviews average 2,155 words, substantially denser than conference venue reviews. All data, parsing pipelines, and evaluation scripts are released to enable reproducible benchmarking of AI scientific judgment across disciplines.

</details>


### [11] [Agents Don't Paginate: First-Chunk Selection for LLM Tool Responses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26130)
*Tatiana Petrova,Andrei Mazniak,Radu State*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies how much it matters where the relevant (“gold”) tool output appears inside the first chunk that LLM coding agents read, and finds that improving rank-1 placement barely affects downstream accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In LLM-based coding agents, tool outputs (e.g., search results, file contents) often exceed the model’s token limit per turn, so they are paginated into chunks. In practice, agents almost never request additional chunks, meaning they only see the first chunk. This raises a key question: how important is it to place the needed information (the gold item) at the top of that first chunk, and can better first-chunk ranking improve agent performance?

Method: The authors use a controlled offline benchmark on 500 SWE-bench Verified tasks, modelling first-chunk selection as a 0/1 knapsack problem. They compare six value functions for ranking/selecting items, compute precision-at-1 (p1) for the gold item, and then run a single-turn file-localisation probe on five different LLMs (4,800 total calls) to see how p1 changes affect downstream accuracy. They also test adding file-metadata features to a keyword-based scorer and evaluate statistical significance of changes in p1 and accuracy.

Result: They find that increasing p1—i.e., making the gold item more likely to be placed first—does not systematically improve downstream LLM accuracy: per-model accuracy changes are under 3 percentage points, inconsistent in direction, and never statistically significant. The LLM can typically recover the gold item from anywhere in the first chunk, so what matters is whether the gold is included in that chunk at all, not its rank within the chunk. Additionally, augmenting a keyword scorer with four file-metadata signals actually decreases p1 by 4.8 percentage points (p = 0.001). A simple, parameter-free keyword scorer raises p1 from 24.2% to 35.0% (and to 35.8% with a fallback ordering), highly significant statistically, but these rank-1 gains do not translate into better downstream task accuracy.

Conclusion: For LLM-based coding agents, optimizing which items appear at the very top of the first tool-response chunk (higher p1) provides little to no benefit for downstream problem-solving accuracy. What matters is ensuring that the relevant item appears somewhere in the first chunk, not its exact rank within that chunk. Moreover, adding extra metadata-based heuristics can hurt ranking quality, while simple keyword-based ranking can significantly improve p1 without improving end-task outcomes.

Abstract: Coding agents built on large language models (LLMs), such as Claude Code, Cursor, OpenAI Codex, GitHub Copilot, and Aider, receive tool responses that routinely exceed the agent's per-turn token budget. The standard remedy, pagination, is available in every protocol that produced these responses; yet across the corpus of session logs from a public Model Context Protocol middleware we observed no agent-initiated requests for a second chunk. The first chunk is what the agent reads, so we ask how often the gold item (the one the agent needs) is placed first in it: the precision-at-1 rate $p_1$.
  In a controlled offline benchmark we treat first-chunk selection as a 0/1 knapsack and compare six value functions on 500 SWE-bench Verified tasks, then test whether $p_1$ matters with a single-turn file-localisation probe on five language models (4,800 LLM calls; not an end-to-end resolve-rate test). Two pre-registered hypotheses did not hold and are our main findings. The central one is negative: raising $p_1$ does not systematically raise downstream accuracy. Per-model deltas stay under three percentage points (p.p.), are not consistently signed, and no model is significant; the agent recovers the gold from anywhere in the chunk, so what reaches its answer is first-chunk inclusion, not the gold's rank within it. The second: adding four file-metadata signals to a keyword scorer hurts $p_1$ by 4.8 p.p. (paired significance test, $p = 0.001$).
  A parameter-free keyword scorer does raise $p_1$, from a 24.2% baseline to 35.0% (+10.8 p.p., far beyond chance; $p = 3.9 \times 10^{-8}$), and to 35.8% with a fallback to the tool's native ordering when no keyword matches. But by our central finding this is a rank-1 gain, and rank-1 is the part that does not reach the agent's answer: downstream accuracy does not move.

</details>


### [12] [Evaluating Language Models in Realistic Conversational Contexts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26131)
*Ilija Subasic,Andrew Rabinovich,Zhao Chen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces UPHELD, a large human-annotated benchmark of long, realistic dialogues to evaluate conversational quality of LLMs and proposes a Mixture-of-Judges framework that better aligns automatic evaluation with expert human judgments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluation methods for LLMs are largely designed for short, single-turn tasks (like summarization or QA) and rely heavily on synthetic data. They do not capture the nuanced, multi-turn, human-scale aspects of conversation or correlate well with human expert judgments. There is a need for a robust, human-grounded benchmark and better automatic metrics for long, open-ended dialogues.

Method: The authors create UPHELD, a benchmark consisting of hundreds of professionally scripted, human-to-human long dialogues with realistic structure. They collect over 36,000 per-turn human annotations on more than 30,000 dialogue turns. Using this dataset, they systematically test classical automatic metrics and LLM-as-a-judge methods against expert labels, analyze their reliability, and then design a Mixture-of-Judges framework that combines several evaluative signals to better approximate human assessments.

Result: UPHELD reveals that both traditional automatic metrics and standalone LLM-as-a-judge approaches correlate poorly with expert human judgments on long, human-scale dialogues. The proposed Mixture-of-Judges framework, which aggregates multiple evaluation signals, achieves roughly a 30% improvement in correlation with human expert assessments compared to prior methods.

Conclusion: UPHELD fills an important gap in the evaluation ecosystem for LLMs by offering a large, human-grounded benchmark for long, realistic dialogues and showing limitations of existing metrics. The Mixture-of-Judges approach demonstrates that combining multiple evaluators yields substantially better alignment with human judgments, positioning UPHELD as a strong foundation for future work on evaluating conversational intelligence beyond mere factual correctness.

Abstract: As Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed to serve open-ended, multi-turn interactions, evaluating conversational quality at human scale has become a central challenge. Existing evaluation frameworks built for summarization, translation, or short-form QA tasks fall short of adequately measuring the consistency of human-scale dialogue, especially when derivation and validation of these metrics themselves often rely on synthetic rather than human sources. We fill the gap by introducing UPHELD (UPwork Human-Scale Evaluated Long Dialogues), a large, reference-full benchmark for evaluating human-scale conversational ability beyond factual correctness. UPHELD consists of hundreds of complete human-to-human dialogues authored by professional script writers, with realistic turn densities and 36,000+ per-turn human annotations across 30,000+ expert-generated dialogue turns. Using UPHELD, we systematically evaluate classical automatic metrics and reference-free LLM-as-a-judge approaches, and find them unreliable when correlated with expert human judgment. Building off this analysis, we use UPHELD to develop a Mixture-of-Judges framework that combines multiple evaluative signals and improves correlation with human assessments by approximately 30%. Overall, UPHELD provides a robust, human-grounded foundation for evaluating human-scale conversational intelligence that fills a crucial gap in the pre-existing LLM dataset landscape.

</details>


### [13] [Agent Seer: Synthesizing Scenarios from Specification Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26133)
*Harish Karumuri,Mahesh Vemula,David Lopes Pegna*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper presents Agent Seer, a pipeline that automatically generates realistic, multi-turn evaluation scenarios for tool-using AI agents from tool specifications alone, avoiding manual curation or live tool access.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluating AI agents that call external tools is hard because realistic scenarios are usually hand-crafted, need deep domain expertise, don’t scale to large or changing tool ecosystems, and quickly become outdated as APIs evolve. The authors want a scalable, domain-agnostic way to build realistic benchmarks directly from the machine-readable information already contained in tool specifications.

Method: The authors introduce Agent Seer, which takes a single MCP (Model Context Protocol) tool specification as input, without examples, live tool execution, or domain-specific tuning. The pipeline enriches the raw parameter schemas, synthesizes graded evaluation scenarios with synthetic tool outputs, and then expands these into mock-data-grounded, multi-turn dialogues. These dialogues are designed to reflect realistic practitioner usage while enabling measurement of tool-calling correctness and conversational coherence.

Result: Applied to seven diverse MCP specifications with varying domains and tool-suite sizes, Agent Seer produces evaluation scenarios that show strong tool-calling correctness and conversational coherence, achieving full tool coverage for small and medium suites. Analysis reveals that complexity of the parameter schemas is the main factor driving quality variation, while overall tool-suite size has a smaller and largely independent effect. Most remaining errors stem from inaccuracies in argument values rather than from calling the wrong tools, a type of failure that is missed by coarse, name-based evaluation metrics.

Conclusion: Agent Seer demonstrates that rich, realistic benchmarks for tool-using AI agents can be automatically derived from existing tool specifications, avoiding manual scenario design and remaining robust to changing APIs. The work highlights parameter schema complexity as a key challenge for high-quality scenario generation and shows that fine-grained evaluation of argument values is critical, since many failures are not captured by simple tool-name matching metrics.

Abstract: Evaluating AI agents that use external tools requires realistic test scenarios that capture how practitioners compose tools and iterate across conversation turns. Constructing such scenarios by hand demands deep domain expertise, does not scale across tool ecosystems, and produces static benchmarks that cannot track evolving APIs. We observe that tool specifications -- function names, natural-language descriptions, and typed parameter schemas -- already encode sufficient semantic information to synthesize realistic evaluation scenarios without manual curation or live tool execution. Agent Seer builds off this latent information: from a single Model Context Protocol (MCP) specification, with no examples, no live tool access, and no domain-specific tuning. This pipeline enriches raw schemas, generates graded scenarios with synthetic tool outputs, and expands them into mock-data-grounded multi-turn dialogues that exhibit strong tool-calling correctness and conversational coherence.
  Evaluation quality is measured by applying this pipeline on seven MCP specifications spanning diverse domains and tool-suite sizes and measuring the tool-calling correctness and conversational coherence. The pipeline achieves strong quality across all domains, with complete tool coverage on small and medium specifications. Two findings emerge within this analysis: parameter schema complexity is the strongest correlate of quality variation -- tool-suite size plays a smaller, orthogonal role -- and argument value accuracy is the dominant failure mode among imperfect scenarios, a sub-dimension invisible to coarse-grained name-match metrics.

</details>


### [14] [Data Science Approaches to Evaluating Honours Candidates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26135)
*Francesca von Braun-Bates,Sunreeta Sen,Indraayudh Talukdar,Anirban Lahiri*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A modular NLP pipeline uses OSINT to estimate public sentiment toward individuals, showing that a tailored sentiment algorithm (MINOS) outperforms generic ones for reputational risk assessment, demonstrated on UK Honours recipients.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a need for systematic, reproducible ways to assess public sentiment and reputational risk around individuals using openly available online information, especially for high-stakes decisions like awards or appointments.

Method: The authors build a chained data-science pipeline that gathers web data via search, extracts and filters relevant text, performs tokenisation and co-reference resolution, and then applies sentiment analysis. They compare standard sentiment tools (AFINN, VADER) to a custom, domain-informed algorithm (MINOS) tailored to reputational risk and public conduct language. They apply this to cases of public figures with known reputational outcomes, including in the UK Honours system.

Result: The pipeline converts heterogeneous online text into person-level sentiment distributions. The domain-specific MINOS algorithm provides a clearer separation between individuals with positive, ambiguous, and negative reputational outcomes than standard sentiment tools, demonstrating better suitability for reputational assessment tasks.

Conclusion: A modular OSINT+NLP pipeline can enable transparent, auditable, and reproducible human-in-the-loop sentiment assessments about individuals. Domain-informed sentiment models like MINOS are more effective than generic ones for detecting reputational risk and positive contribution, and such tools can support high-stakes decision-making processes such as oversight of UK Honours recipients.

Abstract: We present a modular data-science pipeline for estimating public sentiment towards individuals from fragmented, unstructured open-source intelligence (OSINT). The method chains web search, text extraction, relevance filtering, tokenisation, co-reference resolution, and sentiment analysis to convert heterogeneous web material into auditable person-level sentiment distributions. We compare AFINN and VADER with MINOS, a domain-informed sentiment algorithm designed to detect language associated with reputational risk, misconduct, and positive public contribution. Applied to public figures with known reputational outcomes, MINOS gives the clearest separation between positive, ambiguous, and negative cases. The results show that chained NLP and OSINT methods can support transparent, reproducible, human-in-the-loop sentiment assessment for high-stakes decision support. We demonstrate the approach on the UK Honours system, where individuals are required to display high standards of public conduct to maintain an Honour.

</details>


### [15] [Reward-Informed Sparse Autoencoders and the Solution-Completeness Confound](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26136)
*Tanvi Nagilla,Alexander Jameson,Daniel Manta,Shayaan Uddin*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They use sparse autoencoders trained on RL reward-filtered trajectories to find features correlated with “good” vs “bad” reasoning, and show that most discovered structure reflects superficial completion form rather than genuine reasoning quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Interpretability researchers hope sparse autoencoders can expose internal features corresponding to reasoning quality. RL training already produces reward signals distinguishing better from worse trajectories, suggesting a cheap way to obtain supervision for interpretability without manual labeling. The authors want to know whether using reward-filtered data actually surfaces meaningful reasoning-related features, or if it just picks up on superficial patterns.

Method: They construct a reward-informed sparse autoencoder (RI-SAE) by taking GRPO trajectories from Llama-3.1-8B, splitting them into high-reward (“good”) and low-reward (“bad”) reasoning continuations, and training a standard JumpReLU SAE on the activations. They then analyze whether a sparse subset of the learned features can separate good vs bad examples, and compare this to several controls: (1) a generic SAE trained without reward-selected data, (2) a TF-IDF text classifier on the raw completions, and (3) very simple structural cues such as completion length, presence of a closed reasoning block, and whether the answer is boxed. They compute clustering metrics (silhouette scores), AUCs, and inspect individual features qualitatively.

Result: The RI-SAE produces a subset of 16,384 features that cleanly separate good vs bad continuations, with a high silhouette score (0.79) on the selected features, while the full latent code barely separates them (0.005). However, simple baselines already capture most of this separation: a TF-IDF classifier gets AUC 0.75–0.83, and three crude structural indicators alone reach AUC 0.70, with 99% of good vs 69% of bad completions using boxed answers. A generic SAE trained without reward-filtered data fails to separate classes (silhouette 0.01 and no discriminative features). They identify two RI-SAE features that are interpretable and discriminative (related to symbolic mathematics and procedural/evaluative language).

Conclusion: Reward-informed training of sparse autoencoders does produce features that separate high- from low-reward trajectories, but most of this signal corresponds to surface-level completion form and formatting, not deep reasoning quality. The strong separation is largely in-sample fitting to artifacts of the reward-curated dataset, rather than structure that a reward-blind SAE would uncover. Reward filtering remains a cheap, label-free technique to reuse RL signals for interpretability, but its outputs should be interpreted cautiously: it tends to highlight stylistic and structural patterns, with only a minority of truly reasoning-related features. The authors present both the method and their control battery as a recommended evaluation recipe for similar future work.

Abstract: Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) decompose language-model activations into sparse, interpretable features, and an appealing way to aim them at reasoning is to curate their data with a signal reinforcement learning already produces: the reward. We build such a reward-informed SAE (RI-SAE): we split GRPO trajectories into high-reward ("good") and low-reward ("bad") reasoning continuations, train a standard JumpReLU SAE on their activations, and then ask what the resulting good/bad separation actually measures. On Llama-3.1-8B a sparse subset of the 16,384 features does separate the classes (silhouette 0.79 on the selected features versus 0.005 for the full code), but a control battery shows the separation is largely solution completeness rather than reasoning quality: a TF-IDF text classifier already splits the classes (AUC 0.75--0.83), and three structural cues alone (length, a closed reasoning block, and a boxed answer) reach AUC 0.70 (99% of good versus 69% of bad completions are boxed). A generic SAE that never saw the reward does not separate the classes at all (silhouette 0.01, no discriminative features), so the 0.79 is in-sample fitting of this curated signal rather than structure that a reward-blind dictionary recovers. We therefore present the recipe and its control battery together: reward filtering is a cheap, label-free way to reuse RL signals for interpretability, but most of what it surfaces is completion form. Two discriminative features are still readable (symbolic mathematics; procedural and evaluative language), which we take as illustrative rather than as isolated reasoning.

</details>


### [16] [Interpretable, Fairly Evaluated Automated L2 Speaking Assessment that Beats the Single-Human Ceiling and Why Pause Encoding Does Not Change LLM Fluency Scores](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26137)
*Eichi Uehara*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper develops and evaluates an interpretable hybrid system combining engineered speech-timing features with an LLM-based fluency judgment to automatically score second-language English speaking performance in spontaneous dialogue, achieving near top-human rater reliability and carefully testing fairness and where the fluency signal comes from.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: L2 English learners have limited access to speaking partners, and speaking is both critical and anxiety-provoking. This has created demand for automated speaking practice and scoring. However, automated scores are only useful if they are accurate, interpretable, fair, and benchmarked against human raters. Existing systems often lack interpretability and rigorous validation, especially for spontaneous dialogue. The authors aim to build a system that addresses these gaps while clarifying what aspects of the speech signal actually drive performance.

Method: The authors design an interpretable hybrid model that combines deterministic speech-timing features (a De-Jong-based composite capturing pause and timing patterns) with a single large language model (LLM) judgment of fluency based on the speech transcript. They evaluate the system on the ICNALE Global Rating Archive, which provides 140 L2 English speeches rated by about 80 trained raters on 10 analytic criteria. Focusing on 130 speeches with usable audio, they compute correlations between automated scores and the consensus human gold standard without fitting to human labels. They use Spearman correlation, paired-bootstrap confidence intervals, learner-isolation analyses, null and negative controls, and per-feature replication of classical measures. They also systematically manipulate how pauses are encoded in LLM prompts to test whether the LLM is using pause information beyond the engineered features and conduct a per-L1 fairness audit.

Result: The deterministic De-Jong speech-timing composite alone correlates strongly with the consensus human ratings (Spearman rho = 0.764). When blended with a single text-only LLM fluency judgment, the correlation rises to rho = 0.818. This hybrid system matches or exceeds the agreement level of more than 81% of individual trained human raters, surpassing the median rater (rho = 0.73) and approaching the best raters, achieving about 83% of the estimated reliability ceiling (kappa_max = 0.99). The LLM blend improves over the timing composite by +0.054 in rho, with a paired-bootstrap 95% CI [0.017, 0.108] that excludes zero, indicating a reliable gain. Experiments varying the way pauses are represented in the LLM prompt show no reliable benefit of detailed inline pause marking over aggregate pause statistics, and a mid-clause pause grounding criterion yields no consistent gain; any effects are bounded to within roughly ±0.1 in rho. A negative control using monologues and multiple robustness checks (learner-isolation methods, per-feature reproductions, fairness audit by L1) support the validity of the findings.

Conclusion: An interpretable feature-plus-LLM hybrid model can automatically assess L2 English speaking fluency in spontaneous dialogue at a level that rivals or exceeds most trained human raters, while remaining transparent and fair. The dominant fluency signal comes from objective speech-timing features, with the LLM providing a complementary but coarse ranking refinement rather than extracting significant additional information from pause encodings in the prompt. Careful benchmarking against many human raters, reliability ceilings, and fairness audits suggests that such hybrid systems can offer trustworthy automated speaking scores when rigorously validated and constrained by interpretable measurements.

Abstract: Second-language (L2) English learners can rarely rehearse speaking with a partner. Speaking is also the most anxiety-laden skill. These gaps drive a fast-growing market for automated speaking practice and scoring. But an automated score is trustworthy only if it is accurate, interpretable, fair, and benchmarked against the right human bar. We build an interpretable feature-plus-LLM hybrid for spontaneous L2 dialogue. We evaluate it without ever fitting to the human labels, against the ICNALE Global Rating Archive: 140 speeches rated by ~80 trained raters on 10 analytic criteria. We score the 130 L2 speeches with usable audio. A deterministic De-Jong speech-timing composite reaches rho=0.764. Blended with a single text-LLM fluency judgment, it reaches Spearman rho=0.818 against the consensus gold. This agrees with the consensus better than 81% of the 80 individual trained raters: above the median rater (rho=0.73) and near the best, and at ~83% of the reliability-corrected maximum (kappa_max=0.99). The blend improves on the composite alone by +0.054 (paired-bootstrap 95% CI [0.017, 0.108], excludes 0); the LLM adds a coarse fluency ranking that the continuous composite refines. We also report a controlled null on pause encoding, bounded to effects below about +/-0.1 rho at this sample size. Holding the LLM and learner words fixed and varying only how pauses are written into the prompt, inline pause locations do not beat aggregate pause statistics (-0.069, CI [-0.15, +0.08]), and a grounded mid-clause criterion gives no reliable gain. The fluency signal comes from the measured speech-timing features, not from how pauses are written for the LLM. We back every claim with two agreeing learner-isolation methods, paired-bootstrap CIs, a monologue negative control, per-feature reproduction of classical measurements, and a per-L1 fairness audit.

</details>


### [17] [Cross-Platform Generalisation Failure in Mental Health Natural Language Processing: A Five-Axis Fairness Audit of Transformer Models on Social Media](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26138)
*Rajveer Singh Pall,Sameer Yadav*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Proposes a five-axis Cross-Platform Fairness Evaluation (CPFE) framework and shows large performance, calibration, equity, and attribution gaps when mental-health NLP models are moved across platforms (Kaggle → Reddit/Twitter).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mental-health NLP models are often trained on one social media platform and then implicitly assumed to work fairly and reliably on others, but cross-platform shifts in language, label distributions, and user populations can severely degrade both accuracy and fairness. There is a need for a standardized, multi-dimensional way to evaluate how well such models generalize and behave across platforms, beyond simple in-domain metrics.

Method: Define CPFE, a five-axis audit protocol (discriminative performance, calibration, statistical significance, prediction equity, attribution stability). Train four transformer models on a Kaggle mental health corpus and evaluate them on in-domain (Kaggle) and out-of-domain Reddit and Twitter test sets. Use AUC for discriminative performance, Expected Calibration Error (ECE) and temperature scaling for calibration, equity metrics such as disparate impact and equalized odds for prediction equity, and Jaccard similarity of top attributions to assess attribution stability. Perform multiple random seeds and a fine-tuning experiment with target-platform labels.

Result: All models suffer substantial cross-platform AUC drops (≈30–39% relative) from near-perfect in-domain AUC (~0.98). Calibration degrades sharply, with ECE increasing up to ~0.5 on Twitter. Platform-specific temperature scaling greatly improves calibration (~88% ECE reduction) without affecting AUC, indicating separable issues in discrimination vs. calibration. Equity metrics show strong cross-platform disparities (low disparate impact, large equalized odds differences). Attribution stability is very low, with almost no overlap in top influential vocabulary across platforms. Fine-tuning on target-platform labels boosts mean AUC by ~0.22, more than calibration alone.

Conclusion: Cross-platform deployment of mental-health NLP models leads to large and multi-faceted failures in accuracy, calibration, fairness, and interpretability that are not visible from in-domain evaluation. CPFE’s five-axis protocol reveals these issues and should be treated as a standard requirement before using such systems in heterogeneous environments. Target-platform labeled data is more effective for improving discriminative performance via fine-tuning than merely for post-hoc calibration.

Abstract: We introduce the Cross-Platform Fairness Evaluation (CPFE) framework -- a five-axis audit protocol covering discriminative performance, calibration, statistical significance, prediction equity, and attribution stability -- and apply it to four transformer models (BERT, RoBERTa, Emotion-DistilRoBERTa, GoEmotions-RoBERTa) trained on a Kaggle mental health corpus (n=35,556) and evaluated on Reddit (n=6,257) and Twitter (n=2,883) test sets with emotion labels mapped to clinical proxies. All three independently evaluated models exhibit consistent and substantial cross-platform AUC degradation (30.3-35.4% on Reddit, 37.9-39.5% on Twitter) relative to within-platform performance (AUC 0.983-0.987), confirmed across five independent training seeds. Calibration failure is concurrent and severe: ECE rises from 0.056-0.060 in-domain to 0.196-0.229 on Reddit and 0.499-0.542 on Twitter. Platform-specific temperature scaling reduces mean ECE by 88.0% without altering discriminative performance (mean |delta AUC|<0.01), confirming separable failure modes. Prediction equity analysis reveals large cross-platform disparities (raw DI < 0.17; prior-shift-adjusted DI: 0.11-0.29 on Reddit), with equalized odds differences of 0.753-0.830 for mental health proxy classes on Reddit and 0.755-0.831 for anxiety on Twitter. Attribution stability analysis shows near-complete vocabulary divergence across platforms (Jaccard J=0 in 14/16 model-class pairs at K=10). These findings support treating cross-platform validation across all five CPFE axes as a standard requirement for mental health NLP systems in heterogeneous environments. In a single-seed fine-tuning experiment, mean AUC improved by 0.216, suggesting target-platform labels provide greater benefit as training signal than as calibration signal.

</details>


### [18] [Syntax vs. Semantics: How Transformers Learn Deep Dependencies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26139)
*Jiangrui Zhao,Xiaoting Du*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies why large language models struggle to learn deep semantic and structural reasoning compared to surface patterns, explaining it via a phenomenon called gradient starvation, and proposes a new training objective that helps models learn variable binding much more effectively than standard fine-tuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Although large language models are syntactically fluent, they often fail at tasks requiring deep semantic dependencies and structural reasoning. The optimization dynamics that cause this gap between surface pattern learning and genuine reasoning are not well understood. The authors aim to mechanistically explain this gap and understand phenomena like sudden phase transitions in reasoning performance and why chain-of-thought prompting works so well.

Method: The authors build a mechanistic framework that views training as a competition between features based on surface statistics and features encoding deep semantics. Through theoretical analysis, they identify a gradient starvation effect where gradients for sparse semantic features are suppressed early in training by dense, easy-to-learn surface features. They empirically study this in both toy transformers and real LLMs (e.g., Llama-3.1-8B, Qwen2.5-Coder-7B). They then design a topology-aligned contrastive objective meant to reshape the gradient geometry so that gradients for structural, semantic dependencies are strengthened and less starved. They test this objective on variable binding tasks, comparing it to standard cross-entropy fine-tuning.

Result: They find that gradient starvation indeed occurs: early in optimization, dense surface features dominate the learning signal, delaying the acquisition of sparse semantic dependencies and causing reasoning to appear suddenly, like a phase transition. They show that chain-of-thought prompting mitigates this by turning implicit reasoning into explicit token-level supervision, thus avoiding the starvation regime. Their proposed topology-aligned contrastive objective leads to substantially better learning of variable binding, with performance gains more than twice those from standard cross-entropy fine-tuning.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that the difficulty of learning deep semantic dependencies in LLMs can be explained by a competition between surface and semantic features that leads to gradient starvation. This explains sudden phase transitions in reasoning capabilities and provides a mechanistic account of why chain-of-thought prompting is effective. By explicitly altering the gradient geometry with a topology-aligned contrastive objective, models can learn structural reasoning skills like variable binding much more efficiently than with standard fine-tuning, offering a principled path toward better reasoning in LLMs.

Abstract: Large Language Models demonstrate remarkable syntactic fluency, yet the optimization dynamics governing their acquisition of deep semantic dependencies remain poorly understood. We propose a mechanistic framework that models this learning process as a competition between Surface Statistics and Deep Semantics. Our theoretical analysis identifies a ``Gradient Starvation" phenomenon where the error signals for sparse semantic dependencies are actively suppressed during early optimization. This suppression impedes the learning of structural reasoning and causes its emergence to manifest as a sudden phase transition. Furthermore, this framework offers a mechanistic basis for the effectiveness of Chain-of-Thought (CoT) strategies. By externalizing intermediate reasoning steps into concrete tokens, CoT effectively bypasses the suppression regime inherent to implicit reasoning. We validate these findings across scales ranging from toy transformers to production models (Llama-3.1-8B, Qwen2.5-Coder-7B). Finally, guided by this theory, we propose a topology-aligned contrastive objective that explicitly rectifies the gradient geometry. Experiments on variable binding tasks demonstrate that our method achieves an improvement that is over 2x larger than that obtained via standard cross-entropy fine-tuning.

</details>


### [19] [Affix Cache for Diffusion Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26140)
*Kaihua Liang,An Zhong,Xin Tan,Zafar Ayyub Qazi,Hong Xu,Jian Weng,Marco Canini*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper introduces ACache, a cache reuse mechanism for Diffusion Large Language Models that enables efficient reuse of cached key-value states for shared affix spans by selectively recomputing only influential tokens (Anchor Tokens), significantly reducing recomputation cost while preserving accuracy and improving throughput.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diffusion Large Language Models support non-autoregressive decoding and bidirectional context, but they lack an efficient cache reuse mechanism analogous to KV caching in autoregressive models. In DLLMs, bidirectional attention tightly couples the representations of shared context tokens with newly generated tokens, so naive cache reuse yields stale states and harms accuracy, while full recomputation is too slow. There is a need for a principled method to partially reuse caches for shared text (including non-prefix affixes) without paying the full recomputation cost or losing quality.

Method: The paper proposes ACache, an affix-oriented cache reuse framework for DLLMs. It first identifies a small, request-specific set of critical affix tokens called Anchor Tokens, based on how much they influence masked generation tokens. It then selectively recomputes the key-value states only for these Anchor Tokens while reusing the remaining cached affix tokens as-is. ACache is implemented on top of Fast-dLLM for general affix reuse and as a shared-prefix prototype on Nano-vLLM, making it compatible with existing DLLM inference frameworks.

Result: Experiments show that directly reusing affix caches degrades accuracy, but ACache can almost fully recover this accuracy by recomputing only about 20% of affix tokens across different settings. In the Nano-vLLM shared-prefix prototype, ACache reduces recomputation latency by up to 55.7% and increases end-to-end throughput by up to 1.68×, demonstrating substantial efficiency gains with limited recomputation overhead.

Conclusion: ACache enables effective and efficient cache reuse for Diffusion Large Language Models by exploiting token influence to identify critical Anchor Tokens for selective recomputation.
