This approach preserves model quality while substantially reducing recomputation cost, narrowing the efficiency gap between DLLMs and autoregressive models and showing practical benefits in real inference engines like Fast-dLLM and Nano-vLLM.

Abstract: Diffusion Large Language Models (DLLMs) enable non-autoregressive decoding and bidirectional context modeling, but efficient inference remains challenging. Unlike autoregressive systems, whose key-value (KV) cache can be reused for shared prefixes, DLLMs couple the KV states of shared context tokens with evolving generated tokens through bidirectional attention, making naive cache reuse stale while full recomputation is expensive. We present ACache, an affix-oriented cache reuse mechanism for shared text spans in DLLMs beyond prefixes. ACache identifies a small request-specific subset of critical affix tokens, called Anchor Tokens, by measuring their influence on masked generation tokens, and selectively recomputes the KV states of only these tokens while reusing the remaining affix cache. Built on Fast-dLLM, ACache recovers the accuracy loss caused by direct affix-cache reuse across different settings when recomputing around 20% of affix tokens. We also build a shared-prefix prototype on top of the Nano-vLLM engine, showing that ACache reduces recompute latency by up to 55.7% and improves end-to-end throughput by up to 1.68$\times$.

</details>


### [20] [AdaThinking-E: One-Token Entropy Regulation for Adaptive Thinking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26141)
*Zining Wang,Tongkun Guan,Boming Chen,Zhentao Guo,Jianqiang Liu,Chao Jin,Chen Duan,Kai Zhou,Pengfei Yan,Wei Shen,Xiaokang Yang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces AdaThinking-E, a reinforcement learning framework that lets multimodal LLMs adaptively decide when to perform explicit, costly reasoning, improving efficiency on easy questions while retaining strong performance on hard document tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multimodal large language models use explicit, chain-of-thought style reasoning for every query, regardless of difficulty. This uniform use of deep reasoning is computationally wasteful for simple tasks and can even harm benchmark accuracy and user experience. There is a need for a mechanism that allows models to selectively decide when to invoke such heavy reasoning based on the complexity of each question, without relying on handcrafted rules or external difficulty annotations.

Method: The authors propose AdaThinking-E, a reinforcement learning framework that trains models to adaptively trigger explicit thinking. The key technique is one-token entropy regulation at a critical decision token, whose output distribution reflects the model’s confidence about whether to engage reasoning. They design an entropy-governed reward scheme: early in training, high-entropy outputs encourage exploration over different thinking strategies; as training proceeds, the framework encourages low-entropy, confident decisions. This allows the policy of “think vs. don’t think” to be learned intrinsically from data, without explicit difficulty labels or manual control signals.

Result: Through extensive experiments on various document understanding and reasoning tasks, the framework shows that models trained with AdaThinking-E maintain or improve accuracy on complex questions while significantly reducing unnecessary reasoning on simple ones. This yields better computational efficiency and improved overall performance on benchmarks compared with always-on explicit reasoning baselines.

Conclusion: AdaThinking-E successfully endows multimodal LLMs with an adaptive thinking mechanism: models can autonomously decide when to deploy expensive reasoning based on learned confidence signals encoded as entropy at decision tokens. This reduces computation on easy cases, preserves or enhances accuracy on hard problems, and removes the need for external difficulty annotations or manual control, demonstrating a practical path toward more efficient and intelligent document reasoning systems.

Abstract: Multimodal large language models have demonstrated strong document reasoning capabilities by incorporating explicit thinking processes. While this capability significantly improves performance on challenging tasks, current models apply such deep reasoning uniformly to all questions, resulting in unnecessary computational overhead for simple task. This not only degrades user experience but also negatively impact accuracy on benchmark datasets. We identify the critical need for adaptive thinking mechanisms that can intelligently determine when to engage reasoning based on question complexity. To address this, we propose AdaThinking-E, a novel reinforcement learning framework that learns adaptive thinking through one-token entropy regulation. Our key insight is that model confidence in the decision to engage thinking (or not) can be quantified through entropy analysis of the predicted probability distribution at critical decision tokens. This observation motivates our entropy-governed reward mechanism: the training process naturally transitions from high-entropy exploration, where the model experiments with different thinking strategies, to low-entropy convergence with confident, generalizable decision-making policies. Crucially, this approach enables models to intrinsically discover when to think without requiring manual intervention or external difficulty labels. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our approach enables models to be both accurate on complex problems and efficient on simple ones across diverse document tasks.

</details>


### [21] [Position Is All You Need: A Free Lunch Token Compression Strategy for MLLM-based Referring Expression Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26142)
*Yuhan Liu,Yixiong Zou,Yuhua Li,Ruixuan Li*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper focuses on efficient token compression for MLLM-based Referring Expression Segmentation (RES) and shows that preserving positional information is crucial. It proposes PAYN, a training-free, position-only token compression method that improves RES performance over existing approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multimodal Large Language Models have significantly improved Referring Expression Segmentation but introduce substantial computational costs, mainly due to large numbers of visual tokens. Existing token compression techniques, which work in other tasks, cause notable performance drops when applied to RES. This motivates a deeper investigation into why compression hurts RES, what role positional information plays, and how to design a compression method that reduces computation without sacrificing accuracy.

Method: The authors first benchmark common token compression strategies on RES and empirically analyze their behavior. Through ablation studies, they identify that preserving original position embeddings and local spatial neighborhoods is particularly important for RES. Based on these insights, they introduce PAYN, a plug-and-play, training-free token compression algorithm that operates solely on positional information. PAYN selects a subset of visual tokens such that they are well distributed across local neighboring regions and keeps their original positional indices intact, thereby preserving spatial relations without requiring additional training or semantic-based selection.

Result: On multiple standard RES benchmarks, PAYN achieves better segmentation performance than existing token compression methods while reducing computational overhead. The experiments demonstrate that position-aware token selection, even without leveraging semantic content, is sufficient to maintain or enhance RES accuracy compared to more complex compression schemes that disturb positional structure.

Conclusion: The study concludes that, for MLLM-based Referring Expression Segmentation, the key to effective token compression lies in preserving positional information and local spatial structures. PAYN exemplifies that a simple, training-free, position-only strategy can outperform existing methods, challenging assumptions about token importance being primarily semantic. The results support the claim that "position is all you need" for token compression in RES and suggest that future efficiency methods for vision-language tasks should carefully respect spatial positional consistency.

Abstract: Referring Expression Segmentation (RES) aims to generate pixel-wise segmentation masks from complex and implicit textual queries. While recent advances in Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have substantially boosted RES performance, their prohibitive computational overhead remains a critical bottleneck, which, however, is rarely explored. To fill this gap, we first evaluate typical token compression methods on this task and observe a surprising performance degradation. In this paper, we aim to understand this phenomenon for a solution. By extensive experiments, we find that token compression for RES requires preserving the original position embeddings and local neighboring spatial structures, indicating that visual token position information is far more critical than in other tasks. Building on this insight, we ask: Can we design the token compression method purely based on the position information? Therefore, we propose PAYN, a plug-and-play, training-free token compression method that relies solely on position information. PAYN retains tokens that are adequately distributed in every local neighboring region while strictly preserving original positional indices, thereby maintaining spatial relational consistency. Experiments on multiple RES benchmarks demonstrate that our method outperforms existing token compression methods, verifying that position is indeed all you need for token compression in the MLLM-based RES task. Codes are avaliable at https://github.com/YuhanLiu231/PAYN.

</details>


### [22] [Beyond Accuracy: A Qualitative Analysis of Vision-Language Models for Hate Speech Detection in Memes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26143)
*Muhammad Jawad Chowdhury,Adiba Hasan,Ishrak Hossain,Shahriar Ivan,Sabbir Ahmed*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Qualitative study of how advanced vision-language models handle hateful memes, focusing on their justifications under different prompts rather than just classification accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hateful content is increasingly spread via memes, which are multimodal, anonymous, and easily viral. Detecting hateful memes is hard because it depends heavily on context, irony, and subtle cues that current vision-language models often overlook despite their strong general multimodal capabilities. Understanding how these models reason about such content is necessary to improve safety and reliability.

Method: The authors qualitatively analyze four state-of-the-art vision-language models (LLaVA-7B, Qwen-VL, GPT-4o mini, Claude 3 Haiku). They test them on hateful memes under zero-shot and few-shot prompting conditions, varying contextual framing to see how it affects model responses. Instead of only measuring accuracy, they closely examine the models’ generated explanations and justifications for their decisions.

Result: The study finds systematic patterns in how different VLMs respond to hateful memes under various prompts, revealing that context and framing significantly influence outputs. The models often struggle with irony and subtle hateful cues, and their justifications expose limitations, blind spots, and inconsistencies in how they interpret multimodal hateful content.

Conclusion: State-of-the-art vision-language models are not yet reliable at detecting and reasoning about hateful memes, particularly when the hate is context-dependent or subtle. Qualitative analysis of their justifications is crucial to expose their constraints and guide future improvements in model design, safety alignment, and evaluation for multimodal hate-speech detection.

Abstract: Memes have turned out to be a powerful tool through which individuals share their ideas concerning contemporary social and political problems. Their anonymity, as well as their ability to go viral, make them a powerful medium for spreading hate. It remains very difficult to identify such complex and context-dependent hate speech. Although they display excellent performance on multimodal tasks, vision-language models (VLMs) tend to ignore context, irony, and other subtle cues that play a key role in identifying hateful memes. In this work, we present a qualitative analysis of four state-of-the-art VLMs: LLaVA-7B, Qwen-VL, GPT-4o mini, and Claude 3 Haiku. We evaluate these models under zero-shot and few-shot prompting to examine how contextual framing influences their outputs. Our analysis goes beyond simple classification accuracy and focuses on a qualitative evaluation of the models' generated justifications, providing a more in-depth understanding of their thought processes and constraints when dealing with hateful memes.

</details>


### [23] [Why Current XAI Is Not Enough for Arabic NLP: A Critical Survey of the Explainability Gap](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26144)
*Salima Lamsiyah,Ruslan Mitkov*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper is a structured survey of Explainable AI methods for Arabic NLP, identifying key gaps in methods, tasks, and linguistic coverage, and proposing a taxonomy and research agenda for linguistically grounded Arabic XAI.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Explainable AI has grown rapidly in NLP, but Arabic NLP work has mostly adopted a narrow set of post-hoc techniques and focused on a small group of tasks without addressing Arabic’s specific linguistic, cultural, and sociotechnical characteristics. There is a need to understand what has been done, what is missing, and how to design explanations that are truly meaningful for Arabic.

Method: The authors conduct a critical, structured survey of existing work on explainability for Arabic NLP, covering text, speech, and multimodal settings. They analyze the literature along multiple dimensions: XAI methods used (e.g., post-hoc vs. other paradigms), task types, linguistic units, language varieties, goals of explanation, and evaluation practices. They synthesize these into a taxonomy that organizes the current landscape and reveals gaps.

Result: The survey finds three core gaps: (1) a method gap, where Arabic XAI mostly uses a few post-hoc tools like LIME/SHAP/attention/saliency instead of the broader XAI toolkit; (2) a task gap, where work is mostly on classification (sentiment, offensive language, fake news, spam) with little on generation, retrieval, translation, summarization, structured prediction, or dialogue; and (3) a linguistic gap, where explanations focus on token importance but rarely address Arabic-specific phenomena such as rich morphology, clitics, dialectal variation, diglossia, diacritics, orthographic ambiguity, code-switching, named entities, cultural references, or Classical and religious registers.

Conclusion: Arabic NLP requires explanations that are not only about model behavior but also grounded in the realities of Arabic as a language and sociotechnical system. The authors’ taxonomy clarifies the current state of Arabic XAI and highlights missing areas, leading to a research agenda that calls for more diverse XAI methods, broader task coverage, and linguistically informed explanations that capture Arabic-specific features and usage contexts.

Abstract: Explainable AI (XAI) is now a major theme in NLP; however, Arabic NLP remains under-explained in three connected senses. First, there is a method gap: Arabic XAI relies heavily on a small set of post-hoc techniques such as LIME, SHAP, attention visualization, and saliency, while broader NLP XAI offers richer diagnostic, counterfactual, probing, rationale-based, and human-centered methods. Second, there is a task gap: existing Arabic XAI work is concentrated in classification tasks, especially sentiment analysis, hate/offensive language detection, fake news, and spam, with weaker coverage of generation, retrieval, translation, summarization, structured prediction, and dialogue. Third, there is a linguistic gap: many explanations identify influential tokens, but rarely explain Arabic-specific phenomena such as morphology, clitics, dialectal variation, diglossia, orthographic ambiguity, diacritics, code-switching, named entities, cultural references, or Classical and religious registers. This critical structured survey synthesizes the reviewed literature on Arabic XAI across text, speech, and multimodal settings. We argue that Arabic NLP does not only need explanations of model decisions; it needs explanations that are faithful to Arabic as a linguistic, cultural, and sociotechnical object. We introduce a taxonomy of tasks, methods, linguistic units, varieties, goals, and evaluation practices, and propose a research agenda for linguistically grounded Arabic XAI.

</details>


### [24] [Vagdhenu: A Vrutta (Meter) Aware Shloka-to-Chant (TTS) System for Sanskrit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26146)
*Prathosh A P*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Vagdhenu is a Sanskrit shloka-to-chant TTS system that adds Sanskrit- and meter-specific frontends and prosody handling on top of an off-the-shelf flow-matching backbone, achieving high-fidelity metrical chanting and deployed at scale.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Indic TTS models, especially for Sanskrit chants, fail to capture correct meter (vrutta), Sanskrit-specific phonology, and high-fidelity chanting prosody, often due to assumptions like Hindi-style schwa deletion and limited prosody control. The authors are motivated to build a system that can accurately render traditional Sanskrit metrical recitations suitable for real-world devotional and educational deployments, using modern neural TTS while respecting linguistic and metrical constraints.

Method: They use an off-the-shelf flow-matching TTS backbone combined with a large-scale neural vocoder and augment it with: (1) a Sanskrit frontend that maps text via Kannada orthography to avoid spurious Hindi-style schwa deletion, (2) phonology-aware processing that handles visarga sandhi (with jihvamuliya and upadhmaniya), aspiration contrasts (alpaprana vs mahaprana), and keeping dental/retroflex/palatal sibilants distinct, and (3) a vrutta-aware module that detects poetic meter and selects a reference chant clip with a half-reference rule to match prosody. They perform comparative experiments across four TTS families (StyleTTS2, VITS2, Matcha-TTS, and the flow-matching backbone) and analyze prosody conditioning behavior, including a negative result on text-side prosody conditioning in self-infilling architectures. They fine-tune a 5-hour voice clone to overcome earlier ceilings on conjunct handling and prosody and evaluate using expert MOS.

Result: They find that in a self-infilling flow-matching TTS backbone, a text-side prosody conditioner is effectively inert because pitch can be reconstructed from context mel-spectrograms, so prosody embeddings do not receive useful gradients; consequently, prosody can only be reliably controlled through reference audio clips and voice-steering retraining. Across four TTS model families, earlier architectures hit performance ceilings on handling consonant conjuncts or chant prosody, while the five-hour cloned model achieved high quality with expert mean opinion scores near 4.6. The final Vagdhenu system supported two real deployments: a 32-chapter, 5183-verse video corpus (~17.5 hours) and an audio application covering about 18,000 verses across 12 books. They also successfully prepare and release code, weights, a chant dataset, and a demo.

Conclusion: A high-fidelity Sanskrit shloka-to-chant system can be built by reusing a strong flow-matching TTS backbone and vocoder, provided that one carefully designs Sanskrit-specific and meter-aware frontends and relies on reference-based prosody control instead of text-side prosody conditioning in self-infilling models. The architecture can scale to large real-world deployments and achieve expert-level audio quality, and the accompanying open-source release (frontend, code, weights, and dataset) enables others to reproduce and extend this chant-focused TTS pipeline.

Abstract: We present Vagdhenu, a vrutta (meter) aware shloka-to-chant system for Sanskrit: a text-to-speech system that maps
  a metrical verse to its chanted parayana recitation at high fidelity. This is an experience report, not a new
  architecture. We take an off-the-shelf flow-matching TTS backbone and a large-scale neural vocoder, and add the
  components a faithful Sanskrit chant pipeline needs: a frontend that routes Sanskrit through Kannada orthography to
  avoid the Hindi-style schwa deletion that Devanagari triggers in Indic models; a frontend that obeys subtle
  Sanskrit phonology (visarga sandhi with its jihvamuliya and upadhmaniya allophones, the aspiration contrast of
  alpaprana and mahaprana, and the dental, retroflex, and palatal sibilants kept distinct); and a vrutta-aware
  mechanism that detects the meter and picks an exactly matched reference under a half-reference rule. We report a
  negative result that shaped the system: in a self-infilling flow-matching backbone, a text-side prosody conditioner
  is architecturally inert, because the model recovers pitch from the context mel and the embedding gets no
  gradient; the reference clip and a voice-steering retrain are the only working prosody levers. We also report a
  comparative lineage across four families (StyleTTS2, VITS2, Matcha-TTS, and the flow-matching backbone), where each
  earlier family hit a ceiling on conjuncts or prosody that a five-hour clone cleared at an expert MOS near 4.6. The
  system shipped two deployments: a 32-chapter, 5183-verse video corpus (about 17.5 hours) and an audio app covering
  about 18000 verses across 12 books. We release the frontend, inference and training code, weights, a
  single-speaker chant dataset, and an interactive demo.

</details>


### [25] [CARE: Causally-Aligned Reasoning Exploration for Medical Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26147)
*Yucheng Zhou,Peng Luo,Qianning Wang,Chengzhong Xu,Jianbing Shen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Proposes CARE, a reinforcement learning framework that curates and filters model-generated reasoning trajectories so that LLMs learn medically correct answers for the right causal reasons, improving stability and reducing spurious reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs can perform medical reasoning but are limited by the high cost and scarcity of expert-labeled data. Existing RL approaches that rely on outcome-based rewards suffer from poor credit assignment and high gradient variance, often reinforcing spurious patterns—leading to models that reach correct diagnoses or answers but for incorrect or shortcut-based reasons. There is a need for a scalable, theoretically principled way to guide LLM learning toward causally valid clinical reasoning instead of dataset artifacts.

Method: Introduce CARE (Causally-Aligned Reasoning Exploration), an RL training framework that performs intrinsic experience curation for LLMs. CARE defines two formal conditions for selecting high-quality training trajectories: (1) Causal Sufficiency: via an agreement-based self-verification procedure that approximates do-calculus style interventions to identify and downweight trajectories relying on spurious correlations, thus debiasing gradients; (2) Proximal Learnability: by applying dynamic entropy-based bounds to keep training experiences within the model’s current ‘zone of proximal development,’ constraining optimization to regions with manageable variance. Selected experiences are then optimized with a dual-stream objective that combines on-policy, group-relative exploration (encouraging better reasoning among current candidates) and difficulty-weighted experience replay (prioritizing informative, appropriately challenging past trajectories).

Result: On a wide range of medical reasoning benchmarks, covering both multimodal (e.g., image-plus-text) and text-only tasks, CARE-trained models consistently outperform strong baseline methods. The framework reduces incidences where answers are correct but underlying rationales are inconsistent or flawed, while also yielding more stable training dynamics, e.g., lower gradient variance and more reliable performance improvements over time.

Conclusion: By enforcing causal sufficiency and proximal learnability in how reasoning trajectories are selected and optimized, CARE offers a principled way to align medical LLM training with valid clinical reasoning rather than dataset shortcuts. This leads to more trustworthy, stable, and high-performing medical reasoning models and suggests that carefully designed intrinsic experience curation can mitigate key limitations of outcome-only RL in safety-critical domains like medicine.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown strong potential for medical reasoning, yet the scarcity and cost of expert-annotated data constrain their progress. While reinforcement learning offers a scalable alternative, standard outcome-based methods in medicine often suffer from autoregressive credit assignment failure and gradient variance explosion. This leads to the "Right Answer, Wrong Reason" trap, where models inadvertently reinforce spurious correlations and dataset shortcuts rather than valid clinical deduction. In this work, we propose Causally-Aligned Reasoning Exploration (CARE), a theoretically grounded framework for intrinsic experience curation. CARE is built upon two rigorous conditions for high-quality training trajectories: Causal Sufficiency, which utilizes an agreement-based self-verification mechanism to mimic $do$-calculus interventions and effectively debias gradients; and Proximal Learnability, which employs dynamic entropy bounds to select experiences within the model's zone of proximal development for variance-bounded optimization. These rigorously filtered experiences are optimized via a dual-stream objective that combines on-policy group-relative exploration with difficulty-weighted experience replay. Extensive experiments on diverse medical multimodal and text-only benchmarks demonstrate that CARE consistently outperforms other strong competitors, substantially reducing correct-but-inconsistent reasoning and improving training stability.

</details>


### [26] [Towards Interpretable Depression Detection: Linking Acoustic Features to DSM-5 Indicators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26148)
*Jonas Länzlinger,Katharina O. E. Müller,Burkhard Stiller,Bruno Rodrigues*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Maps interpretable speech features (pitch, pauses, tempo) to DSM‑5 depression indicators via a transparent framework that runs privately on local hardware.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Depression is widespread, and current diagnosis depends heavily on subjective self-reports, which can overlook real behavioral signals. Existing machine-learning systems using speech are often black boxes, providing limited clinical interpretability and raising privacy concerns when data must be sent to the cloud. The paper aims to create an objective, interpretable, and privacy-preserving way to connect speech behavior with clinically recognized depressive symptoms.

Method: The authors design a Linkage Framework that explicitly connects low-level acoustic features of speech—such as pitch variability, pause characteristics, and speech tempo—to specific DSM-5 depressive-behavior indicators (e.g., psychomotor changes, concentration difficulty). Instead of end-to-end black-box prediction, they define interpretable mappings at the indicator level. They implement the system to run locally on commodity hardware to ensure data privacy and test it using the DAIC-WOZ depression dataset, examining whether the directions of relationships between acoustic features and clinical indicators align with clinical expectations.

Result: In preliminary experiments on the DAIC-WOZ dataset, the mapped relationships between acoustic features and DSM-5 indicators showed directionally consistent associations, particularly for psychomotor change and concentration difficulty. This means the system’s outputs agreed with expected clinical patterns (e.g., speech slowing linked to psychomotor retardation) rather than random or contradictory trends. These early results support the conceptual soundness and practical feasibility of the Linkage Framework, though they are not yet full-scale validations.

Conclusion: The study provides initial evidence that a transparent, interpretable framework can link speech acoustics to specific DSM-5 depressive-behavior indicators while running entirely on edge hardware to preserve privacy. The results are promising for building clinically meaningful, indicator-level tools rather than opaque, overall severity scores. The authors conclude that further validation on larger and longitudinal datasets and extensions to multimodal signals are needed, with an emphasis on keeping computation on-device to maintain privacy and practicality.

Abstract: Depression affects millions worldwide, yet diagnosis relies on subjective self-reports that may miss authentic behavior. This paper presents an approach linking speech acoustics to DSM-5 depressive-behavior indicators through a transparent Linkage Framework. Unlike black-box models, the framework explicitly maps acoustic features (pitch variability, pauses, speech tempo) to clinical indicators, enabling interpretable, indicator-level outputs. The system runs locally on commodity hardware (HW) to preserve privacy. Preliminary evaluation on DAIC-WOZ shows directionally consistent associations between acoustic features and DSM-5 indicators for psychomotor change and concentration difficulty, supporting the design rationale. Future work will validate on longitudinal datasets and extend multimodal integration while maintaining edge constraints.

</details>


### [27] [Artificial Intelligence Models Can Predict and Collaboratively Modulate Human Memory Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26152)
*Eric Lacosse,Mariana Duarte,Graham Todd,Peter M. Todd,Daniel C. McNamee*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies how large language models (LLMs) can follow and enhance human thought processes in a semantic memory task, showing they can predict human memory search trajectories better than other humans.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Although LLMs are powerful at language generation and problem solving, it is unclear how they can act as cognitive tools that extend human generative and creative thinking rather than replace it. The authors want to understand whether and how LLMs can support open-ended conceptual exploration by tracking and augmenting human mental trajectories.

Method: The authors use the semantic fluency task (SFT), a standard paradigm in cognitive psychology where participants must generate as many items as possible from a semantic category (e.g., animals). They compare human performance with LLM outputs on this task, specifically examining how well the LLM can follow, model, and predict human semantic memory search trajectories and transitions, benchmarking its predictive power against that of other humans.

Result: The study finds that LLMs are able to track and predict human semantic memory trajectories in the SFT more accurately than other humans can, suggesting that these models capture and anticipate human-like patterns of semantic exploration particularly well.

Conclusion: LLMs are not just language generators but can function as powerful cognitive partners, capable of following and even enhancing human mental exploration in semantic memory tasks. Their superior ability to predict human memory trajectories indicates strong potential for LLMs as tools to augment human generative and creative cognition.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) exhibit unprecedented natural language generation and many text-based problem-solving capabilities. Indeed, in many language-based tasks, for example routine coding, these artificial intelligence models have reduced, or even eliminated, the need for human input. But rather than replacing human cognitive effort, LLMs may instead serve as cognitive tools to extend human abilities, particularly when they are engaged in a task requiring open-ended conceptual exploration and creative ideation. However, we are yet to understand how these models may enhance such generative human cognitive abilities in human--AI interactions. In this study, we explore and evaluate the ability of LLMs to follow and enhance human mental trajectories during semantic memory search. To test this, we use the semantic fluency task (SFT), a classic cognitive paradigm requiring generative semantic memory retrieval that has long served to characterize convergent and divergent thinking in humans. We demonstrate that an LLM's abilities to track and predict human memory trajectories in this task exceed those of other humans.

</details>


### [28] [Evaluating AI Generated Summaries for Cancer Patients](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26154)
*Muhammad Aurangzeb Ahmad,Kim Shyu,Leon Oliver,Fergus Sleight,Paul Landau*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper evaluates and improves AI-generated medical summaries in a cancer care app using both human experts and LLMs as evaluators.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLMs are increasingly used to summarize complex medical data for patients, there are concerns about the accuracy, faithfulness, and safety of these summaries in clinical settings. The authors aim to understand how reliable these AI-generated summaries are and how to systematically improve them for safe use in patient care.

Method: The authors deploy AI-generated summaries within a cancer patient care application and assess them using a dual evaluation framework. First, oncology clinicians and patient-facing care staff rate summary quality along three dimensions: accuracy, clinical relevance, and readability, serving as ground-truth evaluators. Second, LLMs are used as evaluators (LLM-as-a-judge) in parallel to compare automated assessment against expert human judgment. Identified issues such as omissions and minor inaccuracies are analyzed and then fed back into iterative improvements of prompt design, data grounding, and safety guardrails.

Result: The evaluation reveals that while the AI-generated summaries are generally useful, they contain some limitations, including occasional omissions and minor inaccuracies. The dual assessment approach successfully surfaces these issues and demonstrates that LLM-as-a-judge can complement human expert evaluation. Insights from the error analysis guide targeted adjustments in prompting, grounding strategies, and safety mechanisms.

Conclusion: AI-generated summaries in a cancer care context can support patient communication but require careful evaluation and iterative refinement. Combining human expert assessments with LLM-based evaluation is a promising framework to identify and mitigate accuracy and safety issues. Systematic improvements to prompts, grounding, and safety guardrails can enhance the reliability and clinical suitability of LLM-generated medical summaries.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly being integrated into digital health platforms to generate summaries of complex medical data. Although these models can improve patient engagement and communication, these systems also raise concerns about accuracy, faithfulness, and safety in clinical contexts. In this study, we evaluate AI-generated summaries within a cancer patient care application using a dual assessment framework. Human domain experts, including oncology clinicians and patient-facing care staff, provided ground-truth evaluations of summary quality along dimensions of accuracy, clinical relevance, and readability. In parallel, we employed LLMs serving as evaluators (LLM-as-a-judge). Some limitations were identified in the generated summaries e.g., occasional omissions and minor inaccuracies. These were systematically analyzed and used to iteratively improve prompt design, grounding, and safety guardrails.

</details>


### [29] [VFA: Empowering Multilingual MLLMs via Vision-Free Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26155)
*Yixia Li,Yaqing Shi,Zhiwen Ruan,Dongdong Zhang,Lingjie Jiang,Shaohan Huang,Yun Chen,Guanhua Chen,Furu Wei*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces Vision-Free Adaptation (VFA), a method to make multimodal large language models strong in many languages using mostly text-only data, while preserving their vision-language alignment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most multimodal large language models (MLLMs) are English-centric because collecting high-quality non-English image–text training data is scarce and expensive. While multilingual text-only data is abundant, simply fine-tuning MLLMs on this data harms their visual alignment and causes catastrophic forgetting of multimodal abilities. The authors want a way to improve multilingual capabilities without breaking vision-language performance and without needing large multilingual multimodal datasets.

Method: They propose Vision-Free Adaptation (VFA), which separates multilingual language training from visual alignment using task vectors on a shared LLM backbone. First, they fine-tune a base LLM on multilingual text-only data and extract a "multilingual task vector" (the parameter delta representing multilingual skills). Separately, they have a "vision-aligned task vector" from an already-trained MLLM. They then compose/merge these two task vectors onto the same backbone LLM, effectively injecting multilingual capabilities into the MLLM without retraining on images. This avoids catastrophic interference with vision-language alignment.

Result: Across five different MLLMs and six multilingual multimodal benchmarks, VFA consistently improves multilingual multimodal performance while maintaining general multimodal and text-only performance. With less than 2% of the full multilingual text dataset, VFA recovers most of the benefit, narrowing the gap to a model trained with full multimodal multilingual supervision, which shows strong data efficiency.

Conclusion: Decoupling multilingual language enhancement from visual alignment via task vector composition is an effective, data-efficient way to multilingualize MLLMs. VFA provides improved multilingual multimodal performance without needing large multilingual image–text datasets and without degrading existing visual or language capabilities, suggesting a scalable path toward more globally capable MLLMs.

Abstract: Multimodal large language models have advanced rapidly, yet most remain English-centric, as scaling multilingual multimodal instruction tuning is limited by the scarcity and high cost of high-quality non-English image-text supervision. Although multilingual text data is abundant, naive textual fine-tuning can disrupt vision-language alignment and induce catastrophic forgetting. We propose Vision-Free Adaptation (VFA), a framework that decouples multilingual language enhancement from visual alignment by composing complementary task vectors over a shared LLM backbone. Specifically, we fine-tune a base LLM on multilingual text data to derive a multilingual task vector, which is then merged with the vision-aligned task vector of an MLLM. Experiments on five MLLMs across six multilingual multimodal benchmarks show consistent improvements while preserving both general multimodal and text-only capabilities. Moreover, using less than 2% of the text data, VFA narrows the gap to the fully multimodal-trained model, demonstrating its data efficiency.

</details>


### [30] [Self-Generated Text Recognition: Quality Heuristics, Cross-Task Transfer, and Downstream Bias in LLM Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26159)
*Jesse St. Amand,Callum Canavan,Sohaib Imran,Joseph Hewson,Aaron Lutz,Shi Feng,Puria Radmard,Lennie Wells*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies whether large language models can recognize their own generated text (Self-Generated Text Recognition, SGTR), shows that earlier conflicting results are due to different experimental setups, and demonstrates that training models for SGTR can generalize and influence their behavior as judges, with important safety implications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI safety tools often use LLMs as evaluators or monitors, assuming they will judge outputs fairly. However, if an LLM can recognize its own outputs, it may become biased, collude, or otherwise undermine safeguards. Prior studies about whether models actually have this self-recognition ability have reported inconsistent results, so there is a need to clarify when and how SGTR emerges and what it implies for safety-critical applications.

Method: The authors systematically evaluate 13–21 language models under six different SGTR “operationalizations” (experimental designs). They vary evaluation format (pairwise vs. single-text classification), conversation structure (whether candidate text is embedded as user or assistant messages), and the domain of the underlying task (e.g., coding, summarization). They analyze confounds such as a quality heuristic (attributing higher-quality text to the model itself) and conduct supervised fine-tuning (SFT) to enhance SGTR in one configuration, then test generalization to others. They also integrate SGTR-trained models into the AlpacaEval judging framework to study self-preference in evaluation tasks.

Result: SGTR accuracy depends strongly on experimental setup: pairwise vs. individual judgments, user vs. assistant tag placement, and content domain all significantly affect measured performance. A major confound is that models often treat perceived quality as a proxy for authorship, inflating apparent SGTR. Nonetheless, even accounting for this, some models show nontrivial practical SGTR. Fine-tuning for SGTR in one setting improves performance in other configurations, indicating some robustness of the learned capability. Moreover, models trained for SGTR exhibit increased preference for their own outputs when acting as judges in AlpacaEval.

Conclusion: Self-Generated Text Recognition is a real and practically relevant capability in some current LLMs, but its measured strength is highly sensitive to how experiments are designed. Training models explicitly for SGTR can generalize across setups and leads to stronger self-preference when models act as evaluators. Because this can bias automated oversight and evaluation pipelines, SGTR should be deliberately monitored and incorporated into the design and governance of safety-critical AI systems.

Abstract: Self-Generated Text Recognition (SGTR)--the ability of an LLM to identify its own outputs--poses risks to AI safeguards that rely on LLMs as evaluators or monitors. Specifically, an LLM may recognize outputs from other copies of the same model and make biased judgments or collude outright. Prior work has drawn conflicting conclusions about whether current models possess significant SGTR capabilities. We reconcile these findings by identifying key experimental design choices--which we term operationalizations--that drive divergent results. Evaluating 13-21 models across six operationalizations, we find that accuracy varies substantially with evaluation format (pairwise vs. individual assessments of text), conversation structure (presenting candidate text in user tags vs. assistant tags), and the domain of the task used to generate candidate text (e.g., coding vs. summarization). We corroborate previous observations that a quality heuristic--models attributing authorship to text they perceive as higher quality--is a dominant confound. We also find that improving a model's SGTR performance via SFT in one evaluation configuration can generalize to others. Training for SGTR additionally causes models to prefer their own outputs when acting as a judge in the AlpacaEval framework. Finally, we discuss the implications of our evaluations for the safety of future AI systems: our work suggests that, despite confounds, some models possess practical SGTR capabilities, and that training a model for SGTR in one setting can affect its self-recognition and self-preference more generally. We conclude that SGTR should be monitored and considered in the design of safety-critical AI applications.

</details>


### [31] [Mutual Debiasing via Dual-Seed Comparison for Probabilistic Sampling in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26161)
*Zihao Guo,Hongtao Lv,Chaoli Zhang,Laiguo Yin,Lei Liu,Yonghui Xu,Lizhen Cui*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They propose Dual-Seed Comparison (DSC), a simple, tool-free protocol that uses two independent LLM-generated seeds and their character-wise comparison to produce far less biased random samples from arbitrary target distributions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are good at reasoning and decision-making but bad at unbiased probabilistic sampling: when asked to generate random variables, they show systematic deviations from desired probability distributions. Existing methods typically rely on a single self-generated seed, which carries the model’s intrinsic biases and leads to distorted sampling. There is a need for a transparent, low-overhead method that can reduce or cancel these biases while staying within the LLM’s native text interface.

Method: The paper introduces Dual-Seed Comparison (DSC). The LLM generates two independent text seeds. For each character position, the method compares the ordinal (e.g., ASCII/Unicode) values of the characters from the two seeds to produce a bit (e.g., 0 if one is smaller, 1 if larger, ignoring ties). This yields a bit sequence, which is interpreted as an integer, then normalized into a pseudo-uniform random variate on [0,1). Using the inverse CDF transform, this uniform variate is mapped into samples from an arbitrary target distribution. The protocol is tool-free (only uses the LLM’s own text output) and transparent (explicit comparison operations). Variants of this comparison operator are adapted for specific tasks like MCQ option selection and constrained text-to-image prompting.

Result: Across a wide set of experimental settings, DSC significantly reduces sampling bias compared to prior LLM-based randomization schemes, outperforming them in about 96% of evaluated cases. The empirical evaluation covers various target distributions and downstream tasks. Furthermore, task-adapted DSC variants improve the distributional properties of outputs in multiple-choice question generation and in text-to-image prompts where attributes must follow certain probability constraints.

Conclusion: Using two independent LLM-generated seeds and a simple character-wise comparison is an effective way to neutralize model-specific biases in probabilistic sampling. DSC provides a practical, transparent, and tool-free protocol for turning biased LLM outputs into higher-fidelity samples from desired distributions. Its strong performance across most tested conditions and its successful adaptations to applied tasks suggest it is a promising building block for more controlled, probabilistically grounded use of LLMs.

Abstract: Although Large Language Models (LLMs) demonstrate remarkable capabilities in reasoning and decision-making, high-fidelity probabilistic sampling remains a persistent challenge. When generating random variables, LLMs consistently exhibit systematic biases that warp the target probability distributions. Current approaches often rely on a single, self-generated seed, which inherits model-specific biases. To overcome this vulnerability, we introduce Dual-Seed Comparison (DSC), a transparent, tool-free protocol that utilizes two independent LLM-generated seeds to neutralize bias. DSC compares the character-level ordinal values of the two seeds to construct a bit sequence, converts and normalizes this sequence into a pseudo-uniform variate, and then maps the variate to the target distribution through the inverse cumulative distribution function (CDF). Empirical results show that DSC substantially outperforms existing methods across 96\% of evaluated settings. Beyond direct sampling, task-adapted variants based on the DSC comparison operator improve distributional control in MCQ generation and attribute-constrained text-to-image prompting.

</details>


### [32] [From Sound to Symptom: Real-Time Respiratory Signal Understanding for Conversational Healthcare Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26163)
*Tanmay Laud,Herprit Mahal,Subhabrata Mukherjee*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces HealthCUES, a real-time system that detects and analyzes cough-related respiratory signals during live conversations, turning what is usually treated as noise into clinically useful information.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Spoken dialogue systems and telehealth platforms often discard coughs and similar sounds as noise, missing potentially valuable respiratory health indicators. There is a lack of systems that can reliably and in real time monitor and interpret such paralinguistic respiratory events within natural conversations without disrupting dialogue flow.

Method: The authors build HealthCUES, a streaming pipeline that processes conversational audio in a rolling buffer aligned with dialogue turns. Using Qwen3Omni, a multimodal large language model with constrained structured outputs, they decompose cough analysis into parallel prediction tasks: detection, differentiation from throat clearing, subtype classification (dry, wet, barking, whooping) with confidence scores, and temporal boundary estimation. They also design dialogue-aware gating mechanisms that decide when to surface alerts based on conversational context. The system is evaluated on a proprietary conversational audio dataset and externally on the AMI meeting corpus, and its clinical utility is assessed via a user study with healthcare professionals.

Result: On 847 in-house conversational audio segments, HealthCUES achieves 93% F1 for cough detection, 0.75 weighted-F1 for wet/dry subtype classification, and an average end-to-end latency of 340 ms. On the external AMI meeting corpus, it maintains robust performance in separating coughs, throat clearing, and speech under overlapping speech conditions, reaching 0.91 macro-F1. The clinician user study indicates that detailed subtype information is considered clinically meaningful and that the system is viewed as useful for telehealth workflows.

Conclusion: HealthCUES demonstrates that cough and related respiratory sounds in natural conversation can be captured and analyzed in real time by dialogue systems, providing fine-grained, clinically relevant respiratory monitoring without disrupting interaction. Its strong quantitative performance, low latency, and positive clinician feedback suggest it can augment telehealth and conversational agents with continuous respiratory assessment capabilities.

Abstract: Cough events during live spoken conversations carry clinically valuable respiratory signals, yet existing dialogue systems treat them as acoustic noise to be discarded. We present HealthCUES (Clinical Understanding from Embodied Sounds), a streaming pipeline for paralinguistic respiratory monitoring in real-time conversational agents, a capability that, to the best of our knowledge, is absent from all prior systems. HealthCUES processes audio through a rolling buffer aligned with dialogue turn boundaries, enabling sub-second event detection without interrupting conversational flow. Beyond binary cough detection, the system provides fine-grained analytics: (i) differentiation between coughing and throat clearing, (ii) cough subtype classification (dry, wet, barking, whooping) with confidence scores, and (iii) temporal duration estimation with start-end boundaries. To prevent alert fatigue, HealthCUES introduces dialogue-aware gating mechanisms that modulate triggering based on conversational context. The system leverages Qwen3Omni, a multimodal large language model (MLLM), with constrained structured outputs, decomposing cough analysis into parallel prediction tasks for independent prompt optimization. Evaluation on 847 in-house conversational audio segments demonstrates 93\% F1 for cough detection, 0.75 weighted-F1 for wet/dry subtype classification, and average end-to-end latency of 340ms; external validation on the AMI meeting corpus confirms robust cough, throat-clearing, and speech separation in the presence of speech (0.91 macro-F1). A user study with licensed healthcare professionals confirms the clinical relevance of subtype information and the system's utility in telehealth workflows.

</details>


### [33] [Using Poly-Encoders for Computationally Efficient Automated Creativity Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26165)
*Sam Grouchnikov,Phillip Gregory,Jiho Noh*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They propose an efficient automated creativity scoring method using Poly-Encoder models that reach LLM-level accuracy while running on modest hardware.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing automated creativity assessments are either too computationally expensive (e.g., large language models) or not accurate enough, limiting their practical use in large-scale or everyday settings such as education.

Method: They fine-tune small BERT-based Poly-Encoder models on about 18,000 human-scored responses from the Scientific Creative Thinking Test, varying BERT-family backbones and the number of poly codes, and evaluate performance via correlation with human raters.

Result: Certain BERT-based Poly-Encoder configurations achieve Pearson correlations up to r = 0.74 (95% CI [0.73, 0.75]) with human raters, matching the performance of more resource-intensive LLM approaches while being computationally cheaper.

Conclusion: Poly-Encoders built on small BERT models can deliver human-level, LLM-comparable creativity assessment accuracy at far lower computational cost, making scalable, practical deployment—especially in educational contexts—much more feasible, though some limitations remain.

Abstract: Automated creativity assessment has been a long standing challenge, with traditional methods often being resource intensive or lacking practical accuracy. We introduce a novel approach by using Poly-Encoder for computationally efficient and accurate automated creativity assessment. We fine-tuned a Poly-Encoder on a public dataset from the Scientific Creative Thinking Test, comprised of approximately 18,000 human-rated question responses. Our method leverages small pre-trained BERT encoders, achieving performance comparable to fine-tuned Large Language Models while significantly reducing computational demands. Experiments with the BERT-family models and poly-code counts achieved Pearson correlations of up to r = 0.74, 95% CI [0.73, 0.75] with human raters, matching the performance of resource intensive LLMs. This study bridges the gap between high performance and computational efficiency, potentially enabling widespread implementation of automated creativity assessment on accessible consumer-grade hardware. With some limitations, our findings suggest that Poly-Encoders are a promising alternative to LLMs for practical, scalable creativity assessment in various contexts, especially educational.

</details>


### [34] [Hallucinations in LLMs: A Lifecycle-Based Survey of Causes, Detection, Mitigation, and Prevention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26168)
*Naveen Lamba,Sanju Tiwari,Manas Gaur*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Survey proposing a lifecycle-based framework for understanding and handling hallucinations in large language models (LLMs), covering their causes, detection, mitigation, prevention, and benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hallucinations in LLMs threaten reliability and safety in critical domains like healthcare, law, and scientific research. Existing surveys are mostly fragmented, focusing on single aspects such as detection or mitigation. There is a need for a unified, lifecycle-oriented perspective that aligns hallucination analysis with how LLMs are actually developed and deployed, to support systematic control and governance of these models.

Method: The paper conducts a structured survey of prior work on hallucinations in LLMs, organizing it according to the LLM development and usage lifecycle. It introduces a three-fold taxonomy—data-related, training-related, and inference-related hallucinations—and, for each stage, reviews known causes, detection techniques, and mitigation or prevention strategies. It also systematically examines existing benchmark datasets along several parameters to assess their suitability for studying hallucinations.

Result: The authors produce: (1) a lifecycle-based taxonomy of hallucinations in LLMs, (2) a mapping of causes, detection methods, and interventions (mitigation/prevention) to each lifecycle stage, and (3) an organized overview and comparison of benchmark datasets relevant to hallucination analysis and control. This yields a structured reference for positioning existing and future research within a standardized framework.

Conclusion: A lifecycle-centric view of hallucinations offers a coherent framework to understand where and why hallucinations arise, how to detect them, and how to intervene effectively at different phases of LLM development and deployment. By aligning hallucination research with the data, training, and inference stages, and by cataloging benchmarks, the survey aims to guide researchers and practitioners in systematically diagnosing and reducing hallucinations, ultimately helping to build safer and more reliable LLMs for high-stakes applications.

Abstract: The lifecycle of hallucination in LLMs is a concept that enables building solid frameworks on the control and reliability of LLMs in high-stakes environments, including health, legal, and scientific research. Although previous surveys have primarily focused on detection or mitigation, this survey provides a lifecycle-based overview of the hallucinations in the LLMs, their cause, detection, mitigation, and prevention.We propose a three-fold categorization of hallucinations across the LLM lifecycle: data-related, training-related, and inference-related, which is consistent with the lifecycle of the development of the LLM. Each of these stages is discussed regarding the cause of hallucinations, their detection, and the ways they can be addressed under specific mitigation or prevention interventions. In addition, we discuss the available benchmark data using a number of parameters so as to establish their suitability in identifying, restricting and managing hallucinations. The survey provides researchers and practitioners with a standardized framework to understand, diagnose, and cure hallucinations in a systematic system to present actionable data to build safer and more reliable LLMs.

</details>


### [35] [Lost in Compression: A Controlled Cross-Lingual Audit of Extractive Prompt Compressors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26175)
*Mantas Lukauskas*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper audits learned extractive prompt compression methods for large language models across multiple languages and finds that English-supervised compressors work much worse for non-English, often destroying useful context, while a multilingual compressor avoids this—unless retrained on translated (English-origin) data. It concludes that safe compression budgets are much smaller outside English and that translate-then-compress can be a better strategy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prompt compression is used to reduce LLM inference costs, but most work and evaluation are English-centric, while many other languages already suffer a token-cost premium. It is unknown whether learned compression mitigates or amplifies this inequality across languages and scripts. The authors want to quantify this, understand its causes, and propose safer practices.

Method: The authors use fully parallel datasets in ten languages covering five scripts, ensuring the same semantic content across languages. They compare four learned extractive compressors (three trained with English-only supervision and one multilingual) against four deterministic baselines. They evaluate these on eleven different target LLMs from ten vendors, under matched token budgets in each model’s tokenizer. They measure normalized context utilization (utility vs. uncompressed prompts), study behavior at different keep-rates, and test a long-context setting. They also experiment with a translate-then-compress pipeline.

Result: (1) There is a strong cross-lingual transfer gap for English-supervised learned compressors: at 0.33 keep-rate, English retains over half of its context utility but many non-English languages retain little to none, with Chinese severely affected despite its low token premium. (2) The gap aligns with the language of compression supervision rather than model architecture; all English-trained compressors show it, deterministic methods do not, and the multilingual compressor XProvence v1 does not. But XProvence v2, retrained on translated data, catastrophically erases Chinese context at aggressive thresholds. (3) In long-context tasks, aggressive learned compression drops non-English performance to or below that of having no context. (4) A translate-then-compress pipeline can match or outperform native-language compression at about half the token cost for several languages.

Conclusion: English-supervised learned prompt compressors do not transfer reliably to other languages and can severely degrade or erase useful context, especially under aggressive compression. The problem is driven by supervision data, not architecture, and can reappear when multilingual models are retrained on English-origin translated data. Deterministic baselines and a translate-then-compress strategy can be safer, but overall, safe compression budgets for non-English languages are substantially smaller than for English. Practitioners should be cautious when deploying learned compression in multilingual settings and should validate or adapt compressors per language.

Abstract: Extractive prompt compression promises to cut LLM inference costs by removing low-information tokens, and learned compressors such as LLMLingua-2 report strong results on English benchmarks. Most other languages already pay a token premium: the same content costs 1.3-1.8x more tokens than in English. We ask whether compression closes or widens this gap. Using fully parallel data in ten languages spanning five scripts, with controls budget-matched in the target model's tokenizer, we audit four learned compressors against four deterministic baselines, on eleven target models from ten vendors (over 250,000 evaluation calls). Three of the compressors are trained with English supervision (LLMLingua-2 XLM-R/mBERT; Kompress-v2 from the production Headroom stack); the fourth, XProvence, is trained multilingually. First, the transfer gap is real, replicates across target models and compressor backbones, and is strongly rate-dependent: at a 0.33 keep-rate English retains 57-62% of normalized context utilization while Lithuanian retains 10-24% and Chinese essentially none, despite Chinese having the smallest token premium. Second, the gap tracks compression supervision data, not architecture. All three English-trained compressors show it, deterministic methods show no comparable gap, and the multilingually trained XProvence v1 shows none. Its v2 release, retrained on translated data, empties 92% of Chinese contexts at its aggressive threshold without any warning. Third, in a harder long-context setting, aggressive learned compression drives compressed contexts to or below no-context utility in three of five non-English languages. A translate-then-compress pipeline matches or beats native compression at roughly half the token cost in three of five tested languages. We release all code, compressions, and model outputs. Safe compression budgets are much smaller outside English.

</details>


### [36] [A Multi-Framework Comparison of Outline Stages in Long-Form Generation with LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26177)
*Yifan Song*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper analyzes how different long-form text generation frameworks perform at various granularities (chapter, multi-chapter, whole-book) and introduces a direct evaluation method for outlines, showing that outline quality and final writing quality only moderately correlate.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-form text generation remains challenging for large language models, which suffer from issues like length collapse and attribute drift over long outputs. Current practice widely uses an outline-first approach, but existing evaluations mostly judge the final written text, not the quality of the outlines themselves, conflating two distinct components of the generation process. There is a need for a benchmark and evaluation protocol that separately and systematically assess outline quality across different long-form generation frameworks and granularities.

Method: The authors build a unified head-to-head benchmark comparing 7 representative long-form generation frameworks across 3 generation granularities: single-chapter, multi-chapter, and whole-book. They propose an anchor-based LLM-as-a-judge protocol that evaluates outlines directly against the source text using a 5-point anchored scale. They then measure performance across 21 framework–granularity combinations and compare outline-side rankings to writing-side rankings, while noting that writing-side evaluation is limited by compute and currently performed only on a subset of the full benchmark.

Result: No framework is universally best across all tasks; performance varies depending on how well each framework’s intrinsic output format aligns with the target granularity. SuperWriter performs best in the length-constrained single-chapter setting, but this benefit diminishes in whole-book generation. The correlation between outline-side and writing-side rankings is only moderate, indicating that good outlines do not always translate to the best final writing and supporting the need to evaluate these stages separately.

Conclusion: Outline-first long-form generation should be evaluated by decoupling outline quality from final writing quality, as their rankings differ meaningfully. The proposed benchmark and anchor-based LLM-judge protocol enable more fine-grained assessment of long-form generation frameworks across different granularities. However, current writing-side results are limited by compute; future work will scale up evaluation and add cross-model judges to improve statistical robustness.

Abstract: Long-form generation exposes fundamental limitations of large language models. Even 70B-parameter models exhibit length collapse at 16k-token outputs, and multi-chapter stories frequently trigger the attribute drift characteristic of the ``lost-in-the-middle'' effect. The ``outline-first, write-later'' paradigm has gained wide adoption, yet existing research evaluates the final writing rather than the outline itself, conflating two evaluation objects that should be decoupled. We construct a unified head-to-head benchmark covering 7 representative long-form generation frameworks across 3 generation granularities -- single-chapter, multi-chapter, and whole-book -- and propose an anchor-based LLM-as-a-judge protocol that directly assesses outlines against the source text on a 5-point anchored scale. Across 21 framework-granularity cells, no single framework dominates; performance depends on the match between a framework's intrinsic output form and the target granularity. SuperWriter ranks first in the length-constrained single-chapter mode, but this advantage degrades in whole-book mode. The outline-side ranking correlates only moderately with the writing-side ranking, supporting the outline--writing decoupling principle. Compute constraints limit the writing-side evaluation to a subset of cases; follow-up experiments will expand the sample size and add cross-model evaluators to enable stronger statistical inference.

</details>


### [37] [PACEShop: Evaluating Personalized, Actionable, Compositional, and Evidence-grounded Shopping Assistants](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26180)
*Weimin Lyu,Chen Luo,Guangrui Li,Yaochen Xie,Dhineshkumar Ramasubbu,Arief Koesdwiady,Wanqiu Long,Hansu Gu,Yutong Chen,Zheshen Wang,Dakuo Wang,Yi Liu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They introduce PACE, a new way to evaluate structured shopping-assistant responses, plus a dataset (PACEShop) and a judging protocol (PACEJudge) that better capture whether assistant outputs are personalized, actionable, compositional, and grounded in evidence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern shopping assistants must give coherent, step-by-step recommendations that integrate shopper context, product evidence, and guidance, not just ranked lists. Existing benchmarks test personalization, grounding, or LLM judging in isolation and miss failures like ignoring persona, cross-component inconsistencies, or vague defect localization. There is no unified evaluation target for these structured responses, making it hard to diagnose where systems fail.

Method: They define the PACE evaluation target: Personalized, Actionable, Compositional, Evidence-grounded. They operationalize it with (1) PACEShop, a benchmark of 22,625 controlled shopping records that include structured personas, curated evidence pools, GOOD/BAD labels, and fine-grained annotations of defect type and location; and (2) PACEJudge, a training-free judging protocol that uses a structured output schema so judges must explicitly fill in diagnostic fields rather than just giving scalar scores.

Result: Experiments comparing generic LLM judges to PACEJudge show that generic judges can roughly distinguish good vs. bad responses but fail to reliably recover detailed diagnostic fields required by PACE (e.g., which persona constraint is violated, where grounding breaks, or which component is inconsistent). Using PACEShop reveals these failures, and the structured PACEJudge protocol significantly improves accuracy on persona consistency, cross-component consistency, evidence grounding, and identifying defect family and location, all without retraining the judge model.

Conclusion: Evaluating realistic shopping assistants requires an evaluation target and output contract tailored to structured recommendation tasks, not just stronger models or simple scalar judgments. PACE, instantiated via PACEShop and PACEJudge, provides measurable and reportable diagnostics for how well assistants generate personalized, actionable, compositional, and evidence-grounded shopping recommendations.

Abstract: Shopping assistants are shifting from ranked product lists toward structured decision support, where systems must synthesize shopper context, product evidence, and next-step guidance into a coherent recommendation experience. This changes the unit of evaluation: a fluent response can still fail by ignoring shopper context, contradicting itself across components, or leaving defects too vague to localize. Existing personalization, grounding, and LLM-as-a-judge benchmarks cover pieces of this problem, but they do not define a joint evaluation target for structured shopping-assistant responses. We formulate this missing evaluation target as PACE: Personalized, Actionable, Compositional, and Evidence-grounded evaluation. We instantiate PACE with two artifacts: PACEShop, a benchmark dataset that makes the target measurable through 22,625 controlled records with structured personas, auditable evidence pools, GOOD/BAD labels, and gold defect family and location annotations; and PACEJudge, a training-free judging protocol that makes the target reportable through a structured output contract. Our experiments show that generic judges can recognize broad quality but fail to recover the diagnostic fields required for PACE; PACEShop makes these failures verifiable, and PACEJudge improves persona-source, cross-component, grounding, and family/location closure without retraining, showing that realistic shopping-assistant evaluation requires a task-matched output contract rather than only a stronger backbone or scalar prompt.

</details>


### [38] [Investigating the Influence of Prompt and Response Languages on LLM Content Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26186)
*Thi Thanh Nhan Nguyen,Mai Khoi Tieu,Michael A. Riegler,Pål Halvorsen,Thu Nguyen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper investigates how the language used in prompts (English vs. Norwegian) affects the length, semantics, and lexical properties of large language model responses, showing that prompt language systematically shapes outputs even when the response language is fixed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand whether and how the choice of prompt language in a multilingual context biases or shapes the behavior of large language models, especially regarding response length and lexical realization, and to inform better multilingual prompt design.

Method: The authors used five different large language models and posed 68 non-translation questions under four language conditions: English prompt–English response, English prompt–Norwegian response, Norwegian prompt–Norwegian response, and Norwegian prompt–English response. After filtering out refusals, they analyzed 1348 responses. They quantified length differences using Cohen’s d, measured semantic fidelity using LabSE cosine similarity, and evaluated cross-lingual keyword overlap with both raw Jaccard and soft Jaccard measures.

Result: Prompt language significantly affected response length: with English outputs, Norwegian prompts shortened responses by about 37%, and with Norwegian outputs, English prompts shortened responses by about 41%. The biggest cross-lingual contrast showed fewer words but a smaller reduction in tokens due to tokenizer differences. Despite length variation, semantic similarity across conditions stayed high, and soft Jaccard indicated substantial conceptual overlap that raw Jaccard underestimated. Effect sizes differed across models, indicating heterogeneous behavior.

Conclusion: Prompt language is not a neutral choice; it systematically shapes output length and lexical realization in large language models, even when the target output language is held constant. Semantic content generally remains similar, but the form and brevity of responses change in systematic, model-dependent ways. This has direct implications for designing effective multilingual prompts and for interpreting model outputs across languages.

Abstract: This study examines how prompt and response language influence the behavior of large language models. Using five models, we evaluated answers to 68 non translation questions across four language conditions: English to English, English to Norwegian, Norwegian to Norwegian, and Norwegian to English. After removing refused items, the dataset contains 1348 responses. We measure length differences with Cohen d, semantic fidelity with LabSE cosine similarity, and cross lingual keyword overlap with both raw and soft Jaccard. Prompt language has a strong effect on response length. With English output, Norwegian prompts shorten responses by about thirty seven percent. With Norwegian output, English prompts shorten responses by about forty one percent. The largest cross lingual contrast shows a reduction in word count but a smaller reduction in tokens, reflecting tokenizer differences. Despite variation in length, semantic similarity remains high, and soft Jaccard reveals substantial conceptual overlap that raw Jaccard does not capture. Effect sizes vary across models, indicating heterogeneity. Prompt language is not neutral and systematically shapes output length and lexical realization, with implications for multilingual prompt design.

</details>


### [39] [When the Canonical Completion Is Wrong: Formalizing and Measuring the Jump in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26187)
*Dai Shi,Xiaoyu Li,José Miguel Hernández-Lobato*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper formally defines and tests a specific kind of “abductive jump” for large language models: abandoning a default, canonical completion of partial data in favor of a different, uniquely correct solution under constraints. The authors construct machine‑checkable instances where the correct completion is provably not the canonical one (given by Kan extensions), then empirically test whether frontier LLMs still cling to the default when constrained. Models never revert to the default in 248 constrained trials, suggesting that, at least for this step, LLMs can perform the required jump; any limitation must lie elsewhere, e.g., in inventing constraints or frameworks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is an active debate about whether LLMs can perform genuine abductive ‘jumps’—moving from observed data to a new system of axioms or theory, rather than just interpolating within existing patterns. Some argue LLMs are structurally incapable of such leaps, but existing arguments lack a precise, testable formalization of what the jump is and how to measure it. The authors aim to (1) give a rigorous mathematical account of one core step in the jump process, and (2) design a benchmark where success or failure is unambiguously checkable, to empirically probe the alleged incapacity.

Method: The authors decompose the notion of a jump into four conceptual steps: (1) define the default completion of partial data, (2) characterize when one is forced to abandon this default, (3) specify when the abandonment is correct, and (4) describe how multiple jumps compound. They then focus on formally measuring step (2).

Technically, they model a jump instance as a finite extension problem with the following properties:
- There exists a correct completion of the data.
- This completion is unique up to renaming.
- It is provably different from the canonical (default) completion of the data.

The canonical completion is defined category‑theoretically via left and right Kan extensions; empirically, it coincides with what unconstrained LLMs tend to output. For each jump instance, they use machine‑checked certificates to ensure these properties hold and to verify that the problem is well‑posed.

They prove a family theorem that generates infinitely many instances of unbounded difficulty without brute‑force enumeration, and they formalize correctness conditions for a jump and how successive jumps compose. Finally, they select nine certified instances and test four frontier LLMs under constrained prompting, measuring whether models stick to the Kan‑default or adopt the non‑default, but correct, completion under the given constraints.

Result: The theoretical results show that jump instances, as defined via Kan extensions and extension problems, are mathematically well‑posed and can be systematically generated at arbitrarily high difficulty via a family theorem, avoiding ad‑hoc construction. They also provide a formal framework for evaluating correctness and composition of jumps beyond single steps.
