Effective alignment methods must be selective—reducing Unsupported-Yielding while preserving or enhancing Rational-Updating—potentially by developing finer-grained, mechanism-aware interventions rather than broad-brush penalties on changing answers or agreeing with users.

Abstract: Large language models often exhibit sycophancy, revising their answers to align with users when users push back. Such answer flips, however, can arise from different causes. One possibility is that the model simply aligns with the user's feedback in order to satisfy them. Another is that the feedback genuinely contains useful evidence, prompting the model to update its answer in a rational way. We distinguish them as Unsupported-Yielding and Rational-Updating. Prior work focuses primarily on suppressing Unsupported-Yielding, while overlooking its effect on Rational-Updating. We address this gap with a two-turn evaluation framework that measures the two behaviors separately. Across representative training-time and inference-time interventions, we find that anti-sycophancy methods often encounter a trade-off in which reducing Unsupported-Yielding can sacrifice Rational-Updating, and vice versa, even when the two objectives are optimized jointly. Mechanistic analysis suggests that the two behaviors share an internal substrate: the MLP neurons and attention heads driving them overlap substantially, and their associated steering directions are positively aligned. We further conduct a preliminary orthogonalized steering exploration, which yields modest, backbone-dependent selectivity gains. Overall, our results suggest that anti-sycophancy should be treated not as a simple suppression problem, but as a selectivity problem, where effective interventions should preserve Rational-Updating while reducing Unsupported-Yielding.

</details>


### [59] [Multi-Expert Conformal Risk Control for Pairwise LLM Judging in Open-Ended Dialogue](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26529)
*Ming Cheng,Yusheng Dai,Qiuhong Ke,Zhaolin Chen,Lizhen Qu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes multi-expert Conformal Risk Control methods to make LLM-as-a-judge pairwise dialogue evaluation more reliable, especially with heterogeneous panels of LLM judges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-as-a-judge is popular for evaluating open-ended dialogue, but individual LLM judges can be unreliable and have inconsistent scoring scales, especially when multiple heterogeneous LLMs are used. Existing Conformal Risk Control methods help by abstaining near a decision threshold but do not address inconsistencies in the underlying scores themselves. The authors aim to combine conformal guarantees with multi-expert aggregation to improve both reliability and coverage when multiple LLM judges are available.

Method: They formulate a multi-expert Conformal Risk Control framework for pairwise LLM judgments. Two basic aggregation schemes are introduced: Score Averaging, which averages expert scores before applying CRC, and Decision Voting, which aggregates binary decisions from each expert. Observing that these fail to fully recover coverage on heterogeneous panels due to differing expert scales, they propose Marginal-Calibrated Conformal Consensus (MC3). MC3 introduces per-expert threshold ratios to capture each expert’s scoring scale, while learning a single unified decision function C_t(x) used identically in calibration and testing to maintain exchangeability. They also construct a new benchmark, Panel, with 1,800 human-labeled pairwise preferences based on responses from four open-weight LLMs on three dialogue datasets, with full logit access.

Result: On homogeneous expert panels, both Score Averaging and Decision Voting outperform single-expert CRC in terms of accuracy and acceptance rate while maintaining risk validity. On heterogeneous panels of LLM judges, the naive aggregation methods stay risk-valid but achieve limited coverage because a single threshold cannot accommodate diverse scoring scales. MC3 successfully adapts to these differing scales and extends the accuracy and acceptance gains of multi-expert CRC to heterogeneous panels across all three dialogue datasets in the Panel benchmark.

Conclusion: Multi-expert aggregation and Conformal Risk Control are complementary: CRC manages risk via abstention at the decision threshold, while aggregation improves the quality of the underlying score function. Simple aggregation strategies help on homogeneous expert panels but struggle with heterogeneous LLM judges due to scale mismatch. The proposed MC3 method resolves this by modeling per-expert scales while preserving conformal validity, thereby providing more accurate and higher-coverage LLM-as-a-judge evaluations across varied expert panels, as demonstrated on the new Panel benchmark.

Abstract: In this paper, we explore multi-expert Conformal Risk Control (CRC) algorithms for pairwise LLM-as-a-Judge evaluation in open-ended dialogue. Our core insight is that multi-expert aggregation offers a complementary remedy to CRC: whereas CRC controls risk at the decision threshold through abstention, aggregation sanitizes the scoring function at its source. Guided by this, we first design two multi-expert CRC methods: Score Averaging and Decision Voting, which aggregate at the score and decision levels, respectively. While both strategies outperform single-expert methods on homogeneous expert panels, on heterogeneous LLM judges they remain risk-valid but recover only limited coverage, because a uniform threshold cannot match the experts' distinct scoring scales. To resolve this issue, we further propose Marginal-Calibrated Conformal Consensus (MC3): it captures distinct per-expert scales via initial threshold ratios, while jointly tuning a unified decision function $C_t(x)$ applied identically in both calibration and test, thereby preserving exchangeability. To evaluate our framework, we construct Panel, a 1,800-pair human pairwise-preference benchmark for open-ended dialogue. It is built on responses generated by four open-weight LLMs over dialogue contexts from three domains (ESConv, MSC, DREAM), with full logit access. In experiments, we find that both Score Averaging and Decision Voting substantially improve accuracy and acceptance rate on homogeneous panels. Notably, MC3 extends these gains to heterogeneous panels by accommodating distinct per-expert scoring scales across all three datasets.

</details>


### [60] [SPEAR: Distilling Domain-Adaptive Reasoning Skeletons via Sequential Symbolic Alignment in Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26550)
*Zhuochun Li,Yuelyu Ji,Yiming Zeng,Daqing He*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes SPEAR, a training-free symbolic process reward method that uses LCS-based alignment between teacher and student reasoning traces to provide dense, order-aware rewards for reinforcement-learning-based knowledge distillation, effectively narrowing the reasoning gap with low computational cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RL-based knowledge distillation for reasoning must either rely on sparse, outcome-only rewards that lack guidance about intermediate reasoning steps, or on expensive neural process reward models that provide dense signals but introduce significant computational overhead and complexity. There is a need for an efficient, interpretable, training-free way to reward stepwise reasoning alignment between teacher and student without training separate neural verifiers.

Method: SPEAR (Symbolic Process Evaluation and Alignment Reward) is introduced as a plug-and-play, training-free process reward method. It converts natural-language reasoning traces from the teacher into domain-adaptive symbolic milestones, then compares the student’s generated reasoning against these milestones using the longest common subsequence (LCS). This yields a dense, order-aware reward signal that measures how well the student’s reasoning sequence aligns with the teacher’s process. SPEAR is integrated into sequence-level, on-policy reinforcement learning distillation so that the student is directly optimized to follow the teacher’s reasoning trajectory without needing a neural PRM.

Result: Across math, science, and commonsense reasoning benchmarks, SPEAR improves student model reasoning performance and alignment with the teacher compared to baselines that use only outcome-based rewards or that lack process-level alignment. The experiments show that the symbolic LCS-based reward is an effective and computationally efficient proxy for process-level reward, significantly narrowing the reasoning performance gap between student and teacher models.

Conclusion: SPEAR provides a practical and efficient solution to process-level reward design in RL-based knowledge distillation by replacing costly neural PRMs with symbolic, LCS-based alignment of reasoning traces. It offers dense, logically meaningful feedback that helps student models mimic teacher reasoning across domains without additional training of reward models, making sequence-level distillation more scalable and accessible.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning-based knowledge distillation has the potential to transfer complex reasoning from teacher to student models, yet it currently faces a critical dilemma: researchers must choose between sparse outcome-based rewards, which provide insufficient logical guidance, or expensive neural Process Reward Models (PRMs) for dense signals. We resolve this by introducing SPEAR (Symbolic Process Evaluation and Alignment Reward), a training-free and plug-and-play process reward method for sequence-level on-policy distillation. SPEAR projects natural-language reasoning traces into domain-adaptive symbolic milestones, providing an efficient proxy for process-level reasoning alignment. By utilizing the longest common subsequence (LCS) to align student explorations with teacher milestones, SPEAR provides a dense, order-aware reward signal that enforces logical consistency without the need for an external neural verifier. Our experiments across math, science, and commonsense reasoning tasks demonstrate that SPEAR effectively bridges the reasoning gap between student and teacher models via sequence-level distillation with efficient dense process rewards. Our code and data are available at: https://github.com/zhuochunli/SPEAR.

</details>


### [61] [SPT: Skills as Pre-Training Data for Agentic Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26563)
*Yufei Sun,Yudong Li,Yiming Cheng*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They propose Skill Pre-Training (SPT), a mid-training method that uses public skill packages as language modeling data to improve tool-using abilities of LMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing agentic LMs rely heavily on costly tool-call traces and agent trajectories that require environments, executions, and verification, limiting tool and task coverage. Meanwhile, public skill packages encode tool semantics and workflows but are underused, mainly as inference-time context. The authors want a cheaper, broader-coverage way to train agentic capabilities by leveraging these existing skill repositories.

Method: They introduce Skill Pre-Training (SPT), a mid-training phase where the model is trained with causal language modeling on SkillCorpus, a dataset of public multi-file skill packages. They optionally mix this corpus with general data. To retain the structural and referential relationships between files in a skill package, they propose Reference Insert, a reference-aware assembly strategy that arranges supporting files close to where they are referenced in the primary instruction. They test SPT with different model sizes and downstream post-training recipes and compare it against mid-training on general data and on trajectory data.

Result: Across various model scales and post-training setups, SPT consistently boosts agentic (tool-using) performance relative to mid-training on either general corpora or trajectory data, while keeping general language capabilities mostly unchanged. Data-mixture experiments further show that combining skill data with general annealing corpora yields additional gains.

Conclusion: Public skill packages are an effective and underexploited data source for pre-training agentic language models. Incorporating them via the proposed SPT method and the Reference Insert strategy improves tool-use performance without sacrificing general abilities, and mixing them with general data can bring further benefits.

Abstract: Agentic (tool-using) language models are mainly trained on tool-call traces and agent trajectories during post-training. These data provide direct behavioral supervision, but producing them requires task environments, execution, and verification, making broad tool and task coverage expensive. Publicly available skills offer another source of training data: they encode reusable tool semantics and workflows but are typically used only as inference-time context. We introduce Skill Pre-Training (SPT), a mid-training method that applies causal language modeling to SkillCorpus, a collection of public multi-file skill packages, optionally mixed with general data. To preserve relations among files within each package, we also introduce Reference Insert, a reference-aware assembly strategy that places supporting files near their mentions in the primary instruction. Experiments across multiple model scales and post-training recipes show that SPT consistently improves agentic performance over mid-training on general or trajectory data, while largely preserving general performance. Data mixture experiments show additional benefits from combining skill data with general annealing corpora. These results indicate that skill packages are a valuable data source for pre-training agentic language models.

</details>


### [62] [Dependency-Aware Revocable Decoding for Efficient Diffusion Large Language Model Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26574)
*Wooje Park,Insu Lee,Minyoung Noh,Jaeyun Jang,Sungmin Lee,Kyuhong Shim,Byonghyo Shim*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces Dependency-Aware Revocable Decoding (DARD), a training-free framework that improves the speed–quality trade-off of diffusion LLMs by selectively masking and verifying tokens using dependency-aware contexts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diffusion LLMs can decode multiple tokens in parallel, offering faster generation than autoregressive models, but their quality deteriorates when decoding becomes too parallel because early errors contaminate later tokens. Existing revocable decoding methods try to fix this by remasking unreliable tokens, yet they ignore that those unreliable tokens may already have corrupted the verification context itself. This overlooked failure mode limits the effectiveness of current revocable decoding approaches and motivates a more context-sensitive mechanism.

Method: The authors propose Dependency-Aware Revocable Decoding (DARD), a training-free decoding framework that manages three token states: masked, candidate, and unmasked. During generation, DARD forms a selective context for verification that deliberately excludes less reliable tokens so that the model judges each candidate token using cleaner evidence. It then adaptively regulates how much influence these candidate tokens have on future decoding steps, thereby reducing the propagation of early mistakes while retaining parallelism benefits.

Result: Across 12 text and multimodal benchmarks using 3 open-source diffusion LLMs, DARD consistently outperforms recent revocable decoding baselines on the speed–quality Pareto frontier. Notably, on the Flickr30K benchmark, DARD achieves a 2.71× decoding speedup and a 4.35-point improvement in CIDEr score compared to Saber, a strong existing revocable decoding method.

Conclusion: Dependency-aware control of token contexts during revocable decoding substantially mitigates error propagation in diffusion LLMs, enabling higher decoding parallelism without sacrificing—and often improving—generation quality. Because DARD is training-free and consistently improves the speed–quality trade-off across diverse benchmarks and models, it represents a practical, general upgrade for diffusion-based language generation systems.

Abstract: Diffusion large language models (dLLMs) offer a promising alternative to autoregressive generation by decoding multiple tokens in parallel through iterative denoising. However, increasing decoding parallelism often degrades generation quality, as early errors can contaminate later contexts. Revocable decoding mitigates this issue by re-evaluating decoded tokens and remasking unreliable ones, but existing methods overlook that unreliable tokens may also corrupt the verification context itself. We identify this failure mode and propose Dependency-Aware Revocable Decoding (DARD), a training-free framework that separates tokens into masked, candidate, and unmasked states. DARD verifies candidate tokens using a selective context that excludes less reliable tokens and adaptively regulates their influence on subsequent decoding. Experiments across 12 textual and multimodal benchmarks on 3 open-source dLLMs show that DARD consistently improves the speed-quality Pareto frontier over recent revocable decoding methods, achieving a 2.71$\times$ speedup and a 4.35-point CIDEr score gain over Saber on Flickr30K.

</details>


### [63] [Double Trouble: Bilingual Pretraining Leaves Language-Conditioned Effects in Shared-Language Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26576)
*Anjishnu Mukherjee,Ziwei Zhu,Antonios Anastasopoulos*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper questions the assumption that aligned embedding spaces in multilingual decoder-only models imply comparable internal representations, showing that deeper hidden states can diverge significantly even when token embeddings appear similar.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Researchers often align embedding spaces of multilingual models to compare representations across languages or models, assuming that once embeddings are aligned, shared-language representations (e.g., English) are comparable. The authors suspect this may be an unwarranted assumption, especially for decoder-only models, and want to rigorously test whether alignment at the embedding level truly reflects similarity in internal processing and representations.

Method: They pretrain two 310M-parameter decoder-only models: one English-only and one bilingual, trained across eight typologically diverse languages. They carefully control for English exposure, total compute, and document overlap. After training, they align the models based on their shared English vocabulary in the embedding space. They then examine both token embeddings and deeper hidden states for held-out English words, comparing similarity after alignment. They explore multiple languages and test robustness with different controls and alternative alignment techniques, and they analyze how divergence varies across transformer layers.

Result: Post-alignment, token embeddings for shared English vocabulary look similar between the English-only and bilingual models. However, the deeper hidden states used for prediction show substantial differences for held-out words. This discrepancy is consistent across all eight languages and remains even when controlling for document overlap and using different alignment methods. They also find that the mismatch between models grows in the middle transformer layers, indicating that differences emerge during contextual processing rather than at the initial input representation level where alignment is performed.

Conclusion: Aligning embedding spaces can create the illusion that multilingual or differently trained models represent a shared language similarly, while their internal contextual representations actually differ. These hidden-state mismatches, which intensify through intermediate layers, imply that embedding alignment alone is insufficient for assuming model interchangeability in probing, interpretability, or cross-lingual transfer studies.

Abstract: When researchers compare multilingual models for probing, interpretability, or cross-lingual transfer, they often align embedding spaces and assume that shared-language representations are comparable. We show that this assumption can be premature for decoder-only models. We pretrain paired 310M-parameter models (one English-only, one bilingual) across eight typologically diverse languages, separately controlling for English exposure, total compute, and document overlap. After aligning on shared English vocabulary, we test held-out words and find that token embeddings look similar after alignment, but the deeper hidden states that the model uses for prediction do not. This gap holds for all eight languages and survives controls for document overlap and alternative alignment methods. This hidden-state mismatch grows through middle transformer layers, suggesting that it arises from contextual processing rather than the input representations where alignment is performed. Embedding alignment can mask real differences in how models internally represent a shared language, which matters for any downstream study that treats aligned models as interchangeable.

</details>


### [64] [Surgical Alignment in Knowledge Graph Training for Clinical Diagnosis with Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26587)
*Saksham Khatwani,He Cheng,Majid Afshar,Dmitriy Dligach,Yanjun Gao*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper systematically studies how to best integrate biomedical knowledge graphs into large language models and finds that a KL-regularized KG-judgment training regime yields sparse, “surgical” parameter updates that transfer knowledge better than standard task-specific fine-tuning with similar accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Biomedical KGs contain rich, structured clinical knowledge that could improve LLM-based diagnosis, but it is unclear which training strategies effectively inject KG information into LLMs without harming general reasoning. Existing work focuses mostly on task accuracy and lacks tools to understand how fine-tuning actually alters pretrained models.

Method: The authors evaluate KG-to-LLM integration along several axes: five KG task formulations, three training paradigms (including KG-judgment with KL regularization and task-specific supervised fine-tuning), two biomedical KGs, and three base LLMs. They introduce two optimization-geometry metrics—Gradient Intervention Density (GID) to quantify how widely gradients affect model parameters, and Gradient Distortion (GD) to quantify how much they alter pretrained directions. They compare paradigms using these metrics plus task accuracy, and conduct ablations separating the effects of the KG-judgment objective and the KL regularizer.

Result: All KG-aware training paradigms outperform non-finetuned LLMs on KG-related tasks, but they differ markedly in how updates spread through the network and how well knowledge transfers. KG-judgment training with KL regularization yields sparse, localized gradient interventions (low GID and GD), a regime termed “surgical alignment.” In contrast, task-specific supervised fine-tuning generates dense, widespread changes. Ablations show that both the KG-judgment objective and KL regularization independently increase sparsity. Paradigms producing sparser updates lead to better reasoning quality and knowledge transfer, even when their in-domain task accuracy is lower than that of dense SFT.

Conclusion: The way KG information is injected into LLMs matters beyond raw task accuracy. KL-regularized KG-judgment training achieves “surgical alignment,” introducing KG knowledge via sparse, localized updates that better preserve and enhance reasoning than dense task-specific fine-tuning. Evaluating KG–LLM integration should therefore combine standard accuracy metrics with optimization-geometry diagnostics like GID and GD to understand and select training regimes that transfer knowledge effectively without over-writing pretrained capabilities.

Abstract: Biomedical knowledge graphs (KGs) offer structured medical knowledge that can ground large language model (LLM) reasoning in clinical diagnosis application, yet how KG signal should be integrated into LLMs remains an open question. We present a systematic study spanning five KG task formulations, three training paradigms, two KGs, and three base LLMs. At the task level, all paradigms improve over the non-finetuned baseline, but methods with comparable in-domain accuracy show substantially different knowledge transfer behavior. We introduce Gradient Intervention Density (GID) and Gradient Distortion (GD) to measure how broadly an optimizer modifies the pretrained model. GID and GD together reveal a clear divide: KG-judgment training under KL regularization produces sparse, localized updates (a regime we term as surgical alignment), while task-specific SFT produces dense ones. A controlled ablation shows that the objective and KL contribute to sparsity independently, and the paradigms that produce sparse updates also improve reasoning quality, even when their in-domain accuracy is lower than task-specific SFT. Assessing KG-LLM integration thus requires complementing accuracy with optimization-geometry diagnostics. Our implementation can be found at https://github.com/LARK-NLP-Lab/Surgical-Alignment.

</details>


### [65] [Benchmarking Clinical Decision Pathway Adherence in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26592)
*Nuo Chen,Xinyang Jiang,Zilong Wang,Zhifei Zhang,Xiaoye Qu,Jiajun Deng,Yulan Guo,Cairong Zhao*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces MEGA-CDP, a benchmark to test whether medical LLMs can follow clinical practice guidelines when generating clinical decision pathways, revealing that current models struggle with reliable, guideline-adherent decision support.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing medical LLM benchmarks largely focus on final-answer accuracy and do not sufficiently assess whether models follow clinical practice guidelines step-by-step. In real clinical settings, safe and reliable care depends on adherence to guideline-defined clinical decision pathways (CDPs), not just correct end answers. The authors aim to fill this evaluation gap and better understand LLMs’ capability to generate guideline-consistent decision processes.

Method: The authors build MEGA-CDP from 2,274 English and Chinese clinical practice guidelines using a guideline-to-case generation pipeline that produces 42,353 clinical cases paired with explicit reference CDPs. The benchmark supports both single-turn vignette questions and multi-turn interactive dialog settings. They also design a CDP-oriented evaluation framework that measures pathway consistency between model-generated CDPs and the reference pathways. They then evaluate 16 representative LLMs on this benchmark.

Result: MEGA-CDP provides a large, bilingual dataset of clinical cases and corresponding reference clinical decision pathways, along with metrics to assess adherence to those pathways. Experiments across 16 LLMs show that, even when models can sometimes get final answers, their ability to consistently follow clinical guidelines and generate guideline-adherent CDPs is limited and remains challenging.

Conclusion: The study concludes that current LLMs are not yet reliably capable of guideline-adherent clinical decision-making. This underscores the importance of evaluating models on clinical decision pathways rather than only on final-answer accuracy and establishes MEGA-CDP as a valuable resource for developing and benchmarking medical LLMs aimed at safer, guideline-based clinical decision support.

Abstract: Following clinical decision pathways (CDPs) defined by clinical practice guidelines is essential for safe and reliable medical decision-making. However, existing medical large language model (LLM) benchmarks mainly evaluate final-answer accuracy, providing limited evaluation of models' ability to adhere to guidelines. To address this gap, we introduce MEGA-CDP, a benchmark for evaluating whether medical LLMs can generate guideline-adherent CDPs using provided guidelines as references. MEGA-CDP is constructed from 2,274 English and Chinese clinical practice guidelines through a guideline-to-case pipeline, yielding 42,353 clinical cases with explicit reference CDPs. It supports both single-turn vignette and multi-turn interactive settings, and introduces a CDP-oriented evaluation framework for measuring pathway consistency. Experiments on 16 representative LLMs show that reliable clinical decision support remains challenging for current models, demonstrating the need for CDP-oriented evaluation and the value of MEGA-CDP for advancing guideline adherence in medical LLMs.

</details>


### [66] [Not Just Reason, Not Just Scan: Reinforcement Learning for Proactive Scientific Error Verification over Academic Paper](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26596)
*Rongjin Li,Yuanxin Liu,Hao Zhou,Fandong Meng,Jie Zhou,Xu Sun*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces VERA-RL, a reinforcement-learning framework and dataset (VERA-13K) for training multimodal large language models to autonomously detect and justify scientific errors in academic papers using evidence-grounded reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current multimodal large language models can assist with scientific tasks but are not yet capable of fully autonomous, trustworthy research. A key missing capability is issue- and evidence-absent verification: models should proactively inspect papers, identify potential errors without being told what to look for, and provide traceable, evidence-based justifications. Existing benchmarks and training setups generally prespecify questions or evidence, so they do not reflect this open-ended verification setting. The authors aim to build a task formulation, dataset, and training method that push models toward robust, verifiable scientific error detection over full papers.

Method: The authors cast scientific error detection as a reinforcement learning (RL) problem over academic papers, defining a Reason–Verify–Scan workflow. They build VERA-13K, a dataset of 12,900 samples grouped into 4,300 matched reasoning chains, each reflecting different stages or styles of the Reason–Verify–Scan process and spanning 6 types of scientific errors across the research lifecycle and diverse natural science domains. They then design fine-grained RL reward signals targeting three aspects: (1) reasoning completeness (covering relevant aspects of the paper), (2) evidence alignment (using correct, localized evidence from the paper), and (3) error precision (accurately identifying and characterizing errors, if any). Using this setup, they train a base multimodal LLM, Qwen3-VL-8B, with the VERA-RL formulation.

Result: After RL training on VERA-13K with the proposed reward design, Qwen3-VL-8B shows substantially improved verifiable, evidence-grounded reasoning in the context of scientific error detection. Specifically, on the “Scan” stage—where the model must inspect for and identify errors—its performance approaches that of much larger flagship multimodal LLMs such as Gemini 3 Pro and Qwen3-VL-235B-A22B, indicating strong gains in efficiency and reliability relative to model size.

Conclusion: VERA-RL demonstrates that framing scientific error detection as an RL problem with structured Reason–Verify–Scan workflows and fine-grained verifiability rewards can significantly enhance the ability of mid-sized MLLMs to autonomously and reliably detect scientific errors in papers. The VERA-13K dataset and RL framework provide a task paradigm and training resource for open-ended, evidence-absent verification, pushing MLLMs closer to functioning as trustworthy, semi-autonomous scientific auditors and assistants.

Abstract: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) are increasingly capable scientific assistants, yet they remain far from fully autonomous research. This transition requires models to actively inspect academic papers, build global evidence views, and make traceable judgments without prespecified issues or evidence. However, existing work provides limited task paradigms or training studies for such issue- and evidence-absent verification. We study this challenge through scientific error detection, where models must determine whether errors exist and justify them with evidence-based reasoning. To fill this gap, we present VERA-RL, a reinforcement-learning formulation for scientific error detection over academic papers. Following a Reason--Verify--Scan progression, we construct VERA-13K, a 12,900-sample dataset organized into 4,300 matched chains, covering 6 scientific-error categories across the research workflow and broad natural-science domains. We further introduce fine-grained rewards for reasoning completeness, evidence alignment, and error precision. Training Qwen3-VL-8B with VERA-RL substantially improves verifiable reasoning, approaching flagship MLLMs such as Gemini 3 Pro and Qwen3-VL-235B-A22B on Scan.

</details>


### [67] [Which Metrics Save the Most Human Annotation? Prediction-Powered Evaluation and Meta-Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26638)
*Mingqi Gao,Anthony Sicilia,Weiyan Shi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They propose a prediction-powered evaluation framework that combines a small number of human judgments with many automatic metric scores to compare systems in a provably unbiased and data-efficient way.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Human evaluation for non-verifiable tasks (like translation quality) is accurate but costly and doesn’t scale, while automatic metrics are scalable but can be biased and unreliable for final evaluation. There is a need for a principled method that uses automatic metrics to reduce human annotation cost without sacrificing the reliability and unbiased nature of human-based evaluation.

Method: They extend prediction-powered inference (PPI) to prediction-powered evaluation. The method combines limited human judgments with large-scale automatic metric predictions to produce unbiased system comparisons. They develop both parametric and non-parametric statistical procedures, analyze efficiency trade-offs between paired and unpaired experimental designs, and apply the framework to six WMT machine translation datasets. They also introduce the Prediction-Powered Saving Ratio (PPSR), which quantifies how much human annotation can be saved by using a given automatic metric within this framework.

Result: On six WMT datasets, the proposed prediction-powered evaluation framework successfully yields unbiased system comparisons while reducing the amount of human annotation required. The newly proposed PPSR meta-metric produces more discriminative and stable rankings of automatic metrics compared to existing system-level meta-metrics, better reflecting their utility in the prediction-powered evaluation setting.

Conclusion: Automatic metrics should be viewed and designed as tools to reduce human annotation costs rather than as replacements for human judgment, especially in non-verifiable tasks. The prediction-powered evaluation framework, along with the PPSR meta-metric, offers a general, statistically sound paradigm for integrating human and automatic evaluation across such tasks.

Abstract: Across various non-verifiable tasks, human evaluation is reliable but expensive, while automatic metrics are more scalable but often biased. Building on prediction-powered inference (PPI), we propose prediction-powered evaluation, a framework that combines limited human judgments with large-scale automatic scores to obtain data-efficient system comparisons that are provably unbiased. We develop parametric and non-parametric procedures, analyze the efficiency trade-off between paired and unpaired designs, and validate the framework on six WMT datasets. We further introduce the Prediction-Powered Saving Ratio (PPSR), a meta-metric that measures how much human annotation an automatic metric can save when used within prediction-powered evaluation. PPSR directly targets metric utility for prediction-powered evaluation and yields more discriminative and stable metric rankings than existing system-level meta-metrics. Overall, our new paradigm reframes automatic metrics as tools for reducing human annotation cost rather than replacing human judgment, and applies broadly to non-verifiable tasks.

</details>


### [68] [Information-Guided Frontier Decoding: Contextual Utility-Driven Commitment in dMLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26641)
*Xingyou Fang,Jingxing Zhong,Xiaosong Yuan,Xiaofeng Zhang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They propose a new decoding strategy for diffusion multimodal language models that chooses which tokens to finalize based on information usefulness rather than just confidence, improving performance across tasks without extra training or compute.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current decoding methods for diffusion multimodal language models commit tokens mainly by confidence, which tends to favor structurally simple but semantically uninformative tokens (like punctuation) over meaningful content words. This misalignment causes weak context propagation and higher error accumulation, ultimately hurting generation quality in multimodal understanding and reasoning tasks. There is thus a need for a decoding strategy that better aligns token selection with contextual informativeness and robustness while keeping computation and training costs unchanged.

Method: They introduce Information-Guided Frontier Decoding (IGFD), a training-free decoding algorithm. IGFD ranks candidate tokens using (1) token confidence, (2) neighborhood uncertainty (how uncertain nearby tokens are), and (3) structural commitment risk (whether a token is structurally fragile such as punctuation or formatting). By combining these factors, IGFD prefers early commitment of stable, semantically important tokens and defers committing fragile structural tokens. Additionally, it defines a dynamic candidate frontier that restricts selection to locally expandable regions in the sequence, all under the same overall decoding budget as baselines. The method uses the existing model without additional training, auxiliary networks, or extra forward passes.

Result: Across multiple benchmarks that test multimodal understanding, reasoning, grounding, and hallucination resistance, IGFD yields better performance than existing confidence-based or other decoding strategies. These gains hold for most evaluated benchmarks and across various diffusion multimodal language model backbones, all under identical decoding budgets, meaning improvements come from smarter decoding rather than more computation.

Conclusion: Choosing which tokens to commit in diffusion multimodal language models based on information-centric criteria—rather than raw confidence alone—significantly improves decoding quality. IGFD effectively promotes early, reliable semantic anchors and delays risky structural tokens, leading to better context propagation and lower error accumulation. Because it is training-free and compute-neutral, IGFD is a practical drop-in decoding replacement that robustly enhances performance across diverse multimodal tasks and model architectures.

Abstract: Decoding quality in diffusion multimodal language models (dMLLMs) depends heavily on the order in which masked tokens are committed. Existing confidence-based strategies prioritize locally easy tokens, but confidence does not necessarily reflect contextual usefulness. As a result, structurally easy tokens such as punctuation may be committed before informative semantic anchors, weakening context propagation and increasing error accumulation. We propose Information-Guided Frontier Decoding (IGFD), a training-free decoding strategy that ranks candidates using token confidence, neighborhood uncertainty, and structural commitment risk. IGFD encourages early commitment of reliable semantic anchors while delaying fragile structural tokens, improving contextual support during decoding. A dynamic candidate frontier further constrains token selection to locally expandable regions under the same decoding budget. The method requires no additional training, auxiliary models, or extra forward passes. Experiments across multimodal understanding, reasoning, grounding, and hallucination benchmarks show that IGFD consistently outperforms existing decoding strategies across the majority of benchmarks and diffusion MLLM backbones under identical decoding budgets.

</details>


### [69] [Meta-Learning Where to Allocate Experts: Task-Conditioned Layer-Wise Compression for MoEs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26650)
*Rongfeng Wang,Shichao Weng,Zhiqiang Wang,Xinyu Liu,Yang Yi,Peilong Zhou,Hongwei Tang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces MetaNet, a controller that dynamically decides how many experts to activate per layer in Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models per task, achieving flexible compute–accuracy trade-offs without retraining the MoE itself.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current MoE deployments typically use a fixed number of active experts per layer and per task, even though different layers play different roles and task/difficulty vary. Existing methods either fix layer-wise allocations offline or adjust experts locally at token-level without task-aware context, limiting efficiency and adaptability. The authors want a way to adjust expert activation globally and per task while keeping the base MoE frozen.

Method: They design MetaNet, a support-set-based controller network. Given a small support set for a task, MetaNet predicts, for each layer of a frozen MoE model, (1) an expert-retention threshold (determining how many experts to keep active) and (2) a bounded routing bias that modifies the router’s behavior. The MoE backbone, experts, and original router are not updated. By changing MetaNet’s settings, users can smoothly trade off accuracy versus the average number of experts activated.

Result: On DeepSeek-MoE-16B-Chat, MetaNet achieves configurable compute savings. Compared to a standard fixed configuration of k=6 experts per layer, a conservative MetaNet setting activates 3.61 experts on average (40% fewer) while slightly improving MMLU accuracy (0.489 vs. 0.474). A more aggressive setting uses only 2.28 experts on average (62% fewer), with about a 3.7-point drop in MMLU accuracy. The controller trained on MMLU also transfers to C-Eval without retraining, activating 2.90 experts on average (52% fewer than k=6) and obtaining 0.386 accuracy.

Conclusion: MetaNet can dynamically control expert usage in large MoE models on a per-layer, per-task basis, enabling a tunable trade-off between accuracy and computation without modifying or retraining the underlying MoE. It generalizes reasonably across benchmarks, suggesting it is a practical way to deploy more compute-efficient MoE systems.

Abstract: Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models route each token to a subset of expert networks, increasing capacity while keeping per-token computation sparse. In many deployed MoEs, the number of active experts is fixed across layers and tasks, although layer roles and expert redundancy vary with depth and demand varies with difficulty. Existing approaches address only part of this setting: layer-wise allocations are usually determined offline and reused for all tasks, while token-level methods vary expert activation using local routing signals without task-level context. We propose MetaNet, a support-set controller that predicts, for each layer, an expert-retention threshold and a bounded routing bias. The backbone, experts, and router remain frozen. On DeepSeek-MoE-16B-Chat, MetaNet provides a tunable accuracy-expert-activation trade-off. Relative to fixed k=6, a conservative setting activates 3.61 experts on average (40% fewer) and achieves comparable MMLU accuracy (0.489 vs. 0.474), whereas an aggressive setting activates 2.28 experts on average (62% fewer) with accuracy approximately 3.7 percentage points lower. The MMLU-trained controller also transfers to C-Eval without retraining, activating 2.90 experts on average (52% fewer than fixed k=6) at 0.386 accuracy.

</details>


### [70] [Do LLMs Understand Personality? Rethinking Persona Fidelity Evaluation through Structured Behavioral Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26674)
*Mengfan Li,Zesheng Wei,Xuanhua Shi,Yang Deng*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces PRISM, a new framework to evaluate how faithfully language model agents stick to a given persona, avoiding weaknesses of current holistic or static tests.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: With LLMs increasingly used to simulate distinct human-like personas, there is a growing need to rigorously measure how consistently an agent adheres to the intended psychological and stylistic traits. Existing methods either depend on holistic LLM judges that can hallucinate global impressions, or on static psychometric inventories that ignore dialogue context and dynamism, leading to unreliable persona fidelity assessment.

Method: The authors propose PRISM (Persona Reasoning with Inverse SFL-based Modeling), which reconceptualizes persona fidelity evaluation as an inverse inference problem grounded in Systemic Functional Linguistics (SFL). PRISM decomposes persona fidelity into three functional dimensions—Task Framing, Interpersonal Stance, and Linguistic Style—then collects dimension-specific evidence over a persona-conditioned label space and aggregates these signals into a structured, interpretable, and auditable evaluation pipeline.

Result: Empirical experiments demonstrate that PRISM produces more accurate and more stable persona fidelity judgments than conventional holistic LLM-based judging approaches, suggesting improved robustness and reliability for evaluating persona-aligned behavior in dialogue agents.

Conclusion: By framing persona fidelity evaluation as a structured, SFL-inspired inverse reasoning task over multiple functional dimensions, PRISM overcomes limitations of holistic and static approaches, yielding a more interpretable and dependable framework for assessing how well LLM agents embody target personas in dynamic contexts.

Abstract: As large language models are increasingly deployed to simulate diverse human characters, ensuring persona fidelity, defined as the extent to which an agent's behavior consistently reflects the psychological and stylistic characteristics of a target persona, has become a critical requirement. However, existing evaluation paradigms primarily rely on either holistic LLM-based judges, which are prone to "holistic appraisal hallucination'', or static psychometric inventories, which fail to capture the context-dependent fidelity required in dynamic dialogue. To address these limitations, we propose PRISM (Persona Reasoning with Inverse SFL-based Modeling), a psycholinguistically grounded framework that reformulates persona fidelity evaluation as a structured inverse inference task. Inspired by Systemic Functional Linguistics (SFL), PRISM decomposes persona fidelity into three functional dimensions: Task Framing, Interpersonal Stance, and Linguistic Style. It estimates dimension-specific evidence over a persona-conditioned label space and aggregates these signals into an interpretable and auditable evaluation process. Experiments show that PRISM yields more accurate and stable judgements than traditional holistic judging, providing a more reliable framework for persona fidelity evaluation.

</details>


### [71] [FOCUS & RePAIR: Mitigating Text Degeneration via Token-Level Guidance for Pruned Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26676)
*Junyoung Lee,Sehyeon Park,Shinhyoung Jang,Seonha Ryu,Hojeong Kim,Hyunsei Lee,Il Hong Suh,Yeseong Kim*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Pruning LLMs increases harmful repetition loops even when standard metrics look fine; this paper analyzes why at the token level and proposes two fine-tuning methods (FOCUS and RePAIR) that reduce repetition and improve generation quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Model pruning is widely used to compress LLMs, but practitioners observe more text degeneration—especially repetitive loops—despite similar perplexity and task accuracy. Existing evaluations fail to explain or fix this behavior, and there is limited token-level understanding of how pruning interacts with decoding dynamics. The paper aims to diagnose the mechanisms behind pruning-induced degeneration and to design targeted, lightweight post-pruning training strategies that restore generation quality without sacrificing compression benefits.

Method: The authors reinterpret decoding as a dynamical process over contexts and focus on recurrent contexts that cause repetition loops. They decompose degeneration into two factors: (1) loop entry risk (how likely the model is to enter a repetitive loop) and (2) loop persistence (how hard it is to escape once in the loop), and show that persistence is governed by the probability mass ("escape mass") on plausible non-repetitive alternatives within the sampling set. Based on this analysis, they propose two token-level objectives for post-pruning fine-tuning: (a) FOCUS, which reweights distillation to emphasize high-confidence teacher regions, reducing probability leakage that fuels loops; and (b) RePAIR, which constructs onset-centered positive/negative continuation pairs and applies a margin loss to explicitly raise probability of plausible alternatives and delay commitment to repetition. They evaluate these methods on open-ended text continuation and instruction-following benchmarks.

Result: Empirically, pruning increases repetition loops not captured by perplexity or accuracy metrics. The two proposed methods, FOCUS and RePAIR, both significantly reduce repetition-induced degeneration on open-ended and instruction-based generation tasks. They improve subjective and automatic quality metrics over standard pruning baselines, while maintaining the benefits of compression. The analysis confirms that adjusting token-level probability mass around loop onsets and escape options can reliably mitigate persistence in repetitive loops.

Conclusion: Pruning alters the token-level dynamics of LLM decoding in a way that especially harms generation by increasing both the likelihood of entering and remaining in repetition loops, an effect that standard metrics miss. By decomposing degeneration into loop entry and persistence and linking persistence to escape mass in the sampling set, the paper provides a concrete diagnostic lens. The proposed post-pruning fine-tuning objectives, FOCUS and RePAIR, operationalize this insight and consistently reduce repetition and enhance generation quality without undoing pruning. This establishes token-level, dynamical analysis as a useful tool for designing compression-aware training and decoding strategies.

Abstract: Pruning is a practical approach to compress large language models (LLMs), but it can amplify text degeneration, especially repetition loops, even when perplexity and task accuracy remain largely unchanged. In this work, we present a token-level analysis of this failure mode by viewing decoding as a dynamical process that enters and persists in a small set of recurrent contexts. Our analysis decomposes degeneration into loop entry risk and loop persistence, and shows that persistence is controlled by the escape mass assigned to plausible alternatives within the token sampling set. Motivated by these findings, we propose two token-level guidance objectives for post-pruning fine-tuning. FOCUS reweights distillation toward high-confidence teacher regions to suppress leakage, while RePAIR uses onset-centered positive/negative continuation pairs with a margin loss to promote plausible alternatives and prevent early commitment to repetition loops. Experiments on open-ended continuation and instruction-based generation show that both methods consistently reduce repetition and improve generation quality.

</details>


### [72] [Beyond Reflection: Affirmation as a Promising Behavioral Marker Associated with Quality in Text-Based Counseling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26689)
*Michimasa Inaba*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies which counselor text behaviors in AI-assisted counseling are most associated with higher-quality sessions, finding that Affirmation is a stronger and more consistent quality signal than Reflection, using large-scale Japanese and English counseling datasets with new annotations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI-assisted text counseling is growing, but it is unclear which specific counselor strategies actually contribute to better dialogue quality. Prior work overemphasizes Reflection, largely adopting Motivational Interviewing frameworks without broad empirical validation across other skills or in real-world counseling logs. There is a need for data-driven evidence about which counselor behaviors matter most, to guide both counselor training and the design of emotional support systems.

Method: The authors compile and extend KokoroChat, a large-scale Japanese text-based counseling dataset containing dialogues between professional counselors/trainees and clients. They newly annotate counselor utterances with strategy tags (e.g., Affirmation, Reflection) and client states such as distress levels. They then perform a multi-layered quantitative analysis to examine how each strategy correlates with session-level quality indicators. Additionally, they run cross-dataset transfer experiments, applying what they learn from KokoroChat to ESConv, an English emotional support dataset with non-expert supporters, to test whether identified quality signals generalize across languages and counselor expertise levels.

Result: The analysis reveals that, across the examined strategies, Affirmation shows a more stable and stronger positive association with multiple dialogue quality indicators than Reflection, which has been traditionally emphasized. This pattern holds in KokoroChat and, to a lesser degree, transfers to ESConv, suggesting that Affirmation as a quality-related behavior is not limited to one dataset, language, or counselor population.

Conclusion: Affirmation emerges as a key counselor behavior more reliably linked to text counseling quality than the widely emphasized Reflection strategy, challenging common assumptions derived from Motivational Interviewing-focused work. The findings suggest that counselor training programs and AI-based emotional support systems should prioritize and explicitly model Affirmation behaviors. The authors support further research and reproducibility by releasing their new annotations for KokoroChat and the experimental codebase.

Abstract: While AI-assisted text-based counseling is gaining attention, it remains empirically unclear which counselor behaviors are associated with higher dialogue quality. Existing research often focuses heavily on Reflection, borrowing frameworks from Motivational Interviewing. To address this gap, we conduct a multi-layered analysis using KokoroChat, a large-scale Japanese text counseling dataset conducted by professional counselors and trainees, newly annotated with counselor strategy tags and client distress levels. Our results show that, under the quality indicators used in this study, Affirmation is more consistently associated with session quality than Reflection among the analyzed strategies. Cross-dataset transfer experiments further suggest that this quality signal can be observed to some extent on ESConv, an English dataset with non-expert supporters. These findings provide empirical implications for counselor training and emotional support system design. We release the additional KokoroChat annotations and experimental source code at https://github.com/UEC-InabaLab/BeyondReflection.

</details>


### [73] [Scaling phoneme-based TTS augmentation for ASR: A unified pipeline and controlled study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26697)
*Zhen Wang,TianRui Wu,RongQi Han,Hao Wu,Wei Liang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper proposes a unified phoneme-based pipeline to use multilingual TTS to generate synthetic speech for improving ASR, introducing a phoneme-frequency-guided sentence selection method that significantly reduces WER across multiple languages and test sets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Synthetic speech can scale ASR training data, but its effectiveness highly depends on which texts are synthesized, which reference speeches are used, and how much synthetic data is generated. There is a need for a principled, unified way to control these factors and to quantify their impact on ASR performance across languages.

Method: The authors build a unified TTS-to-ASR augmentation pipeline using a multilingual phoneme-based TTS model (F5-TTS architecture with language-ID conditioning). The pipeline includes grapheme-to-phoneme conversion, reference-speech filtering, candidate-text selection, synthesis, and matched ASR continuation. They introduce phoneme-frequency-guided selection (PFGS), which ranks candidate sentences based on phoneme frequencies estimated from real ASR training labels. They run controlled experiments with monolingual ASR systems in Arabic, French, Italian, and Portuguese, systematically varying synthesis scale and selection strategies.

Result: Random synthetic augmentation already outperforms a matched real-only continuation baseline on 11 of 13 test sets across four languages. With a 60% synthetic data budget, PFGS beats real-only training on 12 test sets and random selection on 9, achieving up to 19.3% relative WER reduction over random selection. Additionally, improving reference-speech quality via filtering gives further absolute WER reductions of 0.29 and 0.59 on Italian and French Common Voice test sets, respectively.

Conclusion: The work shows that TTS-based augmentation for ASR is strongly influenced by three controllable variables: the total amount of synthetic data, the phoneme-level content of selected texts, and the quality of reference speech. Their unified pipeline and PFGS strategy provide an effective way to systematically exploit synthetic speech, yielding consistent WER gains across multiple languages and datasets.

Abstract: Synthetic speech provides scalable supervision for automatic speech recognition (ASR), but its benefit depends on the selected texts, reference speech, and amount of synthesized data. We present a unified phoneme-based TTS-to-ASR augmentation pipeline built around a multilingual TTS model trained from scratch using the F5-TTS architecture with language-ID conditioning. The pipeline combines language-specific grapheme-to-phoneme conversion, reference-speech filtering, candidate-text selection, synthesis, and matched ASR continuation. We further propose phoneme-frequency-guided selection (PFGS), which ranks candidate sentences using phoneme frequencies estimated from real ASR training labels. Experiments with separate monolingual ASR systems for Arabic, French, Italian, and Portuguese span 13 test sets. Across the synthesis-scale sweep, random augmentation improves over matched real-only continuation on 11 test sets. Under a nominal 60% synthesis budget, PFGS improves over real-only training on 12 test sets and over random selection on 9. Its largest relative word error rate (WER) reduction against random selection is 19.3%. With target texts and synthesis counts fixed, reference-speech filtering reduces absolute WER by 0.29 and 0.59 points on Italian and French Common Voice, respectively. These results identify synthesis scale, candidate-text content, and reference quality as important control variables in TTS-based ASR augmentation.

</details>


### [74] [PragAlign: Evidence-Sensitive Reply Assistance Across Chinese and Japanese Appropriateness Judgments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26700)
*Xin Zhong,Satori Hachisuka*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces PragAlign, a method for multilingual reply assistance that separates context reading from selective clarification, showing improved performance over baselines in Chinese and mixed results in Japanese.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To improve automated reply assistance systems in multilingual contexts by accounting for both linguistic competence and culturally grounded judgments of appropriateness, where existing baselines may not adequately capture pragmatics across languages.

Method: The authors propose PragAlign, which structurally separates the step of reading conversational context from the step of asking selective clarifying questions. They evaluate PragAlign against two baselines (Direct and Rule) using human judgments. Nine native Chinese speakers evaluated Chinese materials and three native Japanese speakers evaluated the corresponding Japanese versions, ranking the different reply assistance conditions across 10 scenarios.

Result: In Chinese evaluations, PragAlign was ranked significantly better than both Direct and Rule baselines. In Japanese evaluations, Direct had the lowest mean rank, PragAlign achieved the highest rate of top rankings, but the overall omnibus test did not show significant differences among conditions. Across languages, the same top condition was chosen in 5 of the 10 scenarios, including four scenarios where PragAlign was the shared top choice.

Conclusion: PragAlign can better support reply assistance in some multilingual settings, particularly Chinese, and demonstrates promising but not uniformly significant advantages in Japanese. The findings reveal both shared and language-specific patterns in judgments of reply appropriateness, offering guidance for designing reply assistance systems that are sensitive to linguistic and cultural variation.

Abstract: Reply assistance in multilingual settings requires linguistic competence and culturally situated judgments of appropriateness. We present PragAlign, which separates context reading from selective clarification, and evaluate it alongside Direct and Rule. Nine native Chinese speakers judged Chinese materials, while three native Japanese speakers judged matched Japanese versions. In the Chinese evaluation, PragAlign received significantly better ranks than both baselines. In the Japanese evaluation, Direct had the lowest mean rank, PragAlign had the highest top-rank rate, and the omnibus difference was not significant. The groups selected the same top condition in 5 of 10 scenarios, including four shared PragAlign selections. The results identify shared and language-specific judgment patterns and inform reply assistance designed to support linguistic and cultural understanding.

</details>


### [75] [Towards Expert Financial QA via Self-Improving RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26706)
*Junjie Xiong,Shawheen Ghezavat,Aum Hirpara*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces Self-Improving RAG, a multi-agent retrieval-augmented generation framework for financial QA that uses a judge-driven retry mechanism to improve accuracy, interpretability, and compliance-ready audit trails.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard single-pass RAG systems used for expert-level financial question answering suffer from numeric hallucinations and lack transparent audit trails, both of which are critical weaknesses in regulated financial contexts. The authors aim to build a QA system that can verify answers, correct itself when wrong, and provide interpretable decision logs suitable for compliance requirements.

Method: They propose Self-Improving RAG, which decomposes document QA into three specialized agents—Retrieval, Reasoning, and Judge—coordinated by an orchestrator. The Judge Agent scores candidate answers and, if the score falls below a dynamic threshold, the orchestrator triggers a retry phase with progressively escalated strategies: expanding the retrieval scope, adjusting prompts for more careful reasoning, and relaxing acceptance criteria. Importantly, the retrieval pipeline remains fixed, and all decisions and confidence scores are logged to support interpretability and auditability.

Result: On the FinanceBench benchmark focused on SEC filing QA, Self-Improving RAG attains 86% oracle-guided accuracy, reflecting strong agreement with gold-standard answers. It also achieves a 36.4% “Lazarus Rate,” meaning that nearly 40% of answers that were initially incorrect are successfully corrected through the targeted retry process.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that a fixed retrieval pipeline combined with judge-driven self-correction can deliver high accuracy and interpretability without requiring complex dynamic routing. The resulting system not only mitigates numeric hallucinations but also provides detailed logs and confidence scores, making it well-suited for regulated financial applications that demand robust verification and auditable decision trails.

Abstract: Expert-level financial question answering requires both grounded verification to catch numeric hallucinations and audit trails for regulatory compliance, attributes that standard single-pass RAG systems lack. We take a step toward this goal with Self-Improving RAG, a framework that decomposes document QA into three specialized agents (Retrieval, Reasoning, and Judge) coordinated by an orchestrator with feedback-driven self-correction. When the Judge Agent scores an answer below a dynamic threshold, the system triggers retry with escalated strategies: broader retrieval, more careful prompting, and relaxed acceptance criteria. We evaluate on FinanceBench (SEC filing QA), where Self-Improving RAG achieves 86% oracle-guided accuracy (measuring agreement with gold answers) with a 36.4% Lazarus Rate, recovering nearly 4 in 10 initially incorrect answers through targeted retry. A key finding is that a fixed retrieval pipeline with judge-driven retry achieves strong results without dynamic routing, providing full interpretability. Every decision is logged with confidence scores, enabling the audit trails required for regulated financial applications.

</details>


### [76] [Preserving General Capabilities during Domain Specialization with Uncertainty-Calibrated MOPD](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26735)
*Ziyuan Liu,Jiao Ou,Jian Liang,Ruiming Tang,Cheng Luo*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Paper studies how to specialize LLMs for specific domains without losing general abilities, introducing an uncertainty-calibrated multi-teacher on-policy distillation method that better balances domain and general performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Adapting large language models to specific vertical domains (e.g., role-playing, medical) typically improves in-domain performance but harms broadly useful skills like reasoning, coding, instruction following, and creative writing. Existing multi-teacher on-policy distillation approaches do not sufficiently resolve this trade-off because they rarely focus learning on high-value tokens and cannot tell whether advantage-based updates are reliable. The authors are motivated to design a training procedure that preserves or even improves general capabilities while still achieving strong domain specialization.

Method: They extend Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation (MOPD), where a student model is trained on trajectories it samples, supervised by both domain and general-capability teacher models. The proposed uncertainty-calibrated MOPD introduces: (1) dual-temperature sampling to diversify and broaden the set of candidate trajectories, increasing exposure to tokens where teachers offer strong positive guidance; (2) positive-advantage-density filtering to select trajectories with high density of tokens where teacher log-likelihood significantly exceeds the student’s, focusing updates on stronger learning signals; and (3) centered log-likelihood (CLL) filtering, which computes an entropy-calibrated teacher-endorsement score at the token level and probabilistically keeps or discards token updates based on how consistent the update direction is with teacher confidence. Together these trajectory-level and token-level mechanisms attempt to better exploit multi-teacher signals while avoiding unreliable gradient directions.

Result: On role-playing and medical-domain specialization benchmarks, the proposed method achieves higher average scores on general capabilities compared to standard MOPD, with reported relative improvements of 4.73% and 10.84%, respectively, while maintaining comparable or unchanged performance in the target vertical domains. Ablation studies show that the benefits are not simply due to using more rollouts and that removing the proposed components (dual-temperature sampling, positive-advantage-density filtering, CLL filtering) degrades the balance between general and specialized capabilities. Diagnostic analyses demonstrate that the mechanisms behave as intended: they increase focus on high-advantage tokens and filter out unreliable or low-endorsement updates.

Conclusion: Uncertainty-calibrated MOPD can specialize LLMs for narrow domains without sacrificing, and even improving, general capabilities. By broadening candidate trajectories, prioritizing those rich in strong positive teacher–student advantages, and probabilistically filtering token updates based on an entropy-aware endorsement signal, the method addresses key weaknesses of standard on-policy distillation. This leads to a more favorable trade-off between vertical-domain performance and general skills, and the analyses suggest that both trajectory-level and token-level components are necessary to obtain the observed gains.

Abstract: Specializing large language models to vertical domains improves domain-specific behavior but often degrades general capabilities such as reasoning, coding, instruction following, and creative writing. We study this domain--general trade-off in Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation (MOPD), where a specialized student is supervised on its own sampled trajectories by domain and general teachers. Standard MOPD faces two limitations: ordinary on-policy sampling rarely exposes tokens with large positive teacher--student advantages, while the advantage sign alone does not establish whether the resulting update direction is reliable. We propose uncertainty-calibrated MOPD to address these limitations. Dual-temperature sampling broadens the candidate trajectory pool, and positive-advantage-density filtering selects trajectories with stronger positive learning signals. Centered log-likelihood (CLL) filtering then computes an entropy-calibrated teacher-endorsement score and probabilistically retains token updates according to direction--endorsement consistency. Experiments on role-playing and medical-domain specialization show that our method improves the general-capability average over standard MOPD by $4.73\%$ and $10.84\%$, respectively, while maintaining vertical-domain performance. Ablations and diagnostic analyses further confirm that the gains do not merely result from a larger rollout budget and that the proposed trajectory- and token-level mechanisms address their intended failure modes.

</details>


### [77] [Letters hide the truth from our eyes: English homophones have meaningfully different phonetic realizations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26749)
*Yu-Hsiang Tseng,Mirjam T. C. Ernestus,Louis F. M. ten Bosch,R. Harald Baayen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper shows that homophones like “weight” and “wait” are pronounced differently in subtle, systematic ways that depend on meaning and context, not just word duration or frequency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditionally, homophones are assumed to share the same phonetic form, with known variation mainly in duration linked to frequency. The authors want to know whether deeper, more fine‑grained phonetic differences also systematically track meaning and usage context, challenging the idea that homophones are truly identical in pronunciation.

Method: They quantitatively analyzed about 14,000 tokens of English homophones from American television news broadcasts. Using time‑normalized spectrograms, they compared the detailed acoustic realizations of homophone pairs (e.g., “weight” vs. “wait”) while statistically controlling for duration‑related variation. They examined how these realizations varied with meaning and utterance context, without relying on traditional phonetic transcriptions.

Result: They found robust, systematic phonetic differences between members of homophone pairs that correlate with their meanings and contextual use. These differences persist even after accounting for differences in duration, indicating that homophones are not phonetically identical at a fine‑grained acoustic level.

Conclusion: Homophones can have meaning‑conditioned phonetic signatures: their realizations vary systematically with semantics and context, beyond duration alone. Time‑normalized spectrograms provide a powerful way to uncover such subtle differences, making phonetic transcription insufficient to fully capture the ‘phonetic truth’ of how words are realized in real speech.

Abstract: The distribution of spoken word duration of English homophones is known to co-vary with frequency of use. This study investigates whether other aspects of the phonetic realization of homophones also differ. A series of quantitative investigations of 14,000 homophone tokens in American television news broadcasts revealed that the tokens of homophone pairs such as \textit{weight} and \textit{wait} have different phonetic realizations, and that these can be predicted from their meanings in utterance context. These systematic differences remain even when taking duration-related variation into account. Time-normalized spectrograms emerged as an excellent tool for probing the fine details of phonetic realization, and obviate the need for phonetic transcriptions, which inevitably hide the phonetic truth from our eyes.

</details>


### [78] [Equal Ranking Quality, Different Decisions: Training Order-Consistent LLM Scorers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26762)
*Markus Frohmann,Mahdiyar Alavi,Elizabeth Lingg,Navid Rekabsaz*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper shows that LLM-based scorers (rerankers, reward models, QA scorers) are highly sensitive to the order of candidates within a single prompt, which affects downstream decisions even when ranking metrics look similar. It proposes Order-Consistency SFT (OC-SFT) to make scores invariant to candidate order, improving decision stability without hurting ranking quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based scorers are usually evaluated using ranking metrics like nDCG, but in practice they are used to make decisions: which items pass a score threshold, which answers a reader model picks, or which option a preference model selects. The authors observe that these models score multiple candidates in one prompt, so the assigned scores can depend on the order in which candidates are presented. This prompt-order dependence is largely ignored but can cause unstable or inconsistent decisions, even when evaluation shows similar ranking quality. The paper aims to quantify this instability and introduce a method that yields more reliable, order-robust decisions.

Method: 1) Empirically measure order dependence: train several scorers (e.g., passage rerankers) with similar ranking performance (within 0.010 nDCG@10) and then compare the overlap of items retained under different permutations and models. Evaluate how permutations change three types of decisions: thresholded retention sets, reader-model answers, and preference selections. 2) Test simple prompt-level fixes (e.g., reordering, formatting) to see if order dependence can be removed at inference time. 3) Propose Order-Consistency SFT (OC-SFT), a supervised fine-tuning objective that explicitly trains the model so that a candidate’s score is invariant to the order of candidates in the prompt. 4) Compare OC-SFT against other order-targeting objectives, including order-averaged distillation, across multiple tasks and 12 base models, measuring both ranking quality and stability metrics (overlap, flip rate, etc.).

Result: The authors find substantial order dependence: even scorers with nearly identical nDCG@10 (within 0.010) retain sets whose overlap is only 0.66–0.84 after reordering. A strong published reranker, despite achieving the best retained-set F1 among those compared, still yields only 0.667 overlap. None of the prompt-time modifications tested eliminates order dependence; the only prompt change that improves ranking quality does not change any of the three decision outcomes. OC-SFT significantly reduces order dependence while preserving ranking quality. It yields the best decision-stability metrics across all three tasks: e.g., it changes the reader’s answer on 0.125 of permutation pairs versus 0.149–0.164 for other order-focused objectives. OC-SFT is more stable than order-averaged distillation on 12 base models, and using a single OC-SFT permutation yields higher overlap of retained sets than averaging over ten permutations of off-the-shelf models.

Conclusion: Ranking metrics alone are insufficient to characterize the reliability of LLM-based scorers that operate on sets of candidates in a single prompt. Order dependence can cause large variation in which items are retained, which answers are chosen, and which preferences are expressed, even when ranking performance appears similar. Order-Consistency SFT provides a more stable alternative by explicitly training scores to be invariant to candidate order, improving decision stability without sacrificing ranking quality. Therefore, evaluations of such scorers should report decision-level outcomes—such as what a threshold retains and what answer a reader produces—in addition to standard ranking metrics.

Abstract: Rerankers, reward models and multi-document QA scorers score candidate documents or responses in one LLM prompt, so each score depends on their order. Such scorers are selected on ranking quality, but their scores determine a decision: what a score threshold retains, a reader answers, or a preference model selects. However, equal ranking quality does not imply equal decisions: on passage reranking, five trained scorers within 0.010 nDCG@10 retain sets that overlap by only 0.66-0.84 when reordered. A published reranker takes the highest retained-set F1 in our comparison and still overlaps by only 0.667. No prompt-time change we test removes that order dependence: the only one that gains ranking quality leaves all three decisions unchanged. Order-consistency SFT (OC-SFT) attenuates it in the weights, training a candidate's score not to depend on the order. It holds ranking quality and leads every decision-stability measure among trained scorers on all three tasks: it flips the reader's answer on 0.125 of permutation pairs against 0.149-0.164 for three other objectives that target order. It is more stable than order-averaged distillation on 12 base models, and one OC-SFT permutation retains sets that overlap more than ten averaged off-the-shelf permutations.
