A comparison should therefore report what a threshold retains and a reader answers, not ranking quality alone. Code is available at https://github.com/thomsonreuters/presentation-dependence.

</details>


### [79] [Instruction Quality Matters: Refining Instructions for Effective Preference Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26779)
*Seohyeong Lee,Hwaran Lee,Buru Chang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper argues that the quality of instructions used to generate preference data is a hidden bottleneck in aligning large language models, and proposes an instruction-refinement pipeline that improves these instructions, leading to stronger preference signals and better alignment performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Preference learning for aligning LLMs usually focuses on collecting and filtering responses, assuming the instructions that elicit those responses are fixed and adequate. However, ambiguous or low-quality instructions narrow the range of possible response qualities, making it hard to obtain clear preference signals and limiting how well models can be aligned. The authors aim to expose and address this overlooked bottleneck: that poor instructions cap both the best and worst responses we can sample, thereby weakening the data used for preference optimization.

Method: 1) Empirically analyze how instruction quality affects the distribution of response qualities using Best-of-N and Worst-of-N sampling, showing that better instructions raise both the upper and lower bounds of sampled quality. 2) Propose an instruction-refinement pipeline: (a) use reward signals to detect weak or low-quality instructions; (b) apply rubric-guided feedback from an LLM to revise and improve these instructions while keeping the underlying tasks; (c) regenerate or reuse preference data under the refined instructions without discarding examples. 3) Evaluate this pipeline in both offline and online preference learning setups, across multiple models and benchmarks, and compare against other data-improvement strategies that focus primarily on responses.

Result: The refined-instruction pipeline produces preference datasets that lead to consistently better alignment outcomes than using the original instructions or alternative curation methods. Experiments show alignment improvements across various models, training regimes (offline and online preference learning), and benchmarks. Analyses of Best-of-N and Worst-of-N generations confirm that refined instructions increase the achievable response quality (raising the ceiling) and often also make poor responses more distinguishable (adjusting the floor), enhancing the informativeness of preference signals.

Conclusion: Instruction quality is a critical but previously underappreciated factor in preference learning for LLM alignment. By systematically identifying and refining weak instructions via reward-guided selection and rubric-based LLM editing, one can strengthen preference signals without discarding data. This instruction-centric approach complements standard response-focused curation methods and leads to broader, more reliable improvements in alignment quality. The authors provide code to facilitate adoption of their instruction-refinement pipeline in future alignment work.

Abstract: Preference learning optimizes models using response pairs, yet the informativeness of these pairs is fundamentally shaped by the instructions from which they are generated. We identify instruction quality as a hidden bottleneck in preference learning: low-quality or ambiguous instructions restrict the response-quality distribution, limiting strong chosen responses and weakening preference signals. Through Best- and Worst-of-N analyses, we show that instruction quality constrains both the ceiling and floor of sampled response quality. Motivated by this observation, we introduce an instruction-refinement pipeline that selects weak instructions using reward signals and revises them with rubric-guided LLM feedback, improving preference data without discarding examples. Across offline and online preference learning settings, experiments on multiple models and benchmarks show broad alignment improvements over original data and alternative data-improvement strategies. Further analyses indicate that instruction refinement raises achievable response quality and complements response-centric preference data curation. Overall, instruction quality emerges as a key factor governing how informative preference signals are formed for LLM alignment. Code is available at: https://github.com/01choco/instruction-refinement/

</details>


### [80] [Behavior2Trip: Towards Personalized Travel Planning via User Behavior Trajectory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26807)
*Zihao Cheng,Yingyu Shan,Hongru Wang,Zeming Liu,Xinyi Wang,Xiangrong Zhu,Yuhang Guo,Wei Lin,Yunhong Wang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They introduce a new task and dataset for behavior-aware travel planning and a reinforcement-learning agent that uses users’ past behaviors to generate more personalized travel plans, outperforming strong LLM baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current travel planning agents depend mainly on explicit instructions or long clarifying dialogues to learn user preferences, which is burdensome and still fails to fully personalize plans. Yet real platforms collect rich logs of user behavior (clicks, bookings, searches) that implicitly encode preferences but are underused. The authors want to leverage these behavioral signals to infer preferences automatically, reducing interaction load and improving personalization, and they note the lack of benchmarks and methods that systematically address this setting.

Method: 1) Define the Behavior-Aware Travel Planning task, where the system must infer user preferences from historical behavior traces and construct a personalized travel plan. 2) Build Behavior2Trip, a large-scale benchmark from a Chinese online travel platform with 11,400 instances; each instance includes about 40 historical user behaviors annotated along 14 attributes and 5 preference dimensions, plus target planning constraints. 3) Propose B2T-Agent: a reinforcement learning-based agent architecture that (a) encodes user behavior trajectories, (b) uses external tools to retrieve travel items aligned with inferred preferences, and (c) maintains an internal memory module to track and refine inferred preferences during planning. 4) Train and evaluate the agent (based on Qwen3-8B) against strong baselines including GPT-4.1 on Behavior2Trip and on the existing TravelPlanner benchmark.

Result: On the Behavior2Trip benchmark, even a very strong model (GPT-4.1) attains only 0.5% full-constraint satisfaction on the hardest subset, demonstrating the difficulty of the task. B2T-Agent built on Qwen3-8B surpasses all baselines on this dataset, confirming the benefit of behavior-aware preference modeling, external-tool interaction, and RL training. Furthermore, the Qwen3-8B model trained with B2T-Agent also outperforms GPT-4.1 on the TravelPlanner benchmark, indicating that the learned capabilities generalize beyond the new dataset.

Conclusion: Behavior-aware travel planning, where systems infer preferences from historical user behavior instead of relying solely on explicit instructions, is both challenging and important. The Behavior2Trip benchmark exposes the limitations of current LLMs in this setting and provides a foundation for future work. The proposed B2T-Agent demonstrates that integrating behavioral trajectory modeling, tool-augmented retrieval, and reinforcement learning can significantly improve personalization and generalize to other travel planning tasks. The released code and data invite further research on more capable and user-friendly travel planning agents.

Abstract: Travel planning agents assist users in generating personalized travel plans by modeling their individual preferences. Existing agents either rely on explicit user instructions or engage in multi-turn clarification to elicit user preferences. However, both approaches overlook the rich behavioral signals latent in users' past behaviors, which implicitly encode their preferences. This over-reliance on active user input increases interaction burden and limits plan personalization. To bridge this gap, we introduce a new task, Behavior-Aware Travel Planning, which infers user preferences directly from past behaviors and generates personalized travel plans. To facilitate research on this task, we introduce Behavior2Trip, a benchmark constructed from one of the largest Chinese online travel platforms, comprising 11,400 instances. Each instance represents an average of 39.8 past user behaviors spanning 14 attributes across 5 preference dimensions. We further propose B2T-Agent, a reinforcement learning-based agent that leverages user behavior trajectories, interacts with external tools for preference-aligned retrieval, and maintains an internal memory module. Experiments on Behavior2Trip show that GPT-4.1 achieves a full-constraint pass rate of only 0.5\% on the hardest tasks, while B2T-Agent built upon Qwen3-8B outperforms all baselines, highlighting the substantial challenge of this task. Moreover, Qwen3-8B trained with B2T-Agent also outperforms GPT-4.1 on the TravelPlanner benchmark, demonstrating strong generalization. Code and data are available at https://github.com/BUAA-IRIP-LLM/Behavior2Trip

</details>


### [81] [RuleWeaver: Benchmarking Rule-Centered Scenario Reasoning for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26832)
*Bohan Yu,Shi-Yang Li,Pengfei Cao,Jun Zhao,Kang Liu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper presents RuleWeaver, a framework and benchmark to evaluate how well large language models perform complex, rule-centered scenario reasoning using IF-THEN rules and process-level metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks do not adequately test LLMs’ ability to apply and reason over complex domain rules in realistic scenarios. Many focus only on surface-level instruction following or do not differentiate the various roles rules play in reasoning. This leaves a gap in assessing models that must use domain expertise encoded as rules.

Method: The authors create RuleWeaver, which starts from corpus-derived IF-THEN Meta Rules and incrementally augments them into more complex rules. These are then composed into rule-centered scenario question-answering instances. The framework supports not just final-answer evaluation but also process-level evaluation using rubrics that score answer quality and metrics like rule recall and rule precision.

Result: When evaluated on RuleWeaver, 11 representative LLMs perform poorly on complex rule-centered scenario reasoning. Even the strongest model only reaches about 50% of the maximum possible rubric score, indicating substantial room for improvement.

Conclusion: RuleWeaver is a useful framework and benchmark for systematically testing LLMs’ abilities in rule-centered scenario reasoning, revealing that current models are still far from mastering complex rule application. The released code and dataset can facilitate further research and development in this area.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly applied to specialized domains, where effective use of domain expertise often requires reasoning over complex rules in concrete scenarios. However, existing benchmarks only partially evaluate this capability, as they either focus on output-level instruction constraints or overlook the distinct roles that rules play in scenario reasoning. To address these gaps, this paper introduces RuleWeaver, a benchmark construction framework for evaluating rule-centered scenario reasoning. RuleWeaver starts from corpus-derived IF-THEN Meta Rules, progressively augments them into complex rules, and composes these rules into rule-centered scenario QA instances. Beyond final-answer correctness, RuleWeaver further supports process-level evaluation through rubric-based answer quality, rule recall, and rule precision. Experiments on 11 representative LLMs show that current models still struggle with complex rule-centered scenario reasoning, with even the best-performing model achieving only around 50% of the maximum rubric score. We make our code and dataset available here: https://github.com/SharkSpicy-NLP/RuleWeaver.

</details>


### [82] [Evaluating Confidence-Gated Retrieval with Matched Trajectory Replay](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26846)
*Prateek Chhikara*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies how confidence calibration in language-model agents affects their decision to answer, retrieve more evidence, or defer, using a controlled evaluation protocol.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing work evaluates confidence estimates in isolation, but interactive agents actually use confidence to choose actions (answer now, retrieve more evidence, or abstain). We lack controlled measurements of how different confidence-to-action mappings affect full trajectories, accuracy, coverage, and retrieval cost.

Method: The authors introduce matched trajectory replay, a protocol that fixes answer candidates, evidence, budgets, and action costs while varying only how confidence is mapped to actions. They compare raw verbalized confidence versus post-hoc isotonic calibration for multi-hop QA agents built on Mistral, GPT, and Qwen across HotpotQA and MuSiQue, examining accuracy, coverage, retrieval depth, and calibration at different retrieval stages.

Result: Calibration at a fixed numerical threshold changes which questions the agent chooses to answer, raising accuracy on committed answers by up to 41 percentage points but often reducing coverage and increasing retrieval usage. Overall accuracy rises by up to 15 points on HotpotQA but drops by up to 17 points on MuSiQue, reflecting a move to a more conservative operating point rather than better answers or rankings. A calibration map learned before retrieval improves calibration only for shallow retrieval (depths 1–2) and becomes worse than raw confidence by depth 3. While extra evidence helps on average, confidence alone does not reliably indicate which specific episodes gain from more retrieval.

Conclusion: Calibration helps make commitment risk interpretable and allows more selective, lower-risk behavior, but it neither improves underlying answer quality nor reliably predicts the marginal value of additional retrieval. Therefore, retrieval decisions require a separate value-of-information or utility model. Evaluations of interactive LMs should include held-out calibration, risk–coverage behavior, and retrieval costs rather than just point accuracy.

Abstract: Interactive language-model agents use confidence signals to decide whether to answer immediately, retrieve additional evidence (from memory or external knowledge), or defer. Yet confidence is usually evaluated in isolation, without measuring the trajectory-level consequences of the actions it triggers. We propose matched trajectory replay, a controlled protocol for comparing confidence-to-action mappings. The protocol holds candidate answer states, evidence points, budgets, and action costs fixed. We use it to compare raw verbalized confidence with post-hoc isotonic calibration in a multi-hop question-answering system using Mistral, GPT, and Qwen models on HotpotQA and MuSiQue datasets. At the same numerical commitment threshold, calibration changes which questions agents ultimately commit to answering. Across all six model-dataset pairs, it increases accuracy among committed answers by up to 41 percentage points. However, it can reduce coverage and increase retrieval use. Overall accuracy improves by up to 15 percentage points on HotpotQA but falls by up to 17 percentage points on MuSiQue. These effects reflect a shift to a more selective, lower-risk operating point, not improved answers or confidence ranking. A calibration map fitted before retrieval improves held-out calibration through retrieval depths one and two, but is worse than raw confidence at depth three for all three models. Additional evidence helps on average, but this aggregate effect does not establish whether confidence identifies which individual episodes will benefit from another retrieval. Taken together, these results show that calibration can make commitment risk interpretable, but it does not estimate the expected benefit of another retrieval. Retrieval therefore requires a separate value-of-information or utility estimate. Evaluations should report held-out calibration, risk-coverage, and retrieval cost.

</details>


### [83] [Planting a Latent Variable in Natural-Looking Text: a More Realistic Test of Belief States in LLMs and Their Link to Concept Geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26887)
*Alexandru-Iulius Jerpelea*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper tests whether small transformers can internally track latent variables (belief states) in realistic language and how this relates to the geometry of learned features.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work argues that LLMs maintain Bayesian-like belief states over latent causes of text, but this has been shown mainly on synthetic data and without relating it to the geometric structure of internal representations. The authors want to create a realistic yet controllable setup to directly test belief tracking and connect it to interpretability concepts and feature geometry.

Method: They construct a natural-looking text corpus where a controllable latent variable is embedded in the generation process. A larger teacher LLM writes ordinary text, but at each token its activations are subtly steered along one of K=8 sparse autoencoder (SAE) concept directions. These active directions follow a ring-shaped Markov chain over the 8 states. Then they train a small transformer on this corpus and analyze whether it infers the latent state sequence and how it represents those states geometrically.

Result: The small transformer learns to track the Bayesian posterior over the planted latent variable across tokens, effectively maintaining belief states. In its internal representation space, the 8 latent states are arranged in a ring matching the order in the underlying Markov chain, showing that the geometry of its concepts mirrors the transition structure of the latent dynamics.

Conclusion: The study provides empirical evidence that transformers can learn Bayesian belief tracking over latent variables even in naturalistic text, and that the geometry of learned concepts in activation space can be shaped by the statistical dynamics (e.g., Markov structure) of the underlying latent variables. This links belief-state interpretations of LLMs to feature-geometry findings in interpretability.

Abstract: LLMs are thought to track "belief states," i.e., running probability distributions over the latent variables that govern language (Shai et al., 2024; Sarfati et al., 2026), but so far this has only been comprehensively demonstrated on toy synthetic data and in a few isolated case studies. It has also never been empirically connected to the geometry of LLM features (the concepts interpretability finds in model activations). In this work, we plant a controllable latent variable inside natural-looking text. An LLM teacher writes ordinary text while we "subliminally" steer it along one of K = 8 unrelated sparse autoencoder directions at each token, with the active directions following a ring-shaped Markov chain. A small transformer model trained on this corpus does indeed track the Bayesian posterior belief about our planted latent variable. Moreover, it also arranges the 8 states themselves on a ring, in the exact order of the Markov chain, which is supporting evidence that a concept's geometry can be formed by the statistical dynamics of the latent variable behind it.

</details>


### [84] [TabuLM: Morphology-Aware Tabular Pre-training for Low-Resource Languages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26923)
*Ireddi Rakshitha,Devavarapu Yashwanth,Ntakirutimana Pierre*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: TabuLM is the first table-focused language model for Kinyarwanda, extending a morphological KinyaBERT with table-aware embeddings and pre-training tasks, and it sets a new state of the art on a new Kinyarwanda table QA benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Kinyarwanda, despite having over 12 million speakers and being morphologically rich, lacks tabular representation learning resources and dedicated models for table understanding and question answering. Existing multilingual models underperform on Kinyarwanda tables, and there is no native benchmark for table QA in this language, limiting the development of data tools and applications in the Rwandan context.

Method: The authors extend KinyaBERT-large, a two-tier morphological transformer, with additive embeddings representing table rows, columns, and cell types, plus a learned attention bias that strengthens attention among cells in the same row or column. They introduce two pre-training objectives tailored for tabular data: (1) Masked Cell Recovery (MCR), which masks full cells and requires reconstruction from their row and column context; and (2) Column Type Prediction (CTP), which predicts the semantic type of each column from its observed cell values. The model is pre-trained on 172 Kinyarwanda government tables (~35k cells) drawn from several open-data portals and then evaluated on a newly created table QA benchmark.

Result: TabuLM attains 62.0% exact match on the new TabQA-kin benchmark, outperforming its backbone KinyaBERT-large by 5.7 EM points and beating multilingual baselines mBERT (49.3% EM) and XLM-R (50.0% EM) by 11.7–12.7 EM points. Ablation-style analysis indicates that structural table embeddings particularly improve performance on comparison and lookup questions, while the model’s morphological design delivers additional, complementary improvements.

Conclusion: Table-specific architectural biases and objectives can significantly improve table understanding in low-resource, morphologically rich languages such as Kinyarwanda, even with relatively small tabular datasets. Combining structural embeddings with a morphology-aware backbone yields strong gains over multilingual models, and the release of TabuLM, its data, and the TabQA-kin benchmark provides a foundation for future Kinyarwanda table QA and structured-data applications.

Abstract: We present TabuLM, the first language model pre-trained on Kinyarwanda tabular data. Kinyarwanda is a morphologically rich Bantu language spoken by over 12 million people in Rwanda, yet lacks any dedicated tabular representation learning resource. TabuLM extends KinyaBERT-large, a two-tier morphological transformer, with additive row, column, and cell-type embeddings and a learned table-structure attention bias that sharpens same-row and same-column attention. Pre-training uses two new objectives: Masked Cell Recovery (MCR), which masks entire cells and forces reconstruction from row and column context, and Column Type Prediction (CTP), which predicts column semantic types from observed cell values. We pre-train on 172 Rwandan government tables (~35,000 cells) from NISR, RAB, REB, and MoH open-data portals, and introduce TabQA-kin, the first native Kinyarwanda table question-answering benchmark comprising 526 QA pairs across 31 tables and four question types. TabuLM achieves 62.0% exact match on TabQA-kin, outperforming KinyaBERT-large by 5.7 EM points and all multilingual baselines (mBERT 49.3%, XLM-R 50.0%) by 11.7-12.7 points. Analysis shows that structural table embeddings are most decisive for comparison and lookup questions, while morphological awareness provides complementary gains. Our code, data, and pre-trained checkpoint are publicly available.

</details>


### [85] [Mapping Written Words to Spoken Words in a Different Language Using Only Visual Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26925)
*Gabriel Pirlogeanu,Dan Oneata,Horia Cucu,Herman Kamper*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They learn to map written English keywords to segments in Hindi speech using images as a bridge, without any transcriptions or training a complex model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Collecting and annotating speech data in low-resource languages is hard, even just getting people to talk on command. A partial workaround is to have people describe pictures, which produces paired image–speech data. However, turning such visually grounded speech into useful word-level models, especially across languages (English text to Hindi speech) and without transcriptions, remains challenging. Prior solutions rely on end-to-end multimodal neural models that can be complex and data-hungry, motivating a simpler and more data-efficient alternative.

Method: They start with a dataset of images paired with Hindi spoken captions. Off-the-shelf image captioning systems are used to automatically generate written English tags/keywords for each image. For any given English keyword, they collect all Hindi utterances whose images have that tag. They represent these Hindi speech signals using self-supervised speech features (rather than supervised ASR features). Using an alignment-based procedure, they align multiple Hindi utterances that share the same keyword and aggregate the alignment evidence to detect recurring segments in the speech that consistently correspond to the target word. They also extend the alignment to include negative examples (utterances that should not contain the keyword) to sharpen the mapping. No explicit supervised model training or transcriptions are used; the mapping emerges from alignment over self-supervised representations guided by visual tags.

Result: In keyword spotting and localization tasks—finding whether a keyword occurs in an utterance and where—the proposed alignment-based approach outperforms a prior attention-based end-to-end multimodal neural model that used the same kind of data. Incorporating negative examples during alignment further improves performance, demonstrating that contrastive information helps in isolating the correct speech segments corresponding to the target word.

Conclusion: Their work shows that accurate cross-lingual mappings from written words (in English) to spoken realizations (in Hindi) can be learned directly from visually grounded speech data, without manual transcriptions or explicit neural model training. A relatively simple alignment-based method built on self-supervised speech representations can surpass more complex attention-based neural models, highlighting the strength of self-supervised features and visual grounding for low-resource speech technology and cross-lingual applications.

Abstract: In many low-resource settings, even just eliciting speech for data collection is difficult. One promising approach has been to ask speakers to describe images. But how do we build models from such visually grounded speech data? Given a dataset of images with Hindi spoken captions, we consider how we can map a written English keyword to spoken realisations of that word in Hindi. Previous work trained end-to-end multimodal neural models. Instead, we explore a simpler alignment-based approach built on self-supervised speech representations. Written English tags are automatically obtained from images using off-the-shelf image captioning systems. Hindi utterances associated with the same keyword are then aligned (using self-supervised features), and alignment evidence is aggregated to identify recurring speech segments corresponding to the target word. Experiments evaluating keyword spotting and localization show that our alignment-based approach outperforms a previous attention-based neural model. We also show the benefit of incorporating negative examples during alignment. Our work demonstrates that cross-lingual word-to-speech mappings can be learned directly from visual grounding without transcriptions or explicit model training.

</details>


### [86] [KinyaEmbed: Contrastive Sentence Embeddings for Kinyarwanda via Multi-Stage Curriculum Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26941)
*Ireddi Rakshitha,Devavarapu Yashwanth,Ntakirutimana Pierre*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Introduces KinyaEmbed, the first dedicated sentence embedding model for the Kinyarwanda language, significantly outperforming existing multilingual models and releasing all resources publicly.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multilingual sentence embedding models perform poorly on Kinyarwanda due to its under-representation in pre-training corpora, leaving a major language with over 12 million speakers without high-quality embedding tools for downstream NLP tasks like semantic similarity and clustering.

Method: Build on KinyaBERT-large and train a sentence embedding model via a four-stage MultipleNegativesRankingLoss curriculum: (1) pre-train on ~18k paraphrase pairs from Rwanda’s Official Gazette with multiple temperature scales; (2) fine-tune using 715 NLLB-translated MNLI triplets to inject entailment/contradiction structure; (3) perform representation alignment using English–Kinyarwanda OPUS-100 parallel data; (4) refine on 2,936 high-quality Kinyarwanda pairs selected with KinyaCOMET at a 0.8 quality threshold. Finally, ensemble seven checkpoints (with the last-stage checkpoints double-weighted) for inference.

Result: On the SemRel2024-rw benchmark, the seven-checkpoint ensemble attains a Spearman correlation of 0.7298, outperforming mE5-large by 20.9% and OpenAI text-embedding-3-large by 41.0%. It also achieves the best document clustering performance among evaluated models, with a silhouette score of 0.2146.

Conclusion: A dedicated, carefully curriculum-trained Kinyarwanda sentence embedding model can substantially outperform large multilingual baselines on semantic similarity and clustering tasks. The released checkpoints, filtered training pairs, and a new Wiki-RW-STS benchmark provide a strong, reproducible foundation for future Kinyarwanda NLP research and applications.

Abstract: We present KinyaEmbed, the first dedicated sentence embedding model for Kinyarwanda, a morphologically rich Bantu language spoken by over 12 million people in Rwanda. Existing multilingual embedding models such as LaBSE, mE5-large, and OpenAI text-embedding-3-large perform poorly on Kinyarwanda due to severe under-representation in their pre-training corpora. KinyaEmbed is built on KinyaBERT-large and trained via a four-stage curriculum using MultipleNegativesRankingLoss (MNRL): Stage 1 leverages ~18,000 paraphrase pairs from the Official Gazette of Rwanda with three temperature scales; Stage 2 fine-tunes on 715 NLLB-translated MNLI triplets for entailment structure; Stage 3 aligns representations using English-Kinyarwanda OPUS-100 translation pairs; Stage 4 refines with 2,936 high-quality pairs filtered from KinyaCOMET at quality threshold 0.8. We evaluate on SemRel2024-rw and introduce Wiki-RW-STS, a new contamination-free Kinyarwanda STS benchmark of 300 pairs derived from Kinyarwanda Wikipedia. A seven-checkpoint ensemble (all5+23A*2, with the final stage double-weighted) achieves Spearman \r{ho}=0.7298 on SemRel2024-rw, surpassing mE5-large by 20.9% and OpenAI text-embedding-3-large by 41.0%. KinyaEmbed also achieves the best document clustering silhouette score (0.2146) across all evaluated models. All checkpoints, the KinyaCOMET filtered pairs, and the Wiki-RW-STS benchmark are publicly available.

</details>


### [87] [Squeezing More from Limited Data with Recursive Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26973)
*Serdar Gülbahar,Lukas Edman,Alexander Fraser*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Paper studies how to scale language models when pre-training data is very limited and proposes architectural changes that work better than standard Transformers in this regime.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard scaling laws and Transformer designs assume web-scale data, but many realistic scenarios have a small, fixed data budget. In these low-data regimes, simply increasing model size leads to overfitting and worse generalization, so new scaling strategies and architectures are needed.

Method: Empirically compare models trained with pre-training budgets between 10M and 100M words on two corpora and multiple downstream tasks. Analyze how optimal model size varies with data budget and downstream target. Investigate why standard Transformers scale poorly in this regime, focusing on the parameter/computation coupling via embeddings and depth. Propose and train recursive Transformer architectures that reuse a shared block across depth to decouple compute from parameter count, combined with factorized embeddings to shrink the vocabulary-related parameters.

Result: Find that for fixed small data budgets, there is an optimal model size beyond which performance degrades due to overfitting, and this optimum depends heavily on both data budget and downstream task. Show that standard Transformers are inefficient at small scale because embeddings dominate parameters and per-token compute is tied to capacity. Demonstrate that three proposed recursive Transformer models outperform standard Transformers at 10M and 100M word budgets and perform competitively with BabyLM Challenge 2025 winning systems.

Conclusion: Effective pre-training with limited data requires different scaling principles and architectures than web-scale language modeling. By decoupling parameter count from depth via recursive blocks and reducing embedding parameters with factorization, it is possible to better exploit abundant compute under a fixed, small data budget and achieve state-of-the-art or competitive performance in low-data benchmarks.

Abstract: Pre-training under limited data requires a different view of scaling than web-scale language modeling. With a fixed data budget but relatively abundant compute, increasing parameter count helps only up to an optimal scale; beyond that point, models overfit and generalization worsens. We study this behavior across 10M-100M word pre-training budgets, two corpora, and multiple downstream evaluations, and find that optimal size depends strongly on both the data budget and the downstream target. We argue that standard Transformers scale down poorly to this setting, because embeddings consume a large fraction of the parameter budget and per-token computation is tied to representational capacity. To address this coupling, we study recursive Transformers, reusing a shared block across depth to scale compute, together with factorized embeddings to reduce vocabulary-map parameters. We train three recursive models and find that they outperform standard Transformers at 10M and 100M words, while remaining competitive with BabyLM Challenge 2025 winners.

</details>


### [88] [JudgeStealer: Extracting LLM Judging Capabilities across Evaluation Protocols](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26982)
*Chen Chen,Yaolin Chen,Xuehan Sun,Juan Lin,Xueluan Gong,Yuhang Zheng,Qian Wang,Kwok-Yan Lam*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A framework, JUDGESTEALER, efficiently extracts and replicates LLM judging capabilities across multiple evaluation protocols under a limited query budget.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM judges (used for scoring, comparison, and ranking) are valuable IP but can be attacked via model extraction. Existing extraction methods are not tailored to LLM judges, are inefficient with limited queries, and do not jointly handle multiple evaluation protocols.

Method: JUDGESTEALER performs query-efficient extraction by: (1) exploiting cross-protocol agreement—collecting only pointwise scores from the victim and converting them into pairwise and listwise supervision signals; (2) dynamically selecting which pointwise inputs to query based on semantic diversity, predictive uncertainty, and estimated judge biases to maximize information per query; and (3) using score smoothing and multi-protocol review during surrogate training to better preserve score ordinality and prevent catastrophic forgetting when adapting the surrogate model.

Result: On state-of-the-art LLM judges and reward models, JUDGESTEALER surpasses prior extraction baselines, reaching up to 73.3% accuracy for pointwise scoring, 87.0% for pairwise comparison, and 71.6% for listwise ranking, and works well across different surrogate scales, adaptation strategies, and reasoning modes while remaining robust to several extraction defenses.

Conclusion: JUDGESTEALER is an effective, query-efficient extraction framework specifically tailored to LLM judges, capable of replicating their behavior across multiple evaluation protocols with high fidelity and robustness, even under limited queries and common defenses.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) judges are increasingly used across various evaluation scenarios, making their judgment capabilities valuable intellectual property. However, black-box access exposes these capabilities to model extraction attacks. Existing extraction methods do not specifically target LLM judges and provide limited support for multiple evaluation protocols under restricted query budgets. In this study, we propose JUDGESTEALER, the first query-efficient model extraction framework for replicating judging capabilities across pointwise scoring, pairwise comparison, and listwise ranking protocols. JUDGESTEALER exploits the strong cross-protocol agreement to acquire pointwise scores and transform them into pairwise and listwise supervisions without additional victim queries. To capture informative judge patterns and improve query efficiency, JUDGESTEALER dynamically selects pointwise inputs based on semantic diversity, predictive uncertainty, and potential judge biases. It further applies score smoothing and multi-protocol review to preserve the ordinal structure of scores and mitigate catastrophic forgetting during surrogate adaptation. Extensive experiments on state-of-the-art LLM-as-a-judge and reward models show that JUDGESTEALER consistently outperforms existing extraction baselines, achieving up to 73.3%, 87.0%, and 71.6% accuracy for pointwise, pairwise, and listwise evaluation, respectively. JUDGESTEALER also remains effective across different sur- rogate model scales, adaptation strategies, and reasoning settings. Moreover, JUDGESTEALER demonstrates robustness against representative extraction defenses.

</details>


### [89] [ITL: Interpretable Document Alignment with Structured Reference Frameworks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27031)
*Raúl Giráldez,Dayrelis Mena,Jesús S. Aguilar--Ruiz*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces Intelligent Target Locator (ITL), a method to measure how well documents align with structured reference frameworks by producing traceable, concept-level affinity scores between text segments and framework concepts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing retrieval and classification methods mostly yield overall similarity scores or discrete labels, offering little insight into how specific concepts in a structured framework are represented across a document. There is a need for quantitative, interpretable, and traceable measures of alignment between documents and structured reference documents (SRDs), especially at the concept level.

Method: ITL is a domain-agnostic, language-portable methodology that computes affinities between textual units in a target document and concepts in a Structured Reference Document (SRD). From the SRD, it builds concept-specific terminological profiles using independent terms, bigrams, trigrams, and co-occurrences. Each term receives an importance weight based on concept membership, term-type specificity, and inter-concept discriminability. ITL outputs a textual-unit–concept affinity matrix, which can be aggregated at different granularities.

Result: Using the 17 Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) as a test case, the authors evaluated each official goal statement against an SRD derived from the same set of descriptors. Each statement showed the highest affinity with its corresponding SDG concept, and affinities with non-matching concepts were comparatively low. This demonstrates that ITL can clearly separate and identify the conceptual profiles of the framework.

Conclusion: ITL provides a general, framework-agnostic way to quantify how well documents align with structured reference frameworks, while maintaining traceability from each affinity score back to the specific terminological evidence that supports it. This enables more interpretable and fine-grained assessments of conceptual alignment than traditional similarity or classification methods.

Abstract: Measuring alignment between documents and structured reference frameworks requires identifying conceptual evidence distributed throughout the text and reporting it through measures that are quantitative, interpretable, and traceable. Many commonly used retrieval and classification approaches return either pairwise similarity scores or one or more class labels, whereas fewer methods provide concept-level scores that are directly traceable to the terminological evidence supporting them. We present \emph{Intelligent Target Locator} (ITL), a domain-agnostic and language-portable methodology that estimates the affinity between the textual units of a target document and the concepts defined in a \emph{Structured Reference Document} ($SRD$). From the $SRD$, ITL induces concept-specific terminological profiles built from independent terms, bigrams, trigrams, and co-occurrences. Each term is assigned an importance weight that combines concept membership, term-type specificity and inter-concept discriminability. The output is a textual-unit--concept affinity matrix that can be aggregated at different levels of granularity. We conduct an internal consistency assessment using the 17 Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), evaluating each official goal statement against the $SRD$ induced from the same set of descriptors. Every statement reached its highest affinity with the corresponding concept, and the mean affinity across the remaining concepts stayed marginal relative to the mean reference affinity. This separation indicates that ITL distinguishes the conceptual profiles of the framework. ITL thus offers a general basis for quantifying document alignment with structured frameworks while keeping each result traceable to the terminological evidence that supports it.

</details>


### [90] [Representing and Parsing Korean Constituency Structure at Different Levels of Granularity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27035)
*Jungyeul Park,KyungTae Lim,Zihao Huang,Eunkyul Leah Jo,Yige Chen,Chulwoo Park*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper evaluates different ways to represent Korean text in constituency parsing and finds that using fine-grained morpheme-level terminals with detailed POS tags yields the best parsing performance, though eojeols remain preferable as the main surface annotation units.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Korean presents a challenge for constituency parsing because the basic written unit, the eojeol, is morphologically complex and does not align neatly with simple word terminals in phrase-structure trees. Existing treebanks diverge in how they model eojeol-internal morphology and null elements, making it unclear which representation is best both empirically (for parsers) and linguistically. The authors want to systematically compare representations to understand how terminal choice and POS granularity affect parsing quality, while also considering linguistic interpretability and resource design.

Method: The authors derive three alternative constituency representations from the Penn Korean Treebank: (1) Morpheme+XPOS, where terminals are morphemes with fine-grained Korean POS tags; (2) Eojeol+XPOS, where terminals are eojeols with fine-grained POS tags; and (3) Eojeol+UPOS, where terminals are eojeols with coarse universal POS tags. They normalize the treebank by removing null elements, aligning phrase-structure with overt eojeol tokens, and preserving original phrase labels as much as possible while varying terminal and preterminal layers. Using these representations, they train standard non-binary transition-based constituency parsers with top-down, in-order, and bottom-up strategies under a unified modeling and evaluation framework. All experiments assume gold terminal segmentation and gold preterminal tags, focusing on parsing performance conditioned on perfect morphosyntactic annotation.

Result: When evaluated with gold segmentation and POS tagging, representations that use eojeols as terminals lead to shorter transition sequences for the parsers, making parsing procedurally simpler. However, Eojeol+UPOS, which uses only coarse-grained POS tags, performs significantly worse than representations with richer morphology. Adding fine-grained XPOS tags at the eojeol level (Eojeol+XPOS) improves performance and reduces the gap, but the best results come from the Morpheme+XPOS representation, even after projecting its output back to the eojeol domain for comparison. These results indicate that access to detailed morphological information and fine-grained XPOS tags clearly benefits the evaluated constituency parsers.

Conclusion: The empirical findings show that, under gold morphosyntactic conditions, fine-grained morphological segmentation and detailed XPOS tagging provide substantial advantages for Korean constituency parsing. Morpheme-level terminals with XPOS yield the strongest parsing performance, suggesting that rich morphosyntactic information is highly informative for parsers. At the same time, the authors argue that these results do not settle the theoretical question of the linguistically correct terminal domain. From a linguistic and annotation-design perspective, they recommend keeping eojeol as the primary, stable surface unit for phrase-structure trees, while aligning morpheme-level and XPOS information to eojeols as auxiliary evidence to combine linguistic interpretability with parsing effectiveness.

Abstract: Korean constituency parsing raises a representational challenge because the terminal units of a phrase-structure tree do not straightforwardly correspond to simple surface words. Korean eojeols are morphologically complex spacing units, and existing constituency resources differ in how they represent eojeol-internal morphology and non-overt elements. This paper compares three constituency parsing representations derived from the Penn Korean Treebank: Morpheme+XPOS, Eojeol+XPOS, and Eojeol+UPOS. We construct these representations by removing null elements, aligning Penn Korean phrase structure with overt eojeol tokens, preserving Penn Korean phrase labels where possible, and varying the terminal and preterminal layers. We then evaluate canonical non-binary transition-based constituency parsers in top-down, in-order, and bottom-up orders under a shared modeling and evaluation setup. All experiments use gold terminal segmentation and gold preterminal labels and therefore evaluate constituency parsing conditioned on gold morphosyntactic annotation. Eojeol terminals yield shorter transition sequences, but Eojeol+UPOS parsing substantially underperforms the morphologically richer conditions. Eojeol+XPOS narrows this gap, while Morpheme+XPOS gives the strongest results even after its predictions are projected to the eojeol terminal domain. Under these gold-annotation conditions, the results show that fine-grained morphological and XPOS representations provide valuable evidence for the evaluated parsers. This empirical finding concerns the information available for parsing and does not by itself determine the linguistically preferable terminal domain. Independently, linguistic and resource-design considerations motivate eojeol as a stable and interpretable surface domain for phrase-structure annotation, with morpheme-level and XPOS information retained as aligned morphosyntactic evidence.

</details>


### [91] [Reasoning about In-Context Samples for Machine-Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27036)
*Maxime Bouthors,Josep Crego,François Yvon*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They propose a new chain-of-thought-style, fragment-based reasoning framework for LLM-based machine translation that extracts and uses parallel source-target fragments from in-context exemplars, trained via distillation from a larger teacher LLM, and show it improves MT quality across multiple languages and domains compared to standard few-shot and drafting baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM-based MT often relies on simple prompting (e.g., k-shot) or basic drafting without explicit reasoning, which limits reliability and adaptability across languages and domains. While chain-of-thought has helped reasoning tasks, it is unclear how to best adapt explicit reasoning to MT, where latent alignment and reuse of example translations are crucial. The authors aim to design a reasoning process that decomposes translation into reusable aligned fragments from retrieved examples, to improve translation quality and robustness.

Method: They design a fragment-based reasoning framework for LLM MT: given a source sentence and retrieved similar source-target exemplars, the LLM first extracts parallel fragments (aligned spans) between the input and exemplars on both source and target sides. These extracted fragments serve as intermediate reasoning traces that guide generation of the final translation. To enable this, they use a large teacher LLM to automatically generate "silver" (noisy but useful) fragments and translation drafts, and distill this behavior into a smaller Qwen3 family model via supervised training on these traces. At inference, the student model performs this two-stage process with in-context examples: fragment extraction/selection followed by final translation generation conditioned on fragments.

Result: Across 6 languages and up to 5 domains per language, using Qwen3 models, the fragment-based MT method yields significantly better translation performance than standard k-shot prompting and simple drafting-based approaches. The improvements likely manifest in higher automatic MT metrics (e.g., BLEU/COMET) and better robustness across domains, demonstrating that explicit fragment-level reasoning and teacher-student distillation provide measurable gains for LLM-based MT.

Conclusion: Explicit, fragment-based reasoning is an effective way to adapt chain-of-thought-style methods to machine translation with LLMs. By extracting and using parallel source-target fragments from retrieved examples as intermediate traces, and training via distillation from a stronger teacher, smaller LLMs can achieve significantly better MT quality across languages and domains than with traditional few-shot or drafting methods. This suggests that structured reuse of example translations at the fragment level is a powerful inductive bias for LLM-based MT.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) can be trained to perform chain-of-thoughts reasoning in order to improve the reliability of their responses. In this work, we investigate how explicit reasoning can be leveraged for LLM-Based Machine Translation (MT) with in-context samples. We introduce a novel fragment-based reasoning framework in which the model first extracts parallel source-target fragments from retrieved similar exemplars, and uses these fragments as intermediate reasoning traces to produce the final translation. To train our model, we distill silver fragments and drafts from a large teacher model. Our experiments with the Qwen3 model family, over 6 languages, including up to 5 domains per language, demonstrate that fragment-based MT significantly outperforms alternative methods like standard k-shot or basic drafting.

</details>


### [92] [Cascaded Batch Prompting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27038)
*Sho Hoshino,Peinan Zhang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces cascaded batch prompting, a two-stage prompting strategy that maintains batch efficiency while stabilizing and improving task performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Batch prompting accelerates large language model inference by handling multiple inputs together, but its task performance is unreliable and can degrade unpredictably. The authors want a method that preserves the speed benefits of batching while making downstream performance competitive or better than standard single-instance prompting.

Method: They design a two-stage ‘cascaded’ prompting scheme that separates complex reasoning from symbol grounding. In stage one, the model focuses on reasoning over the inputs in a way that is less sensitive to batch interference. In stage two, it performs symbol grounding or final decision mapping, again in a batched fashion but with the reasoning already disentangled. They evaluate this approach on multiple-choice question answering and natural language inference tasks, comparing it to standard single prompting and conventional batch prompting across different batch sizes.

Result: Across benchmarks in multiple-choice QA and NLI, cascaded batch prompting consistently outperforms standard single-instance prompting while still benefiting from the computational speedups of batching. Performance remains stable or improves as batch size grows, in contrast to conventional batch prompting where accuracy can fluctuate or drop.

Conclusion: Cascaded batch prompting resolves much of the unpredictability associated with conventional batch prompting by decoupling reasoning from symbol grounding. It delivers better or comparable accuracy to single prompting while achieving near-linear speedup with batch size, thus setting a new state of the art on the efficiency–performance Pareto frontier for LLM inference.

Abstract: Although batch prompting makes large language model inference more efficient by processing multiple instances simultaneously, it suffers from unpredictable downstream task performance. We propose cascaded batch prompting, a two-stage approach designed to resolve the unpredictability of conventional batch prompting by disentangling complex reasoning from symbol grounding. Experiments on multiple-choice question answering and natural language inference demonstrate that the proposed method outperforms the standard single prompting baseline while achieving a speedup proportional to batch size, establishing a new state of the art on the Pareto frontier.

</details>


### [93] [Research Design Tracking and Assessment for the Social Sciences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27049)
*Marco Rovera,Sergiu Burlacu,Dominique Cappelletti,Alessio Tomelleri,Sonia Marzadro,Martina Bazzoli,Annalisa Tassi,Jessica Gagete-Miranda*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces ARDTrA, an automated system for detecting and evaluating causal research designs in social science papers, showing that passage length strongly affects LLM-based performance and that machine difficulty differs from human expert disagreement across design types.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Causal research designs are central to evidence-based policy in the social sciences, but assessing whether a paper uses an appropriate design and applies it well has required labor-intensive manual expert review. With growing volumes of research and the need for scalable, consistent evaluation tools, there is a strong motivation to automate identification and quality assessment of research designs.

Method: The authors define a new task, Automated Research Design Tracking and Assessment (ARDTrA), which consists of detecting which counterfactual research design a paper uses and evaluating the quality of its implementation. They compile an expert-annotated dataset of papers spanning six major families of counterfactual designs. They then build a multi-turn retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) conversational pipeline and systematically evaluate performance across combinations of four retrieval strategies, four large language models, and six embedding models, analyzing what factors drive performance.

Result: Empirical analysis shows that the length of retrieved passages is the dominant factor affecting system performance, accounting for 52–66% of the variance across configurations. The per-design analysis reveals that the research designs that are hardest for the automated system are not the ones where human experts most frequently disagree, indicating that the system’s challenges are not simply mirroring human ambiguity. Instead, there are distinct sources of difficulty for humans and machines.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates that LLM-based, RAG-style systems can be applied to automate aspects of research design detection and assessment, but their performance is heavily influenced by how much textual context they receive. Moreover, machine and human difficulty patterns diverge, implying that improving such systems will require addressing model-specific limitations beyond resolving human disagreements or ambiguities in the designs themselves.

Abstract: Reliable assessment of causal research designs in the social sciences is critical for evidence-based policy-making, yet has so far relied entirely on manual expert analysis. We introduce Automated Research Design Tracking and Assessment (ARDTrA), a task that involves detecting the research design used in a paper and assessing the quality of its application. We create an expert-annotated dataset of papers covering six families of counterfactual research designs and evaluate the task using a multi-turn RAG-based conversational pipeline. Across four retrieval strategies, four LLMs and six embedding models, we find that passage length is the main driver of performance, explaining 52-66% of the variance. A per-research-design analysis also shows that human and machine difficulty do not align: the designs that prove hardest for the system are not those on which expert annotators disagree most, pointing to two independent sources of task difficulty.

</details>


### [94] [DocTalkBN: A Novel Dataset of Expert Telemedicine Conversations in Bengali](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27110)
*Anik Saha,Fahmida Sultana Naznin,Sadatul Islam Sadi,Ananya Shahrin Promi,Wahid Al Azad Navid,Rifat Shahriyar*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: DocTalkBN introduces a large-scale, real-world Bengali telemedicine conversation dataset with audio-text pairs and benchmarks for several clinical NLP tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a lack of authentic, large-scale medical conversational datasets for low-resource languages like Bengali, which impedes the development of reliable, culturally grounded medical conversational AI and clinical NLP systems.

Method: The authors collected nationally broadcast telemedicine program recordings featuring board-certified physicians, transcribed and aligned the audio with text, organized the data into structured conversations, and derived three benchmark tasks—medical triage classification, advice safety evaluation, and medical NER—on which they evaluated various large language models and encoder-based baselines.

Result: They created DocTalkBN with 557.63 hours of audio-text pairs, 1,515 multi-turn patient calls, 10,274 host–doctor QA exchanges, and 1.7M tokens across 26 specialties, and showed through experiments that models trained/evaluated on this corpus perform well on clinically grounded reasoning tasks, confirming the dataset’s practical utility.

Conclusion: DocTalkBN is a valuable resource for advancing medical NLP in Bengali, enabling research on safer, clinically grounded, and culturally appropriate healthcare AI in low-resource language settings; the dataset and code are publicly released to support further work.

Abstract: Reliable medical conversational AI requires authentic expert--patient interaction data, yet such datasets remain scarce, especially for low-resource languages such as Bengali. We present DocTalkBN, a large-scale multimodal dataset of real-world expert telemedicine conversations in Bengali, collected from nationally broadcast telemedicine programs featuring board-certified physicians. DocTalkBN contains 557.63 hours of paired audio and text, 1,515 multi-turn patient calls, 10,274 host--doctor question--answer exchanges, totaling 1.7M tokens, spanning 26 medical specialties. Unlike prior resources derived from medical forums, written health content, or synthetic data, our dataset preserves the spontaneity, contextual richness, and spoken characteristics of authentic medical interactions in a low-resource setting. To support benchmark-driven research, we further construct three downstream tasks from the corpus, medical triage classification, advice safety evaluation, and medical named entity recognition, and benchmark a diverse set of large language models and encoder-based baselines. Our results show that DocTalkBN is a practically useful resource, particularly for clinically grounded reasoning tasks. We release this resource to facilitate future research on reliable medical NLP and safer, more culturally grounded healthcare systems for low-resource languages. Our source codes and dataset are publicly available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/doctalk.

</details>


### [95] [Cross-Lingual Alignment Without Joint Training: Do Monolingual Language Models Converge on Universal Representations?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27115)
*Ej Zhou,Suchir Salhan,Catherine Arnett,Anna Korhonen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper investigates whether cross-lingual alignment can naturally emerge in strictly monolingual language models trained on non-parallel data, showing that such models do learn alignable representations without any joint multilingual training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most explanations of cross-lingual alignment in multilingual language models rely on joint training mechanisms (shared parameters, mixed-language batches, explicit alignment losses). The authors want to isolate and test whether alignment can instead arise purely from the structure and shared information content of different languages, even when models are trained independently on non-parallel corpora.

Method: They study strictly monolingual models, including the Goldfish families and models independently developed by different labs, ensuring no joint training or shared data. They measure cross-lingual representational similarity layer by layer, apply a single Procrustes rotation learned from parallel sentences to map hidden states across models, and run a causal test by patching rotated residual streams from an English model into a German model during factual cloze tasks to see if predictions switch to the donor model’s output.

Result: (1) Correlation: Independently trained monolingual models exhibit alignable representational geometries across layers, and the degree of alignment increases with data scale, model size, and linguistic similarity. (2) Construction: A simple, global Procrustes rotation is sufficient to map hidden states between models across languages. (3) Causation: After applying this rotation, directly patching English residual activations into a German model often flips its factual cloze prediction to match the English model’s capital, demonstrating that aligned dimensions carry transferable functional content.

Conclusion: Cross-lingual alignment can emerge spontaneously in independently trained monolingual language models due to shared structural regularities and information in languages, without needing joint multilingual training. This insight enables new practical directions such as stitching or merging separately trained monolingual models and constructing modular multilingual systems from independently trained components.

Abstract: Cross-lingual alignment in multilingual language models is typically attributed to joint training: shared parameters, mixed-language batches, or explicit alignment objectives. We ask whether monolingual models trained on non-parallel data learn alignable representations without joint training. By testing on strictly monolingual language models, such as the Goldfish model families and independently developed models from different research labs, we find three results. Correlation: these models develop alignable representational geometry across layers, with alignment strengthening as data scale, model scale, or linguistic proximity increases. Construction: a single Procrustes rotation fit on parallel sentences maps hidden states between models. Causation: the same rotation transfers functional content; patching a rotated English residual into a German model on a factual cloze flips the prediction to the donor's capital in most cases. We confirm that cross-lingual alignment can emerge from the structure of language and the information it carries rather than from joint training, and this points to practical future directions including model stitching, merging, and modular multilingual systems built from monolingual components.

</details>


### [96] [TwinKV: A Composable Repair Pass for KV Cache Eviction via Pairwise Key Redundancy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27128)
*Hong Chen,Yudong Zeng,Yongwei Huang,Zuhao Ouyang,Junyan Zhang,Xuming Hu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces TwinKV, a training-free, attention-free method that repairs existing KV-cache eviction policies by detecting redundant and orphaned tokens in long-context inference, improving performance under tight memory budgets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-context inference with transformer models is limited by the large memory required for key-value (KV) caches, which especially constrains small models. Existing cache eviction methods largely rely on attention magnitudes or simple distance heuristics, assuming attention correlates with a token’s causal importance. The authors show this assumption is flawed using a leave-one-out probe, motivating a more reliable, training-free signal for deciding which tokens to retain or discard under strict KV budgets.

Method: The authors first run a controlled leave-one-out analysis to measure each token’s causal contribution to model outputs and correlate it with attention magnitudes, finding near-zero correlation. They then propose TwinKV, an attention-free redundancy signal that checks for near-duplicate keys in the KV cache. TwinKV is used as a composable repair layer on top of existing eviction policies: given the set of tokens chosen to be retained by a base policy, it finds (1) evicted tokens with no surviving similar counterpart (orphans) and (2) retained tokens whose information is duplicated elsewhere (redundant donors). TwinKV swaps orphans with suitable donors while preserving the original memory budget and base scoring rule. The approach is evaluated in composition with four recent eviction policies across multiple long-context benchmarks and compression ratios.

Result: Empirically, attention magnitude is shown to be uninformative about causal contribution (Spearman ρ ≈ 0). When composed with four existing eviction policies at compression ratios 0.3, 0.5, and 0.7, TwinKV improves performance in most tested settings without additional training. On Qwen3-4B, it improves a majority of configurations for two policies, is roughly neutral for a third, and brings smaller or occasional gains for a fourth policy that was already near a performance ceiling; improvements shrink as compression becomes looser. On Llama-3.2-1B, that same fourth policy benefits in all evaluated RULER settings, and the model shows smaller average gains on LongBench but a higher fraction of improved evaluation cells across LongBench and LooGLE, plus consistent improvements on RULER. The authors also find that few-shot classification exemplar setups do not benefit from TwinKV for either model.

Conclusion: TwinKV provides a simple, training-free, attention-independent redundancy signal that can be layered on top of diverse KV cache eviction policies to improve long-context inference under memory constraints. The method challenges the common assumption that attention magnitude is a good proxy for token importance and instead focuses on representational redundancy in the KV cache. While gains depend on the base policy, model, compression ratio, and task structure—and are limited when the base policy is already near-optimal—TwinKV generally yields consistent improvements on long-context reasoning benchmarks, with clear cases where it does not help (e.g., few-shot exemplar classification).

Abstract: Long-context inference is bottlenecked by the memory footprint of the key-value (KV) cache, especially for small models under tight resource budgets. Existing KV cache eviction methods score tokens using the model's attention distribution or, in attention-free variants, each key's distance from a global reference point. Using a controlled leave-one-out probe, we find that attention magnitude is unrelated to a token's causal contribution to the answer (Spearman $ρ=-0.004$), challenging the premise behind dominant eviction methods. We introduce TwinKV, a training-free, attention-free redundancy signal that detects whether a token's key has a near-duplicate elsewhere in context. Rather than replacing existing policies, TwinKV acts as a composable repair pass: given a policy's fixed retained set, it identifies evicted tokens with no surviving duplicate (\emph{orphans}) and retained tokens whose information is duplicated elsewhere (\emph{redundant donors}), then swaps them while preserving the original budget and scoring rule. We compose TwinKV with four recent eviction policies across LongBench, LooGLE, RULER, and a short-context MMLU-Pro no-harm control at compression ratios ${0.3,0.5,0.7}$. On Qwen3-4B, TwinKV improves a majority of configurations for two policies, is near-even for a third, and helps only a minority for a fourth adaptive baseline already near a performance ceiling; gains across the three non-ceiling policies are smallest at the loosest ratio. On RULER with Llama-3.2-1B, however, that fourth policy improves in every evaluated cell because its Alone score leaves substantial room to improve. More broadly, Llama-3.2-1B shows a smaller average LongBench gain but a higher fraction of improved cells on LongBench and LooGLE than Qwen3-4B, plus a clean RULER win. We also identify few-shot classification exemplars as a task structure where TwinKV does not help on either model.

</details>


### [97] [Said Aloud, Read Different: Cross-Modal Instability in Multimodal Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27135)
*Basel Mousi,Fahim Dalvi,Shammur Chowdhury,Firoj Alam,Nadir Durrani*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces a benchmark to test whether multimodal foundation models are consistent when answering visually grounded questions across text vs. speech and English vs. Arabic, revealing notable inconsistencies, especially for speech.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multimodal foundation models are increasingly deployed in speech-first assistants that must understand spoken queries and make vision-based decisions, but it is unknown whether they behave consistently when the same semantic content is delivered via different modalities (text vs. speech) or languages (English vs. Arabic). This gap is especially critical in culturally diverse regions like MENA, where language and modality variation is common and reliability of AI assistants is crucial.

Method: The authors construct a speech-augmented, visually grounded contrastive triplet benchmark using 10,150 culturally grounded images from 18 MENA countries. For each image, they design one true (supported) statement and two plausible but unsupported alternatives, forming contrastive triplets. They define a new metric, contrastive instability, as the conditional rate at which a model fails to correctly resolve all three statements in a triplet, distinguishing fragmented reasoning from total failure. They then evaluate recent multimodal models under both text and speech inputs, in English and Arabic, comparing behavior across these settings using both accuracy and contrastive instability.

Result: Across evaluated multimodal models, shifts in modality (text vs. speech) and language (English vs. Arabic) lead to substantial inconsistencies at the triplet level: models often change their decisions despite semantically equivalent queries. These inconsistencies are not adequately reflected by standard aggregate accuracy metrics. Speech input in particular tends to amplify partial failures, increasing contrastive instability.

Conclusion: The study shows that current multimodal foundation models are not reliably consistent across modality and language, even for semantically equivalent, visually grounded queries, and that this issue is especially visible when using speech. Contrastive instability provides a more sensitive measure of fragmented reasoning than raw accuracy. The authors release their culturally grounded benchmark to support further research on robust, cross-modality and cross-lingual multimodal understanding.

Abstract: Multimodal foundation models are increasingly used in speech-first assistants that must interpret spoken queries and produce visually grounded decisions. Yet it remains unclear whether semantically equivalent queries yield consistent judgments across modality (text vs. speech) and language (English vs. Arabic). We introduce a speech-augmented visually grounded contrastive triplet benchmark spanning 10,150 culturally grounded images from 18 MENA countries, where each image is paired with one supported statement and two plausible but unsupported alternatives. We define contrastive instability as the conditional rate at which a model fails to resolve all statements within a triplet, isolating fragmented reasoning from complete failure. Evaluating recent multimodal models under text and speech in English and Arabic, we find that modality and language shifts introduce substantial triplet-level inconsistencies that are not fully captured by aggregate accuracy, with speech amplifying partial failures. We make the benchmark publicly available to the community.

</details>


### [98] [STAR : Sentence Translation Alignment Rate for Document-to-Document Machine Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27161)
*Yichen Dong,Hao Wang,Junhui Li,Linlong Xu,Longyue Wang,Weihua Luo*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces STAR, a metric for sentence-level structural fidelity in document-to-document machine translation with LLMs, and StarPO, a preference-optimization framework that uses STAR to improve alignment and translation quality, enabling smaller models to outperform larger proprietary systems like GPT-4o in both quality and efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Document-to-document translation with LLMs promises better global coherence than sentence-level MT but often suffers from structural misalignment, such as omitted or hallucinated sentences, breaking source-target correspondence. Existing evaluation and training methods do not explicitly target this structural fidelity, so there is a need for a metric and training approach that directly address sentence-level alignment within document-level generation.

Method: The authors propose Sentence Translation Alignment Rate (STAR), a metric that measures how well sentences in the source document align structurally with sentences in the translated document. Using STAR, they design STAR-masked Preference Optimization (StarPO), which ranks alternative document-level translations by their structural quality and applies a dynamic alignment mask to focus optimization on misaligned segments during training, thus combining document-level preference optimization with fine-grained sentence-level structural supervision.

Result: Experiments on news and literary translation benchmarks show that StarPO substantially improves both overall translation quality and structural integrity compared to baselines. Furthermore, models trained with StarPO, despite being relatively compact, can outperform large proprietary LLMs such as GPT-4o on these Doc2Doc translation tasks while using tokens more efficiently.

Conclusion: Explicitly modeling sentence-level structural fidelity within document-to-document translation leads to better-aligned and higher-quality outputs. STAR provides a practical metric for this alignment, and StarPO leverages it effectively during preference optimization. This approach enables smaller open models to reach and even exceed the performance of much larger systems, suggesting that careful alignment-aware training can substitute for sheer scale in Doc2Doc machine translation.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have enabled a shift from sentence-level to document-to-document (Doc2Doc) machine translation, promising improved global coherence. However, document-to-document generation in a single pass frequently suffers from structural misalignment, manifesting as sentence omissions or hallucinations that violate the core requirement of source-target correspondence. To address this, we introduce Sentence Translation Alignment Rate (STAR), an auxiliary metric that explicitly quantifies sentence-level structural fidelity. Building on this, we propose STAR-masked Preference Optimization (StarPO), a framework that ranks document-level hypotheses by structural quality and utilizes a dynamic alignment mask to focus optimization on misaligned segments. Experimental results across news and literary domains demonstrate that StarPO significantly enhances translation quality and structural integrity. Notably, StarPO allows compact models to surpass the performance of massive proprietary systems like GPT-4o while maintaining superior token efficiency.

</details>


### [99] [Prediction of Prediction (PoP): Inter-Layer Activation Fusion for Single-Pass Hallucination Detection in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27165)
*Himal Badu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They introduce a lightweight method, PoP, that uses internal hidden-state dynamics of an LLM during a single forward pass to detect when the model is likely making factual errors, achieving good accuracy with negligible overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autoregressive LLMs often output factually wrong statements with high confidence, which is problematic in high-stakes settings. Existing uncertainty metrics can be overconfident on falsehoods, and multi-sampling verification methods are costly in memory and latency. The authors want a reliable, low-overhead way to detect factual errors during generation without extra decoding passes.

Method: They analyze internal hidden-state transition dynamics of autoregressive transformers while they generate text. They propose Prediction of Prediction (PoP), which fuses intermediate hidden representations across model depth to quantify layer-transition uncertainty. PoP is computed within a single standard forward pass during generation, requiring no auxiliary decoding calls. They then train/evaluate PoP as a factual-correctness classifier on TruthfulQA, measuring its ability to discriminate true vs false model statements via AUROC.

Result: On the TruthfulQA benchmark, PoP reaches 75.5% AUROC for classifying whether generated outputs are factually correct, while running entirely within the base forward pass, adding under 1.2% runtime latency and no additional generation passes.
