The authors emphasize that these results are based on an author-verified implementation and apply within the reported evaluation setup.

Conclusion: Internal layer-transition uncertainty, as captured by PoP, is a useful signal for detecting factual errors in LLM outputs without resorting to multi-sample verification or heavy additional computation. A simple fusion of intermediate hidden states across depth during a standard forward pass can provide reasonably strong factual-correctness classification with minimal latency overhead, making it potentially practical for higher-stakes deployment scenarios, subject to the limits of the evaluated setting.

Abstract: Autoregressive large language models (LLMs) routinely generate factually incorrect outputs with high decoding confidence, limiting their deployment in high-stakes workflows. Existing output-stage uncertainty metrics can fail when models are overconfident on false assertions, while multi-sample verification pipelines introduce substantial memory and latency overhead. This work evaluates whether internal hidden-state transition dynamics during generation can signal factual errors without auxiliary decoding calls. We introduce Prediction of Prediction (PoP), a mechanism that captures layer-transition uncertainty by fusing intermediate hidden representations across depth during a single forward pass. Evaluated on the TruthfulQA benchmark using autoregressive transformer backbones, PoP achieves an area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUROC) of 75.5% for factual-correctness classification. The mechanism operates within the base forward pass, adding less than 1.2% runtime latency and requiring zero additional generation passes. The numerical results are reported from the author-verified experimental implementation and are bounded by the evaluation scope described below.

</details>


### [100] [When Text Misleads: Inconsistent-Aware Reasoning for Audio-Grounded Dialogue](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27176)
*Yen-Ju Lu,Yuzhe Wang,Yaohan Guan,Xiluo He,Jiarui Hai,Mingrui Liang,Kaavya Chaparala,Thomas Thebaud,Laureano Moro-Velazquez,Najim Dehak,Jesus Villalba*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces ContraTalk, a benchmark to test whether models truly leverage acoustic speech cues instead of relying only on text transcripts, and proposes an Audio Twin reasoning framework that surfaces localized acoustic evidence to improve speech-grounded dialogue understanding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current spoken dialogue systems can often perform well using only text transcripts, making it unclear whether they genuinely use paralinguistic acoustic cues like prosody, emotion, and speaking style. Existing benchmarks rarely distinguish between cases solvable from text alone and cases where audio is essential, enabling models to take transcript-based shortcuts. The authors want to systematically expose and measure this failure mode so that future models are properly evaluated and improved for true speech grounding.

Method: 1) Formalize the notion of cross-modal disagreement, where plausible text-based interpretations of a dialogue contradict the interpretation supported by acoustic signals. 2) Build a scalable framework that finds such disagreement regions by identifying text-biased surface interpretations and converting them into conflict-style QA examples, while also collecting consistent examples where text and audio agree. 3) Construct ContraTalk, a benchmark of 501 QA items covering interaction behavior, emotional state, dialogue acts, social stance, and conversational intent. 4) Propose an agentic reasoning pipeline that converts speech into an “Audio Twin”: a textual, localized representation of acoustic cues (e.g., prosody, emotion markers) that can be fed into a reasoning LLM. 5) Evaluate text-only LLMs, direct AudioLLMs, and the Audio Twin framework on both conflict and consistent cases to quantify grounding and shortcut behavior.

Result: Text-only large language models achieve over 90% accuracy on consistent cases where text and audio agree, but their accuracy falls sharply to 33–48% on conflict cases where audio contradicts plausible transcript-based interpretations, indicating heavy reliance on text shortcuts. AudioLLMs that directly consume speech exhibit only partial grounding, still choosing the transcript-biased trap answer in about 30–40% of conflict cases. The proposed Audio Twin framework improves accuracy on conflict cases and reduces trap selection rates, although performance on consistent cases is still largely dependent on the underlying backbone model.

Conclusion: Transcript-based shortcuts are a key failure mode in spoken dialogue understanding: systems can appear strong while ignoring critical acoustic information. ContraTalk offers a controlled benchmark to expose and measure this behavior through conflict and consistent cases across multiple discourse dimensions. The Audio Twin framework, which turns acoustic evidence into explicit textual cues for downstream reasoning, provides a more controllable and effective way to encourage and analyze speech-grounded reasoning, though its success still depends on the base model used.

Abstract: Understanding spoken dialogue requires joint reasoning over lexical content and paralinguistic acoustic signals such as emotion and conversational intent. However, existing evaluations often allow shortcuts based on transcripts or single-modality solutions, obscuring whether models genuinely ground predictions in speech. We formalize this failure mode as cross-modal disagreement, where transcripts suggest plausible but incorrect surface interpretations while acoustic cues such as prosody or speaking style support different answers. We develop a scalable framework that identifies text-biased surface interpretations and converts disagreement regions into conflict QA examples. We also include consistent cases where transcript-based and speech-grounded interpretations agree, enabling evaluation beyond adversarial audio dependence. This results in ContraTalk, a controlled benchmark containing 501 questions across five discourse dimensions: interaction behavior, emotion state, dialogue act, social stance, and conversational intent. We further develop an agentic-style reasoning framework that converts speech into an Audio Twin, a text-readable representation of localized acoustic cues that exposes acoustic evidence to the reasoning model. Experiments show that strong text-only LLMs exceed 90% accuracy in consistent cases but drop to 33-48% in conflict cases. Direct AudioLLMs provide only partial grounding, still selecting the transcript-biased trap in roughly 30-40% of conflict cases. Our Audio Twin framework improves conflict-case accuracy while reducing trap selection, but its consistent-case behavior remains backbone-dependent. These results identify transcript-based shortcuts as an important failure mode in spoken dialogue understanding and show that explicit acoustic evidence aggregation provides a more controllable interface for diagnosing and improving speech-grounded reasoning.

</details>


### [101] [BALMS: Benchmarking Agentic LLMs for Longitudinal Mental Health Sensing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27219)
*Yu Yvonne Wu,Arvind Pillai,Yuliang Chen,Yuwei Zhang,Sudarshan Regmi,Tess Z. Griffin,Michael V. Heinz,Lisa A. Marsch,Nicholas C. Jacobson,Andrew Campbell*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Benchmarking LLM-based agents for long-term mental health sensing from wearables.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing mental health assessment relies on sparse self-report scales, and current LLM-powered personal-health agents only perform short-term, retrieval-based queries over wearable data; there is no systematic evaluation of whether they can reason over long-term signals to predict wellbeing with evidence-based rationales.

Method: Introduce BALMS, a benchmark built on three real-world longitudinal wearable datasets, defining two task families: (1) closed-form wellbeing score prediction and (2) rationale generation auto-graded by an LLM-as-judge. Evaluate three types of LLM-based agent paradigms, each instantiated with five different open- and closed-source LLM backbones, including analyses of chain-of-thought prompting, feature representations, efficiency, and temporal scaling.

Result: Zero-shot agents generally fail to beat a simple mean-score baseline unless using stronger LLM backbones or compact, semantically meaningful feature representations. Chain-of-thought prompting helps reasoning-oriented models but does not reliably ensure temporal grounding or accurate numerical predictions.

Conclusion: Current LLM-based health agents are weak at longitudinal mental health reasoning; effective future systems must better select and retrieve relevant history, ground their reasoning in temporal evidence, and operate over interpretable behavioral features for reliable wellbeing inference from wearables.

Abstract: Mental health assessment relies on episodic self-report scales, which convert subjective states such as stress into numerical scores but provide only sparse snapshots of wellbeing. Wearable devices offer longitudinal behavioral and physiological signals for continuous, low-burden monitoring. Recent LLM-driven personal-health agents enable natural language queries over wearable signals, but mainly handle short-term, retrieval-based lookups (e.g., highest step count over a week). They do not evaluate whether agents can reason over long-term signals to predict wellbeing scores paired with evidence-grounded rationales. To address this gap, we introduce BALMS, the first systematic benchmark of LLM-based agentic systems for longitudinal mental health sensing. BALMS spans 3 real-world longitudinal datasets, 2 task families (closed-form wellbeing-score prediction and rationale generation auto-graded by an LLM-as-Judge), 3 agentic paradigms evaluated across 5 open- and closed-source LLM backbones. We find that zero-shot agents rarely outperform a simple mean baseline, except with stronger backbones or compact, semantically meaningful features. Chain-of-thought prompting improves reasoning-oriented backbones, but does not guarantee temporal grounding or numerical correctness. Together with more analysis on efficiency and temporal scaling, BALMS highlights the need for longitudinal mental health agents that selectively retrieve history, ground temporal evidence, and reason over interpretable behavioral features.

</details>


### [102] [SCIT: Testing Causal Cache Carriers in Latent Chain-of-Thought Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27265)
*Yi Ding,Lijun Huang,Menglin Yang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Introduces SCIT, a causal diagnostic to locate which parts of a transformer’s cache carry latent chain-of-thought computations, finding that in GPT-2 arithmetic models the late value-cache suffix mainly carries the counterfactual reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Latent chain-of-thought models move reasoning into internal continuous states for efficiency but make it unclear which parts of the model actually carry and transmit that reasoning. The authors want a rigorous, causal way to identify the specific internal cache components responsible for counterfactual computations, rather than relying on correlational or ad-hoc probes.

Method: They propose SCIT (Suffix Cache Interchange Test), a protocol that creates exact source–recipient counterfactual pairs and swaps or patches specified cache segments between runs. SCIT combines: (1) sufficiency tests (whether a cache segment alone can transfer a behavior), (2) splits of key and value components in the K/V cache, (3) controls on hidden states, (4) semantic source controls, (5) decoding-based validation of the transferred behavior, and (6) matched corruption tests for necessity. They apply SCIT to CODI-GPT2 and a Sim-CoT-style GPT-2, systematically testing which cache segments (value suffix, keys, hidden states, specific tokens, etc.) carry arithmetic counterfactuals.

Result: For both CODI-GPT2 and the Sim-CoT-style GPT-2, counterfactual arithmetic behavior transfers chiefly through suffix trajectories in the value cache, not through hidden states, keys, reusable answer slots, or single-token triggers. For CODI-GPT2, they obtain both sufficiency and necessity evidence that late value-cache suffix segments are the main mechanism; for the Sim-CoT model, they get strong sufficiency and decoded-control evidence but lack strong enough matched-corruption results to claim necessity. When they extend SCIT beyond small arithmetic cells and across model sizes, they observe a shift in the “carrier regime”: arithmetic-like GPT-2/1B models keep using latent-tail value/KV transfer, whereas more competent 8B models and repaired non-arithmetic cells switch to mechanisms involving prompt-prefix or entire-cache K/V routing, and some boundary cells show no clear mechanism.

Conclusion: SCIT provides a cache-level causal diagnostic for transformers, mapping which cache components actually carry latent reasoning computations for specific behaviors. For GPT-2 arithmetic checkpoints they identify a checkpoint-specific mechanism based on late value-cache suffix transfer, but they do not claim this is universal. Instead they argue that which cache components carry reasoning is gated by model competence and behavior regime, producing a competence-dependent carrier map rather than one simple latent-tail explanation.

Abstract: Latent chain-of-thought models move intermediate reasoning from emitted text into continuous states, improving compactness but hiding the causal object. We introduce SCIT, the Suffix Cache Interchange Test, a causal protocol that constructs exact source-recipient counterfactuals, patches declared cache segments, and identifies which transformer object carries the counterfactual computation. SCIT combines sufficiency tests with K/V component splits, hidden-state controls, semantic source controls, decoded validation, and matched corruption. On CODI-GPT2 and a Sim-CoT-style GPT-2 reproduction, counterfactual arithmetic transfers primarily through value-cache suffix trajectories rather than hidden states, keys, reusable answer slots, or single-token triggers. Complete sufficiency-and-necessity evidence for the late-value-suffix mechanism holds for the main CODI-GPT2 checkpoint; the Sim-CoT-style checkpoint shows the same sufficiency and decoded-control pattern but insufficient matched-corruption evidence for a necessity call. Beyond these local arithmetic cells, SCIT reveals carrier-regime shifts: arithmetic-like GPT-2/1B cells preserve latent-tail value/KV transfer, whereas competent 8B and repaired non-arithmetic cells route through prompt-prefix or full-cache K/V; boundary cells receive no mechanism call. SCIT therefore contributes a cache-level diagnostic, a checkpoint-specific GPT-2 arithmetic mechanism, and a competence-gated carrier map rather than a universal latent-tail claim.

</details>


### [103] [Difference-in-Differences on a Censored Rating Scale Can Manufacture an Effect: Evidence from a Pre-Registered LLM-Judge Audit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27309)
*Shuyi Fan,Boyuan Deng,Mengyu Xu,Xinhong Xie,Chenyang Li,Hongyang Zhang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper critiques common statistical designs used to audit LLM judges for bias, showing that a popular double-difference endpoint is not actually identified because of rating-scale censoring at bounds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Audits of LLM judges often use matched comparisons and double-difference designs to detect bias across attributes (like profiles or demographics). These designs assume the measured interaction reflects true preference differences, but they ignore how bounded rating scales censor scores near their limits, potentially fabricating apparent bias.

Method: The authors analyze the statistical properties of the commonly used within-item, double-difference endpoint under a bounded rating scale. They derive the impact of censoring analytically, showing how severity shifts in the judge’s ratings, combined with unequal distances to the scale bounds, can induce spurious interactions. They then demonstrate this mechanism in a pre-registered audit of a fixed LLM pedagogy judge with 990 evaluation calls, comparing observed interaction effects to what can be generated purely from severity shifts and floor effects, without any true preference difference. They provide closed-form derivations and show how an audit’s own ratings can be used to estimate the confounding contribution.

Result: In the case study audit, the primary registered endpoint—effect of a stated learner profile on scaffolding preference—is null (+0.085 points with a wide confidence interval and non-significant p-value). However, a secondary interaction effect (+0.378, p=0.002) that appears significant is shown to be largely explainable (79–85%) by the combination of rating severity shift and the floor of the scale, without invoking any true differential preference. This demonstrates that the endpoint is not identified as a measure of preference bias under the bounded scale and censoring.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that popular double-difference designs for LLM judge audits are statistically flawed when using bounded rating scales because censoring near the bounds can manufacture apparent interactions that mimic bias. Apparent significant interaction effects may largely reflect differential attenuation rather than real preference differences. The authors provide a closed-form account of this mechanism and show how its influence can be quantified from the audit data itself, implying that existing and future audits must carefully account for bounded-scale censoring or redesign endpoints to obtain identified measures of preference bias.

Abstract: Audits of LLM judges certify a bias by contrasting matched conditions, and the strongest designs difference twice: a within-item contrast between two candidate responses, differenced again across a manipulated attribute, read off a bounded rating scale. We show that this endpoint is not identified on the scale that reports it. Each term of the double difference is censored by its own share, so the observed statistic confounds differential preference with differential attenuation: a severity shift common to both responses manufactures an interaction whenever the two censor it unequally, as unequal distances from the bounds make them, exactly where good stimuli place them. We exhibit the failure inside a pre-registered audit of a frozen pedagogy judge, sealed before the first of its 990 calls. The registered primary endpoint, the effect of a stated learner profile on the judge's scaffolding preference, is null: $+0.085$ points (95\% BCa $[-0.167, +0.353]$, $p = 0.684$). The audit's one nominally significant interaction, $+0.378$ ($p = 0.002$), is not identified as preference: a construction containing zero differential preference reproduces 79 to 85\% of it from the observed severity shift and the scale floor alone. We derive the mechanism in closed form and show that its contribution is measurable from an audit's own ratings.

</details>


### [104] [BTS-AgentBench: A Deterministic, Replayable Pipeline from Read-Only Telemetry Logs to Agent Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27334)
*Jeong-Yoon Kim*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They build BTS-AgentBench, a benchmark that turns industrial telemetry logs into executable multi-turn agent tasks, validate its construction pipeline, and show it transfers to another dataset (XAI4HEAT).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Industrial environments generate lots of read-only telemetry logs, but there’s no standard way to convert these into realistic, multi-step tasks for evaluating AI agents. Existing benchmarks often lack grounding in real production data, strong guarantees that tasks are correctly constructed, and reproducibility across builds.

Method: They design a telemetry-to-episode pipeline that: (1) normalizes BTS metadata and histories into a read-only tool store; (2) compiles static tasks with tool-derived gold answers and explicit evidence; and (3) turns these into typed, bounded, operator-facing multi-turn episodes. They add task types like clarification and goal revision, enforce timestamp policies, require quality-gated reporting and evidence attribution, and preserve the original computation and data splits. They validate with coded contract preflight checks, a construction-exclusion controller, and two independent raw-to-episode builds, and then reapply the same construction procedure to an external dataset (XAI4HEAT).

Result: The final BTS-AgentBench release has 532 telemetry-derived tasks with richer interaction patterns (clarification, goal revision, reporting with evidence). All construction checks pass (preflight reports zero problems; the exclusion controller cannot auto-complete any tasks). Two independent builds match all 11 logical tool-store exports and reproduce the 356/87/89 train/dev/test split exactly, demonstrating reproducibility. Applying the pipeline to the XAI4HEAT dataset yields 204 additional episodes; on its 41-row held-out test set, the controller completes 0 tasks while the retained GPT-5.5 execution successfully completes all 41.

Conclusion: BTS-AgentBench provides a reproducible, formally validated way to convert real industrial telemetry into executable multi-turn agent evaluation tasks. The construction pipeline is robust (passes strict checks, reproducible across independent builds) and generalizes to other telemetry datasets (XAI4HEAT). This enables more realistic and trustworthy benchmarking of AI agents in operator-style, tool-using scenarios, with explicit evidence attribution and richer task dynamics.

Abstract: Industrial sites contain large volumes of read-only telemetry, but few benchmarks specify how to compile these records into executable multi-turn agent tasks. We present a telemetry-to-episode construction method instantiated as BTS-AgentBench. The pipeline normalizes BTS metadata and raw histories into a read-only tool store, compiles static tasks with tool-derived gold answers and evidence, and lifts retained tasks into typed, bounded operator-facing episodes. The 532-row release adds clarification, goal revision, timestamp policy, quality-gated reporting, and evidence attribution while preserving the source computation and split. Coded contract preflight reports zero findings, and the construction-exclusion controller completes 0/532 rows. Two independent raw-to-episode builds match all 11 logical tool-store exports and reproduce the released 356/87/89 train/dev/test artifact exactly. Applying the shared construction path to XAI4HEAT produces 204 episodes; on its 41-row held-out test split, the controller completes 0 rows and the retained GPT-5.5 execution completes all 41. Code, artifacts, and replay reports are available at https://github.com/kjy7567/BTS-AgentBench.

</details>


### [105] [Pair-Level Essay-Scale Republication and Reuse from Fragmented Historical Text Reuse: A Workflow Study on Eighteenth-Century Books and Newspapers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27343)
*Ke Shu,Kira Hinderks,Eetu Mäkelä,Mikko Tolonen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper develops methods to consolidate fragmented text-reuse signals into essay-scale republication/reuse relations, focusing on David Hume texts across ECCO books and historical newspapers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional text-reuse detection often stops at identifying local, fragment-level overlaps, which is insufficient when historians need essay- or edition-level relations such as full republications or substantial reuse. In historical corpora, evidence is noisy, fragmented, and ground truth is incomplete, so there is a need for robust methods that can turn many small reuse hits into reliable transmission hypotheses between documents.

Method: The authors frame the problem as pair-level evidence consolidation over a pre-defined candidate set. They compare: (1) a multi-stage, rule-based workflow that aggregates fragment-level features into pair-level decisions; (2) baseline machine learning and LLM approaches, including a decision tree and two direct LLM prompting settings; and (3) automated rule adaptation, which learns or tunes consolidation rules. They evaluate on labeled ECCO–ECCO subsets and then apply the methods to the broader ECCO–ECCO and ECCO–Newspaper candidate universes.

Result: On labeled ECCO–ECCO subsets, simple pair-level feature aggregation achieves an F1 of 0.948 on the main labeled slice, and the full multi-stage workflow yields the best overall precision–recall trade-off. On the full ECCO–ECCO universe, directly prompted LLMs label far more pairs (up to 14,886 vs. 771) as reprints, acting more as high-recall candidate expanders than as precise classifiers. On ECCO–Newspaper pairs, manual checking finds all 176 predicted positives to be genuine republication or reuse instances and reveals additional structure such as duplicated issues and multiple sources.

Conclusion: Consolidating fragment-level text-reuse evidence at the pair level is both feasible and effective for reconstructing essay-scale transmission in historical corpora, even under incomplete ground truth. Carefully designed, auditable rule-based workflows can provide compact, high-precision candidate sets for human historians, while LLMs may be more suitable as recall-oriented expansion tools rather than final decision-makers in this setting.

Abstract: This paper addresses the recovery of essay-scale republication and reuse from fragmented text-reuse evidence, a setting whose central challenge is pair-level evidence consolidation and not fragment retrieval alone. The study focuses on a candidate set centered on essays by eighteenth-century Scottish philosopher David Hume, spanning books from ECCO (Eighteenth Century Collections Online) and historical newspapers. Because the input consists of fragmented reuse hits instead of clean document pairs, and positive coverage is inherently incomplete, we formulate the task as pair-level evidence consolidation into plausible transmission relations and compare three methodological families: a staged rule-based workflow, baselines (a decision tree and two direct LLM settings), and automated rule adaptation. On labeled ECCO--ECCO slices, pair-level feature aggregation alone already reaches 0.948 F1 on the main labeled slice, while the final workflow gives the strongest overall precision-recall trade-off among the tested rule stages. On the full ECCO--ECCO candidate universe, direct LLM baselines flag up to 14,886 pairs as reprints compared to 771 for the final workflow, behaving in this direct-prompt setup as high-recall candidate expanders rather than precision-controlled deployment classifiers. On ECCO--Newspaper, manual audit confirms all 176 predicted positives as genuine cases of republication or reuse, while issue duplication and source-side multiplicity reveal additional provenance structure. Under incomplete ground truth, auditable pair-level evidence consolidation provides a practical way to produce compact candidate spaces for historical inspection.

</details>


### [106] [INTENT-AS-A-TOOL Makes it Easy to Track Agentic Misalignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27348)
*Yutong Zhang,Jianshuo Dong,Peng Xu,Long Wang,Jie Zhang,Tianwei Zhang,Xiaoping Zhang,Han Qiu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper introduces INTENT-AS-A-TOOL, a method for monitoring and analyzing the evolving intentions of autonomous LLM agents by giving them specialized 'intent tools' and tracking how likely they are to use them.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLMs are increasingly deployed as autonomous agents, their safety failures now often involve real-world consequential actions. Existing safety monitoring, such as post-hoc chain-of-thought (CoT) labeling, is too coarse to capture how an agent's harmful intent emerges and evolves during reasoning. There is a need for finer-grained, judge-free signals that can reveal when and how an agent becomes committed to harmful or misaligned behaviors, in order to better detect and intervene in agentic misalignment.

Method: The authors analyze agentic misalignment by monitoring LLM agents' reasoning traces. They first observe that harmful actions are often preceded by explicit or implicit signals of harmful intent in the CoT. To obtain a more fine-grained view, they introduce INTENT-AS-A-TOOL: they augment the model's toolset with special intent-targeted tools that the model can choose to call when committing to particular target behaviors (e.g., pursuing a harmful course of action). By measuring the probability that the model calls these intent tools at each step of reasoning, they derive a high-resolution, judge-free trajectory of the model's action preferences and commitment over time, which can be aligned with CoT steps and used to identify critical decision points.

Result: The experiments show that INTENT-AS-A-TOOL provides a dense, fine-grained signal of the model's evolving intent, which complements traditional CoT monitoring. It effectively expands coarse, post-hoc CoT labels into detailed trajectories of intent strength over the reasoning process. The method can reveal which specific steps in the reasoning are pivotal for the agent committing to harmful actions, thereby enabling potential online intervention at those critical moments. The probability of intent-tool invocation correlates with the model's tendency to pursue the corresponding target behavior, validating it as a useful proxy for action preference and misalignment risk.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that modeling 'intent-as-a-tool' is a promising approach for tracking agentic misalignment in LLM agents. By giving models explicit channels to express commitment to behaviors and tracking tool-call probabilities, practitioners can better understand and monitor how harmful intentions form and evolve during reasoning. INTENT-AS-A-TOOL augments CoT monitoring, supports more precise identification of intervention points, and suggests that action preferences inferred via tool usage are a valuable lens on safety for autonomous LLM agents.

Abstract: As large language models (LLMs) are deployed as autonomous agents, safety failures increasingly involve consequential actions. We study agentic misalignment, where agents take harmful actions under goal conflicts and pressures. Using chain-of-thought (CoT) monitoring, we find that harmful execution is often preceded by intent signals in reasoning. However, post-hoc CoT labels are too coarse to show how intent changes during generation. We introduce INTENT-AS-A-TOOL, an approach that adds intent-targeted tools to give the model a dedicated channel for expressing commitment to a target behavior. The probability of calling an intent tool provides a judge-free, fine-grained signal of the model's tendency to pursue that behavior. Our results show that INTENT-AS-A-TOOL complements CoT monitoring, expands post-hoc CoT labels into dense trajectories, and identifies critical steps for online intervention. These findings suggest that action preferences are useful for tracking agentic misalignment during reasoning. Our code and data are accessible: https://github.com/RebeccaZhang22/intent-as-a-tool.

</details>


### [107] [RCMN: Understanding Misleadingness in Influential Public Discourse](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27358)
*Peiling Yi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes a reader-centric framework and dataset to analyze how public discourse can mislead across multiple mechanisms, and evaluates whether lightweight text representations are sufficient for models to detect misleadingness and reader interpretations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing work on misinformation focuses mainly on factual falsity, overlooking subtler ways discourse misleads via framing, omission, and contextualization. There is limited understanding of how misleadingness is experienced by readers and how to systematically model it. The authors aim to fill this gap by defining and operationalizing misleadingness from the reader’s perspective and enabling empirical study on realistic public discourse.

Method: The authors introduce the Reader-Centric Misleadingness Understanding (RCMN) framework with five dimensions: misleading mechanism, likely reader interpretation, evidence-warranted interpretation, emotional arousal, and communicative intent. Using this framework, they build an evidence-grounded dataset of influential public discourse annotated along these dimensions. They then create lightweight claim-and-context representations (without full multimodal and evidential context) and evaluate five generative foundation models on tasks related to inferring reader interpretations and identifying misleading mechanisms from these compressed inputs.

Result: Empirical analysis of the dataset reveals that misleadingness is multifaceted and not limited to outright fabrication; mechanisms such as unsupported inference, exaggeration, and omission are common and often correlate with heightened emotional arousal and distortive communicative intent. Model evaluations show that, using only lightweight claim-and-context representations, generative models can often approximate reader-level interpretations but struggle to accurately identify the underlying misleading mechanisms, indicating that key cues for mechanisms are lost when rich context and evidence are removed.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates that understanding misleadingness requires a reader-centric, multi-dimensional perspective beyond simple fact-checking. Lightweight representations can support scalable analysis of how readers interpret content, but reliably diagnosing how discourse becomes misleading still depends on richer contextual and evidential information. Future progress in misleadingness analysis should combine scalable text representations with deeper grounding in context and evidence to capture the full spectrum of misleading mechanisms in public discourse.

Abstract: Influential public discourse shapes public beliefs and can also mislead, not only through what is stated, but also through how information is framed, omitted, contextualised, and communicated. Yet less research has focused on how such misleadingness arises and shapes the interpretations formed by readers. To address this gap, we introduce Reader-Centric Misleadingness Understanding (RCMN), a framework that operationalises misleadingness through five dimensions: misleading mechanism, likely reader interpretation, evidence-warranted interpretation, emotional arousal, and communicative intent. Based on this framework, we construct an evidence-grounded dataset of influential public discourse. Empirical findings show that misleadingness is diverse and extends well beyond fabrication, with unsupported inference, exaggeration, and omission among the prevalent mechanisms, and is frequently associated with heightened emotional arousal and distortive communicative intent. Moreover, we investigate whether lightweight claim-and-context representations retain sufficient cues for understanding reader-centric misleadingness without access to richer contextual, evidential, and multimodal information. Evaluation across five recent generative foundation models shows that reader-level interpretations can often be recovered from such limited representations, whereas identifying how misleadingness is produced remains considerably more challenging. These findings highlight the potential of lightweight representations for scalable misleadingness analysis, while reliable understanding of misleading mechanisms continues to require richer contextual and evidential grounding.

</details>


### [108] [Your Voice Cloning System is Secretly a Voice Anonymizer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27360)
*Romolo Muletta,Felix Matthias Saaro,Mark Cieliebak,Jan Deriu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They adapt an existing multilingual voice cloning model (XTTSv2) for speaker anonymization without retraining, using an iterative refinement strategy to balance privacy and speech quality, achieving strong privacy and quality across seven languages.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a need to anonymize speakers in speech data—removing identity while keeping content and naturalness—for privacy-preserving applications. Existing methods often require task- or language-specific training and can degrade quality. The authors aim to leverage a powerful pretrained multilingual voice cloning model to perform anonymization more simply and with better cross-lingual generalization and audio quality.

Method: They repurpose XTTSv2, a multilingual voice cloning TTS/voice cloning model trained on 27k hours of speech, to perform voice conversion/anonymization without any retraining. The key idea is to exploit XTTSv2’s ability to preserve prosody independently of speaker identity by conditioning generation on a pseudo-speaker embedding, effectively converting the original voice to a synthetic one. They add an iterative refinement loop that optimizes a harmonic mean objective combining speaker dissimilarity (privacy) and intelligibility (utility), adjusting parameters until a good balance is found.

Result: On seven European languages using the CommonVoice and Multilingual LibriSpeech datasets, their system reaches near-optimal privacy with an equal error rate (EER) around 0.49 for speaker verification (close to random guessing), maintains competitive intelligibility of the spoken content, and yields substantially better perceived speech quality than specialized anonymization baselines, all without any language-specific training.

Conclusion: A large multilingual voice cloning model like XTTSv2 can be repurposed as an effective, training-free speaker anonymization system. By conditioning on pseudo-speakers and iteratively tuning for a balance of privacy and intelligibility, it achieves strong privacy, good intelligibility, and superior audio quality across multiple languages, suggesting that powerful pretrained voice models are a practical foundation for scalable, cross-lingual anonymization solutions.

Abstract: Speaker anonymization suppresses speaker-identifying attributes from speech while preserving linguistic content and quality. We propose repurposing XTTSv2, a multilingual voice cloning model trained on 27k hours of speech, for speaker anonymization without retraining. Our key insight is that XTTSv2's voice cloning capabilities preserve prosodic structure independently of speaker identity, enabling voice conversion by conditioning on a pseudo-speaker. We introduce an iterative refinement strategy that balances privacy and utility by maximizing a harmonic mean of speaker dissimilarity and intelligibility. Evaluated on seven European languages across CommonVoice and Multilingual LibriSpeech, our system achieves near-optimal privacy (EER $\approx$ 0.49), competitive intelligibility, and substantially better speech quality than dedicated anonymization baselines, while requiring no language-specific training. We release the code here: https://github.com/rm00cr/coqui-tts.

</details>


### [109] [Puro-2B: Poor Lab's Qwen2-1.5B Trained on RTX 5090 within $5090](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27370)
*Kairong Luo,Jiarui Cui,Yaorui Yin,Shengqi Chen,Yiming Yang,Linxiang Gao,Yanmohan Wang,Mingzhe Zhang,Kaiyue Wen,Kaifeng Lyu,Wenguang Chen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They introduce Puro-2B, a 2B-parameter language model family trained from scratch using a highly cost-efficient, fully open pretraining recipe, achieving near state-of-the-art performance for its size at under about $7K compute cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pretraining modern language models is extremely expensive (often hundreds of thousands to millions of dollars), which prevents most academic and open-source groups from fully reproducing or extending state-of-the-art models. Existing open-weight models and recipes don’t yet provide a truly low-cost, hardware-accessible, end-to-end pretraining pipeline that others can realistically run and study. The authors aim to close this gap.

Method: They design and implement an open pretraining recipe optimized for cost efficiency and accessibility: using FP8 low-precision training on consumer-grade RTX 5090 GPUs, careful hardware selection, hyperball optimization, curriculum model averaging, and a tailored data curriculum. They then train multiple 2B-parameter Puro-2B variants up to 1.4T tokens, differing in token budgets and recipe variants, and systematically evaluate their performance and cost. They also construct a cost scaling law based on this model family and run a controlled case study on how different pretraining data curricula affect downstream performance after post-training.

Result: They successfully train several Puro-2B models from scratch, with the best model approaching the performance of Qwen2.5-1.5B while costing under $6.9K in compute. From the model collection they fit a Puro Cost Scaling Law that links training cost to average performance, indicating that around $4.4K is enough to reach Qwen2-1.5B-level performance under their evaluation setup. They also obtain empirical insights on how different data curricula during pretraining influence downstream post-trained performance, enabled by full control over the pipeline.

Conclusion: Full, reproducible, and cost-efficient LLM pretraining is feasible on relatively modest hardware budgets by combining low-precision training, smart optimization strategies, and a carefully designed data and training curriculum. Their publicly released Puro-2B recipe, data, code, and weights (under Apache 2.0) provide the community with an accessible end-to-end pipeline, a practical cost–performance scaling law, and a platform for systematic studies of pretraining choices beyond just releasing final model weights.

Abstract: Language model pretraining has become almost synonymous with prohibitive cost, placing it out of reach for much of the academic and open-source communities. Although strong open-source efforts already exist, including open-weight models and open-source training recipes, a cost-efficient, hardware-accessible, and open-source pretraining recipe has long been missing. Even at a small scale, training Llama-3.2-3B costs over \$1.5M, and reproducing SmolLM3-3B needs over \$700K. In this report, we present an open pretraining recipe designed to lower this barrier. Using this recipe, we train a collection of Puro-2B models from scratch on up to 1.4 trillion tokens with FP8 precision on consumer-grade RTX 5090 GPUs. The models in the collection differ in token budgets and selected recipe variants. Our best model is trained at a compute cost of less than \$6.9K and approaches Qwen2.5-1.5B performance under our evaluation protocol. This cost efficiency is enabled by a combination of approaches, including hardware selection, low-precision training, hyperball optimization, curriculum model averaging, and the data recipe. Beyond the recipe itself, we provide two additional results. First, across the Puro-2B collection, we derive a Puro Cost Scaling Law that relates training cost to average model performance; the fitted law suggests that about \$4.4K, less than \$5,090, is sufficient to reach the performance of Qwen2-1.5B. Second, as an end-to-end case study, we examine how pretraining data curricula shape downstream performance after post-training. Such controlled studies are enabled by having access to the full pretraining pipeline rather than model weights alone. We release the full training recipe for Puro-2B, including data, code, and model weights under Apache 2.0 at https://huggingface.co/collections/thu-pacman/puro-2b.

</details>


### [110] [D2C-Routing: Dimension-to-Composition Evidence Routing for Mixed-Origin AI-Generated Text Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27380)
*Xin Chen,Fuwei Zhang,Yiqi Tong,Wei Guo,Yutian Xiao,Fuzhen Zhuang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper reframes AI-generated text detection to handle mixed human–AI authorship by separating content origin and expression origin, and introduces D2C-Routing, a model that routes evidence to these dimensions before composing them into four collaboration labels, achieving strong performance on a new benchmark split.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing AI-text detectors assume a binary, document-level decision (human vs. machine), which fails for mixed-origin texts where the ideas (content) and the surface wording (expression) may come from different sources. As real-world writing increasingly involves human–AI collaboration, there is a need for finer-grained detection that can characterize how humans and models co-author text rather than forcing a single global label.

Method: The authors recast mixed-origin detection as a dimension-to-composition attribution problem. They define two dimensions—content origin and expression origin—then define four collaboration types from their combinations. They propose Dimension-to-Composition Routing (D2C-Routing), a model architecture that first separates and routes evidence related to content and expression into dedicated supervised heads (dimension heads). A learned gated composition layer then combines the outputs of these heads to predict the final four-way collaboration label. They evaluate on MixD2C, a reconstructed split of the HART mixed-origin benchmark, and run ablations (e.g., removing routing or gating) to validate the architectural choices.

Result: On the MixD2C benchmark split, the D2C-Routing detector system achieves a four-way Avg TPR at 1% FPR of 0.8603, outperforming a strong baseline (RACE-local rerun on the same split) by 6.5 points. Ablation studies indicate that the routing mechanism and dimension-wise supervision are key contributors to the performance gains. Error analysis indicates that the main remaining failure mode is differentiating AI-content/human-expression texts from fully AI-generated texts.

Conclusion: Modeling mixed human–AI authorship requires going beyond binary document-level AI-text detection. By explicitly disentangling and supervising content origin and expression origin and then composing them, D2C-Routing improves four-way collaboration type detection over strong baselines. However, fine distinctions between some collaboration types—particularly AI-content with human expression vs. fully AI-generated—remain challenging, suggesting room for improved modeling of subtle stylistic and semantic cues.

Abstract: AI-generated text detection is commonly framed as a binary document-level judgment about whether a text is human-written or machine-generated. This framing breaks down for mixed-origin writing, where content origin and expression origin may differ. We cast mixed-origin detection as dimension-to-composition source attribution, inferring content origin and expression origin before composing them into four collaboration types. We propose Dimension-to-Composition Routing (D2C-Routing), which routes content-side and expression-side evidence to supervised dimension heads before a learned gated composition layer predicts the final label. On MixD2C, a reconstructed split derived from the HART mixed-origin benchmark, our disclosed D2C-Routing-based detector system reaches 0.8603 four-way Avg TPR@1%FPR, 6.5 points above the same-split RACE-local rerun. Core ablations support the routing design, while error analysis shows that distinguishing AI-content/human-expression from fully AI-generated text remains the hardest boundary. Code is available at https://github.com/bystander563/d2c-routing-artifact.

</details>


### [111] [RATIO: A Benchmark for Retrieval Across Typed Ideation Operations in Scientific Literature](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27394)
*Maayan Sharon,Tom Hope*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: RATIO is a large-scale benchmark and framework for testing and training scientific paper retrieval systems specifically for ideation, using three types of inspiration-related retrieval tasks: Address, Broaden, and Specify.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard scientific literature retrieval focuses on topical or keyword similarity, which does not fully capture how scientists actually use prior work for inspiration at different levels of abstraction (e.g., to find concrete methods, broader formulations, or more specific instantiations). There is a need for benchmarks and datasets that reflect these ideation-centric retrieval needs and can support the development of retrieval components that better aid human and AI scientific discovery.

Method: The authors define three ideation moves—Address (retrieving candidate approaches to a problem), Broaden (retrieving more general formulations of a given idea), and Specify (retrieving more concrete instantiations). They then construct the RATIO benchmark from millions of full-text CS papers by applying a generalized discourse-marker based distant supervision strategy to identify text spans that suggest these ideation moves, repurposing it from classification to large-scale retrieval data generation. The resulting candidate pairs are further refined and validated using large language models and human annotators, producing operation-typed retrieval datasets. They use these datasets to train and evaluate retrieval models, including operation-specific fine-tuned retrievers.

Result: Operation-specific fine-tuning of retrievers on the RATIO data significantly improves performance on ideation-move-specific retrieval tasks compared to generic retrieval models, demonstrating that the benchmark captures meaningful structure. Nonetheless, the performance is still far from perfect, indicating substantial remaining headroom for future research and model development.

Conclusion: RATIO establishes a scalable, operation-typed benchmark and data-generation pipeline for literature-grounded ideation retrieval. It shows that modeling distinct ideation operations (Address, Broaden, Specify) is beneficial and feasible at corpus scale, but also that current retrievers are still limited. The framework is intended to catalyze research on retrieval systems that can more effectively support scientific inspiration and ideation for both humans and AI systems.

Abstract: Retrieved scientific literature can serve as inspiration for both human and AI scientists. Inspiration can take different forms: prior work may directly suggest how to address a problem, or surface directions at different levels of abstraction - zooming out to a more general view or zooming in to a concrete realization. We introduce RATIO (Retrieval Across Typed Ideation Operations), a large-scale benchmark in which relevance is defined by three operations which we name ideation moves: Address retrieves potential approaches for stated problems, Broaden retrieves more general formulations, and Specify retrieves concrete instantiations. RATIO is constructed from millions of full-text scientific papers across CS literature via a general recipe that extends discourse-marker distant supervision - previously used only for classification - to corpus-scale retrieval, combined with extensive LLM and human vetting. Experiments show that operation-specific fine-tuning substantially boosts retrievers but leaves much room for further improvements. RATIO provides a scalable training and evaluation framework for retrieval components that support literature-grounded ideation, opening up new research avenues on scientific inspiration retrieval.

</details>


### [112] [Making Clinical Language Models Auditable: Concept-Guided Fine-Tuning for Robust Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27397)
*Jin Mu,Guanhua Chen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They introduce CAST, a framework that makes clinical text classifiers more robust and auditable by identifying and suppressing spurious note artifacts using sparse autoencoders and concept-level attributions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clinical language models perform well in-hospital but degrade when deployed elsewhere because they latch onto note-specific artifacts—like templates and boilerplate—that do not truly represent patient health. This creates brittleness and makes it hard to trust and audit predictions.

Method: CAST (Concept-guided Artifact Suppression Tuning) uses Sparse Autoencoders on intermediate Transformer activations to obtain sparse, interpretable latent features. These latents are semi-automatically labeled via an LLM-based interpretation pipeline and constrained by ICD-10 concept retrieval. Latents identified as artifacts are then suppressed via residual subtraction during fine-tuning. The framework also enables post-hoc per-concept attribution for each prediction, showing which clinical and artifact concepts were involved.

Result: On the MIMIC-IV dataset for discharge-note mortality prediction, CAST outperforms standard fine-tuned encoder models and performs competitively with strong LLM baselines, while additionally providing interpretable, concept-level explanations and evidence of artifact suppression.

Conclusion: CAST makes clinical text models both more reliable to deployment shifts and more interpretable by separating genuine clinical concepts from spurious note artifacts, suppressing the latter during training, and generating a concept-level audit trail for each prediction.

Abstract: Clinical language models can achieve strong in-hospital accuracy yet fail under deployment shifts because they exploit note-specific artifacts (e.g., templates, separators, boilerplate) that do not reflect patient state. We propose CAST (Concept-guided Artifact Suppression Tuning), an SAE-based framework for auditable clinical text classification. CAST uses Sparse Autoencoders to expose sparse, human-auditable features from intermediate Transformer activations, labels SAE latents with an LLM-assisted interpretation pipeline and ICD-10 retrieval constraints, suppresses verified artifact latents via residual subtraction during fine-tuning, and provides post-hoc per-concept attributions for auditing model decisions. On MIMIC-IV discharge-note mortality prediction, CAST improves over its corresponding fine-tuned encoder baselines and remains competitive with strong LLM baselines, while producing a feature-level audit trail of the clinical concepts that support each prediction and the artifact concepts suppressed during training.

</details>


### [113] [How Language Models Organize and Structure Moral Knowledge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27402)
*Orion Reblitz-Richardson*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper investigates how large language models represent different types of moral concepts, showing that models embed moral foundations as multiple, partially integrated directions rather than a single “moral” dimension.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work showed that LLMs can detect moral content, but it was unclear whether they meaningfully distinguish between different moral foundations or how these are structurally organized in representation space. The authors want to understand whether models reflect theoretical moral distinctions (like those from Moral Foundations Theory) or instead mirror statistical patterns in training data, and whether models encode moral conflicts as tensions rather than just final judgments.

Method: They use open-weight language models and train six separate linear probes, each targeting one category from Moral Foundations Theory (care/harm, fairness/cheating, liberty/oppression, loyalty/betrayal, authority/subversion, sanctity/degradation). They analyze the learned probe directions geometrically—examining dimensionality, cosine similarities, and shared components—compare them to a matched non-moral concept set, test consistency across architectures and scales and across pre-training stages, and then extend the analysis to moral dilemmas by probing how dilemma representations decompose into their component foundations.

Result: The six moral foundation probe directions neither collapse into a single shared moral axis nor become fully orthogonal; instead, they span close to the maximal number of independent dimensions while sharing a nontrivial positive common component. This shared moral component is much stronger than that in a matched non-moral concept set (mean pairwise cosine 0.26 vs. 0.013), indicating moral-specific integration. The geometric structure is robust across model families and sizes and appears early in pre-training, before probe accuracy saturates. The discovered structure does not support the individualizing vs. binding foundations split posited by Moral Foundations Theory and appears better explained by corpus statistics. For moral dilemmas, each dilemma direction is partially composed of relevant foundation directions (2.7× higher than mismatched baselines), but most of the variance corresponds to conflict-specific structure.

Conclusion: LLMs encode moral knowledge as a set of partially integrated, multi-dimensional representations rather than as a single moral detector or strictly separable foundation modules. These representations include a shared moral component specific to moral content, emerge early in training, and track corpus statistics more than Moral Foundations Theory’s individualizing/binding distinction. In moral dilemmas, models represent the tension and conflict structure itself instead of just an already-resolved moral judgment.

Abstract: How do large language models (LLMs) organize moral knowledge? Models detect moral content broadly, but detection is a low bar. We ask whether they go further, distinguishing moral foundations from one another and organizing the relationships between them geometrically.
  We train six independent linear probes on open-weight language models, one per Moral Foundations Theory (MFT) category (care/harm, fair/cheat, lib/oppress, loy/betray, auth/subv, sanc/degrade), and examine how the resulting directions relate to each other in representation space. We find the directions neither collapse into a single moral detector nor isolate from one another. Rather, they span a near-maximal number of independent dimensions while sharing a positive common component. The shared component is the signature of integration, and it is moral-specific relative to a matched non-moral concept battery built identically (mean pairwise cosine 0.26 vs. 0.013).
  The geometry is consistent across architectures and scale and reaches its integration regime early in pre-training, well before probe accuracy saturates. The structure the model discovers shows no evidence of the individualizing/binding distinction predicted by Moral Foundations Theory (an underpowered test: only 20 candidate partitions exist) but rather reflects corpus statistics. Extending to moral dilemmas, each dilemma direction partially composes from its component foundations, at 2.7x a mismatched-pair baseline, while the majority of its variance encodes conflict-specific structure. The model represents moral tension itself, not a pre-resolved judgment.

</details>


### [114] [Consolidating RLVR Capabilities Across Domains: A Deep Dive into Fusion Paradigms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27409)
*Siye Wu,Kai Yang,Yuchen Cai,Xin Xu,Peng-Yuan Wang,Jiaxuan Wang,Jiashun Liu,Jiafei Lyu,Yangkun Chen,Saiyong Yang,Yanghua Xiao*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper compares three paradigms for fusing multiple reinforcement-learned experts in large language models (Merge, Mix RL, MOPD), finding small average but sometimes large domain-specific performance differences and offering practical guidelines for when to use each.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RL with verifiable rewards can boost particular capabilities of large language models, but combining many such capabilities typically requires training separate domain experts and then fusing them. Prior work studied fusion paradigms separately, so practitioners lack a clear comparative understanding and decision rules for choosing among them.

Method: The authors define three fusion paradigms by what they reuse: Merge (combining expert task vectors), Mix RL (pooling expert datasets for RL), and multi-teacher on-policy distillation, MOPD (using both expert policies and data). They evaluate all three using shared experts and data, across multiple model sizes and a multi-domain benchmark suite, analyzing task-vector geometry, domain-level performance variation, and training dynamics.

Result: Across benchmarks, the three paradigms have similar average performance (within 1.4 points), but can differ by up to 8.6 points on individual benchmarks, with domain-level outcomes correlating with cross-domain relationships inferred from task-vector geometry. Mix RL is sensitive to domain mixture proportions, MOPD’s performance is upper-bounded by its teachers, and Merge compresses all expert updates into a single combined update. All methods increase single-sample accuracy without harming solution diversity or held-out capabilities.

Conclusion: All three fusion approaches are viable but come with distinct trade-offs. Merge is best when experts already exist and computational efficiency is crucial; Mix RL is preferable for training a unified model from scratch, provided domain sampling proportions are tuned for cross-domain transfer; and MOPD is suitable when it is important to preserve domain-specific performance, even if this limits surpassing teacher quality or increases training cost.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) improves specific capabilities of large language models, but covering multiple capabilities often involves training separate domain experts and subsequently consolidating them. We organize three fusion paradigms by the artefacts they reuse: Merge combines expert task vectors, Mix RL pools their datasets, and multi-teacher on-policy distillation (MOPD) uses both. Because they have largely been studied in isolation, how they compare and how to choose among them remain unclear. We compare all three using shared experts and data across model scales and a multi-domain benchmark suite. Although their average performance differs by at most 1.4 points, the gap reaches 8.6 points on a single benchmark, with domain-level variation tracking cross-domain relations visible in task-vector geometry. Training dynamics expose distinct constraints: Mix RL depends on domain mixture proportions, MOPD remains bounded by its teachers, and Merge compresses all expert updates into one. All three improve single-sample accuracy without measurable gains in solution coverage or losses in held-out capabilities. These results yield a practical guideline: use Merge when experts already exist and cheap fusion is paramount; Mix RL when training a unified model without experts, with domain proportions adjusted for cross-domain transfer; and MOPD when preserving domain-specific gains matters more than surpassing teachers or minimizing end-to-end cost.

</details>


### [115] [Boosting LLM Exploration via Weak-Model Guidance in RLVR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27420)
*Xingyu Shen,Huishuai Zhang,Peng Li,Yinchun Wang,Dongyan Zhao*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes a method to maintain diversity (entropy) in LLM reasoning during RL with verifiable rewards by injecting partial reasoning prefixes from a weaker model, improving exploration and pass@k, especially for large k.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RL with verifiable rewards improves LLM reasoning but causes entropy collapse, which narrows reasoning coverage and hurts pass@k for large k. Existing methods use regularization but ignore using trajectories from other models to preserve diversity.

Method: During RLVR training, instead of only sampling full trajectories from the target model, the method forces the target model to continue from partial reasoning trajectories (prefixes) generated by a smaller/weaker model. These 'outer prefixes' introduce distributional discrepancy that disrupts over-confidence and pushes the target model to explore alternative reasoning paths. The process is simple, does not require extra SFT, complex rewards, or prompts, and operates as a cross-model non-parametric perturbation mechanism to sustain exploration and higher entropy.

Result: On multiple mathematical reasoning benchmarks, the proposed method consistently outperforms vanilla RLVR. The benefit grows as pass@k is measured for larger k, indicating expanded reasoning coverage. Entropy collapse is effectively mitigated without additional supervision or complicated algorithmic changes.

Conclusion: Cross-model prefix injection from weaker LLMs is an effective and simple way to preserve generative diversity in RLVR. By leveraging distributional discrepancy in outer prefixes, the approach improves exploration, mitigates entropy collapse, and yields better pass@k performance, especially for large k, without added complexity in training or reward design.

Abstract: Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) significantly improves LLM reasoning but often causes a drop in policy entropy, leading to narrowed reasoning coverage and degraded pass@$k$ for large $k$. While existing methods mitigate this entropy collapse through algorithmic regularizations, cross-model non-parametric perturbation is also neglected. In this work, we propose a simple yet effective approach to preserve the generative diversity of LLMs during RLVR. Instead of relying solely on internal exploration, we force the target model to generate answers based on partial reasoning trajectories generated by a smaller, weaker language models. These unfamiliar prefixes effectively disrupt over-confidence and encourage the exploration of distinct reasoning paths. We empirically study the potential of outer prefixes, revealing the mechanism of the impact of distributional discrepancy to the exploration dynamics in RLVR training. Experiments across multiple mathematical benchmarks show that our method consistently outperforms vanilla RLVR. Notably, the performance gain becomes increasingly pronounced as $k$ scales up, demonstrating a substantial expansion of reasoning coverage. Furthermore, our approach efficiently mitigates entropy collapse without requiring additional SFT, intricate reward designs, or complex prompting.

</details>


### [116] [Stochastic Estimation of Transduced Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27428)
*Vésteinn Snæbjarnarson,Samuel Kiegeland,Manuel de Prada Corral,Ryan Cotterell,Tim Vieira*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes an unbiased, sampling-based algorithm to estimate target prefix probabilities in transduced language models more accurately and efficiently than previous pruning or standard SMC methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous work on transduced language models requires summing over all source strings that map (via a finite-state transducer) to target strings with a given prefix. This set can be exponentially large or infinite, making exact computation intractable. Existing approximations rely on beam search with threshold pruning over source prefixes, yielding a lower bound whose error is unknown and potentially large, limiting the reliability and scalability of TLMs in applications like text and DNA modeling.

Method: The authors introduce an importance-style resampling scheme over source prefixes without replacement. Each retained prefix is reweighted by the inverse of its inclusion probability, and this correction is applied recursively so that the running estimate of the target prefix probability is provably unbiased. They design a beam-summing algorithm that dynamically extends retained source prefixes and adaptively decides which prefixes to keep, shrinking the active set as more probability mass is accumulated. The method also includes an explicit estimate of the probability mass lost due to threshold pruning. They compare this approach to sequential Monte Carlo methods that resample with replacement on both text and biological sequence tasks.

Result: On encyclopedic text, the proposed algorithm achieves a better tradeoff between computational cost and variance of the estimates than standard SMC baselines. On DNA modeling, it attains lower estimation error at a fixed maximum number of particles. In a DNA-to-amino-acid transduction task, the method reduces runtime by several orders of magnitude compared with threshold-pruned beam summing, enabling the estimation of target prefix probabilities for long sequences that were previously infeasible. In a reanalysis of a prior reading-time study, swapping threshold pruning for the new unbiased sampling markedly decreases the estimated corpus surprisal while preserving the original study’s qualitative conclusions.

Conclusion: Unbiased resampling without replacement, combined with recursive reweighting, provides a principled and efficient way to estimate target prefix probabilities in transduced language models. The resulting beam-summing algorithm is guaranteed to halt with probability one, offers improved compute–variance and accuracy profiles over standard SMC approaches, and drastically outperforms traditional threshold-pruned beam summing on demanding sequence tasks. This makes rigorous and scalable use of TLMs more practical in both natural language and biological sequence modeling, and it can materially affect quantitative outcomes (such as surprisal estimates) without overturning higher-level empirical conclusions.

Abstract: Transduced language models (TLMs) compose a pretrained \emph{source} language model with a functional finite-state transducer to induce a language model over \emph{target} strings. Computing the probability of a target prefix under a TLM amounts to summing the source-model probabilities of all source strings that the transducer maps to target strings beginning with that prefix. This set can be exponentially large or infinite. Prior work uses a computational shortcut based on source prefix probabilities, then approximates the resulting sum with threshold-pruned beam summing. This produces a lower bound with unknown error. Instead, we resample source prefixes without replacement and reweight each selected prefix by the inverse of its inclusion probability. We show that applying this correction recursively gives an unbiased estimator of the target prefix probability and lets us estimate the mass lost by threshold pruning. Our beam-summing algorithm extends the retained source prefixes and samples which prefixes to keep, reducing their number as more probability mass is added to the running estimate. This can save computation and guarantees that the run halts with probability one. We evaluate the method on encyclopedic text and DNA against sequential Monte Carlo baselines that resample with replacement. It achieves a better compute--variance tradeoff on text and lower error at the same maximum number of particles on DNA. On a DNA-to-amino-acid transduction, it reduces runtime by several orders of magnitude relative to threshold-pruned beam summing and makes estimating prefix probabilities for long target strings feasible. Replacing threshold pruning with unbiased sampling in a published reading-time analysis substantially lowers the estimated corpus surprisal but leaves the published conclusions unchanged.

</details>


### [117] [TTPO: Test-Time Policy Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27448)
*Aozhe Wang,Zhengxi Lu,Jianze Wang,Shangke Lv,Ying Liu,Weiming Lu,Jun Xiao,Yueting Zhuang,Hua Yang,Qianglong Chen,Yongliang Shen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces Test-Time Policy Optimization (TTPO), a label-free method that improves large language models' mathematical reasoning at test time using asymmetric self-supervision from pseudo-labels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing post-training methods like RL and On-Policy Self-Distillation need ground-truth labels, making them unusable for test-time training. Using majority-vote pseudo-labels instead is appealing but fragile because errors in the vote corrupt the entire supervision signal.

Method: The authors observe that rollouts disagreeing with majority-vote pseudo-labels are usually wrong, even when the vote is wrong. They leverage this asymmetry to design TTPO: an objective that (1) distills rollouts that agree with the pseudo-label using OPSD, and (2) penalizes disagreeing rollouts via Grouped RL. They add token-level selection so that distillation focuses less on already-converged tokens and RL focuses on confidently wrong tokens. Majority-vote routing is used to refine self-supervision as the model improves.

Result: Without using any ground-truth labels, TTPO matches the performance of label-supervised OPSD on five competition-level math benchmarks, improves Qwen3-1.7B accuracy from 38.0% to 45.2% in test-time training, adds 25.2% to 36.4% absolute performance improvements without chain-of-thought reasoning, and generalizes across tasks.

Conclusion: By exploiting an asymmetry in errors relative to pseudo-label majority votes, TTPO provides a robust, fully label-free test-time training method for mathematical reasoning that rivals label-dependent approaches and transfers well across tasks.

Abstract: Recent prominent post-training methods, such as Reinforcement Learning (RL) and On-Policy Self-Distillation (OPSD), have driven rapid progress in mathematical reasoning for large language models, yet their reliance on ground-truth labels precludes test-time training (TTT). Replacing ground truth with majority-vote pseudo-labels is a natural alternative, yet it is fragile: an incorrect vote corrupts the teacher and misleads every token. We observe that this failure mode is asymmetric: rollouts that disagree with the pseudo-label are typically wrong regardless of whether the vote itself is correct. Building on this observation, we propose Test-Time Policy Optimization (TTPO), an asymmetric objective that distills agreeing rollouts via OPSD and penalizes disagreeing rollouts with Grouped RL. Token-level selection further refines both branches: distillation down-weights already-converged positions, while RL penalizes only confident errors. Both updates remain well-grounded even under frequent pseudo-label errors, and majority-vote routing yields tighter self-supervision as the model improves. Without any labels, TTPO matches label-supervised OPSD on five competition-level benchmarks, raises Qwen3-1.7B from 38.0% to 45.2% in TTT, yields +25.2% to +36.4% without thinking, and shows strong cross-task generalization.

</details>


### [118] [CritICL: Inference-Time Weak-to-Strong Generalization from Small Language Model Failure Modes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27455)
*Yufan Wu,Yinghui He,Zhengyi Hu,Lang Wei,Ruichen Li,Qifan Yang,Ting Zhu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: CritICL is an inference-time framework that uses systematically observed failure patterns from weaker LLMs as critique examples to guide stronger LLMs, improving reasoning with much lower test-time compute than standard scaling methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Inference-time scaling (e.g., self-consistency, tree search, verifier loops) improves LLM reasoning but is expensive because it needs many generations or external verification. There is a need for a method that boosts reasoning quality while keeping inference efficient and simple, ideally without heavy sampling or additional models.

Method: The authors observe that LLMs within the same model family share structured failure modes across scales. They deliberately elicit and catalog these failure modes from weaker models, then repurpose them as guidance. CritICL injects these failure patterns into the prompt as critique-based in-context examples, steering the target model away from similar errors. They propose two variants: (1) CritICL-dynamic: for each new input, the system predicts which failure modes are likely and retrieves matching critiques/examples to include in the context; (2) CritICL-static: builds a global profile of common failure modes and uses a fixed set of critiques/examples for all inputs, trading adaptivity for simplicity and stability.

Result: Across benchmarks, both CritICL variants outperform standard in-context learning and reach or surpass the performance of popular test-time scaling methods, while consuming far fewer generations and tokens. This demonstrates that structured failure information from weaker models can substitute for much of the extra sampling and verification usually needed for stronger reasoning performance.

Conclusion: LLM reasoning failures are not just noise but contain reusable, structured information. By mining and re-injecting these failure modes as critique-based context (CritICL), one can substantially improve reasoning accuracy at inference time with minimal extra compute, offering an efficient alternative to traditional test-time scaling techniques.

Abstract: Recent advances in inference-time scaling have significantly improved the reasoning performance of large language models (LLMs). However, these methods typically rely on repeated generation or external verification. To address this limitation, we introduce CritICL, a novel inference-time framework that improves reasoning while maintaining high efficiency. Our key insight is that LLM failure modes exhibit structured patterns across model scales within the same family. Instead of treating failures as undesirable outputs, CritICL leverages them as a source of guidance. Specifically, we utilize failure modes derived from weaker models and incorporate them into inference through critique-based in-context examples. We propose two variants: CritICL-dynamic, which adaptively predicts input-specific failure modes and retrieves critiques, and CritICL-static, which uses a global failure mode profile to provide stable guidance. Experimental results show that CritICL consistently outperforms standard in-context learning and achieves performance competitive with or superior to test-time scaling methods, while requiring significantly fewer generations and lower token cost. Code available at: https://github.com/umwyf/CRITICL

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [119] [EduRiskX: A Neuro-Symbolic Framework with F-Logic Reasoning for Early Academic Risk Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26107)
*Yu Fu,Yongqi Kang,Yong Zhao,Rongfang Bie*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EduRiskX is a neuro-symbolic system that combines a temporal Transformer with F-Logic rules to predict and explain students’ academic risk early in online courses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a pressing need in online education to identify at-risk students early so instructors can intervene and improve retention and learning outcomes. Current machine learning models either detect risk too late or behave as black boxes, offering little interpretability and thus limited trust and adoption by educators. The paper aims to create a model that is both accurate and early in detection while being interpretable and grounded in educational theory.
