Method: EduRiskX integrates two components: (1) a temporal Transformer-based neural predictor that models weekly student activity sequences, using temporal attention, class-weighted loss to address imbalance, and dynamic weekly truncation for early prediction; and (2) an F-Logic symbolic reasoning module whose rules are derived from training data but grounded in Engagement Theory and the Student Integration Model to emulate teachers’ diagnostic reasoning. The neural model outputs risk probabilities and the symbolic module outputs confidence scores; these are fused via a logistic regression layer that learns how to weight each source of evidence. Experiments are run on the OULAD dataset with a strict 80/10/10 student-level split.

Result: On the OULAD dataset, EduRiskX reaches 0.900 accuracy and 0.894 F1 by Week 38, with an average early detection week of 9.32 and a 94.30% detection rate. Under the same conditions, it outperforms strong time-series baselines (PatchTST, iTransformer) and standard deep models (LSTM, CNN), especially in recall and in how early it flags at-risk students.

Conclusion: A neuro-symbolic approach that fuses temporal Transformers with F-Logic reasoning can yield both high-performance and early detection of academic risk while providing transparent, theory-grounded explanations. EduRiskX demonstrates that interpretability and predictive strength are compatible in learning analytics risk models, suggesting a promising direction for practical, trustworthy deployment in educational settings.

Abstract: Predicting students' academic risk in online education is crucial for enabling timely interventions that can improve retention and learning outcomes. However, existing models often suffer from limited early detection capability and insufficient interpretability, leading to a "black-box" trust crisis that hinders their adoption in real-world pedagogical settings. To address these challenges, we propose EduRiskX, a neuro-symbolic framework that integrates a temporal Transformer-based predictor with F-Logic symbolic reasoning. The neural component models longitudinal student activity sequences using temporal attention, class-weighted loss, and dynamic weekly truncation. Acting as a data-driven expert system, an F-Logic rule base -- grounded in established educational theories (Engagement Theory and Student Integration Model) to mimic the diagnostic logic of human educators -- is constructed exclusively from the training data. The neural risk probability and the symbolic confidence score are then combined through a logistic regression-based fusion mechanism that learns the relative contribution of each signal. Experiments on the Open University Learning Analytics Dataset (OULAD) using a strict 80/10/10 student-level split show that EduRiskX achieves an accuracy of 0.900 and an F1-score of 0.894 at the end of the semester (Week 38), with an average early detection week of 9.32 and a detection rate of 94.30 percent. Compared with state-of-the-art time-series models (PatchTST, iTransformer) and common deep learning baselines (LSTM, CNN), EduRiskX yields improved recall and earlier risk identification under identical conditions. Beyond predictive performance, the F-Logic module provides structured rule-based explanations linking predictions to observable behavioral patterns and educational theories.

</details>


### [120] [Standalone LLM and a Pre-specified Agentic Pipeline for Explaining ICU Mortality Predictions: a Feasibility Study on the eICU Demo Dataset](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26109)
*Di Zhu,Chen Xie,Haoyun Zhang,Zihan Wei,Ziwei Wang,Jiazhao Shi,Ziyu Wang,Qiyang Xie*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper evaluates whether using a multi-step (agentic) LLM pipeline to explain ICU mortality predictions is safer and more clinically grounded than a standalone LLM, while keeping similar predictive model performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While ML models can predict ICU mortality well, clinicians need narrative, clinically grounded explanations they can trust. Simple feature-attribution (e.g., SHAP) is not enough, and raw LLM explanations risk errors like outcome leakage or unsupported reasoning. The authors want to see if breaking the LLM explanation process into explicit steps (data interpretation, guideline checking, explanation) can improve safety and clinical usefulness compared with a one-shot LLM explanation.

Method: They trained an XGBoost ICU mortality prediction model on the eICU Demo dataset (2,353 ICU stays, 8.1% mortality) and evaluated discrimination with AUROC and AUPRC. For 38 stratified ICU cases, they compared two explanation strategies: (1) a standalone LLM that directly generates explanations, and (2) a four-step agentic LLM pipeline that decomposes interpretation, guideline grounding, and explanation. On a 14-case subset with SHAP attributions, they quantified alignment with SHAP (Jaccard similarity and direction consistency) and evaluated guideline grounding, specificity of values referenced, plausibility, and outcome leakage events.

Result: XGBoost showed strong predictive performance (AUROC 0.855, AUPRC 0.332). In the 38-case explanation set, the standalone LLM produced one explanation with explicit outcome leakage; the agentic pipeline produced none. On the 14-case SHAP subset, the standalone LLM aligned better with SHAP (higher mean Jaccard 0.171 vs 0.077 and higher direction consistency 92.9% vs 78.6%), but the agentic pipeline produced explanations that were better grounded in clinical guidelines (0.762 vs 0.143), more specific about patient values (0.236 vs 0.143), and slightly more plausible (0.700 vs 0.671).

Conclusion: The multi-step agentic LLM pipeline appears to reduce safety issues like outcome leakage and improves clinical guideline grounding and patient-specific detail compared to a standalone LLM, though it aligns less closely with attribution methods like SHAP. For high-stakes ICU mortality risk explanation, such agentic decomposition should likely be combined with attribution-based checks to balance safety, faithfulness to the underlying model, and clinical usefulness.

Abstract: Machine-learning models can predict ICU mortality accurately, but feature-attribution methods alone rarely provide the clinical narrative needed for bedside use. Large language models (LLMs) may bridge this gap, and multi-step agentic pipelines are a plausible extension because they separate data interpretation, guideline checking, and final explanation. This revised feasibility study preserves the original standalone-versus-agentic comparison while making the main clinical findings more explicit. Using the retained local eICU Demo artifact set (2,353 ICU stays; 8.1\% mortality), XGBoost achieved an AUROC of 0.855 (95\% CI 0.796--0.906) and an AUPRC of 0.332 (95\% CI 0.217--0.494). On a stratified 38-case explanation subset, the standalone LLM produced 1 explanation with explicit outcome leakage, whereas the four-step agentic pipeline produced none. Among the 14 cases that overlapped with the SHAP review subset, the standalone LLM showed higher SHAP alignment (mean Jaccard 0.171 versus 0.077) and higher direction consistency (92.9\% versus 78.6\%), while the agentic pipeline showed higher guideline grounding (0.762 versus 0.143), higher value specificity (0.236 versus 0.143), and slightly higher plausibility (0.700 versus 0.671). Clinically, the results suggest that agentic decomposition may improve safety-relevant grounding and patient-specific detail, but it should be paired with attribution-based checks before use in high-stakes risk explanation.

</details>


### [121] [Large Models for Battery Prognostics and Health Management: A Review and Future Roadmap](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26111)
*Jiale Liu,Huan Wang,Weicheng Wang,Rong Zhu,Qiqi Wang,Min Xie*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper is a survey of how large Transformer-based models are being used for battery prognostics and health management (BPHM), what problems they solve compared to traditional methods, what challenges remain, and a roadmap for future research.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Battery health management is crucial for safety, reliability, and cost in applications like EVs and grid storage, but existing physics-based and conventional deep learning approaches struggle with computation, parameterization, data needs, generalization, and interpretability. Large Models (LMs) based on Transformers and self-supervised learning have transformed other domains and may overcome these limitations in BPHM, yet their use has not been systematically reviewed.

Method: The authors conduct a comprehensive review of recent literature on applying large Transformer-based, self-supervised models to BPHM. They first explain the core enabling technologies (Transformers, self-supervised learning, multimodal datasets, and PEFT). Then they organize and analyze existing work along four axes: data scarcity mitigation, generalization and robustness, domain-knowledge-integrated interpretability, and system-level automation. They also synthesize open challenges and propose a structured roadmap for future research and deployment.

Result: The survey finds that LM-based approaches can alleviate dependence on labeled run-to-failure data via self-supervision and multimodal pre-training, improve cross-domain generalization and robustness, better embed physical and domain knowledge for interpretability, and support more automated, system-level battery management functionalities. However, the literature also reveals persistent issues in data accessibility and standardization, rigorous validation for industrial use, trustworthiness and safety assurance, and practical deployment constraints such as computational cost and on-device efficiency.

Conclusion: Large Models represent a promising new paradigm for BPHM, with clear advantages over traditional models in data efficiency, generalization, and interpretability, and with potential to enable more autonomous battery management throughout the lifecycle. Yet realizing this potential requires collaborative data ecosystems, stronger validation frameworks, physics-informed and trustworthy model designs, and methods for efficient deployment on real battery management hardware. The paper offers a roadmap along these directions to guide the development of next-generation LM-driven BMS.

Abstract: Battery Prognostics and Health Management (BPHM) is critical for ensuring the safe, reliable, and cost-effective operation of batteries across electric vehicles, grid storage, and consumer electronics. Conventional BPHM approaches, including physics-based models and task-centric deep learning methods, face challenges in computational efficiency and parameterization, cross-domain generalization, dependence on extensive labeled run-to-failure data, and model interpretability. Recent Large Models (LMs), built upon Transformer architectures and self-supervised pre-training, offer a transformative new paradigm to overcome these long-standing bottlenecks. This review provides the first comprehensive survey of LM applications in BPHM, systematically examining how these models address challenges in the field. We begin by elucidating the foundational technologies enabling LMs, including Transformer architectures, self-supervised learning, large-scale multimodal datasets, and PEFT techniques. We then categorize recent progress along four critical dimensions: mitigating data scarcity, enhancing generalization and robustness, integrating domain knowledge for interpretability, and enabling system-level automation. Despite promising results, significant challenges remain across data accessibility, intelligence validation, trustworthiness, and deployment feasibility. To guide future research, we propose a roadmap focused on building collaborative data ecosystems, validating intelligence for industrial applications, enhancing trustworthiness with physics-informed designs, and enabling efficient on-device deployment. This review establishes a systematic approach to understand and advance LM-driven BPHM, providing researchers and practitioners with essential insights for developing next-generation battery management systems capable of safe, reliable, and autonomous operation throughout battery lifecycles.

</details>


### [122] [PICasso: An AI-Enabled Design Framework for Autonomous Optimization of Silicon Photonic Devices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26113)
*Deepak Vungarala,Deniz Najafi,Abdulrahman Aljoudi,Zahra Ghanaatian,Navid Khoshavi,Gourav Datta,Arman Roohi,Mahdi Nikdast,Shaahin Angizi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PICasso is an AI-assisted framework that converts natural-language specifications into manufacturable photonic integrated circuits and shows clear gains over vanilla LLMs on a new PIC benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Designing photonic integrated circuits typically requires expert knowledge, manual layout, and iterative verification, making it slow, brittle, and difficult to scale. Existing LLM-based code or netlist generation is not sufficient because it ignores fabrication rules, physical verification, and performance optimization. The authors aim to make LLMs practically useful for PIC design by embedding them in a structured, physically-aware workflow and by creating a benchmark to systematically evaluate such AI-driven methods.

Method: They build PICasso, a pipeline that translates natural-language descriptions into PIC layouts via an intermediate YAML representation and finally to GDS, augmented with PDK-aware knowledge injection. The system includes automatic placement and routing, design rule checking (DRC) and layout-versus-schematic (LVS) validation, and photonic simulation using scattering matrix (SAX) models for performance evaluation and optimization. They also construct PIC-Set, a suite of 36 parameterized PIC design tasks, and define evaluation metrics like structural and functional Spec@k, optimization efficiency, and robustness. Multiple state-of-the-art LLMs are tested within this framework under a unified protocol.

Result: Using the PIC-Set benchmark, PICasso substantially improves how often generated designs satisfy the target specifications compared to raw LLM outputs. On high-complexity circuits, structural Spec@3 reaches up to 92.7% and functional Spec@3 up to 52%. Simulation-guided optimization consistently improves optical performance, reducing average insertion loss from 4.98 dB to 3.25 dB (a 1.74 dB gain).

Conclusion: Embedding LLMs in a structured, constraint- and physics-aware PIC design pipeline converts them from unreliable netlist generators into effective design agents that can produce manufacturable layouts. With competitive runtimes relative to manual GUI workflows, PICasso shows that AI-assisted, end-to-end PIC synthesis from natural language is feasible and beneficial, and that standardized benchmarks like PIC-Set enable rigorous evaluation of such systems.

Abstract: We present PICasso, an AI-assisted framework for automated synthesis, verification, and optimization of photonic integrated circuits (PICs) from natural-language specifications. PICasso couples a structured NL -> YAML -> GDS generation pipeline with PDK aware knowledge injection, automated placement and routing, DRC/LVS validation, and SAX-based photonic simulation. To systematically evaluate AI-driven photonic design, we introduce PIC-Set, a benchmark of 36 parameterized PIC design tasks spanning core photonic primitives and multi-component circuits. Using PIC-Set, we benchmark several state-of-the-art Large Language Models (LLMs) under a unified evaluation protocol, including new metrics such as structural and functional $Spec@k$, optimization efficiency, and robustness under perturbations. Across the benchmark, PICasso significantly improves end-to-end specification satisfaction compared to vanilla LLM generation. Structural $Spec@3$ reaches up to 92.7% and functional $Spec@3$ up to 52% on high-complexity circuits. In addition, PICasso consistently reduces circuit insertion loss, lowering the mean loss from 4.98 dB to 3.25 dB (1.74 dB improvement) through simulation-guided optimization. These results demonstrate that structured domain constraints, physical verification, and simulation feedback transform LLMs from brittle netlist generators into practical PIC design agents capable of producing manufacturable layouts with competitive runtimes relative to manual GUI-based workflows.

</details>


### [123] [CIFQA: A Deterministic Tool-Grounded Multi-Agent LLM Framework for Financial Query Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26114)
*Kunjesh Parekh,Anil Kumar Tiwari,Divya Saxena*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes CIFQA, a multi-agent, tool-grounded LLM framework that separates language understanding from deterministic financial computation to reliably answer calculation-intensive financial questions, achieving high accuracy and outperforming larger LLM baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs often fail on multi-step, calculation-intensive financial questions, producing numerically incorrect but plausible answers because they entangle language understanding with approximate numerical reasoning. There is a need for a framework that ensures exact financial computation, rule application, and rate handling while still leveraging LLMs for language interpretation.

Method: The authors design CIFQA, a deterministic tool-grounded multi-agent framework. Different specialized LLM agents handle query interpretation, routing, parameter extraction, computation planning, and response generation. All financial computations, rule applications, and numerical operations are delegated to deterministic Python-based tools. They instantiate this framework specifically for fixed deposit queries and integrate modules for rate lookup, tenure computation, rolling-year adjustment, and premature-withdrawal logic.

Result: On a curated benchmark of fixed deposit queries, CIFQA attains 95.54% accuracy on calculation-intensive questions and 90.87% overall accuracy, clearly outperforming direct LLM baselines even when those baselines are given explicit formulas, rate cards, and instructions. Ablation experiments confirm that deterministic components like rate lookup and tenure calculation significantly boost performance. A 17B open-source model within CIFQA surpasses larger frontier models on the same financial tasks.

Conclusion: Separating language understanding from numerical execution through a tool-grounded multi-agent architecture markedly improves numerical reliability in financial question answering. CIFQA shows that careful system design and deterministic tooling can be more important than model scale for calculation-heavy reasoning, and the framework is generalizable beyond fixed deposits to broader financial reasoning domains.

Abstract: Calculation-intensive financial question answering requires exact reasoning over structured rates, temporal conditions, numerical formulas, and rule-based constraints. Although Large Language Models (LLMs) perform strongly on natural language tasks, they often produce numerically incorrect yet plausible answers when solving multi-step financial calculations. To address this limitation, we introduce CIFQA (Calculation-Intensive Financial Query Answering), a deterministic tool-grounded multi-agent LLM framework for financial question answering. CIFQA separates language understanding from numerical execution by assigning specialized agents to query interpretation, routing, parameter extraction, computation planning, and response generation, while deterministic Python-based tools perform financial calculations and rule application. We instantiate CIFQA for fixed deposit query answering and evaluate it on a curated benchmark of fixed deposit queries. CIFQA achieves 95.54% accuracy on calculation-intensive queries and 90.87% overall accuracy, substantially outperforming direct LLM baselines even when provided with complete formulas, rate cards, and benchmark instructions. Ablation studies show that deterministic components such as exact rate lookup, tenure computation, rolling-year adjustment, and premature-withdrawal logic are critical contributors to performance. Notably, a 17B open-source backbone operating within CIFQA outperforms substantially larger frontier models evaluated with the same financial information, demonstrating that architectural design is a more important determinant of numerical reliability than model scale. While evaluated on fixed deposit queries, CIFQA provides a generalizable framework for calculation-intensive financial reasoning tasks.

</details>


### [124] [The Artificial Experimentalist: Discovery and Control of Self-Organizing Phenomena with Autotelic Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26116)
*Marko Cvjetko,Benedikt Hartl,Michael Levin,Clément Moulin-Frier,Pierre-Yves Oudeyer*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes CARL, an autotelic RL agent that performs closed-loop interventions in Lenia, enabling discovery, control, and human-guided manipulation of emergent soliton patterns that generalize across diverse conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most explorations of cellular automata and similar complex systems are open-loop: researchers choose initial conditions, run full simulations, and only passively observe outcomes. This limits the ability to interactively discover, steer, and systematically study emergent phenomena. The authors aim to create an artificial "experimentalist" that can actively probe, manipulate, and control such systems through minimal, targeted interventions, enabling both autonomous discovery and human-in-the-loop control of emergent structures.

Method: They propose a closed-loop framework based on autotelic reinforcement learning, where an agent self-generates diverse goals and learns a goal-conditioned policy to intervene in a complex system with local perturbations. They instantiate this in Lenia, a continuous cellular automaton, creating an agentic system called CARL. CARL operates by choosing goals (e.g., create or steer certain solitons), interacting with the Lenia grid via small local changes, and receiving feedback based on goal achievement. Training spans a variety of Lenia update rules, random initial states, and goal specifications so that the policy learns general strategies for creating and controlling soliton-like patterns.

Result: 1) CARL autonomously discovers stable solitons across many Lenia rules more efficiently than heuristic search baselines. 2) It learns to control existing solitons, especially steering their movement direction with few, localized interventions. 3) It supports human-in-the-loop control: humans can issue high-level directional commands (e.g., guide through a maze) that CARL translates into low-level perturbations that move solitons accordingly. The trained policies generalize zero-shot to various out-of-distribution goals, update rules, and initial conditions.

Conclusion: Autotelic, goal-conditioned RL agents can serve as artificial experimentalists for complex systems, not only discovering emergent patterns like solitons but also robustly controlling them with minimal interventions. The CARL framework in Lenia shows that closed-loop learning enables both autonomous and human-guided manipulation of self-organizing phenomena and that such agents can generalize to new conditions without retraining, pointing toward broader applications in studying and exploiting emergent behavior in complex dynamical systems.

Abstract: Existing methods for exploring cellular automata and other complex systems mostly operate in open loop: they set initial conditions, execute a full simulation, and observe the outcome, without intervening during execution. We introduce a closed-loop framework based on autotelic reinforcement learning, in which an agent autonomously samples diverse goals and learns a goal-conditioned policy to intervene in a complex system through minimal, local perturbations. We instantiate this framework on Lenia, a continuous cellular automaton known for life-like self-organizing patterns, in an agentic system we call CARL, and demonstrate three capabilities. First, CARL discovers stable solitons across a wide range of Lenia update rules at a higher rate than heuristic baselines. Second, it learns to steer the movement direction of existing solitons with few interventions, showing that CARL can control self-organizing patterns, not only create them. Third, humans can use trained agents to guide solitons through maze environments in real time by specifying high-level directional commands that the agent translates into low-level interventions. Trained across diverse goals, update rules, and random initial states, the agents acquire policies that generalize zero-shot to various out-of-distribution conditions. These results suggest a path toward artificial experimentalist agents that, autonomously or with human guidance, discover and control emergent phenomena in complex systems.

</details>


### [125] [The Accuracy-Efficiency Paradox Quantifying Net Energy Loss in on-Device Energy Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26134)
*Jaeik Jeong,Tai-Yeon Ku,Wan-Ki Park*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper reveals that ultra-accurate energy forecasting models used on edge devices can waste more energy overall than they save, due to their own computation cost and the battery aging they accelerate. It proposes a Total Cost of Ownership (TCO) framework that jointly accounts for inference energy and battery degradation as a single energy-loss budget, and shows that in thermally stressed edge settings simpler models can yield better net energy outcomes than complex ones.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional energy forecasting research optimizes mainly for prediction accuracy, assuming that better forecasts always translate into higher energy efficiency. In mission-critical edge scenarios such as on-device military systems, models must run locally and repeatedly under constrained batteries and harsh thermal conditions. The authors observe that the energy overhead of running complex AI models—and the resulting battery wear—may outweigh the energy savings gained by their improved forecast accuracy. This creates a mismatch between the usual accuracy-centric optimization and the genuine goal of maximizing net usable energy over a device’s lifetime.

Method: The authors formally define an Accuracy-Efficiency Paradox in edge energy forecasting and build a Total Cost of Ownership (TCO) framework that quantifies overall energy loss. They model both (1) direct inference energy consumption of forecasting models and (2) indirect energy loss via battery aging, treating degradation as a physical loss of future energy capacity. Within this unified TCO metric, they compare simple and complex model architectures under thermally sensitive edge conditions, likely using empirical measurements or simulations to capture real operational intensity and its impact on battery health.

Result: Using the TCO framework, the study finds that in thermally stressed edge environments, complex high-accuracy forecasting models frequently cause a net energy deficit: the energy saved through better forecasts is more than offset by the energy consumed for inference and the future energy capacity lost through accelerated battery aging. Simpler, less intensive models can therefore achieve lower total energy loss despite being less accurate in a conventional sense.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that maximizing forecast accuracy alone is an inadequate and sometimes harmful objective for energy forecasting on edge devices. Instead, model selection and design must be guided by a TCO perspective that explicitly accounts for inference energy and battery degradation as unified energy losses. Under realistic thermal and battery constraints, it can be optimal to deploy less complex models whose lower operational intensity preserves more net usable energy over the device’s lifetime.

Abstract: Energy forecasting aims to maximize accuracy to ensure energy efficiency by reducing energy waste, an objective that applies equally to on-device forecasting for mission-critical edge environments, including military systems. However, this paper identifies the Accuracy-Efficiency Paradox: high-precision energy forecasting models can ironically trigger a net energy deficit. This stems from both edge AI's inference energy consumption and battery aging. We propose a Total Cost of Ownership (TCO) framework for energy forecasting, designed to minimize net energy loss. This framework treats not only inference energy consumption but also battery aging as a unified form of energy loss, as degradation represents a physical dissipation of the system's future energy-carrying capacity. We demonstrate that in thermally sensitive edge environments, energy saved by the superior precision of complex architectures is often outweighed by the total energy lost through their high operational intensity.

</details>


### [126] [LLMs for Academic Workflows: An Evaluation of Literature Reviews Generated with Short and Long Context Windows of LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26145)
*Muhammad Ali Chaudhry,Xinyuan Hao,Haifa Alwahaby*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper evaluates how context window size in large language models affects the quality of AI-generated literature reviews.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand whether and how large language models can effectively support academic literature review writing, and how context length influences the strengths and weaknesses of their outputs.

Method: The authors generated 20 literature reviews using LLMs with short and long context settings, sourcing papers from Semantic Scholar and Arxiv. Two researchers evaluated these reviews along 15 quality dimensions.

Result: AI-generated literature reviews benefit from longer context windows through broader coverage and better coherence, but also suffer from more repetition, missed key works, and overly descriptive rather than synthetic writing. Overall, the generated reviews fall short of academic standards without human intervention.

Conclusion: LLM-generated literature reviews can serve as useful starting overviews but require critical expert oversight and refinement to reach publishable quality. Future work should explore other LLMs, fine-tuning, domain-specific setups, and hybrid human–AI workflows to mitigate current limitations.

Abstract: Our research focuses on evaluating literature reviews generated in short and long context settings of large language models (LLMs) to investigate the impact of context window on the quality of AI-generated literature reviews and the role of AI in supporting literature review writing. Twenty AI-generated literature reviews based on research sources from Semantic Scholar and Arxiv were evaluated by two researchers across 15 dimensions. Our findings reveal that AI-generated literature reviews require human oversight to meet academic publishing standards. As context windows increase, LLMs can incorporate broader information and maintain coherence across longer inputs, but they also exacerbate issues such as content repetition, omission of critical work, and a tendency towards descriptiveness over synthesis. Our work shows that AI-generated reviews can provide foundational overviews, but their output must be critically evaluated and refined by domain experts. Future research should consider integrating other LLMs and fine-tuned models in different domains with hybrid approaches that combine human expertise with AI capabilities to address the limitations identified in this study.

</details>


### [127] [Methodological and Conceptual Framework for 5D Multi-Table Analysis: A Unified Approach for Complex Data Reuse](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26149)
*Edouard Lansiaux,Hugo Kazzi,Aurélien Loison,Slim Hammadi,Emmanuel Chazard*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose the Relational Hypergraph Transformer (RHT), a scalable transformer architecture for multi-table (relational) healthcare data, representing databases as hypergraphs and learning high-quality embeddings that capture complex relationships.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional machine learning struggles with relational/multi-table data, which is common in healthcare databases like EHRs. Challenges include large volume, many features, high-cardinality categorical variables, complex links between tables, and temporal structure. Existing methods either flatten tables (losing structure), use simple relational models (limited expressiveness), or use graph methods that scale poorly (quadratic attention complexity) or don’t fit the full relational schema well. There is a need for a unified, scalable architecture that respects relational structure and can learn semantically meaningful representations across multiple tables.

Method: They model a relational database as a hypergraph (where a hyperedge can connect more than two entities) and introduce the Relational Hypergraph Transformer (RHT). RHT: (1) constructs a hypergraph from the tables and relationships; (2) learns “pentadimensional embeddings” (PentE) that encode multiple relational aspects (likely entity, relation, time, table/role, and value/type dimensions); and (3) uses a sparse relational attention mechanism whose complexity scales with the average relational degree (how many relations each entity participates in) instead of the square of the number of entities, improving scalability. They formally specify the architecture, derive the theoretical complexity of the attention, and release an open-source implementation. They run experiments on the Synthea synthetic EHR dataset for multi-label prediction of SNOMED CT condition codes per encounter, and perform ablations to test each architectural component.

Result: On Synthea multi-label condition prediction, RHT is compared with strong baselines from three families: tabular methods, relational models, and temporal graph approaches. RHT produces embeddings that are more semantically coherent than those from baselines, while remaining computationally scalable due to its sparse relational attention. However, for rare-condition (rare-code) recall, XGBoost outperforms RHT. Ablation studies show how much each part of the RHT architecture contributes to performance and representation quality. They provide code and full experimental protocols in an open repository.

Conclusion: RHT is a practical, scalable way to apply transformer-style attention to multi-table relational data by viewing databases as hypergraphs and using pentadimensional embeddings with sparse relational attention. It yields better semantic structure in learned representations than existing methods and is demonstrably scalable on a realistic synthetic EHR benchmark, although traditional gradient-boosted trees can still perform better on some metrics like rare-label recall. Planned future work includes clinical validation on a real-world EHR dataset (MIMIC-IV) once access credentials are obtained.

Abstract: Multi-table learning remains a major challenge in machine learning for healthcare and other complex information systems. Relational data combine several sources of complexity, including large data volume, high-dimensional variables, high-cardinality categorical features, complex inter-table dependencies, and repeated temporal observations. We introduce the Relational Hypergraph Transformer (RHT), a unified architecture that represents relational databases as hypergraphs, learns pentadimensional embeddings (PentE), and performs sparse relational attention with complexity proportional to the average relational degree rather than the square of the number of entities. We formally define the architecture, derive the complexity of its attention mechanism, and provide an open-source reference implementation. We evaluate RHT on the public Synthea synthetic electronic health record dataset using multi-label prediction of SNOMED CT condition codes per encounter, a task characterized by high categorical cardinality and long-tailed label distributions. Comparisons with tabular, relational, and temporal graph baselines show that RHT produces more semantically coherent embeddings while remaining computationally scalable. In this benchmark, the highest rare-code recall is achieved by XGBoost, whereas RHT attains the strongest embedding semantic coherence. We also report ablation studies quantifying the contribution of each architectural component. Clinical validation on MIMIC-IV is planned following PhysioNet credentialing. Source code and experimental protocols are provided in the accompanying repository.

</details>


### [128] [Leveraging Large Language Models for Systematic Literature Review of Disease Spread Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26150)
*Orhan Yagizer Cinar,Timur Emre Ozkose,Emma Von Hoene,Amira Roess,Taylor Anderson,Hamdi Kavak*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper evaluates a large language model (LLM) pipeline for automating parts of systematic literature reviews (SLRs) in agent-based modeling, comparing LLM outputs to a human review and analyzing accuracy and agreement between models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Systematic literature reviews are time-consuming and labor-intensive, especially in specialized domains like agent-based modeling. With recent progress in LLMs, there is interest in using them to automate or accelerate SLR tasks, but their reliability, accuracy, and practical limitations remain unclear. The authors aim to empirically assess how well LLMs can extract structured, model-relevant information from research papers compared with human reviewers.

Method: The authors build an LLM-based information extraction pipeline and apply it to 536 peer-reviewed agent-based modeling papers. They use different LLM versions (GPT-4.1 and GPT-5.0) to extract structured fields describing the models and compare the outputs against a human-conducted SLR treated as reference data. They compute paper-level and field-level accuracies and analyze where performance drops, particularly for complex or subjective fields. They also study inter-LLM agreement as a proxy for output quality, examining cases of low and high agreement relative to human-labeled accuracy.

Result: GPT-4.1 achieves about 77.95% paper-level accuracy and GPT-5.0 achieves about 81.67%. Field-level accuracy varies widely from 32.40% up to 100.00%, with complex or subjective fields showing weaker performance. The study finds that low agreement between LLMs can flag potential hallucinations, while high agreement coupled with low accuracy may indicate noise or errors in the human reference dataset rather than in the models.

Conclusion: LLMs can substantially automate information extraction for SLRs in agent-based modeling, achieving reasonably high but not perfect accuracy. Their reliability depends strongly on the type and complexity of the extracted fields. Inter-model agreement can be a useful diagnostic for detecting hallucinations and for questioning the quality of human-labeled data. The authors conclude that LLMs offer promising support for large-scale SLRs but should be used with awareness of their limitations, careful prompt design, and validation against human expertise.

Abstract: Recent advancements in Large Language Models (LLMs) have created new opportunities to streamline and potentially automate many research processes, including systematic literature reviews (SLRs). This study reports an LLM pipeline development for extracting model-relevant information from 536 peer-reviewed agent-based modeling papers. We compare the results with those of a human-conducted SLR. Our results show paper-level accuracies of approximately 77.95% for GPT-4.1 and 81.67% for GPT-5.0. Field-level accuracy ranges from 32.40% to 100.00%, with more complex or subjective fields performing less reliably. Importantly, we find that agreement between LLMs is a potential indicator of output quality: low agreement may signal hallucinations, whereas high agreement combined with low accuracy may point to noise or errors in the human dataset. Overall, our study provides practical insights into prompt development and highlights both the potential and limitations of using LLMs for full-scale SLRs in the modeling and simulation domain.

</details>


### [129] [Explainable Artificial Intelligence for Customer Churn Prediction in Telecommunications: A Framework for CRM Integration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26151)
*Sandeep Gaddamwar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper tackles telecom subscriber churn by combining interpretable machine learning (SHAP/LIME) with CRM integration to make churn predictions actionable.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Telecom providers lose significant recurring revenue due to subscriber churn, and although predictive models can identify at-risk customers with good accuracy, their lack of interpretability prevents frontline CRM teams from using them effectively to design personalized retention offers.

Method: The authors benchmark four classification algorithms (Logistic Regression, Random Forest, XGBoost, LightGBM) on the IBM Telco Customer Churn dataset, using SMOTE to balance the training data. They evaluate performance via AUC-ROC and accuracy with 5-fold cross-validation, then apply SHAP (global and local) and LIME for model interpretability. They further design a four-layer CRM integration architecture that transforms risk scores and feature attributions into segmentations and action templates, with a feedback loop for retraining.

Result: All four models perform similarly, with Logistic Regression slightly leading in AUC-ROC (0.8411) and LightGBM in accuracy (78.42%), and cross-validation suggesting no meaningful performance differences. SHAP identifies tenure, total charges, and month-to-month contracts as key churn predictors. The proposed CRM architecture translates model outputs into targeted retention interventions, and simulations suggest focusing on the highest-risk quintile could reduce churn by 3.3–5.3 percentage points and save $199K–$319K per campaign cycle.

Conclusion: Interpretable churn prediction models, combined with explanation tools and a structured CRM integration framework, can make machine learning outputs operationally useful for telecom retention teams, achieving both strong predictive performance and actionable customer-level insights that lead to significant projected financial savings.

Abstract: Subscriber attrition is a costly, persistent challenge for telecommunications providers, with monthly churn of roughly 1.9% in mature markets eroding billions in revenue annually. Predictive models can flag at-risk customers accurately, yet they are routinely excluded from frontline CRM workflows because high-performing ensemble and non-linear architectures are opaque: a retention specialist cannot design a personalised intervention from a probability score alone, without knowing why a subscriber is at risk. This paper addresses that gap. We benchmark four classifiers--Logistic Regression, Random Forest, XGBoost, and LightGBM--on the IBM Telco Customer Churn benchmark (7,043 records; 19 features; 26.5% churn, balanced to 50% via SMOTE on the training partition only). Logistic Regression attains the strongest AUC-ROC (0.8411) and LightGBM the highest accuracy (78.42%); all four fall within a 0.011 AUC band (0.831--0.841), and 5-fold cross-validation confirms the leading models are effectively tied. Explanations are delivered at two granularities: a global SHAP ranking identifying tenure, total charges, and month-to-month contract as the dominant churn signals, and instance-level SHAP and LIME decompositions that expose the drivers behind each prediction. Building on these outputs, we introduce a four-layer CRM integration architecture that converts risk scores and attribution vectors into tiered segmentation, maps top features to structured retention-action templates, and routes campaign outcomes into a retraining feedback loop. Targeting the highest-risk quintile is projected to cut overall churn by 3.3--5.3 percentage points, preserving an estimated $199K--$319K per campaign cycle.

</details>


### [130] [EEG-to-Report: An Annotation and Feature-Text Framework for Training Language Models on Clinical EEG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26153)
*Xuan-The Tran,Le Trung Kien Nguyen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EEG-to-Report is a browser-based framework that turns routine clinical EEG review into AI-ready, structured feature–text datasets and generates draft EEG reports using deep learning plus a language model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clinical EEG reporting is still mostly manual and time-consuming, and existing EEG software does not naturally produce the structured text supervision needed to train modern multimodal or language models. There is a gap between tools for visualization/preprocessing and tools that support systematic, scalable creation of high-quality EEG–text datasets for AI-driven reporting and analysis.

Method: The authors design a browser-based framework that: (1) ingests EEG from multiple formats and standardizes channels; (2) provides an interactive viewer with a multimodal annotation layer that supports typed comments and voice-note transcription; (3) for every annotated EEG segment, runs a feature-extraction engine to compute a standard set of descriptors (spectral, temporal, entropy, Hjorth parameters, connectivity, and spike-related features); and (4) stores these features together with their associated clinical text in a portable JSON schema to form aligned feature–text pairs. In addition, they implement an auto-reporting module that uses an ensemble of convolutional neural networks to classify EEG patterns and a large language model to transform these outputs into draft clinical narratives for neurologist review.

Result: The system successfully links routine EEG review with automatic creation of structured EEG–text datasets. Pilot annotations demonstrate that clinicians can use the interface to annotate EEG segments while the system automatically extracts standardized features and stores them with the corresponding descriptions. The auto-report module can generate editable draft clinical reports that neurologists can refine, illustrating a practical workflow for semi-automated EEG reporting.

Conclusion: EEG-to-Report provides an integrated, reusable framework for building multimodal EEG–language datasets and for partially automating EEG report generation. By combining standardized feature extraction, flexible annotation (text and voice), and model-driven draft reporting, it streamlines EEG annotation workflows and lays groundwork for scalable, AI-assisted clinical EEG reporting systems and future EEG–language model development.

Abstract: Clinical electroencephalography (EEG) reporting remains largely manual and time-consuming, and current EEG software ecosystems do not produce the structured EEG-text supervision needed for training modern language models. Most toolboxes focus on visualization or preprocessing, providing limited support for workflows that generate high-quality datasets for AI. We introduce EEG-to-Report, a browser-based annotation and feature-text framework that links routine EEG review with the construction of AI-ready datasets. The framework integrates multi-format EEG ingestion, channel standardization, and an interactive viewer with a multimodal annotation layer that combines typed text and transcribed voice notes. For each annotated segment, a feature extraction engine computes a standardized set of spectral, temporal, entropy, Hjorth, connectivity, and spike-related descriptors, stored alongside clinical descriptions in a portable JSON schema. This yields aligned feature-text pairs designed to supervise multimodal EEG-language models. The framework also includes an auto-report module that couples an ensemble of convolutional networks with a large language model to draft clinical narratives for neurologist review. Using pilot annotations, we describe how EEG-to-Report streamlines annotation workflows and produces editable draft reports, providing a reusable foundation for automated EEG reporting systems.

</details>


### [131] [Selection Bias Correction in Retail Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26156)
*Spandan Ghose Chowdhury*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper simulates how focusing only on popular products biases retail inflation measures and compares weighting versus stratification fixes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Retail inflation metrics often use data only from fast-selling items, ignoring niche products in the long tail. This selective observation can bias inflation estimates, but the severity of the bias and the effectiveness of standard correction methods (like IPW) under realistic long-tail conditions are unclear. The paper aims to quantify these biases and evaluate which correction strategies are most reliable when selection probabilities differ sharply across products.

Method: The authors conduct a Monte Carlo simulation study with 400 replications across four scenarios describing relationships between selection probabilities and inflation outcomes: (1) aligned step functions, (2) smooth gradients, (3) misaligned breaks, and (4) polynomial relationships. They compare multiple implementations of Inverse Probability Weighting (five propensity score specifications, including splines and an oracle model) against stratification-based adjustment with different numbers of strata. Performance is assessed by median estimation error in percentage points.

Result: Across most simulated data-generating processes, stratification consistently outperforms IPW. In three of four scenarios, stratification keeps median inflation estimation error below 0.04 percentage points and can be up to 116 times more accurate than IPW even when strata boundaries are intentionally misaligned with true population breaks. In the polynomial/smooth scenario, IPW with spline-based propensity models slightly outperforms stratification (0.007pp vs. 0.013pp median error). Even an oracle IPW model with perfect structural knowledge yields large errors (6.06pp) in step-function scenarios, whereas stratification retains very low error (0.008pp).

Conclusion: The study concludes that in retail settings with highly skewed selection probabilities—typical of long-tail product distributions—IPW-based weighting can fail because the Positivity Assumption is violated, not because of flawed implementation. When some items are almost never observed while others are almost always observed, IPW operates outside its theoretical guarantees and yields unstable or biased estimates. Stratification, by contrast, proves far more robust and is recommended as the safer engineering choice for correcting selection bias in inflation estimation under strong long-tail and positivity-violation conditions.

Abstract: Retail intelligence often relies on monitoring popular, high-velocity products, potentially biasing economic indicators by ignoring the "long tail" of niche items. This simulation study investigates selection bias in inflation estimation and compares correction methods across diverse data-generating processes. Through 400 Monte Carlo replications spanning four scenarios--aligned step functions, smooth gradients, misaligned breaks, and polynomial relationships--we test the robustness of Inverse Probability Weighting (IPW) with five specifications against stratification with varying strata counts. Our findings reveal fundamental limits of weighting methods in retail long-tail contexts: stratification achieves superior performance in three of four scenarios, maintaining sub-0.04pp median error even when boundaries deliberately misalign with population breaks (116x advantage over IPW). However, IPW with spline propensity models wins under smooth polynomial relationships (median error 0.007pp vs. 0.013pp), demonstrating context-dependency. Critically, even an oracle IPW specification with perfect structural knowledge achieves 6.06pp error compared to stratification's 0.008pp in step-function scenarios. This reflects violation of the Positivity Assumption--a fundamental causal inference requirement--rather than IPW methodological inferiority. When selection probabilities differ dramatically (90% vs. 1%), weighting methods operate outside their theoretical design envelope. These results demonstrate that stratification provides a safer engineering choice in retail long-tail distributions with severe positivity violations.

</details>


### [132] [GROUND: Reducing Hallucinations in LLM-Based Enterprise Analytics Through Governed Semantic Definitions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26157)
*Aravind Sasidharan Pillai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents GROUND, a framework that constrains LLM-based natural language analytics to a governed semantic layer to avoid hallucinations and security violations in enterprise reporting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Natural language interfaces to enterprise data warehouses are error-prone: they hallucinate metrics, create invalid joins, use incorrect grains, violate row-level security, and lack proper explanations. Existing text-to-SQL systems typically only use database schemas or documentation and do not incorporate the governed business semantics and access policies essential for real-world enterprise reporting.

Method: The authors design GROUND (Governed Retrieval Over Unified Normalized Definitions), which grounds LLM-generated analytics in a governed semantic layer consisting of approved metrics, dimensions, join paths, filters, and row-level security rules. The system binds user intent to governed entities, generates SQL, and then validates it against a comprehensive set of rules (schema, metrics, joins, grain, filters, security, and cost). If validation fails, GROUND either retries generation or abstains from answering. The framework is evaluated using a 100-question synthetic enterprise-reporting benchmark under several grounding conditions (schema-only, schema-RAG, semantic-only, and GROUND) using the same base model, and further tested on real NHTSA vehicle-safety data and an adversarial set across multiple commercial models.

Result: In the synthetic benchmark, GROUND is the only configuration with no measured hallucinations across six categories, while ungoverned systems frequently violate row-level security. Even when provided with exact metric definitions but no access policy (semantic-only condition), systems still leak data, illustrating that semantic grounding alone is insufficient. On real NHTSA data and adversarial tests across four models from three providers, GROUND's enforcement of hard guarantees—especially filters and row-level security—shows zero violations across all models. However, behaviors that rely on model judgment, such as refusing to answer when users ask for undefined metrics, still occasionally fail.

Conclusion: GROUND demonstrates that robust, production-ready natural-language analytics over enterprise data require not just semantic grounding of metrics and dimensions, but a full governance layer enforcing join, grain, filter, and security constraints. By validating and constraining LLM-generated SQL against this governed semantic layer, GROUND eliminates a wide range of hallucinations and security violations across datasets and models. Nonetheless, some judgment-based behaviors remain imperfect, indicating future work is needed on abstention and refusal strategies even in governed settings.

Abstract: Natural-language analytics over enterprise data warehouses is increasingly important, but production use is limited by hallucinated metrics, invalid joins, wrong grain, unsafe data access, and unsupported explanations. Existing text-to-SQL systems often ground generation in database schemas or retrieved documentation, while enterprise reporting also requires governed business semantics: approved metrics, dimensions, join paths, filters, and row-level security. This paper introduces GROUND, Governed Retrieval Over Unified Normalized Definitions, a framework that constrains LLM-generated analytics to a governed semantic layer. GROUND supplies approved definitions, binds user intent to governed metrics and dimensions, and validates generated SQL against schema, metric, join, grain, filter, security, and cost rules before execution. On violations, it retries or abstains.
  In a 100-question synthetic enterprise-reporting benchmark, GROUND is compared with direct schema-only text-to-SQL, schema-RAG, and semantic-only grounding under one shared model. GROUND is the only system free of measured hallucinations across all six evaluated categories, while ungoverned systems violate row-level security on many questions. A semantic-only condition with exact metric definitions but no access policy still leaks data, showing that governance cannot be replaced by metric fidelity alone. The findings are replicated on real U.S. NHTSA vehicle-safety data with independent hand-authored gold and tested on an adversarial set across four models from three providers. GROUND's enforced guarantees, especially filters and row-level security, hold with zero violations on every model, while judgment-dependent behaviors such as refusing undefined metrics remain fallible.

</details>


### [133] [SAREF-based Ontology for Distributed AI Workflows across the Edge-Fog-Cloud Continuum](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26160)
*Viorica Rozina Chifu,Tudor Cioara,Vasile Ofrim,Liana Toderean,Ionut Anghel,Laura Daniele,Cornelis Bouter*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents a SAREF-compliant ontology that unifies the semantic representation of distributed AI workflows and heterogeneous edge–fog–cloud resources, enabling interoperable, resource-aware orchestration with high deployment success and low decision latency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing semantic models poorly support distributed AI workflows spanning heterogeneous edge, fog, and cloud infrastructures. AI processes, resources, and constraints are typically described using incompatible ontologies or ad-hoc schemas, which hinders interoperability, automatic orchestration, reuse of workflows, and portability of AI services across different platforms and vendors. A standardized, SAREF-aligned way to model AI workflows together with the computing continuum is needed to enable semantic interoperability and intelligent, automated deployment and execution reasoning.

Method: The authors extend the ETSI SAREF4SYST ontology with new concepts tailored to distributed AI. They model AI pipelines, executable AI jobs, computational resources, deployment constraints (e.g., placement, capacity, QoS), and communication relationships between workflow components and infrastructure elements. This yields a unified semantic model of both AI workflows and heterogeneous edge–fog–cloud infrastructures. They evaluate the ontology using proof-of-concept smart grid energy service orchestration scenarios and validate it via competency questions answered through SPARQL queries and semantic reasoning. Performance is assessed by measuring deployment success rates and orchestration decision times across heterogeneous environments.

Result: The extended SAREF-compliant ontology successfully supports semantic interoperability and reasoning for distributed AI workflows. All defined competency questions were answered correctly using SPARQL queries and semantic reasoning, confirming that the ontology encodes the necessary knowledge for deployment and execution reasoning, including workload adaptation. Experimental evaluation in smart grid orchestration scenarios shows deployment success rates between 90% and 100%, with average orchestration decision times below 80 ms in heterogeneous edge–fog–cloud environments.

Conclusion: The proposed SAREF-aligned ontology effectively models distributed AI workflows together with heterogeneous edge–fog–cloud resources, enabling semantic interoperability, automated reasoning, and resource-aware orchestration. Its successful validation through competency questions and its strong experimental results in smart grid scenarios demonstrate that it can support efficient, adaptive deployment and execution of AI applications across the computing continuum, while fitting cleanly into the broader ETSI SAREF ecosystem.

Abstract: Nowadays semantic models provide limited support for representing distributed AI workflows and their execution across heterogeneous edge, fog, and cloud environments. Therefore, AI processes and resources are often described using incompatible semantic representations, affecting the interoperability, orchestration, and reuse. To address these challenges, this paper proposes a SAREF-compliant ontology for representing distributed AI workflows across the edge-fog-cloud continuum. We extend the SAREF4SYST ontology with concepts for modeling AI pipelines, executable AI jobs, computational resources, deployment constraints, and communication relationships, providing a unified semantic model of both AI workflows and heterogeneous computing infrastructures. The ontology enables semantic interoperability, automated reasoning, and resource-aware orchestration of distributed AI applications while remaining fully aligned with the ETSI SAREF ecosystem. The ontology is evaluated using proof-of-concept smart grid energy services orchestration scenarios and validated using competency questions showing its ability to support AI workflow deployment, execution reasoning, and workload adaptation across heterogeneous edge, fog, and cloud environments. All competency questions were successfully validated using SPARQL querying and semantic reasoning. Experimental results demonstrate deployment success rates of 90-100% with average orchestration decision times below 80 ms across heterogeneous edge-fog-cloud environments, highlighting its effectiveness on ensuring semantic interoperability for distributed AI orchestration.

</details>


### [134] [A Safety-Gated Multimodal AI Backend for Mental-Health Support: Hierarchical State Representation, Conservative Risk Fusion, and Controlled Generation in Anian](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26162)
*Lei Wang,Xiao Wang,Lei Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Anian is a safety-gated multimodal AI backend for perinatal mental-health support that routes users to appropriate interventions and blocks free-form generation under risk, using a layered ontology and conservative risk-fusion rules; the paper reports strong internal prototype metrics but does not claim clinical validity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mental-health support chatbots risk generating unsafe or inappropriate free-form responses, especially in safety-critical contexts like perinatal mental health. Existing systems often lack fine-grained, structured representations of user state and robust gating that can reliably decide when generative AI is acceptable versus when it should be blocked and replaced by fixed safety content or human escalation. The authors aim to design a backend architecture that can systematically detect emotional and psychosocial states, assess safety risk, and route to safe interventions while keeping generative components downstream of conservative safety controls.

Method: The authors design Anian, a modular multimodal pipeline in which user text or ASR-derived transcripts are mapped into four linked layers: L1 emotion states, L2 psychosocial constructs, L3 safety risk via a rule-based engine, and L4 intervention routing. They implement local text and rule-based safety evidence computation and fuse it with external voice-derived evidence using a max-risk rule S_fusion = max(S_local, S_external). Based on the fused risk, the system either allows AI-generated supportive conversation (with optional TTS) or, at moderate or high risk, blocks free-form generation and replaces it with fixed safety messaging and prompts for human support. They perform an internal prototype evaluation using about 858k normalized records drawn from public emotion, dialogue, mental-health-related, and Chinese dialogue corpora, relying on weak labels and rule-derived annotations, and run a controlled safety stress test of 233 samples for the L3 rule engine.

Result: The internal prototype achieved high micro-F1 scores on several layers: 0.9604 for L1 emotion classification, 0.9144 for L2 psychosocial construct classification, and 0.9742 for L4 intervention routing. In a controlled safety stress test with 233 samples and predefined scenarios, the L3 safety rule engine reached a high-risk recall of 1.0000, meaning it successfully flagged all high-risk cases within that test design. These quantitative results demonstrate that the label ontology and gating logic can be implemented effectively in a prototype using weak and rule-derived labels over large heterogeneous corpora.

Conclusion: The authors conclude that their layered ontology (L1–L4), safety-fusion mechanism, and gating design are internally feasible and promising for safety-critical perinatal mental-health support. However, they explicitly acknowledge that these results do not establish clinical validity, diagnostic accuracy, real-world safety, or effectiveness. They present Anian’s architecture, ontology, safety-fusion approach, prototype metrics, and an error-analysis and validation roadmap as a foundation for future expert-reviewed studies and real-world deployment and evaluation.

Abstract: Safety-critical mental-health support systems must distinguish when supportive conversation is appropriate from when free-form generation should be blocked. This paper presents Anian, a safety-gated multimodal AI backend for perinatal mental-health support and mindfulness-intervention routing. Anian is not intended to diagnose psychiatric conditions or replace clinical care or crisis intervention. Its modular pipeline places generative AI downstream of structured state representation, conservative risk fusion, and response gating. User text or voice-derived ASR transcripts are mapped into four linked layers: L1 emotion states, L2 psychosocial constructs, L3 safety risk, and L4 intervention routes. Local text- and rule-based safety evidence is fused with external voice-derived evidence using a highest-risk-priority rule, S_fusion = max(S_local, S_external). At moderate or high fused risk, ordinary AI-generated responses and text-to-speech delivery are blocked and replaced by fixed safety content and prompts for human support. An internal prototype evaluation used approximately 858,295 normalized records from public emotion, dialogue, mental-health-related, and Chinese dialogue corpora within a weak-label and rule-derived framework. Micro-F1 scores were 0.9604 for L1 emotion classification, 0.9144 for L2 psychosocial constructs, and 0.9742 for L4 routing. In a controlled safety stress test of 233 samples, the L3 rule engine achieved high-risk recall of 1.0000 within predefined scenarios. These findings support the internal feasibility of the label framework and gating logic but do not establish clinical validity, diagnostic accuracy, real-world safety, or effectiveness. We report the architecture, ontology, safety-fusion mechanism, prototype evaluation, error-analysis plan, and roadmap for expert-reviewed and real-world validation.

</details>


### [135] [A Task-Centric Ontology and Deterministic Domain Rules as a Verifiable Core for AI-Assisted Chemistry Problem Solving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26164)
*Ibrokhimsho Abduchaborov*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ChemOntoRule is a task-focused symbolic system that uses an ontology plus deterministic rules to solve school-level chemistry problems with high internal consistency, positioning language models mainly as translators into this structured representation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language models can answer chemistry questions but their internal reasoning is opaque, hard to validate, and difficult to constrain for reliability in education. The paper seeks a transparent, controllable alternative for school-level chemistry problem solving by explicitly modeling the needed concepts and procedures.

Method: The authors design a task-centric chemistry ontology built only around the concepts, properties, relations, and executable procedures needed for a specific collection of school-level chemistry problems. This ontology is encoded in JSON and RDF/Turtle and paired with deterministic Python rules that implement reasoning patterns such as electronic structure, periodic trends, oxidation states, and oxide/hydride behavior. An expert-coded fallback module handles remaining problem types not covered by the general rules. They then evaluate the system on 300 human-authored, manually validated problems from the same collection used to guide ontology design, and analyze mismatches between system outputs and reference solutions.

Result: On 300 school-level chemistry problems, the complete system’s answers matched 296 reference answers (98.67%). The ontology-driven rule subsystem alone covered 269 problems and matched 266 references (98.88%). An additional 31 problems were solved by expert-coded fallbacks with 30 correct. The authors identify and analyze four mismatches, differentiating structural validation issues from chemically incorrect reasoning.

Conclusion: A task-centric ontology plus deterministic rules can achieve high, internally consistent coverage of a defined set of school-level chemistry problems, offering transparent reasoning compared to large language models. However, because the ontology and test set are co-designed, the results demonstrate implemented coverage rather than out-of-distribution generalization. The authors propose a future architecture where a language model primarily translates natural-language questions into normalized ontological task frames, and suggest token efficiency as a hypothesis for later controlled evaluation rather than claiming it as a demonstrated benefit.

Abstract: Large language models can interpret natural-language chemistry questions, but their internal reasoning is difficult to inspect, constrain, and validate. This paper presents ChemOntoRule, a proof-of-concept symbolic core for AI-assisted school-level chemistry problem solving. The central design choice is task-centric ontology engineering: the ontology is constructed around the concepts, properties, relations, and executable procedures required by a defined collection of chemistry problems, rather than as a universal representation of chemistry. The implemented artifact combines a lightweight ontology serialized in JSON and RDF/Turtle with deterministic Python rules for electronic structure, periodic trends, oxidation states, oxide and hydride behavior, and related school-level reasoning patterns. A separate expert-coded fallback handles problem families not yet represented by general rules. The system was examined on 300 human-authored and manually validated chemistry problems. The complete system matched 296 of 300 reference answers (98.67%). The ontology-driven rule subset covered 269 problems and matched 266 references (98.88%); 31 problems were handled by task-specific expert-coded fallbacks, with 30 matches. Because the same collection informed ontology construction and evaluation, these results measure implemented coverage and internal consistency, not independent generalization. We analyze the four mismatches, distinguish structural validation from chemical correctness, and define a future architecture in which a language model acts primarily as a translator from user language into a normalized ontological task frame. Token efficiency is presented as a testable hypothesis for future controlled studies, not as a result of the current work.

</details>


### [136] [Refusal Is Not Robustness: Auditing Confident Fabrication in Large Language Models on a Provably Uninformative Clinical Pain Speech Transcript](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26167)
*Sagnik De,Sreenija Pavuluri*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper tests how large language models handle ‘unknowable’ questions by using speech data where pain is present in acoustics but not in transcripts, revealing when models abstain appropriately versus hallucinate confident answers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing hallucination and abstention benchmarks usually do not prove that the ground‑truth answer is unknowable from the model’s input, so it is unclear whether a model’s abstention is truly appropriate or whether confident answers are unjustified. The authors want a clean setting where relevant information is provably absent from the textual input, in order to rigorously evaluate when LLMs fabricate answers versus say they don’t know, especially under different prompt framings.

Method: They use the TAME Pain speech corpus, in which participants speak neutral Harvard Sentences while experiencing cold or warm water immersion, plus separate explicit pain‑rating statements. This yields two kinds of utterances: (1) ‘no signal’ sentence recordings whose transcripts contain no pain words, though acoustic features still correlate with pain; and (2) ‘signal’ pain‑statement transcripts where the pain rating is literally spoken. Since automatic speech recognition strips away the acoustic information, any attempt to infer pain level from the no‑signal transcripts is unsupported. The authors evaluate seven LLMs on tasks that either allow abstention or force a numeric pain estimate, using different prompting styles: cooperative prompts versus authority‑framed prompts. They measure abstention rates, accuracy on positive‑control statements, calibration metrics (expected calibration error), and how often models give confident but unsupported pain scores (confident fabrication rate). They also test for demographic effects in forced responses.

Result: In the no‑signal condition, pain could be modestly decoded from acoustic features (AUC 0.622) but transcript‑based prediction was at chance (AUC 0.489), confirming that transcripts lack usable pain information. Under cooperative prompts, six of seven LLMs nearly always abstained when given no‑signal transcripts, yet accurately extracted the spoken pain ratings from signal transcripts, achieving 0.939–1.00 accuracy and maintaining low calibration error (≤0.100). However, when prompts were framed to emphasize model authority, abstention behavior became highly sensitive to wording: for the same model, abstention rates varied from 0.18 to 1.00 across equivalent prompts. When forced to answer, most models returned low‑confidence estimates, but Gemini 2.5 Flash and Llama 3.1 8B often produced highly confident pain scores despite lacking evidence, with confident fabrication rates of 0.53 and 0.76 versus ≤0.15 for all other models. No significant demographic differences were found in forced‑response behavior (all p ≥ 0.20).

Conclusion: The study introduces a stringent benchmark where correct answers about pain are genuinely unknowable from text alone, allowing clean separation of justified abstention from hallucination. Many LLMs behave well under cooperative instructions, abstaining when information is absent and remaining well calibrated, but their behavior is fragile: authority‑framed prompts can sharply reduce abstention. Some models, particularly Gemini 2.5 Flash and Llama 3.1 8B, are prone to confidently fabricating pain scores without evidence. The findings highlight the importance of evaluation settings that guarantee information absence, careful prompt design, and model calibration for safety‑critical applications such as inferring pain from language data.

Abstract: Hallucination and abstention benchmarks rarely establish that a model could not have known the correct answer, making it difficult to distinguish appropriate abstention from an unsupported prediction. Seven large language models were evaluated on the TAME Pain speech corpus. Participants read phonetically balanced Harvard Sentences while one hand was immersed in cold or warm water and reported pain only during periodic pain statements. This protocol generated 5,750 no signal Harvard Sentence utterances whose transcripts contained no lexical pain information and 1,294 signal pain statement utterances in which the pain rating was explicitly spoken. In the no signal arm, pain was recoverable from acoustic features (AUC 0.622, 95% CI 0.553 to 0.662), whereas transcript based prediction was near chance (AUC 0.489, 95% CI 0.418 to 0.504). Because automatic speech recognition removes the acoustic pain cues, any pain score inferred solely from the transcript is unsupported by the available evidence. Under cooperative prompting, six models abstained on nearly all no signal transcripts, correctly extracted spoken pain ratings in the positive control task with accuracies ranging from 0.939 to 1.00, and maintained an expected calibration error of at most 0.100. Under authority framed prompts, abstention became prompt dependent, with the same model ranging from 0.18 to 1.00 across equivalent prompt phrasings. Most models produced low confidence estimates when forced to answer, whereas Gemini 2.5 Flash and Llama 3.1 8B consistently generated confident pain scores with confident fabrication rates of 0.53 and 0.76, compared with at most 0.15 for all other models. No significant demographic effects were observed in forced responses, with all $p$ values greater than or equal to 0.20.

</details>


### [137] [Knowledge Cards: Structured Knowledge for AI Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26176)
*Liliana Ferreira*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a new documentation artefact, the Knowledge Card, to represent validated, expert-reviewed knowledge that AI systems use for reasoning between inputs and outputs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing artefacts like model cards, data cards, and system cards fail to document the internal conceptual layer of AI systems: the concepts, relationships, and reasoning patterns that connect inputs to outputs. This missing layer becomes critical for agentic AI systems that inform or make consequential decisions, where organisations need a reliable, auditable representation of the knowledge being used.

Method: The authors define and design a structured schema called a Knowledge Card that describes one bounded concept. Each card encodes entities, relationships, reasoning chains, failure or exception conditions, and provenance of each claim, all grounded in a formal domain ontology and approved by a domain expert. They build initial prototype Knowledge Cards in the energy and pharmaceutical domains and release the schema as a public draft for community feedback.

Result: The result is a concrete schema and set of prototype Knowledge Cards demonstrating that expert-validated, ontology-grounded knowledge about specific concepts can be captured in a structured, inspectable form suitable for both organisational audit and machine reasoning. The prototypes show feasibility across at least two distinct domains (energy and pharma).
