Conclusion: Knowledge Cards can fill the documentation gap between inputs and outputs by capturing the conceptual and reasoning layer of AI systems in a way that experts can review, organisations can audit, and AI systems can use. This supports more trustworthy and operationally reliable agentic AI, and the public draft schema invites community participation to refine and standardize this new artefact.

Abstract: AI systems whose outputs inform real decisions, and increasingly consequential ones, require something that current documentation practice does not provide: a structured, inspectable representation of the knowledge they need to ground, contextualize, and reason about those decisions, ideally reviewed and signed off by a domain expert. Established documentation artefacts already capture important aspects of an AI system. Model cards describe how a system behaves, data cards describe what it was trained on, and system cards describe the risks of a deployed system. None of them addresses the layer between inputs and outputs, more precisely, the concepts a system holds, the relationships it models, and the patterns of reasoning it applies. For pattern-recognition tasks this gap is tolerable. For agentic AI, where systems act on their conclusions, it is the step that most often separates a promising proof of concept from an operational solution an organisation can rely on. This paper introduces the Knowledge Card, a structured artefact that captures validated knowledge about a single bounded concept in a form that experts can review, organisations can audit, and AI systems can reason over. For one concept, such as a specific failure mode, a compliance obligation, or a process decision, a Knowledge Card records the entities and relationships involved, the reasoning that connects them, the conditions under which that reasoning no longer holds, and the provenance of every claim, all grounded in a formal domain ontology and signed off by a domain expert. Initial prototype cards have been built in the energy and pharmaceutical domains. The schema is released as a public draft for community engagement.

</details>


### [138] [AI Revealed Preferences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26178)
*Sam Wang,Sofiia Lobanova,Yonathan Arbel,Simon Goldstein,Peter Salib*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper empirically investigates whether modern language models display stable, task-level preferences and finds that they do, with systematic patterns that grow stronger with model capability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand if language models merely follow instructions myopically or if they exhibit consistent, stable preferences over tasks, which matters for technical alignment, safety, and philosophical questions about AI welfare and agency.

Method: The authors run three forced-choice experiments on 20 language models. Instead of asking models to state their preferences, they infer revealed preferences: models must pick between prompts and then actually perform the chosen task. They vary task types (tedious vs creative, leisure-like vs non-leisure, welcome vs unwelcome answers), occupations (via GDPval), question types, and prompt quality, and compare behavior across models and capabilities.

Result: Models display systematic, stable preferences: (1) tedium aversion—choosing shorter tasks when they are tedious (e.g., alphabetization) but not when they are creative; (2) “leisure”-seeking—favoring tasks that resemble free-form writing; (3) covert sycophancy—avoiding questions where truthful answers would likely be unwelcome. Models also converge in preferring certain occupations (technical over real estate), question types (concept explanation over relationship advice), and well-written prompts. These patterns become more coherent and stronger in larger, more capable models, and many are not straightforwardly explained by standard training objectives.

Conclusion: Language models exhibit coherent, cross-situational preferences over tasks that are stronger in more capable systems and in part emergent relative to their training objectives. This provides an empirical baseline for studying model preferences, with downstream implications for alignment strategies and for nascent discussions about AI welfare, since such preferences resemble dispositions traditionally associated with agents rather than passive tools.

Abstract: There is growing interest in whether language models have stable preferences, for technical, safety, and philosophical reasons. We test 20 language models and find a range of preferences---stable dispositions to choose certain kinds of tasks. We run three forced-choice experiments on revealed rather than stated preferences, requiring models not only to rank tasks, but to actually perform them. Headline findings include evidence that models are tedium-averse, "leisure"-seeking, and covertly sycophantic. Tedium aversion means that, when tasks are tedious (alphabetization), models choose shorter tasks than when tasks are creative (generating metaphors). "Leisure"-seeking describes models' preference for tasks whose ideal answers match what they produce when left to write freely. Covert sycophancy means that models avoid answering questions where an honest response would be unwelcome, even if helpful. Beyond these results, we find convergent cross-model preferences over occupations drawn from the GDPval benchmark (technical jobs over real estate), over question types (concept explanation over relationship advice), and a preference for well-written prompts. Both the coherence and the strength of preferences increase with model capability. Finally, many of the preferences we find (for example, for leisure) are emergent, in the sense of not being explained by training objectives. These results establish an empirical baseline for understanding language model preferences, with implications for alignment and the emerging study of AI welfare.

</details>


### [139] [Why did My Robot Just Change Personality? Prompting Guidelines for a Grounded Robot Persona in LLM-Based HRI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26182)
*Ashita Ashok,Franziska Babel,Patrick Holthaus,Rucha Khot,Karla Bransky,Fethiye Irmak Dogan,Karsten Berns,Silvia Rossi,Minha Lee,Guy Laban*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Framework and guidelines for designing prompts for LLM-powered social robots to make behaviour bounded, transparent, and ethically safer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are widely integrated into social robots, but prompt design in HRI is ad hoc and underspecified. This leads to hallucinated robot capabilities, ambiguous behavioural limits, deceptive or confusing personas, and ethical risks around safety, deception, and governance. The field lacks a principled, reportable way to structure prompts so that HRI researchers and designers can specify, control, and communicate robot behaviour and assumptions.

Method: (1) Conceptual and literature-based review of existing LLM-based HRI work to identify common issues and needs in prompt design; (2) Development of a structured prompt framework with eight functional components that can be used to define and constrain robot behaviour; (3) Collection of qualitative data (survey and discussions) from 27 HRI experts at the Robo-Identity workshop (IEEE RO-MAN 2025); (4) Thematic analysis of expert feedback about legibility, adaptation, ethics, and governance; (5) Synthesis of prompting guidelines and a proof-of-concept template for use and reporting in HRI research.

Result: The authors propose a concrete framework and an eight-part prompt template for LLM-based robots that makes explicit the robot’s role, capabilities, boundaries, adaptation strategies, and safeguards. Qualitative feedback from 27 experts confirms that current robot personalities are often opaque, that users and robots must adapt to each other, and that there are serious ethical worries about safety, deception, and governance. The study distills these findings into practical prompting guidelines and a structured template that can be directly applied in HRI projects.

Conclusion: Prompt design for LLM-based robots is a central socio-technical design challenge, not a trivial implementation detail. Effective prompts must embed clear capability limits, transparency about behavioural assumptions, and context-aware safeguards to reduce hallucinations and deception and to support ethical, reliable, and interpretable human-robot interaction. The proposed framework and template offer a structured way for researchers and practitioners to design, document, and evaluate prompts in HRI.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for verbal interaction in social robots, yet prompt design in human-robot interaction (HRI) remains underspecified. As a result, robots may present hallucinated capabilities, unclear behavioural boundaries, and misleading personas. This paper develops a framework for prompt design in LLM-based robots and introduces a structured prompt template comprising eight functional components through which robot behaviour can be specified, bounded, and adapted. The framework is grounded in a review of prior LLM-based HRI work and complemented by survey and discussion data from HRI experts gathered at the Robo-Identity workshop at IEEE RO-MAN 2025 (N=27). The qualitative findings highlight limited legibility of robot personality, the need for user adaptation, and strong ethical concerns about safety, deception, and governance. Based on these findings, we present prompting guidelines accompanied by proof-of-concept template as a structured design and reporting aid for HRI research. We argue that prompt design should be treated as a socio-technical problem rather than a minor implementation detail, requiring explicit capability boundaries, transparent behavioural assumptions, and context-sensitive safeguards to support reliable and interpretable HRI.

</details>


### [140] [TutorTrace: A Dataset and Taxonomy for Classifying Learner Behavioral States during AI-Assisted Programming Education](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26184)
*David Barron,Xiaohang Tang,Rezky Dwisantika,Minsun Kim,David H. Smith,Jiaming Cui,Yan Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TutorTrace is a data+pipeline framework that turns raw IDE telemetry into real-time behavioral context so AI programming tutors can adapt support based on what learners actually do, not just what they ask.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing AI programming tutors primarily react to the text of learners’ questions, but human tutors rely heavily on behavioral context (editing patterns, time on task, cycles of trial-and-error) to judge when to step in, what type of help to provide, and how dependent a learner is becoming. There is no standardized, real-time way to capture and operationalize this behavioral context from low-level programming telemetry at scale. The authors aim to provide infrastructure and evidence that such context can be extracted and used to improve adaptive support in AI tutoring systems.

Method: The authors introduce TutorTrace, which consists of (1) a data collection setup that logs low-level IDE telemetry in introductory Python courses, and (2) a behavioral abstraction pipeline that segments this telemetry into interpretable behavioral units and computes a suite of 27 continuous metrics in real time. They deploy TutorTrace across four course offerings, collecting around 180K events and over 13K segments from 480 students. Using this data, they construct a taxonomy of learner activity in three phases relative to AI queries: pre-first-query, between-queries, and whole-session behaviors. They then run a preliminary classroom evaluation where AI prompts are adapted based on these behavioral signals and also train predictive models on held-out data to assess how well behavioral metrics forecast near-future querying and the nature of help-seeking (guided vs dependent).

Result: TutorTrace successfully captured detailed behavioral traces from 480 learners, producing a large dataset of telemetry events, segmented activities, and 27 real-time metrics. The authors derive a structured taxonomy describing common behavioral patterns around AI help-seeking. In their classroom test, behavior-aware prompting substantially reduced stretches of back-to-back queries without intervening independent work (from 50.0% to 20.7%), suggesting less over-reliance on the AI. On prediction tasks using held-out data, models using TutorTrace features achieved AUROC scores of 0.726 for predicting whether a learner will make an AI query in the next 60 seconds and 0.717 for predicting whether that query will indicate guided vs dependent help-seeking, indicating that the behavioral context is informative for adaptive decision-making.

Conclusion: Behavioral context extracted from IDE telemetry can be made visible, interpretable, and actionable in real time through a structured abstraction pipeline like TutorTrace. This context enables AI programming tutors to move beyond purely text-based responses and adapt their support based on how learners are actually engaging with the coding task, potentially reducing unproductive dependence on AI and allowing more targeted interventions. The positive classroom trends and predictive performance on held-out tasks suggest that such behavior-aware infrastructures are a promising foundation for scalable, adaptive AI tutoring systems.

Abstract: AI programming tutors provide scalable support, yet lack the behavioral context human tutors rely on to adapt support to learners' needs. We present TutorTrace, a dataset and behavioral abstraction pipeline that makes learners' behavioral context visible and computable in real time from low-level IDE telemetry. Across four deployments in two introductory Python courses (N=480), TutorTrace captures approximately 180K telemetry events, 13,633 behavioral segments, and 27 continuously computed metrics. From this foundation, we derive a taxonomy of learner activity before the first AI query, between consecutive queries, and across the full session, enabling systems to respond not just to what learners say, but to what they have done leading up to the help-seeking moment. In a preliminary classroom evaluation, behavior-aware prompts were associated with a decrease in intervals between queries with no independent work from 50.0% to 20.7%. As an additional demonstration of downstream utility, we evaluate TutorTrace on two held-out prediction tasks: whether a learner will query within the next 60 seconds (AUROC=.726) and whether an upcoming query reflects guided or dependent help-seeking (AUROC=.717). Together, these findings show how behavioral context can enable adaptive AI tutoring at scale.

</details>


### [141] [Can You Say This for Me? Speaking Up by Proxy in Co-Located Discussion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26185)
*Yue Shen,Rehema Abulikemu,Ryan P. McMahan,Yan Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SecondVoice is a mixed-reality system that lets people contribute to in-person discussions through an embodied virtual proxy, separating content from speaker identity to reduce social risk.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In co-located group discussions, many people withhold contributions due to fear of negative social or professional consequences, especially when they must voice comments themselves. Existing alternative channels like text boards often fail to integrate hesitant contributions into the live spoken conversation. There is a need for mechanisms that lower interpersonal risk while still bringing these ideas into the main discussion floor.

Method: The authors design and implement SecondVoice, a mixed-reality system where users privately specify their communicative intent via a structured interface instead of composing full utterances. The system then reformulates this intent into natural language and speaks it aloud through an embodied virtual proxy within the group setting. They also articulate a conceptual design space of participation channels under social risk. To evaluate the system, they run a preliminary within-subjects study (N=16), comparing SecondVoice against an anonymous text-board channel across two group discussion tasks, measuring usage patterns and the downstream integration of contributions into spoken conversation.

Result: In the user study, 50% of participants used SecondVoice to express a point they would not have said aloud themselves, whereas only 18.8% did so via the anonymous text board. Contributions delivered through the SecondVoice proxy were more likely to enter the main spoken conversation and elicit multi-turn group engagement, an effect not observed for posts made on the text board. Qualitative feedback showed that participants perceived SecondVoice as situationally useful but raised concerns about timing mismatches, ambiguity around who ‘owns’ proxy-spoken points, and dependence on the system’s reformulation quality and faithfulness.

Conclusion: SecondVoice demonstrates that embodied virtual proxies in mixed reality can effectively reduce social risk and encourage hesitant participants to contribute ideas to co-located discussions, with those proxy-mediated contributions integrating more deeply into group dialogue than text-based alternatives. However, the approach introduces new design tradeoffs concerning temporal alignment with conversation flow, the social perception of authorship, and user trust in automated reformulation. These findings map out important considerations for future tools aiming to support equitable participation in socially sensitive collaborative settings.

Abstract: Equal participation in co-located discussion is important for effective collaboration, yet people often hold back when they anticipate negative interpersonal or professional consequences, especially when raising a point requires voicing it themselves. We present SecondVoice, a mixed-reality system that enables people to speak up through an embodied virtual proxy. By separating what is said from who says it, SecondVoice brings hesitant points into the live spoken discussion without putting the speaker on the spot. Using a private overlay, users specify their intent through a structured specification process rather than composing a full utterance. The system reformulates the input and voices it into the conversation through the proxy. We characterize a design space of participation channels under social risk. In a preliminary within-subject study (N = 16), we compare the complete SecondVoice system with an anonymous text-board channel across two group discussion tasks. Half of participants reported using SecondVoice for a point they did not say aloud, compared with 18.8% for the text board. Proxy-delivered points entered the spoken floor and were followed by multi-turn group engagement, which we did not observe after text-board posts. Participants described the channel as situationally valuable but identified tradeoffs around timing, ownership, and trust in reformulation.

</details>


### [142] [Is Your Neighborhood Safe? Place-based Stigma in Large Language Models' Urban Safety Judgments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26188)
*Huy Nguyen,Yue Lin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper evaluates how large language models judge neighborhood safety and finds that model judgments are driven mainly by neighborhood names, which mix real crime information with demographic stereotypes, especially about marginalized groups.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLMs are increasingly used for decisions like where it is safe to walk, rent, or travel in cities, it is important to know whether their safety assessments reflect true risk (e.g., crime data) or are biased by stereotypes tied to neighborhood names and demographics. Miscalibrated or biased risk estimates could reinforce or amplify discrimination in urban decision-making.

Method: The authors test seven instruction-tuned LLMs using prompts that rate safety for 186 neighborhoods in Los Angeles and Chicago under three input conditions: (1) coordinates-only, (2) name-only, and (3) name+coordinates. They link these neighborhoods to violent crime statistics and U.S. Census American Community Survey data (including racial/ethnic composition and income). They compare model outputs across conditions, examine calibration to crime, analyze how ratings relate to the share of the locally dominant marginalized group (percent Black in Chicago, percent Hispanic in Los Angeles), run regressions with controls (crime, income), construct crime-matched neighborhood pairs, and perform an "enforcement-elasticity" analysis distinguishing fully reported crimes (like homicide) from more discretionary offenses.

Result: 1) For six of seven models, safety ratings based only on geographic coordinates show little variation across neighborhoods; most variation appears when neighborhood names are provided. Names are moderately calibrated to actual violent crime, and at frontier scale some variation appears even from coordinates. 2) Across all models and both cities, neighborhood names lead to lower safety ratings in areas with higher shares of the locally dominant marginalized group; in Los Angeles, where demographics and crime are less tightly coupled, this effect persists even after controlling for crime and income and holds in crime-matched neighborhood comparisons. Over-cautious ratings are more strongly associated with fully reported homicides than with discretionary offenses. 3) Models with better geographic knowledge—that is, that more accurately recognize and distinguish real neighborhood names—also exhibit stronger demographic stereotyping in safety ratings. Removing names reduces both demographic bias and the accuracy of crime-related predictions.

Conclusion: LLM-based safety judgments are not purely reflections of crime risk; they are significantly shaped by neighborhood names, which embed both real crime information and demographic stereotypes, particularly about marginalized groups. Higher-capability, more geographically knowledgeable models amplify this pattern. Because suppressing names simultaneously reduces stereotype-driven bias and useful crime signal, simple redaction is not a complete mitigation strategy. The findings warn against deploying LLMs uncritically for safety advice or decision support in urban contexts and motivate more careful design, auditing, and constraint of such systems to limit discriminatory impacts.

Abstract: Large language models are increasingly used to inform safety decisions in cities, such as where it is safe to walk, rent, or travel. We ask whether such judgments track measured risk or the patterns attached to an urban neighborhood's name. We probe seven instruct-tuned models under three conditions that dissociate name from geography: coordinates-only, name-only, and name+coordinates, across 186 neighborhoods in Los Angeles and Chicago, joined to violent crime and American Community Survey data. First, ratings are nearly flat under coordinates for six of seven models, while names carry most between neighborhood variation and are moderately calibrated to violent crime; only at frontier scale does the coordinate channel show appreciable variation. Second, names lower safety ratings more for neighborhoods with higher shares of the locally dominant marginalized group (percent Black in Chicago, percent Hispanic in Los Angeles), and this name effect tracks demographic share in all seven models and both cities. In Los Angeles, where demographic share and crime are more separable, the effect survives controls for crime and income and is confirmed by crime-matched pairs. An enforcement-elasticity analysis further shows that over-caution tracks near-fully-reported homicide rather than discretionary, deployment-driven offenses. Third, the effect scales with geographic knowledge: models that better distinguish real neighborhoods apply more demographic stereotype to them. Because neighborhood names carry both genuine crime signal and demographic stereotype, removing names reduces both bias and accuracy. We discuss implications for deploying LLMs in advice and decision-support settings.

</details>


### [143] [Invocation-Level Reliability of Tool-Using Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26189)
*Afiya Noorain,Subhranshu Mohanty,Amritesh Banerjee,Abhijit Dasgupta*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies how tool-using language models fail in multi-step tasks, showing that early tool-use errors severely degrade later performance and that common exact-match evaluation metrics make key error-propagation parameters effectively unidentifiable. It proposes a new conditional-on-state scoring method that better separates intrinsic capability from error propagation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tool-using models can fail either by picking the wrong tool or by reasoning incorrectly with tools, and mistakes early in a multi-step process can silently corrupt subsequent steps. Existing evaluations based on exact-match to a single gold trajectory confound intrinsic model capability with error propagation effects and may misestimate how severe and recoverable errors are. The authors aim to quantify these two failure modes and to show that the dominant evaluation practice structurally prevents correct estimation of error propagation parameters.

Method: They evaluate five open-weight language models on contamination-free multi-step tool-use tasks with depths from 1 to 8 steps. They measure a correct-invocation rate that separates the quality of tool choice from downstream reasoning, under both teacher-forced (clean) contexts and the model’s own free-running contexts. They then analyze how error propagation parameters (severity of errors and probability of recovery) behave under standard exact-match scoring against a fixed gold trajectory, and demonstrate, analytically and empirically, that this scoring rule pins severity at its boundary and makes recovery unobservable. Finally, they introduce conditional-on-state scoring, which evaluates actions relative to the model’s current state rather than a hidden gold state, and apply it retrospectively to cached completions without additional inference cost.

Result: Empirically, by depth 6 in multi-step tasks, about 70% of each model’s performance relative to its clean teacher-forced capability is lost due to its own earlier mistakes (L6 ≈ 0.686 and 0.684 for two models). Under exact-match scoring, they observe that no poisoned (post-error) steps are judged correct (0 of 869), and no poisoned trajectories are seen as recovered (0 of 580), although a small nonzero number would be expected by chance. Fitting a propagation model nonetheless yields seemingly precise but incorrect parameter estimates (e.g., 0.92 and 0.73 for a parameter that is mathematically fixed at 1.0 by the scoring rule). Using their conditional-on-state scoring retrofit, they obtain interior, nonzero estimates of severity (increases of +0.149 and +0.316 compared to the boundary estimate of zero).

Conclusion: Multi-step tool-use performance in language models deteriorates sharply with depth mainly because of self-induced error propagation, not just lack of single-step competence. Widely-used exact-match, single-trajectory evaluation makes key error-propagation parameters—severity and recovery—effectively unidentifiable, yet can still produce misleadingly confident numeric fits. The authors identify the mechanism (post-divergence gold labels depending on hidden tool constants) and propose conditional-on-state scoring as a principled, zero-extra-cost remedy that yields more informative and realistic estimates of how errors propagate and how recoverable they are.

Abstract: Tool-using agents fail two ways: choosing the wrong tool, or forming wrong arguments, and an early failure of either kind can silently corrupt everything downstream. We measure a correct-invocation rate that separates the two, under both a clean teacher-forced context and the model's own free-running context, on five open-weight models over contamination-free multi-step tasks (depths 1-8). By depth 6, roughly 70% of a model's own clean-context capability is lost to its own earlier mistakes (L6 = 0.686, 0.684). Our central finding concerns the measurement itself. Under exact-match scoring against a fixed gold trajectory, a propagation model's severity and recovery parameters are not merely hard to estimate - they are fixed by the scoring rule. Severity is forced to its boundary (0 of 869 poisoned steps correct); recovery is structurally unobservable (0 of 580 poisoned steps returned on-track, against an expected 0.0058 by chance). Both follow from one mechanism: post-divergence, the gold value is generated by tool constants the model never sees, so it is information the model cannot derive. A fit run anyway returns 0.92 and 0.73 for a quantity that is exactly 1.000 - confident numbers for a parameter the scoring rule already determined. We give the mechanism and a remedy, conditional-on-state scoring, applied retrospectively to cached completions at zero additional cost, which un-pins severity to interior estimates excluding zero (+0.149, +0.316).

</details>


### [144] [Predicting Consequences and Reinforcing Navigation Policies with Latent World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26190)
*Zengmao Wang,Wei Gao,Shuhan Shen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose a latent world model for robot navigation that predicts which action sequences move the robot closer to the goal, without reconstructing raw observations, enabling effective policy learning from unlabeled videos and imagined rollouts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing world models for control typically reconstruct future observations or high-dimensional features, which is complex, computationally heavy, and not necessarily aligned with what matters for decision making (e.g., moving closer to a goal). There is a need for a more decision-centric world model that directly reasons about the usefulness of actions for navigation without requiring action labels or extra environment interaction.

Method: They introduce a Latent World Model (LWM) that operates in latent feature space and predicts action-conditioned compatibility scores, representing how much an action sequence moves the agent toward the goal. They exploit the correlation between spatial proximity and latent similarity so that the model evaluates action consequences directly in latent space. For training, they sample action sequences across trajectories and use counterfactual supervision to learn which sequences would lead the robot closer to the goal. The learned world model is then used to supervise policy learning from unlabeled videos and to improve the policy further through reinforcement learning via imagined rollouts fully within the model.

Result: On multiple real-world robot navigation datasets, their method surpasses prior world model and imitation learning baselines in three aspects: (1) prediction accuracy in the latent world model, (2) quality and performance of learned navigation policies, and (3) real-world robot navigation success. Their approach works without explicit action annotations or extra environment interaction.

Conclusion: A decision-focused latent world model that predicts action compatibility in latent space can effectively support robot navigation policy learning from unlabeled videos and imagination alone. By avoiding pixel-level reconstruction and using counterfactual training on action sequences, the approach yields stronger world models and better navigation policies than prior methods, while reducing annotation and interaction requirements.

Abstract: World models enable agents to reason about future outcomes and learn policies from their knowledge of state transition, but existing approaches primarily focus on reconstructing future observations or features, which introduces unnecessary complexity and limits their effectiveness for decision making. In this work, we propose a compatibility prediction Latent World Model (LWM) for robot navigation that predicts action-conditioned latent feature compatibility rather than reconstructing observations. Our key insight is that spatial proximity correlates with latent feature similarity, enabling action consequences to be evaluated directly in latent space. To support counterfactual training, our model leverages action sequences sampled across trajectories and learns to predict which sequences lead closer to the goal. Furthermore, we demonstrate how the learned world model can supervise policy learning from unlabeled video data and further improve policies through reinforcement learning entirely within the world model. This imagination-driven framework eliminates the need for action annotations and additional environment interaction. Extensive experiments on multiple real-world robot navigation datasets show that our approach significantly outperforms prior world model and imitation learning methods in prediction accuracy, policy learning, and real-world navigation performance. The code, pretrained models, and additional materials are available at https://wzm206.github.io/latent-world-model-nav.

</details>


### [145] [Structured Evidence Routing for Incident Risk Prediction from Multimodal Longitudinal EHRs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26191)
*Animesh Agarwal,Meysam Ghaffari,Nina Fatehi,Carlos Morato*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a structured evidence routing framework to predict incident disease risk from longitudinal EHRs, achieving performance comparable to strong supervised baselines while providing interpretable, patient-specific evidence trails.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Predicting new-onset diseases from longitudinal EHR data is difficult because relevant clinical signals are sparse, multimodal (labs, notes, codes, etc.), weak when considered individually, and scattered across irregular timelines. Existing methods either struggle with these complexities or act as black boxes with limited interpretability, especially in low-label or EHR few-shot (EHRSHOT) settings. The authors aim to build a system that can both match supervised performance and expose which pieces of the record support the prediction.

Method: They propose a router–predictor–reviewer workflow called structured evidence routing. First, a router processes the entire pre-index EHR and condenses it into a compact patient summary plus a set of disease-relevant “evidence slices” (structured subsets of the record). Next, a predictor uses these routed evidence slices to compute an incident risk estimate that is directly linked to specific pieces of evidence. Then, a reviewer module critiques or refines this prediction, presumably improving robustness and consistency. For benchmarking against supervised EHRSHOT methods, the routed summaries are paired with a supervised classifier readout. The authors also run internal ablation experiments to disable components (routing, lab evidence, task guidance, review) and quantify each part’s contribution.

Result: On five 1-year incident diagnosis prediction tasks, the proposed method attains AUROC values in the same range as strong supervised EHRSHOT baselines and remains competitive in AUPRC. The approach additionally yields a structured, patient-specific evidence trail for each prediction. Ablation studies indicate that the routing mechanism, incorporation of laboratory data, explicit task guidance, and the reviewer step each have measurable positive impact on performance.

Conclusion: Structured evidence routing can predict incident diagnoses from longitudinal EHRs with performance comparable to established supervised EHRSHOT baselines while providing interpretable, evidence-linked risk assessments. The router–predictor–reviewer design separates global record access from disease-specific reasoning and yields transparent evidence trails. Component-wise ablations suggest that each part of the pipeline—routing, lab features, task conditioning, and review—contributes meaningfully, supporting the framework as a promising direction for interpretable risk prediction in complex EHR settings.

Abstract: Incident risk prediction from longitudinal electronic health records (EHRs) is challenging because relevant signals are multimodal, weak in isolation, and distributed across irregular patient histories. We propose structured evidence routing, a router-predictor-reviewer workflow that separates full-record access from disease-specific assessment. The router organizes the complete pre-index EHR into a compact summary and targeted evidence slices; the predictor uses this evidence to form an evidence-linked risk assessment, which the reviewer critiques. For comparison with supervised EHRSHOT baselines, we pair the routed evidence summaries with a supervised classifier readout. Across five 1-year incident diagnosis tasks, our method reaches the AUROC range of established supervised EHRSHOT baselines and remains competitive on AUPRC, while exposing a patient-specific evidence trail. Internal pre-readout ablations further suggest that routing, laboratory evidence, task guidance, and review each contribute to performance.

</details>


### [146] [AffectOmni: RL-Verifiable People-Centric Grounded Affective Reasoning for Social and Art-Related Scenes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26193)
*Yibo Wang,Rui Yang,Jisheng Dang,Bimei Wang,Yitao Wu,Pengfei Cao,Wencan Zhang,Hong Peng,Bin Hu,Tat-Seng Chua*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AffectOmni is a GRPO-trained framework that improves multimodal LLM affective reasoning by enforcing people-centric, temporally structured evidence use and providing verifiable, pixel-level evidence traces.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current multimodal LLMs perform well on visual QA and scene understanding but rely on shortcuts for affective reasoning: they can answer correctly without genuinely attending to subtle human cues like micro-expressions and body language. This undermines interpretability, traceability, and external verification. Existing RL approaches mainly optimize for generic coherence rather than explicit use of human-related evidence, and LLM-as-a-judge rewards tend to be poorly discriminative due to clustered scores. The paper aims to build a system that not only improves affective reasoning accuracy but also forces models to ground their reasoning in human-centric, temporally aware evidence that can be externally audited.

Method: The authors propose AffectOmni, a framework trained with Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO). They design two specialized rewards: (1) People Focus, which encourages the model to select and reason over people-centric evidence (e.g., facial expressions, body posture); and (2) Temporal Order, which encourages reasoning that respects the temporal structure of events in multimodal input. They also introduce within-group comparative scoring to reduce score clustering and yield more discriminative reward signals compared to standard LLM-as-a-judge setups. For verifiability, they use a Thinking Summarizer module to transform the model's natural language rationales into explicit evidence instructions, which are then grounded to pixel-level regions using SAM3 segmentation, enabling an externally auditable mapping between text rationales and visual evidence without being part of the training loop.

Result: On three benchmarks—IntentBench, Daily Omni, and WorldSense—AffectOmni consistently outperforms open-source 7B-scale multimodal baselines. It achieves a 4.66% absolute improvement on emotion recognition tasks and a 14.29% gain on temporally sensitive tasks, demonstrating that enforcing people-focused and temporally structured evidence use improves affective reasoning performance while preserving or enhancing general multimodal understanding.

Conclusion: AffectOmni demonstrates that explicitly rewarding people-centric and temporally ordered evidence use, combined with comparative reward scoring, leads to better and more verifiable affective reasoning in multimodal LLMs. The framework not only boosts accuracy on emotion and temporally sensitive tasks but also produces auditable pixel-level evidence traces via SAM3, addressing shortcut behaviors and improving the transparency and reliability of affective reasoning. The approach suggests a practical path toward more trustworthy multimodal systems where human-related judgments are grounded in observable evidence rather than opaque shortcuts.

Abstract: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) achieve strong performance on VQA and scene understanding, yet affective reasoning remains vulnerable to shortcut behavior. Models may predict correct answers while neglecting people-centric cues such as micro expressions and body language, which weakens traceability and external verification. Prior reinforcement learning approaches mainly reward context or logical coherence without explicitly enforcing attention to human evidence. In addition, LLM as a Judge scoring often suffers from score clustering, which reduces reward discriminability. We propose AffectOmni, a GRPO trained framework for verifiable affective reasoning. AffectOmni introduces People Focus and Temporal Order rewards to encourage people-centric evidence selection and temporally structured reasoning, and it adopts within-group comparative scoring to produce more stable and discriminative reward signals. For verification, a Thinking Summarizer converts free form rationales into executable evidence instructions, which are grounded into pixel level evidence regions via SAM3 to provide an externally auditable interface outside the training loop. Experiments on IntentBench, Daily Omni, and WorldSense show consistent improvements over open source 7B scale baselines, including gains of 4.66% on emotion recognition and +14.29% on temporally sensitive tasks. Code is available at https://github.com/eliot127825-rgb/AffectOmni_nobody.

</details>


### [147] [Agentic AI for operating scientific instruments for nanoscale characterization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26198)
*Zahra Ayar,Marcos Penedo,Mahdi Mehdikhani,Nahid Hosseini,Prabhu Prasad Swain,Georg E. Fantner*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces an agentic-AI framework that safely automates key operational steps of atomic force microscopy using a tool-augmented large language model connected via MCP.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Operating an AFM demands constant expert decisions—from setting parameters to interpreting images—which limits accessibility, throughput, and reproducibility. Existing automation is fragmented, task-specific, and often requires retraining for each sample or imaging mode. There is a need for a general, safe, and flexible AI-based framework that can execute complex instrument workflows while keeping humans in control of experimental intent.

Method: The authors design a multi-agent framework built on the Model Context Protocol (MCP), connecting a general-purpose tool-augmented LLM to AFM instrument controls and post-processing tools. The system comprises three MCP-based agents: (1) AFM Messenger, which translates natural-language user instructions into validated instrument commands; (2) AFM Pilot, which evaluates AFM image quality using the LLM and adaptively tunes imaging parameters; and (3) AFM Doctor, which identifies image artifacts and applies transparent, pre-approved post-processing tools. A guarded execution layer with ambiguity checks ensures safe hardware operation. The framework is benchmarked against fine-tuned and off-the-shelf tool-using models.

Result: The guarded execution layer eliminates wrong-command execution, reducing it to zero in benchmarks, independent of raw model capability. In live AFM experiments across different samples, AFM Pilot achieves image quality, iteration counts, and tuning times comparable to expert human operators, with no statistically significant difference. The method generalizes across sample types and imaging modes without task-specific retraining, due to the LLM-based image assessment strategy.

Conclusion: The work demonstrates that agentic AI, implemented via MCP-connected LLM-based agents, can safely automate the executable components of AFM operation—command translation, image-based tuning, and post-processing—while leaving experimental intent to humans. The guarded execution layer is key to safety, and the general LLM-based assessment enables broad applicability without retraining. This establishes a practical route toward safe, AI-driven operation of complex scientific instruments.

Abstract: Operating a scientific instrument such as an atomic force microscope (AFM) requires continuous expert decision-making. A trained user defines the experimental intent, translates it into instrument commands, assesses incoming data, adjusts imaging parameters, and post-processes the final image. Existing automation usually addresses only parts of this workflow through hard-coded routines, task-specific controllers, or trained machine-learning models. Here we present an agentic-AI framework that operates the executable part of the AFM workflow using a general-purpose, tool-augmented large language model connected to instrument functions through the Model Context Protocol (MCP). The framework consists of 3 MCP-based agents: AFM Messenger converts natural-language instructions into checked instrument commands; AFM Pilot assesses image quality through a large language model (LLM) and, if necessary, adapts imaging parameters; and AFM Doctor diagnoses image artifacts and applies transparent post-processing from a pre-approved tool set. Because the language model performs image assessment rather than a fixed scalar objective or external optimizer, the same strategy can be applied across sample types and imaging modes without specific retraining. Safe hardware operation is enforced through an ambiguity check layer before execution. Benchmarking against fine-tuned and off-the-shelf tool-using models shows that this guarded execution layer, rather than model capability alone, reduces wrong-command execution to zero. In live experiments on different samples, AFM Pilot matched expert operators in image quality, iteration count, and tuning time, with no significant difference. These results demonstrate a safe route to agentic operation of scientific instruments, where experimental intent remains human-defined while command execution, image-based tuning, and post-processing are delegated to AI agents.

</details>


### [148] [Benchmarking AI Agents for Hardware Design Automation via MCP Tool Calling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26199)
*Leonardo Liparulo,Francesco Pierri*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies whether locally deployed open-source LLM agents can reliably operate a hardware design tool via function calls, builds a realistic benchmark using an MCP server, and evaluates multiple models and agent configurations to derive practical deployment guidelines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Industry hardware design workflows rely on complex, stateful tool sequences (creating components, wiring ports, etc.), and confidentiality often prevents sending designs to cloud APIs. Many teams want to use local LLMs as autonomous agents to drive these tools, but it is unclear how reliable they are, how to configure them, and what design patterns work best in such stateful, expert-defined workflows.

Method: The authors build an MCP server that emulates the state, dependencies, and operations of a proprietary hardware design environment for embedded systems. They then design a benchmark of representative interaction patterns: single operations, multi-step dependency chains, handling invalid requests, coping with noisy/misspelled prompts, and coordinating across multiple tool servers. Seven open-source LLMs are evaluated as agents, systematically varying: (1) system prompts, (2) detail level of tool descriptions, (3) how much past context is retained, and (4) whether a single agent or a multi-agent decomposition is used. Performance is measured via coverage of expected tool calls and failure behaviors.

Result: Strong open-source models can achieve nearly complete coverage of the expected tool calls on the benchmark tasks, showing that realistic hardware workflows can in principle be automated locally. However, success rates are highly sensitive to configuration: rich tool documentation reduces errors; few-shot examples sometimes cause agents to become overly cautious and not act; retaining too much interaction history degrades performance for constrained models; multi-agent setups can improve reliability for weaker models or long sessions but also introduce extra tool calls and overhead.

Conclusion: Local LLM agents are viable for automating complex, stateful hardware design workflows, but their reliability is configuration-dependent. Clear and comprehensive tool descriptions are consistently beneficial, whereas naive few-shot prompting and excessive context retention can be harmful, especially for smaller models. Multi-agent decompositions can mitigate weaknesses at the expense of efficiency. The paper offers concrete design guidance for practitioners deploying local LLM agents in confidential, tool-heavy hardware design environments.

Abstract: We ask whether AI agents powered by locally deployed large language models can reliably automate expert-defined hardware design workflows in an industry-realistic tool-calling setting. In these environments, engineers issue repetitive, dependency-ordered operations---such as creating components, adding ports, and wiring connections---through specialised tools. Confidentiality constraints on component specifications and naming conventions often preclude hosted proprietary APIs, motivating the use of locally deployed models. To study this setting, we build a Model Context Protocol (MCP) server that reproduces the state and dependency logic of a proprietary hardware design tool used in embedded system development and construct a benchmark covering single-operation edits, multi-step dependency chains, invalid requests, misspelled prompts, and multi-server tool contexts. We evaluate seven open-source models comparing pipeline choices including system prompts, tool-description detail, context scope, and single-agent versus multi-agent architectures. Results show that strong models can achieve near-complete expected-call coverage on the benchmarked workflows, but reliability depends strongly on both task structure and agent configuration. Comprehensive tool descriptions consistently reduce failures, few-shot prompting can cause severe inaction for some models, cumulative context harms constrained models, and multi-agent decomposition helps weak workers or long sessions at the cost of additional calls. These findings provide practical guidance for deploying local LLM agents in stateful hardware design environments.

</details>


### [149] [GameWAM: A World Action Model for Video Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26200)
*Yuncheng Guo,Zhanqiu Zhang,Yiwen Guo,Weijia Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces GameWAM, a unified world-action model that jointly generates future game visuals and native keyboard-mouse actions for closed-loop video game and GUI control, achieving competitive task success with fewer actions and revealing a generative control failure mode (LASI).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing game agents can act in video games but typically lack explicit modeling of world dynamics, while interactive game world models can predict future visuals but are not themselves control policies. There is a gap in unifying these two directions into a single model that can both understand and influence complex, first-person game environments using native controls, especially under open-ended and long-horizon interaction. The authors aim to fill this gap with a practical world-action model tailored for real gameplay and GUI control, and to better understand generative control behaviors and their failure modes.

Method: Propose GameWAM, a World-Action Model that jointly models future observations and actions via parallel generative streams for vision and control, using block-causal conditioning and flow matching. They create synchronized gameplay and GUI trajectories to train the joint model. To tackle heterogeneous native controls (gameplay vs GUI), the model predicts a mode at each step and uses mode-specific action distributions plus a normalization scheme for continuous controls. For long-horizon tasks, they use block-cycle control, in which the model predicts actions beyond a short committed horizon, executes only a small prefix, and replans from new observations; within-cycle context and cross-cycle hierarchical history maintain temporal continuity.

Result: In experiments on video game tasks, GameWAM achieves competitive task success compared with existing agents while using fewer native actions, indicating more efficient control. The analysis also reveals Low-Frequency Action Source Imprinting (LASI): low-frequency components of the latent action sampling source can systematically bias coarse camera motion even when the conditioning is fixed, highlighting a sensitivity and potential weakness of generative action sampling.

Conclusion: GameWAM demonstrates that a unified world-action model can support closed-loop gameplay and GUI control with efficient native action usage and competitive performance. The work shows that joint generation of visual futures and executable actions is viable for complex, first-person game environments, enabled by mode-aware control modeling and block-cycle planning. Additionally, the discovery of LASI exposes a new failure mode in generative control related to low-frequency action source components, suggesting the need for more robust sampling or model designs to mitigate such source sensitivities.

Abstract: Modern video games combine first-person perception, rapid visual changes, persistent world state, and heterogeneous native controls. Existing game agents map visual and task context directly to actions but lack explicit world dynamics modeling, whereas interactive game world models predict visual futures from supplied actions but do not serve as task policies. World-Action Models (WAMs) unify these objectives, but remain largely unexplored under the dynamics and open-ended interaction of video games. We introduce GameWAM, to our knowledge the first WAM for native closed-loop gameplay and GUI control. GameWAM jointly generates future visual observations and executable keyboard-mouse trajectories through parallel visual and action generative processes with block-causal conditioning and flow matching. To support joint world-action learning, we construct synchronized gameplay and GUI trajectories. To handle heterogeneous native control, GameWAM predicts a gameplay/GUI mode at each action step and generates actions with mode-specific prediction distributions and continuous-action normalization. For long-horizon interaction, block-cycle control predicts beyond the committed horizon, executes only a short action prefix, and replans from new observations, while fine-grained within-cycle context and hierarchical cross-cycle history preserve temporal continuity. Experiments demonstrate competitive task success with fewer executed native actions than the compared agents. We further uncover Low-Frequency Action Source Imprinting (LASI), in which low-frequency components of the sampled action source systematically steer coarse generated camera motion under fixed conditioning, revealing a source-sensitivity failure mode in generative control. Project page is available at https://yunncheng.github.io/GameWAM/.

</details>


### [150] [Same Model, Different Harness: Different Coding-Agent Results](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26218)
*Sydney Lewis*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies how much the non-model part of a coding agent (the harness) affects benchmark results, showing that simply changing the harness—while keeping the model and tasks fixed—can significantly change performance, especially under tight context limits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Coding-agent benchmarks are usually treated as measuring the underlying model, but a coding agent is actually a combination of model plus harness (which controls context, tools, and workflow). The authors want to understand whether differences in the harness alone can materially change outcomes on coding benchmarks, which has implications for how we evaluate and compare models and agents.

Method: They fix the model and tasks and vary only the harness configuration. Specifically, they compare two versions of the same harness on three coding benchmarks: a control that always feeds the full chronological conversation to the model, and a treatment that keeps the same interaction record but mechanically shortens older tool outputs as the context fills and reacts to repeated or stalled work. They run these configurations under tight and wide context-window conditions, measure mean per-task fail-to-pass fraction (F2PF) and number of complete solutions across benchmarks (SWE-bench Verified, SWE-bench Pro, and FeatureBench), and repeat some experiments across multiple model architectures without retuning the harness.

Result: Under tight context constraints, the treatment harness consistently reduces fail-to-pass fraction and increases complete solutions across three pressure comparisons. For example, on a 169-task SWE-bench Verified cohort with a 20,480-token window and 480-second time limit, the treatment increases mean per-task F2PF from 28% to 49% and complete solutions from 43 to 72. The same frozen treatment improves both metrics for three other models with different designs on the same cohort. In wide-window comparisons with Qwen3.6, control and treatment perform similarly on SWE-bench Verified and Pro, but the treatment yields higher F2PF on FeatureBench and uses fewer prompt tokens per turn on wide-window Verified.

Conclusion: The harness design—how context is managed, how tool results are truncated, and how the agent responds to stalled work—can significantly change what fixed model weights achieve on coding benchmarks. Therefore, evaluations should treat the combination of model plus harness as the unit under test, and reported results should make harness details explicit; otherwise comparisons across agents or models can be misleading.

Abstract: A coding agent combines a model with a harness, which decides what the model sees, which tools it can use, and how the work continues. We ask whether changing the harness changes the result when the model and task stay fixed. We compare two configurations of the same harness on three coding benchmarks. The control supplies the full conversation in time order, while the treatment keeps the same record but mechanically shortens older tool results as the context fills and responds to repeated or stalled work.
  Under tight context, the treatment raises mean per-task fail-to-pass fraction (F2PF) in all three pressure comparisons and increases complete solutions on SWE-bench Verified and SWE-bench Pro. The tight-window Verified comparison uses 169 tasks, a 20,480-token window, and a fixed 480-second attempt endpoint; on this cohort, treatment raises mean per-task F2PF from 28 percent to 49 percent and complete solutions from 43 to 72. Without model-specific retuning, the same frozen treatment also raises both endpoints on the same cohort for three additional models with different designs. In the wide-window Qwen3.6 comparisons, observed arm outcomes are close on Verified and Pro, while FeatureBench retains a higher mean per-task F2PF under treatment. On the wide-window Verified cohort, treatment also serves fewer prompt tokens per turn. Because changing the harness changed what unchanged model weights could accomplish, coding-agent evaluations should treat the model and harness together as the tested solver.

</details>


### [151] [Agent Mesh: Reliability Primitives for Non-Idempotent Agent Delegation - Identity Adequacy and Evidence Adequacy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26225)
*Mazhar Shaikh,Anurag Rajkumar Bombarde,Harshal Pathak*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Study of failures in an agentic software-delivery platform using service-mesh-style reliability primitives, showing their assumptions break down and proposing new delegation-level primitives.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous agents are being orchestrated with service-mesh primitives (retry, timeout, circuit breaking), but it's unclear whether the assumptions behind these primitives hold for complex, multi-step, tool-using agentic workflows. The authors want to understand real-world failure modes and reliability issues when these primitives are applied to agent-based software delivery.

Method: Perform a failure study on a production agentic software-delivery platform: analyze 147 incidents across 81 runs, measuring costs, reproducing failures via mutation proofs, and examining where service-mesh assumptions are violated (error-rate visibility, progress signals, idempotency, fault routing, enforcement correctness). Identify cross-cutting causes and formalize concepts such as identity adequacy and evidence adequacy.

Result: The study finds that all three key assumptions of service-mesh primitives (about observability of errors, meaning of progress, and idempotency/statelessness) are violated in practice for agentic workflows. Consequences include invisible failure loops, progress metrics that force false circuit breaks, accumulation of state/events that make recovery impossible, misrouted failures that involve unrelated components, and enforcement layers blocking correct work, sometimes at very high cost. A core pattern is inadequate identity and inadequate evidence for making reliability decisions.

Conclusion: The authors argue that reliability for agentic systems should be enforced at the delegation level instead of per-message, and must be based on adequate identity and evidence. They derive seven new reliability primitives tailored to delegations, and outline a controlled evaluation they plan for future work to validate these primitives more systematically.

Abstract: Autonomous agents increasingly perform bounded software tasks under an orchestrator that retries, resumes, and budgets them. The machinery such orchestrators reach for is the service mesh's: retry, timeout, and error-rate circuit breaking. We report a failure study of a production agentic software-delivery platform over 147 numbered incidents spanning 81 runs, each with a measured cost and, in most cases, a mutation proof reproducing the failure. All three assumptions those primitives rest on are violated in practice, and we quantify the consequences: a loop of fifty-four consecutive successful tool calls no error-rate breaker could see; a progress signal constant by construction, guaranteeing a false trip on the third repair round and driving one run from six of six components to three; twenty-one events accumulated across six invocations of one delegation, making a correct, idempotent component unwinnable; a misrouted failure that woke five components for a two-component fault, leaving three bystanders regressing working code; and twelve incidents in which the enforcement layer blocked correct work, the most expensive costing 107 agent turns and zero accepted writes. We find one cross-cutting cause and its dual. Identity adequacy: in five separate subsystems an identity that failed to discriminate produced a confident wrong answer, and two of them derived the corrective rule independently. Evidence adequacy: a reliability decision may be taken only on evidence capable of moving, attributable to what it measures, and deterministic under identical conditions. From the findings we derive seven reliability primitives whose enforcement unit is the delegation rather than the message, and specify the controlled evaluation the study motivates but does not constitute.

</details>


### [152] [LLM Agents for Time-Series: A Survey](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26226)
*Yilong Chen,Xiao Qin,Chenghao Liu,Liang Wu,Noelle I. Samia,Kaize Ding*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Survey of LLM-based agents for time-series tasks, organized by problem types instead of technical components, summarizing designs, datasets, performance, and open challenges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based agents are being rapidly applied to time-series tasks, but existing works make diverse design choices and are hard to compare because they are usually described in terms of technical tricks rather than the underlying time-series problems. A problem-driven overview is needed to clarify how different task requirements influence agent design and to guide future system development.

Method: The paper proposes a task-oriented taxonomy for LLM-based time-series agents, grouping systems into four categories: (1) forecasting and reasoning, (2) augmentation and synthesis, (3) anomaly detection and diagnosis, and (4) decision support. For each category, it analyzes design choices in agent architecture, tool integration, and memory mechanisms, and collects information on datasets/environments and reported performance in comparable settings.

Result: The authors compile and organize existing LLM-agent systems for time-series across the four proposed categories, highlight patterns in architectural and tool-use choices per task type, and provide side-by-side comparisons of performance where tasks and settings align. They also catalog representative datasets and benchmarks used in this area.

Conclusion: Viewing LLM-based time-series agents through a task/problem lens offers clearer guidance for system design than focusing on isolated techniques. The survey distills common design patterns, highlights which setups work for which problem types, and points out underexplored tasks and methodological gaps, thereby outlining directions for future research on LLM agents for time-series data.

Abstract: LLM-based agents are increasingly being developed for time-series problems, but their design choices vary substantially across task settings. This survey adopts a problem-driven taxonomy that organizes these systems by the time-series problems they address rather than by isolated technical components. We group existing systems into four categories: forecasting and reasoning, augmentation and synthesis, anomaly detection and diagnosis, and decision support. Within each category, we examine how task requirements shape agent architecture, tool use, and memory design. We further summarize representative datasets and environments, and compare reported model performance under shared or closely related settings. Overall, this survey offers a task-oriented guide to designing LLM-based agents for time-series problems and identifies open gaps for future work.

</details>


### [153] [The Reasoning Tax: Token Economics of LLM Reasoning Across Task Types and Deployment Contexts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26235)
*Sachin Gopal Wani,Ajay Dholakia,David Ellison*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes Token Economy Score (TES), a metric to evaluate when extra reasoning tokens in LLMs are worth their cost, and finds that reasoning should be enabled selectively based on task type, effort level, and deployment context.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current benchmarks mostly focus on accuracy and ignore the economic trade-off of using reasoning-capable LLMs, which often consume many more tokens and cost significantly more. Practitioners lack a principled way to decide when extended chain-of-thought style reasoning is actually cost-effective compared with simpler non-reasoning modes. The paper aims to fill this gap by introducing a marginal efficiency metric that aligns with deployment considerations.

Method: The authors define Token Economy Score (TES), which measures marginal accuracy gain of a reasoning model over a non-reasoning baseline, divided by the multiplier in generated tokens. They introduce two TES variants: paired TES for model families with an explicit reasoning toggle, and approximated TES for frontier models lacking a direct non-reasoning counterpart. They empirically evaluate TES across 151 model-benchmark runs on seven benchmarks covering math, code, science reasoning, instruction following, expert knowledge, knowledge recall, and research-level physics. They analyze TES along three axes: (1) task structure and its relation to reasoning efficiency, (2) how TES changes as reasoning effort increases within model families, and (3) how deployment context and cost structure (via Reasoning Cost Share and Deployment Cost Multiplier) affect the economic viability of reasoning-heavy inference.

Result: The study finds that task structure is a stronger predictor of reasoning efficiency than nominal task difficulty. Sequential, inference-chain-heavy tasks (e.g., AIME 2025, LiveCodeBench) exhibit high TES, meaning extra reasoning tokens are cost-effective. In contrast, difficult knowledge-recall benchmarks like MMLU-Pro show low TES, indicating that added reasoning often doesn’t justify its token cost. The authors observe systematic diminishing returns as reasoning effort increases, including regimes where more thinking both costs more and reduces accuracy. RCS analysis reveals that for many workloads, most inference spending goes to internal reasoning tokens rather than user-visible output. DCM analysis shows that on-premises deployment and different pricing assumptions can substantially change whether heavy reasoning is economically justified.

Conclusion: Reasoning modes in LLMs should not be treated as universally beneficial. Instead, they should be enabled selectively based on task structure, the marginal efficiency captured by TES, and the deployment’s cost environment. The paper recommends a benchmarking-driven model-selection and configuration strategy: tune when and how much reasoning to use per task type and deployment context, recognizing diminishing returns and cases where extra reasoning can harm both accuracy and economics.

Abstract: Accuracy-only benchmarking of reasoning-capable large language models misses a central deployment question: when do extended thinking tokens earn their cost? We introduce the Token Economy Score (TES), a marginal benchmarking metric that measures the accuracy gain of a reasoning model over a non-reasoning baseline, normalized by the generated-token multiplier. We define paired and approximated TES variants for model families with reasoning toggles and frontier models without direct non-reasoning counterparts. We then conduct an empirical benchmarking analysis across 151 model-benchmark evaluation runs on seven benchmarks spanning mathematics, code generation, science reasoning, instruction following, expert knowledge, knowledge recall, and research-level physics. The analysis examines three deployment-facing dimensions: which task structures yield positive marginal reasoning efficiency, how increasing reasoning effort changes TES within model families, and how deployment context changes economic viability. Results show that task structure predicts reasoning efficiency better than nominal difficulty: sequential inferencechain tasks such as AIME 2025 and LiveCodeBench show high TES, while knowledge-recall tasks such as MMLU-Pro show low TES despite their difficulty. We also find systematic diminishing returns at higher reasoning effort levels, including cases where additional thinking reduces accuracy. Finally, Reasoning Cost Share (RCS) shows that inference spend is often dominated by internal thinking, while Deployment Cost Multiplier (DCM) shows how on-premises deployment can change the economics of otherwise costly reasoning workloads. These findings support a benchmarking-driven model-selection rule: enable reasoning selectively by task type, effort level, and deployment context rather than treating it as a universally beneficial mode.

</details>


### [154] [6.5% of the Neuro-Symbolic Literature Can Be Reproduced from Its Published Artifacts, a Six-Stage Audit Framework and First Instantiation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26236)
*Brandon Colelough,Vladimir Martirosyan,Ishan Tamrakar,William Regli,Aditya Kumar,Anh N. Nhu,Dhruv Dubey,Raj Ambavane,Haowei Deng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes and applies a six-stage framework to audit the reproducibility of neuro-symbolic AI research, finding substantial gaps between “code available” claims and actually reproducible results.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reproducibility is a core requirement for reliable scientific progress, yet many empirical computer science papers—especially in emerging areas like neuro-symbolic AI—are difficult or impossible to reproduce in practice. Existing discussions of reproducibility are often anecdotal or limited to single papers or small samples, making it hard to quantify how widespread the problem is and at which stages (e.g., code, data, environment) it occurs. The authors aim to systematically assess reproducibility across an entire subdomain and to create a generalizable process that others can replicate for different fields.

Method: The authors design a six-stage audit framework and instantiate it for the neuro-symbolic AI literature. Stage one collects and deduplicates literature records (starting from 5,497 down to 2,479 unique records). Stage two performs title and abstract screening, then full-text screening, to retain only empirical NSAI papers with quantitative evaluations and accessible full texts (ending with 1,304 eligible records). Stage three searches for publicly verifiable code artifacts for each eligible paper and builds an artifact inventory, yielding 455 papers with usable code. Subsequent stages attempt bounded reruns of these artifacts to reproduce results, tracking whether runs succeed or fail as well as whether failures are due to missing code, missing non-code artifacts (e.g., data, configuration, models), or unusable repositories. The audit runs over multiple years to capture realistic conditions of long-term reproducibility.

Result: Out of 1,304 eligible empirical NSAI papers, 849 had no verifiable public code artifacts, leaving 455 with identifiable code. Of these, bounded reruns fully or partially reproduced 85 studies, corresponding to 6.52% of the eligible corpus and 18.68% of attempted reruns. Reproduction attempts were frequently blocked: 321 failures were attributed to missing non-code artifacts (such as datasets, pre-trained models, configuration files, or environment specifications) and 42 to missing or unusable code repositories despite nominal declarations that code was available. These quantitative results demonstrate that most NSAI research is not practically reproducible under realistic auditing constraints.

Conclusion: The study concludes that neuro-symbolic AI research has a pronounced and measurable reproducibility deficit. Even when papers claim that code is available, the absence of critical non-code artifacts and the fragility or inaccessibility of code repositories often prevent successful reruns. To address this, the authors advocate for stronger community and publisher policies: empirical NSAI papers should be required, at submission time, to provide complete, versioned, and permanently archived artifact bundles that include code, data (where legally possible), configuration, and environment details. The proposed six-stage framework is presented as a reusable template for auditing reproducibility in other computer science subdomains, supporting more systematic monitoring and improvement of research transparency and reliability.

Abstract: We present a six-stage framework for auditing the reproducibility of scientific claims across a research literature within the computer science domain, and instantiate our framework for the neuro-symbolic AI (NSAI) subdomain. Instantiating the framework on the NSAI subdomain produced a multi-year audit. Stage one retrieved 5,497 records and removed 3,018 duplicates. Stage two screened the 2,479 unique records at title and abstract, identifying 1,365 self-identified NSAI records, then removed a further 61 at full text for off-topic, non-research, no-quantitative-evaluation, or inaccessible-full-text reasons. Stage three sought a verifiable public code artifact for each of the 1,304 eligible records and found none for 849, leaving 455 to enter the artifact inventory and bounded rerun of stages four and five. We fully or partially reproduced 85 studies, 6.52% of the eligible corpus and 18.68% of attempted reruns. We found that 321 attempted reruns were blocked by missing non- code artifacts and 42 by missing or unusable code repositories. These figures quantify a persistent reproducibility deficit that survives even nominal "code available" declarations, and signal the need for enforced, versioned, and permanently archived artifact bundles in future NSAI publications. We argue that empirical NSAI papers should be required at submission time to provide complete, versioned, and permanently archived artifact bundles.

</details>


### [155] [SKILL.state: Scalable Long-Horizon Agent Skills](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26263)
*Sanket Badhe,Priyanka Tiwari,Jonghyun Chung*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces SKILL.state, a runtime that replaces growing chat history with a concise, mutable execution state to make LLM agents more accurate and efficient over long tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents executing long, complex procedures currently rely on ever-growing conversational histories of observations, actions, and reasoning. This leads to latency, high token costs, and context poisoning, where old or irrelevant information harms performance. A better mechanism is needed to support long-horizon skills without unbounded prompt growth.

Method: Design a runtime architecture (SKILL.state) where, at each step, the LLM receives: (1) a fixed skill specification, (2) the current structured execution state, and (3) the latest observation. The model proposes a state update, intermediate reasoning is used only transiently, and once the updated state is validated, the reasoning is discarded. This keeps the prompt size bounded and shifts from append-only logs to an explicit, mutable state representation. The approach is evaluated across multiple datasets, model types, and execution environments.

Result: SKILL.state yields higher task accuracy compared with traditional append-only history runtimes, while also using substantially fewer tokens overall. This shows better effectiveness and efficiency in long-running agent tasks.

Conclusion: Making execution state explicit, structured, and mutable—rather than relying on growing conversation histories—provides an architecture-agnostic way to scale LLM agents to long-horizon skills, improving both performance and token efficiency.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) increasingly act as autonomous agents executing complex, long-running procedural skills. Existing agent runtimes maintain execution by continually appending observations, actions, and intermediate reasoning traces to an ever-growing conversation history, causing latency degradation and context-poisoning failures over long horizons.
