We present SKILL.state, a runtime architecture that replaces append-only conversational history with an explicit, mutable execution state. At each execution step, the model receives only the immutable skill specification, the current structured execution state, and the latest observation. Intermediate reasoning is discarded immediately after producing a validated state update, preventing prompt growth with execution history. Across diverse datasets, models, and execution environments, SKILL.state improves task accuracy while substantially reducing cumulative token consumption. Our results demonstrate that explicit execution state is an effective and architecture-agnostic abstraction for scalable long-horizon agent skills.

</details>


### [156] [Assessing mentalization in humans and large language models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26291)
*Aamir Sohail,Xintong Zhong,Arkady Konovalov,Patricia L. Lockwood,Lei Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper evaluates whether large language models (LLMs) can not only pass theory-of-mind tests but also use mentalization to guide adaptive, strategic behavior in economic games, comparing them to humans via cognitive computational modeling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing work shows that LLMs can solve explicit theory-of-mind tasks, but it is unclear if they can deploy similar capacities in interactive, strategic settings where success depends on modeling other agents’ beliefs and sophistication. The authors want to understand whether LLMs genuinely exhibit mentalization-like processes and how these compare across different model families and to humans.

Method: The authors use two economic games that require recursive reasoning about others (mentalization). They instantiate individual LLM agents from four families (DeepSeek, GPT-4.1, GPT-5, Gemini 2.0 Flash), pit them against opponents of varying levels of strategic sophistication, and apply cognitive computational models to infer the latent reasoning strategies (e.g., recursive depth of thinking). They additionally test a “strategic prompting” procedure intended to elicit more sophisticated reasoning, and they benchmark all LLM results against behavior from a human participant sample.

Result: Across both games, LLMs show behavioral patterns and model-inferred strategies consistent with mentalizing, but these capacities vary substantially by provider and model size. Strategic prompting generally boosts LLM performance by shifting them toward more sophisticated reasoning, although the size of this benefit depends on the task. GPT-5 agents, in particular, adjust their depth of recursive reasoning to match increasingly sophisticated opponents and achieve performance levels that surpass those of human participants.

Conclusion: The study concludes that many modern LLMs exhibit mentalization-like behavior in strategic interactive tasks, with distinct capability profiles across model families and sizes. Cognitive computational modeling provides a rigorous framework to quantify these abilities and to compare humans and machines, revealing that some LLMs (notably GPT-5 in this work) can flexibly adapt their reasoning depth and outperform humans in these specific economic-game settings.

Abstract: Mentalization - the ability to infer others' beliefs and intentions to guide one's own choices - is a key cognitive function underlying human social interactions. Large language models (LLMs) demonstrate behaviour consistent with humans on theory-of-mind tasks, yet whether these models can guide adaptive behaviour through mentalization is unknown. Here we use two economic games with cognitive computational modeling to uncover the latent strategies underlying mentalization in LLMs. We tested individual LLM agents across four model families, DeepSeek, GPT-4.1, GPT-5 and Gemini 2.0 Flash (N = 2,099), against opponents of varying sophistication and examined whether a prompting strategy designed to elicit strategic reasoning improved performance. We benchmarked results against human participants (N = 251) as a comparative measure. Across both games, LLMs showed clear behavioural and computational signatures of mentalizing that differed markedly by model provider and size. Strategic prompting generally improved performance by inducing more sophisticated reasoning, yet the extent of the benefit differed across the two tasks. Last, GPT-5 agents flexibly adapted their recursive depth of reasoning to increasingly sophisticated opponents, demonstrating superior performance to human participants. Collectively, we demonstrate different capacities for mentalization across LLMs, and highlight cognitive computational modeling as a formal method for assessing comparative intelligence across humans and machines.

</details>


### [157] [Approved Too Late: Verdict Staleness in LLM-Guarded Self-Adaptive Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26306)
*Ilai Shraga,Roei Eshel,Lior Gorelik*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies how long LLM-based safety approvals for self-adaptive systems remain valid, identifies high rates of verdict staleness, and proposes a Freshness-Bounded Shield that predicts a safe validity horizon and greatly reduces expired approvals.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In self-adaptive systems using LLM guardrails, a safety or correctness approval can become invalid between the time it is issued and the time the system acts, due to system and environment dynamics. This time-of-check to time-of-use gap creates hidden safety hazards that are not addressed by typical static guardrail designs. The authors aim to formalize and measure this problem—verdict freshness—and to design mechanisms that ensure approvals remain valid when actually used, without requiring detailed models of the underlying system dynamics.

Method: The authors define and distinguish three quantitative notions related to verdict freshness: (1) all-candidate verdict change under fixed-action replay (how often an approval would flip if re-evaluated after a time shift while holding actions fixed), (2) oracle-labeled approval expiry along recorded closed-loop trajectories (how often previously approved actions later prove unsafe under ground-truth labels), and (3) judge-conditioned use-time invalidity (how often LLM-judge-based approvals are wrong at use time). They evaluate these measures across five reproducible self-adaptive system environments, using LLM guardrails as judges. They then propose the Freshness-Bounded Shield (FBS), which uses each approval’s safe-side margin and recent feature volatility to estimate a validity horizon without relying on an explicit plant-dynamics model. FBS is evaluated by applying it to approval streams and measuring how much it reduces oracle-labeled approval expiry while using fixed, documented hyperparameters.

Result: Empirical measurements show that verdicts change frequently over time: for a common replay shift of eight simulator steps, all-candidate verdict-change rates range from 5.3% to 48.4% across five SAS environments. Oracle-based analysis indicates that a nontrivial fraction (3.4–24.7%) of approvals expire by the time they are used. Applying the Freshness-Bounded Shield, using fixed settings provided in the artifact, reduces oracle-labeled approval-expiry rates down to 0–1.8% at the same time shift. An additional audit across four LLM judges demonstrates that every approval stream exhibits nonzero judge-conditioned use-time invalidity, confirming that staleness is not limited to a particular judge configuration.

Conclusion: LLM guardrail approvals in self-adaptive systems are often not fresh—they can become invalid between check and use time, creating TOCTOU safety risks. The authors formalize this as a verdict-freshness problem, provide empirical evidence of substantial verdict-change and expiry rates, and demonstrate that a simple, model-free mechanism—the Freshness-Bounded Shield—can significantly reduce expired approvals by estimating and enforcing a validity horizon per approval. They encapsulate the requirement as a freshness contract: every approval must be correct when issued and remain valid when applied, suggesting that future guardrail designs and evaluations should explicitly account for temporal validity, not just instantaneous correctness.

Abstract: A large language model (LLM) guardrail for a self-adaptive system (SAS) may issue an approval that is correct at check time but stale by actuation. This creates an Execute-stage time-of-check to time-of-use (TOCTOU) hazard. We study verdict freshness: whether a guardrail verdict remains valid when used. We distinguish three quantities that answer different questions: all-candidate verdict change under fixed-action replay, oracle-labeled approval expiry on recorded closed-loop trajectories, and judge-conditioned use-time invalidity. Across five reproducible SAS environments, all-candidate verdict-change rates span 5.3-48.4% at a common replay shift of eight simulator steps. We introduce the Freshness-Bounded Shield (FBS), which estimates each approval's validity horizon from its safe-side margin and recent feature volatility, without an explicit plant-dynamics model. Using fixed settings documented in the artifact, FBS reduces oracle-labeled approval-expiry rates from 3.4-24.7% to 0-1.8% at the same shift. A separate audit of four LLM judges finds nonzero judge-conditioned use-time invalidity in every approval stream. We formulate a freshness contract: every approval must be correct at check time and remain valid at use time.

</details>


### [158] [FaithSieve: Fine-Grained Evaluation of Math Proofs with Faithful Formal Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26310)
*Ziyu Wang,Qiming Dai,Yishan Wu,Zaiwen Wen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FaithSieve is a Lean-assisted framework that decomposes natural-language math proofs into small units, formally verifies them with semantic checks, and significantly improves automatic localization of the first logical error in model-generated proofs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language models can now generate long mathematical proofs, but existing automatic evaluators are unreliable: they rely on natural-language judgments that miss local gaps and cannot rigorously verify correctness. Formal theorem provers like Lean offer rigor, but there are mismatches between informal text and formal statements: provers can ‘solve around’ a local flaw by proving something different or broader, and auto-formalization can drift from the original intent. The paper is motivated by the need for a trustworthy, fine-grained evaluation framework that can detect and localize early logical errors in informal, natural-language proofs while still leveraging the rigor of formal proof assistants.

Method: The authors design FaithSieve, a hybrid pipeline that combines LLM analysis with Lean-based formal verification. First, the framework decomposes a coarse natural-language proof into fine-grained reasoning units. For each local step, it extracts a typed proof obligation capturing the specific claim and context. A formal evaluation agent attempts to verify these obligations in Lean. To ensure the formal reasoning remains faithful to the original text, a semantic alignment component scores how well each formal statement preserves the original context, objects, and logical form; only high-alignment formalizations are allowed to contribute Lean-based evidence. The method is evaluated on two new expert-verified benchmarks specifically constructed for first-error localization in natural-language proofs.

Result: On the ProofLoc-Olympiad dataset with 350 Olympiad-style problems, FaithSieve instantiated with a GPT-5.4 backbone achieves 81.43% exact first-error localization accuracy, improving over a natural-language direct-judging baseline at 72.29%. On the 200-problem ProofLoc-University dataset covering six advanced mathematical domains, FaithSieve reaches 84.5% exact accuracy versus 75.0% for the direct judge. These results show consistent and substantial gains in accurately identifying the first incorrect step in a proof.

Conclusion: Decomposing natural-language proofs into local reasoning units and grounding each with semantically aligned formal proof obligations enables more reliable automatic evaluation of mathematical reasoning. By carefully gating formal verification with semantic alignment checks, FaithSieve avoids common pitfalls of naive auto-formalization and theorem prover use. The framework substantially improves first-error localization over purely natural-language judges, indicating that fine-grained, faithfulness-aware integration of formal methods is a promising direction for robust assessment of LLM-generated mathematical proofs.

Abstract: Large language models can now generate complex, multi-step mathematical proofs, but reliably determining their correctness and localizing early logical errors remains a critical challenge. Existing evaluation approaches largely depend on model-based natural-language judgments, which often overlook local reasoning gaps. While formal theorem provers like Lean offer a path to rigorous verification, using them to evaluate informal text requires solving locality and semantic mismatches: a prover might bypass a local flaw by proving an overly broad target, or validate an auto-formalized statement that drifts from the original mathematical intent. To address this, we introduce FaithSieve, a Lean-assisted framework for fine-grained evaluation of natural-language mathematical proofs. FaithSieve decomposes coarse proof steps into local reasoning units, extracts typed proof obligations, and verifies them through a formal evaluation agent. Formal validation is gated by semantic alignment scoring, so Lean evidence is incorporated only when the formal statement faithfully preserves the context, objects, and logical form of the original claim. We construct two expert-verified datasets, ProofLoc-Olympiad and ProofLoc-University, to benchmark first-error localization. On the 350-problem Olympiad dataset, FaithSieve using a GPT-5.4 backbone achieves 81.43% exact first-error accuracy, outperforming the direct-judging baseline of 72.29%. Furthermore, on the 200-problem ProofLoc-University benchmark spanning six advanced domains, FaithSieve reaches 84.5% exact accuracy, compared to 75.0% for the direct judge. Our work demonstrates that decomposing proofs into fine-grained units and grounding them with faithful formal evidence significantly improves reliable evaluation of natural-language reasoning.

</details>


### [159] [ProofEvolve: Neuro-Symbolic Evolution for Formal Automated Theorem Proving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26334)
*Wenqian Ye,Ziwei Guan,Eric Xie,Bohan Liu,Shivani Modi,Buyun Zhang,Ellie Dingqiao Wen,Henry Kautz,Aidong Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ProofEvolve is a neuro-symbolic framework that uses evolutionary ideas to build, reuse, and grow formally verified proof structures in Lean, leading to higher theorem-proving success rates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neural theorem provers don’t fully exploit recursive self-improvement: they either store experience only in model weights (expensive, opaque, and not modular) or keep useful partial proofs local to a single problem. They also rely on sparse, whole-proof supervision, wasting informative partial attempts. The paper is motivated by the need for a prover that (1) accumulates formally verified intermediate results across time and tasks, (2) reuses them modularly and compositionally, and (3) does so while preserving formal soundness via a proof assistant kernel.

Method: The authors introduce ProofEvolve, a neuro-symbolic evolutionary framework. Neural components propose variation operators—decomposition of goals, repair of partial proofs, and recombination of proof schemas. All candidate proof transitions are checked by the Lean proof assistant kernel. Within each problem, the method maintains and evolves partial AND-OR proof DAGs stored in a behaviorally indexed archive. Across problems, it performs kernel-verified schema extraction: newly proven sub-DAGs are turned into reusable typed schemas and stored in a persistent library. During future proofs, schemas are recombined with current partial proofs through typed schema recombination so that solved parts are inherited while exposing remaining premises as new subgoals.

Result: On three competition-level Lean benchmarks, ProofEvolve attains the highest average theorem solve rate among the evaluated automated proof systems, demonstrating that its evolutionary reuse of verified proof structures improves performance over baselines.

Conclusion: ProofEvolve shows that explicitly evolving and reusing formally checked proof DAGs enables recursive self-improvement in neural theorem proving while preserving soundness. By harvesting and recombining verified partial results across problems, it turns incomplete proof attempts into durable, reusable knowledge, leading to stronger performance on challenging Lean benchmarks.

Abstract: Automated theorem proving offers a natural foundation for recursive self-improvement in scientific discovery. However, existing neural provers do not fully preserve this recursive structure, where the learning process should be self-improving over time. Existing methods either embed proof experience into model parameters through expensive weight updates, or keep verified intermediate deductions only within the current problem. In addition, these methods also heavily rely on sparse whole-proof feedback, even when unsuccessful partial attempts contain useful discoveries. To close the gap, we propose ProofEvolve, a neuro-symbolic framework that evolves explicit, formally verified symbolic proof structures with neural models to decisively expand the knowledge boundary. In this framework, the neural model proposes variation operators, including decompositions, repairs, and schema recombinations. The symbolic Lean kernel verifies every proof transition. Over the evolution loops, ProofEvolve computes verified closure over the resulting proof directed acyclic graphs (DAGs). Within each problem, ProofEvolve evolves partial AND-OR proof DAGs in a behaviorally indexed archive. Across problems, kernel-checked schema extraction adds newly proved sub-DAGs to a persistent schema library. Proof DAGs inherit the solved results through typed schema recombination, with every residual premise exposed as a new subgoal. This evolutionary process preserves verified results from incomplete attempts and makes them available for later proofs without weakening formal soundness. Across three competition-level Lean benchmarks, ProofEvolve achieves the highest average solve rate among the evaluated proof systems.

</details>


### [160] [Fine-Tuning of Transformer models with Frames](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26430)
*Harshavardhan Adepu,Li Zhang,Sanjiv Kumar,Vikas Singh*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FrameFT is a new parameter-efficient fine-tuning method that represents weight updates using sparse coefficients over a shared Fusion Frame basis, cutting memory and compute while matching or beating existing PEFT methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing PEFT methods like LoRA reduce trainable parameters but still require memory that scales with the model’s hidden dimension and chosen rank (O(dr)), which becomes costly for very large models. There is a need for fine-tuning techniques that further reduce memory and compute while retaining or improving performance.

Method: Instead of storing dense low-rank update matrices as in LoRA, FrameFT represents the parameter update ΔW using a Fusion Frame basis that is shared across layers. The update is encoded via a sparse coefficient matrix over this basis. Fusion Frames are constructed algorithmically, designed to be sparse themselves, and only the sparse coefficients are stored and optimized during fine-tuning. The authors analyze the optimization dynamics and provide formal convergence guarantees, leveraging both coefficient sparsity and basis sparsity for computational efficiency.

Result: On a range of supervised fine-tuning benchmarks, primarily for language tasks and additionally for some vision models, FrameFT attains performance comparable to or better than state-of-the-art PEFT strategies, while using significantly fewer trainable parameters and reduced memory footprint.

Conclusion: Modeling parameter updates in a sparse Fusion Frame basis allows highly efficient fine-tuning of large pre-trained models. FrameFT maintains or improves downstream performance relative to current PEFT methods, while substantially reducing trainable parameters and compute, and is backed by formal convergence analysis, making it a strong candidate for scalable fine-tuning of large models.

Abstract: Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning (PEFT) strategies such as Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) are effective solutions for fine-tuning large-scale pre-trained models; however, their memory requirements scale with the size of the model, $\mathcal{O}(dr)$, where $d$ is the model's hidden dimension and $r$ is the rank. Our proposal, FrameFT, models the parameter update $ΔW$ with a sparse coefficient matrix in a Fusion Frame basis. Fusion Frames can be generated algorithmically and shared across model layers, enabling very efficient updates. Only the sparse coefficients of the basis expansion are stored/optimized, reducing the memory footprint. The sparse structure of the coefficient matrix in FrameFT and the sparsity in the Fusion Frames give large compute benefits, and our analysis provides formal convergence results. We evaluate the idea across a suite of supervised fine-tuning benchmarks, focusing on language tasks, but also report application to vision models. Our experiments show that FrameFT achieves performance on par with/exceeding state-of-the-art PEFT techniques, but needs far fewer trainable parameters.

</details>


### [161] [Don't Overthink, Don't Underthink: Toward Adaptive Reasoning in Agentic AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26442)
*Md Jueal Mia,M. Hadi Amini*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies how to dynamically allocate the amount of reasoning LLM agents do, showing that both over-reasoning and under-reasoning harm performance and cost, and arguing for adaptive reasoning mechanisms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing inference-time reasoning methods mostly assume fixed or preplanned reasoning budgets and are evaluated on static benchmarks, which does not match real agentic AI settings where reasoning needs change over time. This mismatch can lead to inefficiencies and failures such as unnecessary computation, latency, planning drift, or incomplete solutions.

Method: The authors conceptually define over-reasoning and under-reasoning as failure modes of misallocated reasoning in agentic workflows, then empirically study these on MATH-500 and GAIA validation benchmarks. They analyze tool-decision latency, token usage, token-limit exhaustion, and answer correctness to relate different reasoning behaviors to cost and performance.

Result: They find that over-reasoning cases consume more computation (tokens, time, tool calls) without delivering proportional gains in accuracy, while under-reasoning cases strongly correlate with incorrect or incomplete answers. This empirically supports the idea that simply increasing reasoning is not always beneficial and that insufficient reasoning is reliably harmful.

Conclusion: The work concludes that the central challenge is not only increasing reasoning capacity but adaptively allocating it over time according to task demands in agentic systems. Their analysis of misallocated reasoning motivates future methods for adaptive control of inference-time reasoning in LLM-based agents.

Abstract: Recent advances in Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown that increased inference-time reasoning can improve performance on complex tasks. However, many existing approaches rely on fixed or preallocated reasoning controls, such as fixed token budgets, pre-execution difficulty estimates, or activation-space interventions, and are often evaluated on standalone reasoning benchmarks rather than full agentic workflows. These assumptions may not hold in agentic AI systems, where reasoning requirements evolve dynamically through planning, tool use, memory retrieval, and agent-to-agent interactions. Consequently, reasoning can become either excessive or insufficient, resulting in unnecessary computation, increased latency, planning drift, excessive tool use, or incomplete solutions. We argue that a major challenge for next-generation agentic AI is not merely how much reasoning a language model should perform, but how it should allocate reasoning according to evolving task demands. We characterize over-reasoning and under-reasoning as recurring failure modes of misallocated reasoning and evaluate them on MATH-500 and the GAIA public validation benchmark. Using tool-decision latency, token consumption, token-limit exhaustion, and answer correctness, our results suggest that cases classified as over-reasoning are associated with higher computational cost without proportional accuracy gains, whereas cases classified as under-reasoning are consistently associated with incorrect or incomplete solutions. These findings motivate future research on adaptive reasoning mechanisms for agentic AI.

</details>


### [162] [PILOT in the Loop: Live Self-Improvement for Long-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26530)
*Yang Xiao,Yusong Sun,Haoyi Wu,Wenyang Hui,Wen Da,Zhaokai Luo,Mu Chuan,Yao Hu,Wenjie Li,Chengyue Jiang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces PILOT, a supervisor-worker agent harness that enables live self-improvement by steering ongoing runs and turning discovered procedures and failure modes into reusable skills and memory, significantly boosting success rates and efficiency over existing methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most existing self-improvement methods for long-horizon agents only process experience after an episode ends, so they cannot redirect an ongoing run or immediately apply insights to it. Current agent architectures either merge execution and assessment in a single context or delegate to subagents that are hard to steer mid-run, limiting live adaptation and data efficiency. The authors want an architecture that uses emerging experience to both steer the active run and continuously upgrade the agent’s persistent capabilities.

Method: The authors propose PILOT, a two-part supervisor-worker harness for language-model agents. A separate supervisor process performs "live steering" by monitoring the worker’s trajectory and dynamically redirecting or aborting it in real time. In parallel, a "live self-evolution" mechanism distills observed procedures, strategies, and failure modes discovered during execution into reusable skills and memory entries, updating the persistent harness without waiting for the run to end. They evaluate PILOT on three benchmarks using two frozen LLM backbones, comparing against other agent harnesses and self-improvement setups.

Result: Across six experimental configurations (two frozen backbones and three benchmarks), PILOT ranks first in five. On Terminal-Bench 2.0 it improves performance by up to 9.8 percentage points over counterpart harnesses. In explicit self-improvement settings, PILOT increases success rates by 14.6 points with GLM-5.1 and 12.4 points with Kimi-K2.6. It also greatly improves efficiency: mean output tokens decrease by about 43–47%, while successful evaluations per million tokens more than double (increasing by 110–134%).

Conclusion: Live self-improvement—via real-time steering of ongoing runs and immediate distillation of experience into reusable skills and memory—is more effective and efficient than post-hoc self-improvement alone. PILOT’s supervisor-worker harness operationalizes this idea and empirically outperforms prior architectures on multiple benchmarks, indicating that actively managing and evolving agents during execution is a powerful design pattern for long-horizon LLM agents.

Abstract: Long-horizon agent runs generate experience that can improve both the current run and future work. Most self-improvement methods process this experience only after execution ends, so they cannot redirect the active run or immediately apply and validate lessons learned from it. We argue that self-improvement should instead be live, using emerging experience both to redirect the active run and to update the persistent harness. Existing agent architectures do not fully support this goal. Single-agent self-correction combines task execution and trajectory assessment within one context, while subagent delegation separates execution but typically cannot redirect an active subagent. We present PILOT, a supervisor-worker harness for live self-improvement through two coupled mechanisms: (1) live steering lets a separate supervisor redirect or abort the active worker during execution; and (2) live self-evolution distils procedures and failure modes revealed during execution into reusable skills and memory. Across two frozen backbones and three benchmarks, PILOT ranks first in five of six configurations. On Terminal-Bench 2.0, PILOT outperforms counterpart harnesses by up to 9.8 percentage points. In the self-improvement setting, PILOT gains 14.6 points with GLM-5.1 and 12.4 points with Kimi-K2.6. Mean output tokens fall by 42.9% and 47.4%, while successful evaluations per million output tokens rise by 110.3% and 134.0%, respectively.

</details>


### [163] [Multi2AV-Safety: Benchmarking Safety in Multimodal-to-Audio-Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26535)
*Kaichao Jiang,Changtao Miao,Baiqi Wu,Zhiyuan Lu,Kang Yang,Peiwei Zhao,Junchi Chen,Yunfeng Diao,He Liu,Qi Chu,Tao Gong,Nenghai Yu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Multi2AV-Safety, a benchmark for evaluating safety in multimodal-conditioned audio-video generation, focusing on how benign-looking multimodal inputs can jointly produce harmful outputs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing safety benchmarks for generative models are largely prompt-centric and assume that harmful intent is visible in a single input (e.g., a text prompt). As audio-video generation moves toward multimodal conditioning—combining text, images, audio, and video—harmful semantics can emerge only when these inputs are considered together over time. Current benchmarks and safety guards struggle to capture and evaluate such compositional, cross-modal risks, leaving a critical gap in understanding and mitigating safety issues in real-world multimodal systems.

Method: The authors construct Multi2AV-Safety, a benchmark that systematically covers all 11 non-singleton conditioning configurations using text, image, audio, and video (T/I/A/V) for audio-video generation. They create 11,024 "attack instances" that probe different ways of composing benign, weakly harmful, and harmful cues across modalities and time. They then evaluate representative multimodal safety guard systems on this benchmark, analyzing performance across different attack mechanisms and patterns of harm evidence distribution (e.g., spread across modalities, partially obfuscated, or temporally separated).

Result: Experiments on Multi2AV-Safety show systematic weaknesses in state-of-the-art multimodal safety guards. The systems fail in two key ways: (1) they often miss harmful semantics that only become apparent when multiple benign-looking inputs are considered jointly; and (2) they have reduced sensitivity to explicit harmful cues when those cues are embedded within a richer, mostly benign multimodal context. These failure patterns are consistent across multiple attack types and harm-evidence configurations, indicating a broad limitation rather than isolated corner cases.

Conclusion: The study concludes that "compositional risk perception"—the ability to integrate and reason over safety-relevant evidence across multiple modalities and over time—is a central missing capability in current safety guards for multimodal audio-video generation. Even when all conditioning inputs are observable, existing systems fail to reliably detect and mitigate harms that arise from their interactions. The authors position Multi2AV-Safety as a foundational benchmark to diagnose and drive research on more robust, composition-aware safety mechanisms, and they commit to publicly releasing the dataset in October 2026.

Abstract: Audio-video generation is rapidly moving from prompt-driven synthesis toward multimodal conditioning, where text, images, audio, and video can jointly shape the generated output. This shift changes the nature of safety evaluation: harmful intent may no longer reside in any single input, but instead emerge from how otherwise benign or weakly harmful conditions interact across modalities and time. Existing safety benchmarks, however, remain largely prompt-centric or tied to fixed conditioning interfaces, leaving such compositional risks difficult to study systematically. To bridge this gap, we introduce Multi2AV-Safety, the first safety benchmark, to the best of our knowledge, to cover all 11 non-singleton T/I/A/V conditioning configurations for audio-video generation, comprising 11,024 attack instances. Evaluation on Multi2AV-Safety reveals systematic weaknesses in representative multimodal safety guards across attack mechanisms and harm-evidence structures. Our evaluation reveals two complementary failure modes: harmful semantics can emerge from the combination of individually benign inputs, while explicit harmful cues can become harder to detect when mixed with benign multimodal context. Together, these results identify \emph{compositional risk perception} as a central capability gap in safeguarding multimodal-conditioned audio-video generation: current safety guards fail to reliably integrate safety evidence across modalities and time, even when all conditioning inputs are observable. The dataset will be publicly released in October 2026.

</details>


### [164] [DuMateBench: Evaluating Autonomous Agents in Complex Real-World Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26546)
*Zechun Niu,Yukun Zhao,Jiaxin Zhang,Xu Shen,Jinhua Si,Han Tian,Can Xu,Yunfan Song,Jiaxin Mao,Yansong Gao,Yuchen Li,Jianmin Wu,Lingyong Yan,Shuaiqiang Wang,Dawei Yin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DuMateBench is a realistic benchmark of autonomous agent sessions, reconstructed from real user interactions, to test multi-tool, multi-capability agents under messy, perturbed environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing autonomous-agent benchmarks are artificial and too clean: they isolate single capabilities, assume stable tools and environments, and fail to reflect real production settings where agents face noisy histories, unstable tools, and imperfect configurations. This prevents reliable assessment of how well agent frameworks and LLMs handle realistic, multi-step workflows. A benchmark grounded in actual production sessions and environmental perturbations is needed to expose robustness gaps and guide research.

Method: The authors reconstruct a benchmark (DuMateBench) from anonymized, privacy-screened real user sessions on a large agent platform. Each task includes true pre-solution interaction history, configurations, and workspace state, and is human-validated. They define 200 tasks across 8 scenarios and 17 capability categories, many requiring coordination of multiple capabilities. Tasks are run in isolated Docker containers with injected environmental challenges of three types: Insufficient (e.g., missing resources), Unstable (e.g., flaky tools or services), and Noisy (e.g., irrelevant or confusing artifacts). Evaluation uses a hybrid scheme combining deterministic checks and LLM-as-judge. They test five representative agent frameworks, each paired with four strong LLMs, and analyze strict completion, robustness, efficiency, and diagnostics.

Result: Across the 200 tasks and three environmental perturbation types, all evaluated agent framework–LLM combinations show large drops in strict task completion compared to ideal expectations, revealing that current systems struggle with realistic, noisy, and unstable conditions. Performance varies widely both by LLM and by agent framework, and the interaction between the two strongly influences robustness and efficiency. Diagnostic analyses highlight systematic weaknesses, such as difficulty in coordinating multiple capabilities and handling environmental perturbations reliably.

Conclusion: DuMateBench provides a realistic, session-based benchmark that exposes significant robustness and reliability gaps in current autonomous-agent systems operating in multi-tool workflows. The experiments demonstrate that both the LLM and the surrounding framework jointly determine performance under real-world-like perturbations. The benchmark, code, and data are released to support more realistic evaluation and drive the development of more robust, capable agent frameworks.

Abstract: Autonomous agents are increasingly adopted to complete complex, multi-tool workflows in real-world settings. However, existing benchmarks typically separate tasks by application or capability and evaluate agents in environments that are cleaner and more stable than those encountered in practice. We introduce DuMateBench, a real-session benchmark reconstructed from anonymized and privacy-screened user sessions collected from a large-scale production agent platform. Each task preserves the relevant pre-solution interaction history, persistent configurations, and workspace state, and is then validated through human verification. The resulting benchmark comprises 200 tasks spanning 8 broad scenarios and 17 fine-grained capability categories, with most tasks requiring multiple capability coordination. We execute these tasks in isolated Docker containers injected with three forms of real-world environmental complexity: Insufficient, Unstable, and Noisy, and assess performance using a hybrid deterministic and LLM-as-Judge evaluation protocol. Experiments across five representative autonomous-agent frameworks paired with four state-of-the-art LLMs reveal substantial gaps in strict task completion. Complementary robustness, efficiency, and diagnostic analyses further show that performance under environmental perturbations is jointly shaped by the capabilities of the LLM and the surrounding agent framework. The code and data are publicly available at https://dumatebench.com/.

</details>


### [165] [AgentJudgeBench: A Multi-Difficulty Benchmark for Evaluating LLM Judges on Agentic Tool-Calling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26623)
*Abhigya Verma,Amit Kumar Saha,Seganrasan Subramanian,Sai Harshitha Aluru*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They build a benchmark to test how reliable LLMs are as judges for agentic tool-use workflows and show current judges hit a performance ceiling, especially on hard tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-as-a-judge is widely used to evaluate AI agents, but prior work mainly focuses on open-ended text evaluation. For agentic tool-calling systems that follow structured, dependency-driven workflows (DAGs), it is unclear how reliable LLM judges are, especially as tasks get more complex. This gap is important because benchmarks and development decisions increasingly depend on LLM-based evaluation.

Method: They create AgentJudgeBench, a benchmark focused on agentic tool-calling over workflow DAGs with six different DAG topologies and three difficulty tiers. The benchmark has 3,808 instances. They generate system outputs using five different generator models (3B–70B open-weight models and GPT-5.4). They then evaluate these outputs using six judge models ranging from 20B to frontier scale, testing both with and without access to ground truth. They measure judge alignment to a programmatic reference and analyze how difficulty, ground-truth exposure, prompts, and various mitigation strategies (chain-of-thought, judge temperature, structured rubrics) affect reliability. They additionally run a human validation study to assess which judge best aligns with human judgments.

Result: Judge alignment systematically drops as task difficulty increases, and this degradation is 1.5x faster when judges lack ground truth. On hard queries without ground truth, all six judges cluster within a narrow 77–82% alignment range, regardless of scale, revealing a difficulty-induced performance ceiling that cannot be overcome by model size alone. Having ground truth does not always help: for GPT-5.4 and Gemini-2.5-Pro, it slightly hurts alignment, suggesting over-anchoring. Chain-of-thought and temperature adjustments barely change alignment. Structured rubrics can boost alignment by up to 6.5 percentage points but effects are inconsistent across judge–generator pairs. With ground truth, QwQ-32B aligns best with the programmatic reference; in human studies, GPT-OSS-120B aligns most closely with human judgments. Without ground truth, frontier judges only marginally outperform others within the same ceiling.

Conclusion: Current LLM judges for agentic tool-calling on workflow DAGs suffer from fundamental reliability limits, especially on difficult tasks and when ground truth is unavailable. Model scale alone cannot break through a structural performance ceiling. Ground-truth exposure must be used carefully since it can induce over-anchoring, and common tricks like chain-of-thought or tuning temperature do little to help. Structured evaluation rubrics can meaningfully but inconsistently improve alignment. The work provides a specialized benchmark (AgentJudgeBench) and practical guidance, highlighting that more robust methods are needed to evaluate agentic systems reliably and suggesting that future research should focus on evaluation design rather than just larger judges.

Abstract: LLM judges are widely used to evaluate agentic tool-calling systems, yet their reliability on structured, dependency-driven workflows remains largely unexamined. We present AgentJudgeBench, the first benchmark to systematically study LLM-as-a-judge reliability for agentic tool-calling over workflow DAGs, as distinct from the broader LLM-as-a-judge task of open-ended text or preference evaluation. The benchmark comprises 3,808 instances spanning six DAG topologies and three difficulty tiers, evaluated with five generators (3B-70B open-weight models and GPT-5.4) and six judges (20B to frontier scale) under paired with- and without-ground-truth conditions. Judge alignment degrades monotonically with task difficulty, 1.5x faster without ground truth, and on hard queries without ground truth all six judges converge to a narrow 77-82% band regardless of scale, revealing a structural ceiling driven primarily by task difficulty, though its height is partly prompt-dependent for weaker generators, that model capacity alone cannot overcome. Ground-truth exposure is not uniformly beneficial: it reduces alignment for GPT-5.4 (1.5 pp) and Gemini-2.5-Pro (3.9 pp), consistent with over-anchoring. Among mitigation strategies, chain-of-thought reasoning and judge temperature both have negligible effect, while structured evaluation rubrics improve alignment by up to 6.5 pp but do not generalize uniformly across judge-generator pairs. With ground truth, QwQ-32B best matches the programmatic reference, while a human validation study identifies GPT-OSS-120B as the most human-aligned judge; without it, frontier judges lead only marginally within the shared ceiling. These results expose fundamental limitations of current LLM judges and yield practical guidelines for reliable evaluation in agentic systems.

</details>


### [166] [SIGMA: Structured Noise-Effect-Aware Grouped Multi-Agent Aggregation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26683)
*Li Mingqian*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces SIGMA, a hierarchical collaboration framework that uses cooperation structures to make cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning more robust to noisy observations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cooperative MARL struggles under noisy and perturbed observations, which degrade coordination. While noise is often independent at the raw observation level, its impact on agents’ decisions becomes structured through the underlying cooperation patterns among agents—creating locally correlated but globally heterogeneous noise effects. Existing robust MARL methods generally treat noise in a more uniform or unstructured way, failing to leverage these cooperation-induced structures to improve robustness.

Method: The authors first conceptualize "structured noise effects"—the idea that independently sampled observation noise leads to locally correlated decision perturbations among strongly dependent agents, and heterogeneous effects across groups. They then propose SIGMA, a hierarchical collaboration framework: (1) Agents are dynamically clustered into local groups using density-based grouping that reflects cooperation structures. (2) Within each group, intra-group consensus aggregation is performed to extract and preserve shared, task-relevant representation components while smoothing out agent-specific deviations caused by noise. (3) Across groups, an inter-group attention mechanism adaptively integrates group-level information, enabling global coordination while handling heterogeneity in each group’s contribution. The approach is implemented on top of cooperative MARL algorithms and evaluated in noisy StarCraft II micromanagement scenarios.

Result: Experiments on StarCraft II benchmarks with noisy observations show: (1) Empirical validation that structured noise effects indeed arise—agents with stronger cooperative ties exhibit more correlated noise-induced decision deviations. (2) SIGMA significantly improves robustness to observation noise compared with baselines, maintaining higher win rates and more stable performance across various noise settings. (3) In noise-free environments, SIGMA achieves competitive performance, indicating that the robustness mechanisms do not sacrifice standard-task effectiveness.

Conclusion: Leveraging the structure of cooperation among agents is effective for combating observation noise in cooperative MARL. By adaptively forming local groups and combining intra-group consensus with inter-group attention, SIGMA learns more robust representations and maintains both local and global coordination. The work highlights that explicitly modeling and exploiting structured noise effects is a promising direction for robust multi-agent learning.

Abstract: Cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) faces significant challenges in maintaining robust coordination under noisy observations. Although observation disturbances are often introduced independently across agents, their downstream effects on cooperative decision-making can become structured through underlying cooperation structures. We characterize this phenomenon as structured noise effects, where noise-induced decision effects exhibit local correlation among agents with stronger task-related dependencies while remaining globally heterogeneous across different agents and local structures. Existing robust MARL methods, however, rarely explicitly characterize or exploit such structure-dependent noise effects. To address this limitation, we propose SIGMA, a hierarchical collaboration framework that exploits cooperation structures to learn robust representations under noisy observations. SIGMA first organizes agents into adaptive local structures through density-based grouping and performs intra-group consensus aggregation to preserve shared task-relevant information while smoothing agent-specific representation deviations. Inter-group attention then adaptively integrates information across different groups to preserve global coordination while accommodating their heterogeneous contributions. Experiments on noisy-observation tasks in StarCraft II empirically validate the structured noise effects and demonstrate that SIGMA consistently improves robustness under observation noise while maintaining competitive performance in noise-free environments.

</details>


### [167] [Relational Over-Regularization: Graph-Based AI-Generated Text Detection via Sentence Transition Deviation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26694)
*Hyeonchu Park,Bugeun Kim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a new relational perspective for detecting AI-generated text by focusing on sentence-to-sentence transition patterns rather than just token statistics or isolated style features.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing AI-text detectors often overfit to specific models because they rely on token-level statistics or simple stylometric cues, which fail under distribution shift and with new or unseen generators. The authors want a more robust, generalizable signal that captures how sentences relate to each other structurally in a document, something harder for current LLMs to mimic human-like variation in.

Method: They identify and formalize a signal called Relational Over-Regularization (ROR), where LLMs exhibit abnormal variance in inter-sentence transitions, especially recurring similarity bursts at paragraph boundaries and templated transitions. They model text as a graph in which sentences are nodes and edges encode positional, sequential, semantic, and transition-deviation information. Using a Graph Neural Network (GNN) framework called Cross-Source Stylometric Fingerprint Graph (CSFG), they learn from these edge features, including a signed deviation metric δ_ij that operationalizes ROR and helps calibrate false positives without hand-crafted thresholds. They validate ROR quantitatively across four benchmarks (p < 0.001).

Result: CSFG achieves 97.14% accuracy on binary human vs AI text detection, outperforming the best prior graph-based baseline by 11.14 percentage points. It maintains a low false-positive rate of 1.57% and shows robust generalization to unseen LLMs, as long as those LLMs exhibit inflated inter-sentence transition variance. The performance degrades when models produce transition variance comparable to or lower than human baselines.

Conclusion: Viewing AI-text detection as a relational problem centered on inter-sentence transition patterns (Relational Over-Regularization) offers a powerful, generalizable signal that goes beyond traditional token-level or independent stylometric features. CSFG, as an instantiation of this idea, demonstrates strong accuracy and robustness, though its effectiveness is tied to the presence of inflated transition variance in the target generators, suggesting an arms race as models improve their discourse-level behavior.

Abstract: Detecting AI-generated text (AIGT) remains challenging because existing approaches rely on token-level statistical signals or independent stylometric features, causing them to overfit to specific generators and fail under distribution shift. We identify a structural signal at the sentence-pair level: LLMs produce inter-sentence transition variance that deviates from human writing through inflated variance driven by recurring similarity bursts at paragraph boundaries and templated transitions. We formalize this as Relational Over-Regularization (ROR) and validate it across four benchmarks (p < 0.001). The central contribution is this relational problem formulation, not a novel GNN architecture; CSFG is one concrete instantiation for operationalizing ROR. To exploit this signal, we propose the Cross-Source Stylometric Fingerprint Graph (CSFG), a graph-based framework that encodes positional, sequential, semantic, and transition deviation signals as learnable GNN edge features. The per-edge signed deviation δ_ij operationalizes ROR without hand-crafted thresholds and acts as a false-positive calibrator. CSFG achieves 97.14% accuracy under binary detection, outperforming the strongest graph-based baseline by 11.14 pp, with a false-positive rate of 1.57% and robust generalization to unseen LLMs in the inflated-variance regime; detection degrades for generators whose transition variance falls at or below the human baseline.

</details>


### [168] [Five Primitives for Governing Autonomous AI Agents at Runtime](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26696)
*Jiten Oswal,John Cadeddu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a runtime governance architecture for autonomous AI agents in enterprises, based on five primitives: discovery, identity, governance, attestation, and supply chain, and reports on a practical implementation and its trade-offs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current enterprise control and security models are designed for human users and long-lived services, and they fail for autonomous AI agents whose identities are ephemeral, whose action space is not pre-known, and whose population is created ad hoc via APIs. There is a need for a structured way to control and audit what these agents can do at runtime, beyond model alignment or build-time controls.

Method: The authors conceptualize agent governance as a runtime control problem and derive five necessary primitives—discovery, identity, governance, attestation, and supply chain—based on the questions that must be answered before and after an agent action is executed. They analyze the failure modes when each primitive is missing and argue why other primitives cannot substitute for it. They then design and partially implement an architecture where agent actions are mediated against policy, authorized via a tenant-specific action vocabulary, and logged in a hash-linked signed ledger verifiable by third parties without involving the vendor.

Result: They present an implementation where an enforcement point intercepts agent requests on the critical path, identity is provided by a per-workload sidecar, and all actions are recorded in an auditable ledger. Four of the five primitives are implemented and running in private pilots; the fifth (supply chain) exists as separate tooling but is not yet wired into the runtime request path. They also quantify architectural costs such as latency from enforcement, operational overhead from sidecars, and the impact of fail-closed design on availability.

Conclusion: Governing autonomous AI agents in enterprises is fundamentally a runtime problem requiring a specific set of distinct but complementary primitives: discovery, identity, governance, attestation, and supply chain. No subset of these can fully replace the others. A practical architecture based on mediating and authorizing actions against policy, with strong identity and verifiable logging, is feasible and already partially deployed, though it introduces performance and availability trade-offs. The five-part framework is proposed as a principled taxonomy of needs rather than a post-hoc description of an existing system.

Abstract: Enterprise deployments of autonomous AI agents inherit a control model built for human users and long-lived services, and the fit fails in three specific ways: agent principals are ephemeral, appearing and vanishing faster than provisioning; their actions are selected by a model rather than programmed, so the set of things they may attempt is not known in advance; and the population is discovered rather than provisioned, because anyone who can call an API can create one. We argue that governing such agents is a runtime problem -- not a model-alignment problem and not a build-time problem -- and we derive five primitives from the questions that must be answered before an action takes effect and after it has: discovery, identity, governance, attestation, and supply chain. For each we state what fails if it is absent and why the others cannot structurally supply it. We describe an implementation in which an agent's action is mediated against policy before it takes effect, authorised against a per-tenant action vocabulary, and recorded in a hash-linked signed ledger a third party can verify with the vendor out of the loop. We report what the architecture costs: the enforcement point sits on the request's critical path, identity requires a sidecar per workload, and fail-closed mediation converts availability incidents into denial. We are explicit about implementation status: four primitives are built and running in private pilots, and the fifth is built as separate tooling and not yet integrated into the request path. We keep it in the set deliberately: a five-part decomposition that exactly matches what its authors happened to build is not a taxonomy but a description of a codebase.

</details>


### [169] [Accelerating Scientific Research with Gemini in the Real-World](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26701)
*Samuel Schmidgall,Xiaokai Zhu,Marian Shaw,Lin Yang,Valentin Liévin,Jingyun Yang,Yuchen Zhuang,Tim Strother,Alex Bijamov,Min Woo Sun,Anil Palepu,Justin Chen,David Steiner,Jacqueline Shreibati,Wei-Hung Weng,Yilin Zhao,Xingjian Hu,Nicholas Zahn,Sadhya Garg,Julia Kirby,Yuxiang Gan,Jiaoli Li,Divy Thakkar,Shekoofeh Azizi,David Racz,Juraj Gottweis,Vivek Natarajan,Chenglin Wu,Tal Danino,Keran Rong,Haozhe Wang,Benoit Schillings,Yong Cheng,Quoc V. Le,Tao Tu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Extension and real-world validation of Co-Scientist, a Gemini-based multi-agent system that closes the loop from hypothesis to experiments and papers across materials science, biology, and computer science.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most AI-for-science systems focus on in silico tasks like hypothesis generation or data analysis and are rarely integrated with real labs, safety constraints, or full scientific workflows from idea to publication. There is a need to test whether large multi-agent models can reliably operate as practical, execution-grounded research partners in real-world, safety-critical domains while reducing hallucination and improving research quality.

Method: Build a specialized, Gemini-based multi-agent Co-Scientist configuration that integrates with lab hardware, data streams, and reliability/safety modules. Deploy it in three domains: (1) materials science, interfaced with a semi-automated CVD reactor to design safe precursor routes and growth recipes; (2) biology, to infer emergent swarming phenotypes from sparse imaging; (3) computer science/ML, to autonomously search for inference-time scaling architectures. Evaluate generated hypotheses, experimental designs, and outcomes, and conduct a double-blind study in which domain experts review end-to-end AI-generated papers, comparing configurations with and without reliability modules.

Result: In materials science, Co-Scientist designed a safe precursor route for MXenes and produced a lamellar 2D material structurally similar to Ti3C2Tx MXene, and generated growth recipes that achieved monolayer MoS2, MoSe2, and WS2 in single attempts. In biology, it predicted swarming phenotypes of engineered E. coli along IPTG gradients that matched unpublished wet-lab quantitative measurements. In computer science, it autonomously discovered an inference-time scaling architecture that outperformed six frontier models on difficult HealthBench settings while reducing potential clinical harm per blinded physician assessment. In the user study, 30 experts across 450 reviews found that reliability modules reduced hallucination and plagiarism and improved perceived safety in the AI-generated papers.

Conclusion: Co-Scientist, when extended into an execution-grounded, multi-agent system, can participate meaningfully in closed-loop, real-world scientific workflows across multiple domains, from experiment design and execution to paper drafting. The system can generate viable experimental routes, make accurate predictions aligned with unpublished lab data, and even discover superior ML architectures, while reliability modules help control hallucination and safety risks. This demonstrates tangible progress toward practical AI co-researchers that accelerate scientific discovery rather than only performing offline analysis.

Abstract: We present an extension and comprehensive real-world validation of Co-Scientist, a Gemini-based multi-agent system designed to accelerate end-to-end scientific research across hypothesis generation, experimentation, and manuscript generation. Moving beyond in silico hypothesis generation, this specialized configuration transitions Co-Scientist into an execution-grounded research partner advancing closed-loop scientific workflows across materials science, biology, and computer science. In materials science, Co-Scientist interfaced with a semi-automated chemical vapor deposition reactor to design a safe precursor route for MXenes; experimental execution produced a lamellar 2D material sharing key structural similarities with the Ti3C2Tx MXene lattice, although further experiments are needed to confirm the atomic structure. Leveraging Gemini 3 Deep Think for rapid, lab-in-the-loop execution, it also tailored growth recipes to laboratory constraints in minutes, enabling single-attempt growth of monolayer MoS2, MoSe2, and WS2 semiconductors. In biology, Co-Scientist predicted emergent swarming phenotypes of engineered E. coli across inducer (IPTG) gradients from sparse imaging data, quantitatively matching unpublished wet-lab morphological measurements. In computer science, Co-Scientist autonomously discovered an inference-time scaling architecture that outperformed six frontier models on HealthBench (Hard and Professional) while reducing potential clinical harm under blinded physician evaluation. Finally, a double-blind study of end-to-end generated papers with 30 domain experts across 450 reviews demonstrates that Co-Scientist's reliability modules reduce hallucination and plagiarism while improving research safety. Together, these results demonstrate progress toward closed-loop multi-agent scientific AI systems capable of accelerating real-world scientific discovery.

</details>


### [170] [Style as a Confound: False Positives in AI Detection of Non-Native Academic Writing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26710)
*Hyeonchu Park,Gahye Jeong,Bugeun Kim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies how professional English editing of non-native academic manuscripts affects AI text detectors, showing that detectors often misclassify well-edited human text as AI-generated and that editing style is a major confounder.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI text detectors are being used in academic environments to police AI-generated writing, but prior evidence suggests they may unfairly flag non-native English authors. However, population-level studies mix together differences in content, topic, and style, making it unclear whether detectors respond to AI authorship itself or to general features of polished academic English. Professional editing creates a natural setting to isolate linguistic style changes while holding content and authorship constant.

Method: The authors collected 135,389 pairs of manuscripts from a professional English editing service between 2018 and 2025. Each pair consisted of the original non-native English version and the professionally edited version. They ran 13 different AI text detectors on both versions and compared false-positive rates and score changes, relating those changes to the magnitude of linguistic edits while keeping content and authorship fixed.

Result: Across the 13 AI detectors, false-positive rates for human-written texts ranged from 0.0% to 100.0%, indicating extreme variability and unreliability. Editing changed detector scores in inconsistent directions: some detectors were more likely to label edited text as AI, others less likely. The degree of score change correlated with how extensively the text had been edited, showing that detector outputs are sensitive to the intensity of polishing rather than to authorship per se.

Conclusion: Professional editing style is a major confounding factor in AI text detector outputs. Detectors do not cleanly separate AI-generated text from human-written text; instead, they respond strongly to linguistic style and level of polish. This raises serious concerns about fairness, especially for non-native authors in academic settings, and challenges the reliability of using current AI detectors for high-stakes judgments about text authorship.

Abstract: AI text detectors are increasingly employed in academic settings, but it remains unclear whether their outputs reflect AI authorship itself or broader linguistic features associated with polished academic English. Previous studies have reported high false-positive rates (FPRs) for non-native English writing, but population-level comparisons confound authorship with differences in topic, domain, and writing style. Professional editing provides a useful setting for examining this issue because it changes the linguistic form of manuscripts while preserving authorship and content. We examined 135,389 document pairs from a professional English editing service (2018-2025), comprising non-native manuscripts and their native-edited versions, to assess how editing affects detector responses controlling for content and authorship. For the 13 AI text detectors, FPRs for human-written texts varied widely, from 0.0% to 100.0%. Responses varied across detectors: the same edits increased AI scores in some detectors but decreased them in others. Notably, score changes correlated with the extent of editing. The findings identify professional editing style as a key confounding variable in AI detector outputs, rather than establishing a full separation of text origin from linguistic style, raising concerns about fairness and reliability in academic settings.

</details>


### [171] [Knowing When Not to Reuse: Conditional Experience Transfer in Autonomous LLM Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26730)
*Tingyun Li,Wenfeng Feng,Weiqing Li,Abudukelimu Wuerkaixi,Guohua Liu,Yuewei Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a method (BCIT) to decide when prior training experiences/updates on a language model are still valid and useful after the model has changed through further training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLMs are repeatedly adapted to new domains and tools, many autonomous fine-tuning systems re-use past successful updates as if they were always still safe and beneficial. But an update that worked on one model version may be harmful when re-applied after more training, wasting compute and potentially degrading performance. There is a need for a principled way to decide which past update evidence remains applicable as the model evolves.

Method: They formalize the issue as a conditional experience transfer problem and propose Boundary-Calibrated Intervention Transfer (BCIT). BCIT links each observed update effect to its specific source context (parent model state, data, training stage), checks if that context matches current conditions, vetoes updates with hard conflicts, and, when uncertain, runs a small bounded training trial to gather fresh evidence before allowing further training. Full training is only done after passing a shared adoption rule, and only directly observed events are stored in memory for future reuse.

Result: In experiments on a 4B-parameter model adapted across three tasks (finance reasoning, text-to-SQL, and function calling), candidate updates show very different behaviors depending on target task and when they are applied. Compared to other evaluated strategies, BCIT, with the same candidate set, evidence, and compute budget, approves fewer harmful updates and produces higher-quality final models under equal compute budgets.

Conclusion: Experience authorization—deciding which past updates to reapply as a model evolves—is a distinct, nontrivial problem in autonomous post-training. The proposed BCIT framework effectively controls which experiences are reused, improving model quality and reducing harmful updates, suggesting that future autonomous training systems should explicitly model and manage conditional experience transfer.

Abstract: Large language models offer broad capabilities, but adapting them to evolving domains, tools, and requirements often entails repeated post-training. Autonomous systems automate parts of this process by proposing updates, training candidates, and using evaluation feedback to select subsequent proposals. As evidence accumulates, a central problem emerges: which past update evidence remains actionable after subsequent training has changed the parent model? An update's effect depends on its parent, data, and training stage. Treating past success as context-free permission can waste compute. If the resulting child is promoted, it can also degrade the subsequent training trajectory. We formulate this problem as conditional experience transfer and introduce Boundary-Calibrated Intervention Transfer (BCIT), a method that authorizes experience reuse before weight-changing training. BCIT binds an observed effect to its source context, checks applicability conditions, vetoes candidates with named hard conflicts, and obtains current-state evidence through a bounded training trial when needed. Fully trained candidates still face a shared adoption rule, and only observed events extend memory. On one 4B model adapted across finance reasoning, text-to-SQL, and function calling, candidate updates exhibit heterogeneous target and retention effects across the evaluated contexts. Under matched candidates, evidence, and compute, BCIT authorizes fewer harmful updates and attains higher equal-budget final-model quality than the evaluated alternatives. These results support treating experience authorization as a distinct problem in autonomous post-training.

</details>


### [172] [Graph-Guided Selective Unlearning for Language Models: Controlling Support Routes Beyond Forget Seeds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26743)
*Waqas Khan,Tabinda Sarwar,Jingyue Cong,Xun Yi,Estrid He*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces GRAPHSU, a graph-guided selective unlearning method that expands deletion beyond explicit forget examples to include related knowledge, thereby better removing proprietary or sensitive information from language models while preserving utility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing selective unlearning methods remove only explicitly marked training examples (forget seeds), which is inadequate when the same knowledge can be reconstructed via paraphrases, aliases, or neighboring samples. Enterprises need stronger, practical ways to ensure that proprietary or sensitive information is truly removed from fine-tuned language models to meet privacy, contractual, and compliance requirements, without fully retraining models from scratch.

Method: The authors propose GRAPHSU, a graph-guided controller for unlearning. It builds a weighted support-route graph that captures relationships between data points, including forget seeds and their neighbors. Deletion pressure is propagated through this graph, and the method applies graded forgetting strengths depending on each node’s estimated risk of leaking the targeted knowledge, thereby expanding the deletion scope beyond only the original forget seeds.

Result: On two benchmarks—TOFU (a synthetic author-profile QA benchmark for fictitious unlearning) and PISTOL (a structural-unlearning benchmark with interconnected factual samples)—using GPT-2 Medium and Llama-3.2-3B-Instruct, GRAPHSU achieves the lowest utility-feasible soft leakage across all deletion regimes. It reduces soft leakage by up to 49.5 percentage points compared with an otherwise-matched baseline that only forgets the seed examples, while maintaining acceptable model utility.

Conclusion: Selective unlearning must address not only explicitly labeled forget seeds but also the broader support routes through which the same knowledge can be reconstructed. By modeling these relationships as a graph and applying graded forgetting, GRAPHSU more effectively removes targeted knowledge while retaining utility, indicating that practical enterprise unlearning solutions should explicitly control support routes rather than focusing solely on seed examples.

Abstract: Enterprises fine-tune language models on proprietary data that may later require removal due to privacy, contractual, or compliance obligations. Selective unlearning removes requested knowledge while preserving model utility, offering a practical alternative to full retraining, but existing methods treat the explicitly identified forget examples as the complete deletion scope. This is insufficient when target knowledge remains recoverable through paraphrases, aliases, or neighboring training examples. We propose GRAPHSU, a graph-guided controller that expands the deletion scope beyond forget seeds by constructing a weighted support-route graph, propagating deletion pressure through it, and applying graded forgetting strengths to high-risk neighbors. On the Task of Fictitious Unlearning (TOFU), a synthetic author-profile question-answering benchmark, and PISTOL, a structural-unlearning benchmark built around interconnected factual samples, with GPT-2 Medium and Llama-3.2-3B-Instruct, GRAPHSU achieves the lowest utility-feasible soft leakage across all deletion settings, reducing leakage by up to 49.5 percentage points over a matched seed-only baseline, demonstrating that effective enterprise unlearning requires controlling support routes, not just forget seeds.

</details>


### [173] [AgentFold: Closed-Loop Agentic Search for Protein Folding Model Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26747)
*Mingquan Liu,Jiangyu Chen,Hanqun Cao,Xujun Zhang,Pengsen Ma,Xiangru Tang,Shuting Jin,Zhuo Yang,Tianfan Fu,Fang Wu,Xiangxiang Zeng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AgentFold is a multi-agent LLM-driven system that automatically edits and tests protein-folding model code, discovering architecture changes that outperform baselines under the same compute budget.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While LLM agents can read literature and plan experiments, it is unknown whether they can autonomously improve large, tightly coupled scientific ML systems via real code edits that must be validated with expensive computations. Protein folding is a challenging testbed because it requires coordinated changes to complex architectures, multi-objective evaluation, and domain-specific reasoning. The authors aim to test whether an LLM-based system can act as an autonomous research engineer, not just a coding assistant.

Method: They build AgentFold, a multi-agent framework that treats model development as a closed-loop search over executable code variants starting from the ESMFold protein-folding model. Specialized agents propose hypotheses, implement and debug code modifications, run evaluations, and analyze results, logging both successful and failed interventions in structured memory. A Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS)-style policy allocates compute across promising branches of the modification tree. They apply this to a ~2,000-line engineering-scale protein folding codebase, exploring ~80 model variants using ~5,000 GPU-hours and 170M LLM tokens, and compare against baselines like independent Codex-generated proposals and random search under matched compute.

Result: Within the fixed compute budget, AgentFold discovers model variants that improve the best lDDT (a protein-structure accuracy metric) by 7.5% over independent Codex proposals, and it also outperforms a random-search control. The system successfully navigates and modifies a real, non-trivial protein-folding codebase, and its search trajectory yields a rich log of intervention traces.

Conclusion: AgentFold demonstrates that multi-agent LLM systems can autonomously improve complex scientific ML models through iterative code changes and evaluation, not just assist with surface-level coding. The discovered patterns suggest that successful, stable improvements in protein-folding architectures tend to leverage early, soft, learnable priors and gated refinement components, while direct geometric perturbations and geometry-conditioned feedback often destabilize training. This indicates that LLM-driven search can both enhance performance and surface empirical design principles, and the authors release code and experimental resources for further study.

Abstract: Scientific LLM agents have shown promise in literature reasoning, tool use, and experiment planning, but it remains unclear whether they can autonomously improve large, tightly coupled scientific machine-learning systems through executable code changes and computationally expensive validation. We study this question in protein folding, where progress requires coordinated architectural modifications, multi-objective evaluation, and domain-aware interpretation. We present AgentFold, a multi-agent framework that formulates folding-model development as a closed-loop search over executable code variants. Starting from ESMFold, AgentFold proposes hypotheses, implements and debugs code-level modifications, evaluates model variants, analyzes experimental outcomes, and stores both successful and failed interventions in structured memory. An MCTS-style policy allocates computational resources across high-scoring search branches. On an engineering-scale protein-folding codebase comprising more than 2,000 lines of code, AgentFold explores approximately 80 model variants using approximately 5,000 GPU-hours and 170 million LLM tokens. Under a matched computational budget, AgentFold improves the best lDDT by 7.5% over independent Codex proposals and outperforms a random-search control. Beyond model improvement, the resulting intervention traces reveal recurring empirical design patterns: stable gains tend to arise from early, soft, learnable priors and gated refinement, whereas direct geometric perturbations and geometry-conditioned feedback often destabilize training. The code and experimental resources are publicly available at https://github.com/lmqfly/AgentFold.

</details>


### [174] [Discovering Relationships in Data Lakes Using Large Language Models: An Industrial Case](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26750)
*Ahlame Diouan,Eric Ferey,Sabine Loudcher,Jérôme Darmont*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They introduce ColRel, a two-stage method to learn semantic relationships between columns in data lakes using both available metadata and data, enhanced with business dictionaries, and show it works well when metadata is weak or coded.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Data lakes become hard to use without good metadata, particularly for discovering semantic relationships between columns across tables. This is worse in ERP-derived datasets where column names are cryptic codes or abbreviations, so existing column relationship discovery methods that rely on explicit or rich metadata perform poorly. There is a need for a method that can infer column semantics even when exposed metadata is limited, weak, or opaque.

Method: ColRel is a two-stage approach. In stage one, it builds vector embeddings for columns using whatever metadata and data are available at ingestion time. In challenging situations with coded schemas, it augments this with business dictionaries to map codes to human-interpretable terms.
