In stage two, it generates short natural-language descriptions of columns, using the enriched information, and incorporates these descriptions into the embedding learning process to better capture semantic relationships between columns.

Result: On public benchmarks and an industrial ERP dataset, ColRel outperforms baselines in discovering semantically related columns, especially in weak-signal scenarios where metadata is sparse, low-quality, or coded. This demonstrates that using dictionaries and generated descriptions substantially improves column relationship discovery performance compared with relying on raw coded metadata alone.

Conclusion: ColRel effectively addresses the problem of discovering semantic relations among columns in data lakes with poor or coded metadata. By combining ingestion-time metadata and data with business dictionaries and generated natural-language descriptions, it can produce informative column embeddings and reliably identify related columns. This makes ERP-derived and similarly challenging datasets more usable and better integrated within data lake environments.

Abstract: Data lakes rely on metadata to remain usable, yet this meta data is often limited or weakly informative for column relationship discovery, especially in ERP-derived datasets with coded or abbreviated schema labels. We propose ColRel, a two-stage method that builds column embeddings from metadata and data available at ingestion time. In difficult cases, such as coded schemata, business dictionaries help better interpret column names and support the generation of short natural-language descriptions used in the second stage. Experiments on public benchmarks and an industrial ERP dataset show that ColRel is particularly effective in semantically related, weak-signal settings.

</details>


### [175] [DEEPCHART: How Far are LLMs from Faithful Data-Science Chart Generation?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26757)
*Jiahui tang,Kuicai Dong,Dexun Li,Hongchao Gu,Haocheng Yu,Wei Han,Chen Zhang,Yong Liu,Hao Wang,Enhong Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They present DEEPCHART, a benchmark to evaluate how faithfully models can generate charts from complex, real-world documents, revealing frequent hidden data hallucinations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLMs can create charts that look correct and follow instructions, but they often hallucinate or mis-handle underlying data, especially with long, noisy, multimodal sources, and there is no rigorous benchmark focused on this data faithfulness problem.

Method: They construct DEEPCHART, a dataset of 1,482 chart-generation tasks derived from real scientific, financial, and ecosystem documents, formalize chart creation as an Extract–Reason–Visualize pipeline, and design stage-wise evaluation metrics for source-data extraction, quantitative reasoning on derived data, and final chart rendering.

Result: Experiments with state-of-the-art models on DEEPCHART show that although models frequently produce charts that look plausible, they commonly make mistakes in extracting data and reasoning about it, leading to subtle but significant data-level hallucinations, especially in long, realistic multimodal contexts.

Conclusion: Simply giving models larger context windows is not enough; achieving faithful chart generation requires robust evidence extraction and quantitative reasoning before visualization, and DEEPCHART provides a benchmark and resources to study and improve these capabilities.

Abstract: Faithful chart generation in real-world data-science workflows requires grounding visualizations in scattered evidence, computing chart-ready quantities, and rendering them accurately. Modern LLMs can produce visually plausible, instruction-compliant charts, yet data-level hallucinations remain difficult to detect in long, noisy, and multimodal contexts. To measure this gap, we introduce DEEPCHART, an expert-annotated benchmark of 1,482 task-conditioned chart-generation instances drawn from real-world scientific papers, financial filings, and ecosystem reports. DEEPCHART formulates chart generation as an Extract--Reason--Visualize pipeline and evaluates source-data extraction, derived-data reasoning, and chart rendering stage by stage. Experiments with state-of-the-art models show that visually plausible charts often conceal data-level hallucinations, with extraction and reasoning errors common in realistic long and multimodal settings. These findings suggest that larger context windows alone are insufficient; faithful chart generation also requires reliable evidence extraction and quantitative reasoning before rendering. Our benchmark and associated resources are available at https://github.com/tangdouer1005/DeepChart.

</details>


### [176] [Categorizer Automata for Discounted-Sum Payoffs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26763)
*Nathalie Bertrand,Pranav Ghorpade,Senthil Rajasekaran,Sasha Rubin,Moshe Vardi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They define a new type of automaton that efficiently determines into which of several value ranges a discounted sum of rewards falls, and apply it to synthesize good policies in Markov decision processes for complex, possibly discontinuous utility functions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In many AI and decision-making problems, continuous quantities (like discounted sums of rewards) must be turned into discrete categories or bins, for example to apply different decisions or utilities to different value ranges. Existing automata-based tools (comparator automata) handle only two categories and lead to an exponential state blow-up when extended to many bins, making them inefficient. Moreover, synthesizing optimal policies in Markov decision processes under non-smooth or discontinuous utility functions is challenging; standard methods assume smoothness or simple structures.

Method: The authors define a new deterministic automaton called a categorizer automaton that reads an infinite reward sequence and determines which of finitely many bins contains its discounted sum. They design a construction whose number of states is linear in the number of bins, improving over the exponential blow-up that comes from combining many comparator automata. They then integrate these automata into algorithms for Markov decision processes, using them to encode piecewise-defined utility functions (piecewise-constant and piecewise-Lipschitz) over discounted sums. They analyze the algorithmic complexity and approximation properties of the resulting synthesis procedures.

Result: They obtain a construction of categorizer automata with a state space that grows only linearly in the number of bins. Using these automata within Markov decision processes, they give an exact, pseudo-polynomial-time algorithm for synthesizing policies that maximize expected utility when the utility function is piecewise-constant. For piecewise-Lipschitz utilities (bounded slope between finitely many jumps), they present a pseudo-polynomial-time algorithm that computes an ε-optimal policy. They also prove that the underlying synthesis problem is PSPACE-hard even when utilities are only piecewise-constant.

Conclusion: Categorizer automata provide a scalable automata-theoretic tool for discretizing discounted sums into multiple value bins, avoiding the exponential blow-up of naive constructions. This enables efficient synthesis of (approximately) optimal policies for Markov decision processes under a broad class of possibly discontinuous utility functions, with exact results for piecewise-constant utilities and ε-approximate results for piecewise-Lipschitz utilities. The PSPACE-hardness result indicates that these complexity bounds are essentially tight for this class of problems.

Abstract: Categorizing continuous data into discrete bins is a fundamental operation in artificial intelligence. We introduce the categorizer automaton, a deterministic automaton that reads an infinite sequence of rewards and identifies which of finitely many bins contains its discounted sum. Categorizer automata generalize comparator automata, the special case of two bins, which have already proven useful in quantitative synthesis. Our main technical contribution is the construction of a categorizer automaton whose state space is linear in the number of bins, rather than exponential as obtained by a cross-product of comparator automata. We then apply categorizer automata to Markov decision processes, where they allow one to synthesize policies that maximize the expected utility of a discounted-sum payoff for utility functions that may be discontinuous. For piecewise-constant utility functions, the resulting algorithm is exact and runs in pseudo-polynomial time. For piecewise-Lipschitz utility functions, a class that includes any utility with bounded slope between finitely many jumps, it again runs in pseudo-polynomial time and yields an $\varepsilon$-optimal policy. We also show that the synthesis problem considered is PSPACE-hard already for piecewise-constant utilities.

</details>


### [177] [AI Control Scientist: LLM-driven Agentic System for Automated Control Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26780)
*Haiteng Wang,Weihao Li,Jing Zhang,Lei Ren*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces AI Control Scientist (AICS), an LLM-based multi-agent system that automatically designs and tunes control systems from natural language requirements, improving efficiency and outperforming existing automated methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional control system design depends heavily on expert knowledge and tedious manual tuning of controller structures and parameters, which limits efficiency, scalability, and accessibility. With the rise of large language models and agentic workflows, there is an opportunity to automate the end-to-end control design process directly from high-level language specifications, thereby democratizing control engineering and accelerating industrial applications such as chemical process control and aero-engine regulation.

Method: The authors propose AI Control Scientist (AICS), an LLM-driven agentic framework composed of three collaborating agents: (1) a Task Modeling Agent that translates user language requirements into precise engineering constraints and control objectives; (2) a Controller Design Agent that generates candidate control structures and executable implementation code; and (3) a Parameter Tuning Agent that automatically optimizes controller parameters using closed-loop performance criteria. The system iteratively refines designs in a loop until satisfactory performance is reached.

Result: Experiments show that AICS can autonomously generate several representative control systems and that it surpasses existing automated control design baselines in both design success rate and optimization efficiency. The agentic framework reliably produces working controllers that meet specified requirements with less human intervention and better performance metrics than prior automated tools.

Conclusion: The work demonstrates that LLM-based agentic systems can take over much of the traditional, expert-driven control design workflow, enabling automatic generation and optimization of controllers from high-level natural language specifications. This shift from human-driven to agent-driven design suggests a new paradigm for control engineering and opens the door to automating more advanced schemes such as model predictive control and other sophisticated control architectures.

Abstract: Control system design is critical for modern industry, such as chemical process temperature regulation and aero-engine control. However,traditional control design workflows rely heavily on expert knowledge and extensive manual parameter tuning, resulting in limited efficiency and scalability. To this end, this paper proposes AI Control Scientist (AICS), the first large language model (LLM)-driven agent capable of automatically generating optimized controller from language design requirements. Specifically, a Task Modeling Agent interprets user requirements to engineering constraints; a Controller Design Agent generate candidate controller structures and executable code; and a Parameter Tuning Agent refine controller parameters under closed-loop performance criteria. Experiments demonstrate that the proposed agentic system can automatically generate multiple representative control systems, outperforms existing automated baselines in both design success rate and optimization efficiency. This work has the potential to transform control system design from human-driven to agent-driven, paving the way for model predictive control and other advanced control systems design.

</details>


### [178] [Decoupling Planning and Control for Instructable Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26788)
*Zineng Tang,Kelsey R. Allen,Sjoerd van Steenkiste,Ishita Dasgupta,Alane Suhr*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They build Instruct-to-Act, a system that combines a vision-language model planner with a fast world-model controller, enabling high-level language instructions to drive low-latency control and outperforming several baselines in diverse environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision-language models are good at interpreting natural-language instructions and devising high-level plans, but they are too slow and unreliable for direct low-level action control in new environments. Conversely, world-model controllers are strong at rapid, reactive action selection but cannot follow open-ended language instructions. There is a need to integrate these two capabilities so that robots or embodied agents can follow rich language instructions while still acting quickly and robustly at the control level.

Method: They propose Instruct-to-Act, a decoupled system where a pre-trained, instruction-tuned VLM generates sparse, high-level text instructions, and a separate world-model-based controller executes fine-grained actions at high frequency. To make the controller language-conditionable, they relabel segments of its rollouts with synthetic language instructions and train it using behavior cloning in addition to standard reward-maximization and world-model learning objectives. This produces a controller that can interpret incoming text instructions as control goals while retaining fast, model-based reactive behavior.

Result: Across seven embodied tasks, including three multi-agent environments, Instruct-to-Act outperforms both controller-only agents and variants where the VLM directly outputs actions, under matched observation and action spaces. The system maintains low-latency control and allows different pre-trained VLM planners to be swapped in without fine-tuning. It is competitive with strong vision-language-action baselines and multi-agent RL methods on six of seven tasks.

Conclusion: Decoupling high-level language planning from low-level control via an instructable world-model controller enables agents to follow complex language guidance while preserving fast, robust control. The approach generalizes across tasks and environments, supports modular replacement of the VLM planner, and offers a promising direction for combining VLM-based reasoning with world-model-based action in embodied AI and multi-agent settings.

Abstract: Recent work shows that pre-trained, instruction-tuned vision-language models (VLMs) perform well at mapping from instructions and observations to high-level plans, but struggle to realize such plans as reliable low-latency action sequences in unfamiliar environments. At the same time, world-model controllers excel at fast observation-to-action control, but lack open-ended task guidance. In this work, we combine these strengths into a single system, Instruct-to-Act, where we train a world-model controller to act autonomously at high frequency when conditioned on sparse, higher-latency, and high-level text instructions generated by a VLM planner. To train controllers to be language-instructable, we relabel segments of controller policy rollouts with synthetic instructions and jointly optimize a behavior-cloning objective along with existing reward-maximizing and world-modeling objectives. We evaluate our proposed approach across seven embodied environments, including three multi-agent environments where VLM planners coordinate through language while trained controllers serve as their actuators. Under matched observation and action spaces, our decoupled approach consistently outperforms controller-only and direct VLM action-generation variants, preserves fast control, and lets us swap in different pretrained VLM planners without fine-tuning, while remaining competitive with strong vision-language-action and multi-agent RL baselines on six of seven tasks.

</details>


### [179] [SymbolLKG: Towards Verifiable Logical Reasoning via Logical Knowledge Graph and Symbolic Solvers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26836)
*Haizhao Fan,Yuchi Xiong,Jize Wang,Xinping Guan,Xinyi Le*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a neuro-symbolic framework that combines a Logical Knowledge Graph (LKG) with dynamic solver routing to improve multi-step logical reasoning of LLMs, achieving better accuracy and providing verifiable reasoning paths compared to CoT and standard RAG.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are strong in general language understanding but weak in strict, multi-step logical reasoning, often hallucinating or producing inconsistent answers. Existing approaches like Chain-of-Thought lack formal verification, and typical RAG setups cannot effectively capture complex structural and logical dependencies. There is a need for a framework that can represent and exploit logical structure explicitly while still leveraging LLMs.

Method: The authors build a neuro-symbolic architecture centered on a Logical Knowledge Graph (LKG) where logical rules, constraints, and their dependencies are explicitly represented as ontology-based graph nodes and edges. From text, they extract these logical components and encode them in the LKG. They also design a Logic Router that, given a reasoning query, dynamically selects the best symbolic solver (e.g., rule engine, constraint solver) and uses a topology-aware hybrid retrieval mechanism to fetch the most relevant subgraph and evidence for reasoning. This is integrated with LLMs to provide natural language interfaces and explanations.

Result: On multiple logical reasoning benchmarks, the proposed framework outperforms state-of-the-art prompting methods (e.g., CoT-style reasoning) and standard RAG systems in terms of accuracy. It also produces reasoning traces that can be verified against the underlying logical rules and graph structure. Quantitative results show significant performance gains, and qualitative analyses highlight improved consistency and reduced hallucinations.

Conclusion: By combining an ontology-based Logical Knowledge Graph with dynamic logic-aware routing to symbolic solvers, the framework addresses key weaknesses of LLMs in strict logical reasoning. The approach captures complex dependencies, improves accuracy on logical benchmarks, and yields transparent, verifiable reasoning paths, suggesting a promising direction for neuro-symbolic integration in future reasoning systems.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable proficiency in natural language understanding, yet they struggle with strict multi-step reasoning, frequently suffering from hallucinations and inconsistency. Existing solutions like Chain-of-Thought (CoT) lack rigorous verification mechanisms, while standard Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) often misses the complex, structural dependencies inherent in logical tasks. To bridge this gap, we propose a Neuro-Symbolic architecture that integrates a Logical Knowledge Graph (LKG) with dynamic solver routing. Specifically, we introduce an ontology-based LKG that treats logical rules and constraints as first-class topological nodes, enabling explicit modeling of dependencies extracted from text. We further design a Logic Router to dynamically dispatch tasks to the optimal symbolic engine, which is supported by a topology-aware hybrid retrieval mechanism. Experimental results on logical reasoning benchmarks demonstrate that our framework significantly outperforms state-of-the-art prompting and RAG baselines, delivering higher accuracy and verifiable reasoning paths.

</details>


### [180] [LiveSim: Simulating Environment-Shaped Users in Multi-Agent Live-Stream Ecosystems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26849)
*Jiaqi Xu,Yiran Qiao,Jing Chen,Qiwei Zhong,Xiang Ao,Xueqi Cheng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LiveSim is an LLM-based framework that simulates user behavior in live-stream ecosystems by treating user models as editable hypotheses that are continually refined with real interaction trajectories, improving both user-level fidelity and ecosystem-level analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based multi-agent simulators typically use static user profiles built from historical data. In fast-changing, socially intensive environments like live streaming, these static profiles fail to capture how ongoing interactions and platform interventions dynamically reshape user behavior, limiting the realism and usefulness of simulations for tasks such as risk control and policy design.

Method: LiveSim models each user as an editable behavioral hypothesis implemented with an LLM. As real trajectories of user behavior are observed, LiveSim runs trajectory-grounded interactions between the simulated user and the environment. It measures discrepancies between simulated and observed trajectories to discover missing or mis-modeled environmental shaping effects. These discrepancies are distilled into transferable environment–behavior patterns and stored in a collective behavioral memory, which is then used to iteratively refine individual user hypotheses and improve subsequent simulations.

Result: On real-world live-stream risk-control data, LiveSim reduces the gap between simulated and actual user trajectories, showing higher user-level behavioral fidelity compared to baselines that rely on static profiles. It also successfully reproduces macro-level ecosystem phenomena such as risk evolution patterns and responses to platform interventions, enabling more reliable what-if analyses and policy evaluation.

Conclusion: By treating user profiles as hypotheses that are continuously updated with trajectory feedback and shared behavioral memory, LiveSim produces more realistic simulations of user behavior in live-stream ecosystems than static-profile-based approaches. This improved fidelity supports ecosystem-level analysis of risk dynamics and the impact of platform interventions, making LLM-based simulators more practical for real-world risk-control and policy design in live-streaming platforms.

Abstract: User behavior simulation with large language models~(LLMs) is increasingly used to support multi-agent ecosystem simulation. Existing simulators typically rely on static user profiles inferred from historical observations, which become inadequate in socially intensive environments such as live streaming where interaction dynamics continuously reshape user behavior. We propose \textbf{LiveSim}, an LLM-based framework for live-stream ecosystem simulation. It represents users as editable behavioral hypotheses and progressively refines them through trajectory-grounded interactions, where discrepancies between simulated and observed trajectories reveal missing environmental shaping effects. These signals are further extracted as transferable environment-behavior patterns and accumulated in a collective behavioral memory to improve user-level behavioral fidelity and support ecosystem-level simulation. Experiments on real-world live-stream risk-control data validate the effectiveness of LiveSim in improving user-level behavioral fidelity and enabling ecosystem-level analysis of risk evolution and platform intervention effects.

</details>


### [181] [BekchiAI: Measuring, Observing, and Controlling LLM Agents in One Click](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26867)
*Mesut Toruk*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BekchiAI introduces both a benchmark suite and a live observability platform to systematically evaluate and monitor multi-step, tool-using LLM agents on realistic, verifiable tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM leaderboards largely measure static accuracy and prompt-following, but not the complex agentic abilities needed for real-world autonomous agents, such as planning across tool dependencies, robustly handling untrusted inputs, and grounding outputs to external data or sources. There is a need for a rigorous, verifier-checkable benchmark and practical tooling to study and control such agents in deployment.

Method: The authors design BekchiAI-Benchmark, a collection of 13 ReAct-style tool-using agents across 7 task categories (arithmetic, structured/SQL querying, security detection, URL grounding, planning, orchestration, and tool-policy compliance) with 2,057 deterministic test tasks. Gold answers are generated via executable and verifiable procedures: canonical SQL on real databases, exact DAG scheduling, and evaluation of closed-form lambda expressions, including adversarial security samples paired with intentionally imperfect scanners so the model’s own reasoning is tested. They also define behavioral metrics beyond simple accuracy, such as adherence to tool-calling protocols, URL hallucination and source matching, and per-model token cost. The benchmark is run with provided scripts and used to compare four different models. In parallel, they implement BekchiAI-Platform, a web-based observability and control interface that exposes full token- and latency-level telemetry and allows remote termination of running agents.

Result: On BekchiAI-Benchmark, the authors evaluate four models (Qwen3.7-Max, gemma-4-31B-it, gemma4:26b, and gpt-oss-120b). Rather than a single winner, they observe nuanced, per-family performance spreads across the various task types and behavioral metrics, illustrating how different models trade off accuracy, tool adherence, grounding, and cost. The benchmark and framework successfully produce deterministic, verifier-checkable scores that isolate model behavior from mere oracle copying and expose concrete failure modes like URL hallucination or poor tool sequencing. The observability platform provides detailed runtime traces and operational control over agents.

Conclusion: BekchiAI demonstrates that meaningful evaluation of agentic LLMs requires structured, tool-rich, verifier-checkable tasks and behavioral metrics that go beyond accuracy, together with strong observability for agents in operation. The released benchmark, evaluation scripts, and web platform offer a unified environment for measuring, comparing, and managing multi-step, tool-using LLM agents, enabling more precise diagnosis of strengths, weaknesses, and real-world readiness across model families.

Abstract: Large language model agents reason, call tools, and act autonomously over many steps, but their agentic skills-correctly sequencing tools, planning under dependencies, judging untrusted inputs, and grounding generated arguments-are hard to measure with accuracy-only leaderboards. We present BekchiAI, which addresses both sides: a benchmark for measuring agentic skill and a platform for observing and controlling live agents. The BekchiAI-Benchmark, a suite of 13 tool-using ReAct agents across 7 task categories (arithmetic, structured/SQL, security detection, URL grounding, planning, orchestration, and tool-policy), totalling 2,057 deterministic, committed test tasks. Every task is verifier-checkable gold answers are computed by running canonical SQL against a real database, computing the exact schedule of a directed acyclic graph (DAG), or evaluating closed-form lambdas including adversarial security samples paired with deliberately imperfect signature scanners so a score reflects the model's own judgment, not the copying of an oracle. We define a small set of behavioral metrics beyond accuracy-tool-call adherence, URL hallucination and source-match, and per-model token cost and report a four-model comparison (Qwen3.7-Max, gemma-4-31B-it, gemma4:26b, gpt-oss-120b) whose story is in the per-family spread, not the aggregate. The benchmark runs are executed using the provided evaluation scripts. BekchiAI-Platform is a complementary web-based observability and control layer for deployed agents, providing full token and latency telemetry as well as remote run termination. The benchmark, evaluation tools, and platform are publicly released.

</details>


### [182] [C-Unseen: Weak Signal Detection in Dynamic Temporal Knowledge Graphs via LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26870)
*Yassir Lairgi,Ludovic Moncla,Khalid Benabdeslem,Rémy Cazabet,Pierre Cléau*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces C-Unseen, a self-interpretable framework that detects weak signals as rare but semantically coherent subgraphs that persist in dynamic temporal knowledge graphs, outperforming keyword-, topic-, and graph-based baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional weak-signal detection methods rely on keyword frequency, topic models, or untyped graph topology, which overlook the rich semantic and relational structures where nascent, low-visibility signals first appear. There is a need for a method that can capture these subtle but structured changes over time within dynamic temporal knowledge graphs and provide interpretable outputs rather than opaque scores.

Method: The authors propose C-Unseen, which operates on Dynamic Temporal Knowledge Graphs (DTKGs) and defines a weak signal as a rare, semantically coherent subgraph that spreads across consecutive time snapshots. The framework has two main modules: (1) a Rare Subgraphs Extractor that uses a large language model with chain-of-thought reasoning to find subgraphs whose content conflicts with or stands out from the dominant narrative of each snapshot, and (2) a Weak Signal Alerter that monitors the recurrence and proliferation of these rare subgraphs across time to distinguish transient anomalies from true emerging weak signals.

Result: Experiments show that C-Unseen consistently detects weak signals more effectively than baseline methods based on keyword frequency, topic modeling, and generic graph metrics, indicating the benefit of modeling semantic coherence and temporal persistence in DTKGs. The empirical evaluation supports both higher detection performance and the practical usefulness of the extracted, interpretable subgraphs as signals.

Conclusion: C-Unseen offers an effective and self-interpretable approach to weak-signal detection in dynamic temporal knowledge graphs by modeling weak signals as rare but semantically coherent subgraphs that persist and spread over time. By leveraging LLM-based reasoning for subgraph extraction and tracking their temporal dynamics, it overcomes the limitations of traditional keyword-, topic-, and topology-based methods and achieves superior performance in experiments.

Abstract: Weak signals are early, low-visibility indicators that precede significant changes before those changes become established. Existing detection methods, based on keyword frequency, topic modeling, or untyped graph topology, fail to capture the semantic and relational structure through which such signals manifest. In this paper, we propose C-Unseen, a self-interpretable framework for weak signal detection in Dynamic Temporal Knowledge Graphs (DTKGs). We define a weak signal as a rare, semantically coherent subgraph that proliferates across consecutive TKG snapshots. The framework operates through two modules: a Rare Subgraphs Extractor, in which an LLM identifies subgraphs whose content is in tension with the dominant snapshot narrative via chain-of-thought reasoning, and a Weak Signal Alerter, in which the persistence of these rare subgraphs is tracked across time steps to isolate true weak signals. Experimental results demonstrate that C-Unseen outperforms keyword-, topic-, and graph-based baselines.

</details>


### [183] [Evaluating human and LLM screening workflows in a conceptually complex scoping review: Recall--workload trade-offs and run-to-run consistency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26885)
*Nikol Figalová,Lynn Huestegge,Anne Böckler-Raettig*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper compares human and large language model (LLM) workflows for title-and-abstract screening in a complex scoping review, showing that no approach achieved perfect recall, that LLM performance depends heavily on workflow configuration, and that LLMs are more appropriate as supervised, auditable tools than as autonomous screeners.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evidence synthesis depends on high-recall screening of large literature sets, where missing eligible studies (false negatives) can bias conclusions. As LLMs are increasingly used to assist or automate screening, there is a need for systematic, preregistered evaluations comparing human workflows with different LLM configurations, especially in complex conceptual reviews rather than narrow, well-structured topics.

Method: Within an ongoing scoping review, the authors embedded a preregistered comparison. After a conservative title-only pre-screen, 1,131 records proceeded to detailed title-and-abstract screening. These were screened by: (1) one review lead; (2) four trained assistants (non-overlapping subsets); and (3) seven LLM-based workflows using different models (e.g., GPT-5.4, Gemini 3.1) and configurations (file-batch vs all-at-once, including a nominally identical repeat run). They measured proportion of records retained (workload), operational recall relative to 316 verified eligible records from the parent review, inter-workflow agreement, run-to-run consistency for LLMs, and procedural burden. Recall is termed “operational” because only advanced records in the parent review had eligibility fully verified.

Result: No workflow—human or LLM—identified all 316 verified eligible records. Human workflows and two GPT-5.4 file-batch runs retained 42.2–45.0% of records and achieved similar recall (82.3–82.9%). Gemini 3.1 file-batch runs achieved the highest recall (83.9%) but at a higher workload, retaining 56.7% of records. Configurations that processed records all-at-once performed worse in terms of eligible records recovered than corresponding file-batch configurations. Two nominally identical GPT-5.4 file-batch runs showed high overall agreement (91.7%) but disagreed on 94 records, including 29 verified eligibles that were retained in only one of the runs, highlighting instability in exclusion decisions.

Conclusion: LLM screening performance is not determined solely by which model is used; workflow design (processing configuration, batching strategy, and decision integration with humans) substantially affects recall and workload. Because even strong LLM configurations missed eligible studies and showed run-to-run variability, LLMs should not autonomously exclude records in high-recall evidence-synthesis tasks. Instead, they are better deployed in validated, transparent, and human-supervised workflows where their decisions can be audited and complemented by human judgment.

Abstract: Background. Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for screening in evidence synthesis, where false negatives can remove relevant studies before full-text assessment. We compared human and LLM title-and-abstract screening workflows in a preregistered study embedded in a conceptually complex scoping review.
  Methods. After a conservative title-only screen, 1,131 records were screened by one review lead, four trained assistants screening non-overlapping subsets, and seven complete LLM runs using different models and processing configurations, including a nominally identical repeat run. We compared retained workload, operational recall against 316 verified eligible records, agreement, run-to-run consistency, and procedural burden. Because eligibility was verified only for records advanced and assessed in the parent review, recall estimates were operational.
  Results. No workflow recovered all verified eligible records. The human workflows and two GPT-5.4 file-batch runs retained 42.2-45.0% of records while achieving 82.3-82.9% recall. Gemini 3.1 file batches achieved the highest recall (83.9%) but retained 56.7% of records. All-at-once configurations recovered fewer eligible records than corresponding file-batch configurations. Two nominally identical GPT-5.4 file-batch runs agreed on 91.7% of records but differed on 94 records, including 29 verified eligible records retained by only one run.
  Discussion. LLM screening performance depended on the implemented workflow, not model identity alone. Processing configuration, workload, record-level variation, and human-LLM decision integration are therefore substantive properties of deployed systems. For high-recall tasks, LLMs are better suited to validated, auditable, human-supervised workflows than autonomous exclusion.

</details>


### [184] [Learning-Augmented Online Allocation under Unreliable Advice: Robustness, Exposure Fairness, and Distribution Shift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26889)
*Fredy Pokou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper designs a robust, fair online allocation algorithm that uses possibly inaccurate predictions while guaranteeing bounded performance loss and improved exposure fairness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Learning-augmented (prediction-using) algorithms can boost online decision-making, but if the advice is poor, they may behave badly and unfairly. There is a need for mechanisms that benefit from predictions when they are good yet remain robust and fair when predictions are adversarial, particularly in settings with irreversible allocation decisions and fairness constraints over exposure (e.g., recommendations, ad allocation).

Method: The authors formalize an online allocation problem with a finite set of candidates, where decisions are irreversible and subject to exposure constraints. They design a decision rule that: (1) uses predictive advice, (2) incorporates a conservative fallback plan to protect against bad advice, and (3) includes an explicit fairness correction to satisfy exposure constraints. They analyze this rule under bounded prediction error, providing theoretical guarantees of consistency and robustness, and evaluate it empirically in controlled experiments including adversarial-prediction scenarios.

Result: Theoretically, the algorithm is consistent and robust: its performance loss is bounded and scales proportionally with the size of the prediction error. Empirically, experiments indicate that the method maintains stable performance even when the predictive advice is adversarial and achieves substantial reductions in exposure disparity compared to baselines.

Conclusion: The proposed learning-augmented allocation rule successfully combines the benefits of predictions with guaranteed robustness and fairness. It leverages accurate advice to improve decisions, but when advice is inaccurate or adversarial, its performance degrades gracefully and it still enforces exposure fairness, making it suitable for practical deployment in online allocation systems.

Abstract: Learning-augmented algorithms improve online decisions using predictions, but unreliable advice may harm efficiency and fairness. We study an online allocation problem with finite candidate sets, irreversible decisions, and exposure constraints. We propose a robust and fair rule combining advice with a conservative fallback and fairness correction. Under bounded-error assumptions, we prove consistency and robustness with loss proportional to prediction error. Experiments show stability under adversarial advice and significant reductions in exposure disparity.

</details>


### [185] [AI agents in Algorithmic Electricity Markets: On the Emergence of Tacit Collusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26896)
*Jakub Seredyński,Georgios Tsaousoglou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies whether independent learning-based bidding agents in electricity markets can unintentionally learn to tacitly collude and sustain supra-competitive prices.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Electricity markets are becoming algorithmic as participants use learning-based agents to optimize bidding strategies. Other algorithmic markets have shown that independent learning can lead to tacit collusion without explicit communication. Given that electricity markets are often oligopolies with repeated interactions—conditions that are structurally prone to non-competitive behavior—there is concern that similar tacit collusion can arise here, threatening market efficiency and fairness.

Method: The authors model strategic bidding in electricity markets as a repeated game with imperfect public monitoring. They use multi-agent reinforcement learning to simulate how autonomous bidding algorithms might behave over time. To identify tacit collusion, they design a multi-dimensional set of criteria that goes beyond simple profit comparisons to Nash equilibria, incorporating several behavioral indicators of collusive outcomes.

Result: The experiments show that in some settings, learning agents converge to strategies that sustain supra-competitive outcomes, such as higher prices or profits than competitive benchmarks. These outcomes satisfy multiple tacit collusion indicators, despite the agents having no explicit instructions or communication channels for collusion.

Conclusion: The study concludes that tacit collusion driven purely by autonomous learning is a realistic risk in algorithmic electricity markets. Learning-based agents can independently evolve bidding strategies that lead to non-competitive, collusive-like outcomes, underscoring the need for appropriate market design, monitoring, and possibly regulation to address these emergent behaviors.

Abstract: As electricity market participants increasingly adopt learning-based agents for their bidding strategies, electricity markets are becoming algorithmic. Evidence from algorithmic markets in other domains shows that tacit collusion can arise purely through independent learning. Moreover, electricity markets are typically oligopolistic and feature repeated interaction among a small number of participants, making them structurally susceptible to non-competitive behavior. In the face of these observations, this paper investigates the hypothesis that tacit collusion may emerge in electricity markets where participants' actions are controlled by autonomous learning-based algorithms. We model strategic bidding as a repeated game with imperfect public monitoring, and model the participants' emergent behavior using multi-agent reinforcement learning. We propose a multi-dimensional set of criteria (going beyond profit comparisons against Nash equilibria) to assess whether the resulting behavior constitutes tacit collusion. Our experimental results showcase that such a danger is realistic for electricity markets: there are cases where agents do learn to sustain supra-competitive outcomes that are supportive of tacit collusion indicators, even though the agents were never instructed to collude.

</details>


### [186] [Counterfactual Bias Testing for Application Tracking System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26899)
*Sai Yashwant,Shruti Bansal,Anurag Dubey,Samaroha Chatterjee,Satyam Kumar,Shreyash Gupta,Gantala Thulsiram*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes an automated, low-cost, LLM-based framework to audit candidate-job matching systems for demographic bias, using synthetic resumes, controlled demographic treatments, embedding-based ranking, and a rich multi-metric fairness suite to generate risk reports.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Auditing hiring recommender and matching systems for demographic bias is increasingly mandated by regulation and critical for trust, but conventional correspondence-audit studies are labor-intensive, slow, and do not scale to frequent model updates. There is a need for a reusable, general, and low-cost method that can rapidly assess bias across multiple protected characteristics and fairness definitions, aligned with emerging legal frameworks.

Method: The authors build a pipeline where task-specialized LLM agents first generate identity-neutral base resumes and then systematically inject controlled demographic variations along five protected-attribute axes (sex/gender, age, residence, language, disability), forming a K x (1+N) correspondence matrix of matched candidate profiles. Another LLM prompt qualitatively flags any inferred protected characteristics to ensure alignment with the EU AI Act. They then rank all candidates against job descriptions using a fine-tuned sentence-embedding model with cosine similarity, and evaluate fairness with a suite of nine metrics spanning counterfactual, group-fairness, and merit-aware perspectives. Statistical reliability is addressed via bootstrap confidence intervals, significance tests, and Benjamini-Hochberg correction, and the system outputs an automated PASS/INVESTIGATE/FAIL audit report with a composite risk score.

Result: Applied to an example dataset of 5 job postings, 100 base candidates, and 10 demographic treatments, the framework evaluates 90 combinations of metrics and demographic variants. Most metrics, such as score shifts, top-K retention, and merit-aware rate differences, fall within pre-defined tolerance thresholds for every treatment. However, certain rank-based metrics—specifically the MARC (rank stability) metric and nDCG@K—highlight borderline issues, including on the ostensibly neutral baseline, that would not be visible if only score or simple retention metrics were inspected.

Conclusion: The study concludes that robust auditing of candidate-job matching systems requires a multi-metric and multi-family fairness approach instead of relying on a single metric or aggregate score. It further argues that LLM-agent-generated, synthetic correspondence audits provide a practical, scalable, and cost-effective complement to traditional, human-curated audits, and can be broadly applied to hiring and candidate-matching pipelines under high-risk AI regulatory regimes.

Abstract: Automated candidate-job matching systems are increasingly classified as high-risk AI under emerging regulation, yet auditing them for demographic bias is expensive: classical correspondence-audit studies require hand-crafted resumes and manual submission, which does not scale to fast pipeline retraining cycles. This paper presents a general, reusable methodology that (1) uses task-specialized LLM agents to synthesize identity-neutral base resumes and inject controlled demographic treatments across five protected-characteristic axes (sex/gender, age, residence, language, disability), producing a K x (1+N) correspondence-audit matrix; (2) qualitatively flags inferred protected characteristics per an EU AI Act-aligned prompt; (3) ranks candidates against a job description via a fine-tuned sentence-embedding model and cosine similarity; and (4) computes a nine-metric fairness suite spanning counterfactual (score delta, mean absolute rank change, flip rate), group-fairness (top-K retention, four-fifths/impact ratio), and merit-aware (Recall@K, nDCG@K, equal opportunity, equalized odds) families, each with bootstrap confidence intervals, significance tests, and Benjamini-Hochberg correction, culminating in an automated PASS/INVESTIGATE/FAIL report with a composite risk score. On an example corpus of 5 job orders, 100 base candidates, and 10 demographic treatments (90 metric x variant evaluations): score shifts, top-K retention, and merit-aware rate gaps stay within tolerance for every treatment, but a rank-stability metric (MARC) and nDCG@K each surface borderline findings - including one on the neutral baseline itself - that a score- or retention-only view would miss. The results argue for multi-metric, multi-family auditing over any single aggregate score, and for LLM-agent-generated audits as a practical, low-cost complement to human-curated audits for any candidate-job matching pipeline.

</details>


### [187] [A Table Is Worth 64 Tokens: Pixel-level Compression for Multi-Table Document Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26949)
*Iñigo Alonso,Mirella Lapata*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies how to compress tables as images for question answering over long, real-world documents, and proposes a two-step, training-free method that first selects relevant tables using heavily downscaled images and then reasons over those tables at full resolution, reducing tokens and improving accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world document QA often involves long inputs combining text and tables, which are expensive in token cost for multimodal LMs. Optical (image-based) context compression can reduce token usage, but its impact on understanding tables—especially when images are downscaled—is not well understood. The authors aim to understand this trade-off and to design a more efficient way to process multi-table documents without sacrificing QA accuracy.

Method: The authors perform a systematic study of pixel-level table compression for QA over documents with multiple tables. They evaluate five vision-language models (VLMs) on two QA benchmarks under five different visual-token budgets, varying the resolution at which tables are rendered as images. They analyze how downscaling affects both performance and the length/quality of reasoning traces. Based on the observation that even highly downscaled tables retain enough information for relevance detection, they introduce a training-free, two-step approach: (1) use a pixel-compressed (heavily downscaled) representation of all tables to let the model identify which tables are relevant to a question, and then (2) rerun QA only over the selected tables rendered at native image resolution, along with the question, to perform detailed reasoning.

Result: Experiments show that rendering tables as images at their native resolution performs on par with text representations in both accuracy and efficiency. However, when tables are aggressively downscaled, models try to compensate for the loss of visual clarity with longer, but less effective, reasoning chains, negating expected token savings. The authors find that even highly compressed tables still allow the model to reliably judge table relevance. Leveraging this, their two-step, training-free method on long documents achieves a 41% reduction in total token usage and a 7-point accuracy improvement over a single-step QA setup using native-resolution tables. Compared to the most token-efficient single-step compressed baseline, the method uses 15% fewer tokens with no loss in accuracy.

Conclusion: Pixel-level compression of tables is viable for document QA when used carefully. While native-resolution table images work as well as text, naive heavy downscaling harms reasoning and eliminates token savings. Nonetheless, very compressed tables remain useful for quickly detecting which tables matter for a question. Exploiting this, a simple, training-free two-step process—relevance filtering with compressed tables followed by high-resolution reasoning on the selected subset—can significantly reduce token costs and even improve accuracy on long, multi-table documents.

Abstract: Answering questions over real-world documents requires processing long inputs that interleave text with tables. Optical context compression, which represents context as images, promises to reduce token cost, but its effect on table understanding remains unclear. We study pixel-level table compression for question answering over documents with multiple tables, evaluating five VLMs across two benchmarks and five visual-token budgets. Representing tables as images at native resolution matches text in both performance and efficiency, but downscaling them makes models compensate the loss in readability with longer, less effective reasoning traces that cancel the expected savings. Highly downscaled tables, however, preserve enough signal to identify whether they are relevant to a question. We exploit this asymmetry with a training-free, two-step method: the model first identifies the tables needed to answer a question from a pixel-compressed context, and then reasons over those at native resolution. On long documents, our method saves 41% of total tokens and gains 7 accuracy points over single-step QA with native resolution tables. It also uses 15% fewer tokens than the most efficient single-step compressed configuration, with no accuracy loss.

</details>


### [188] [From Atomic to Agentic: Towards Interpretable Evaluation of LLMs' Agentic Mathematical Capabilities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26950)
*Jiayi Kuang,Yinghui Li,Yunze Song,Keyu Chen,Zhifeng Shen,Yangning Li,Yidong Wang,Di Yin,Ruizhi Qiao,Xing Sun,Kai Jin,Ying Shen,Liang Lin,Philip S. Yu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They build a new benchmark to evaluate not just whether LLMs get math answers right, but how well they behave like problem‑solving agents during the reasoning process.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing math benchmarks only check final answers, so they can’t tell whether an LLM followed sound reasoning, where its process breaks down, or how “agentic” it is in planning, acting, and using feedback. As LLMs become agents rather than static solvers, we need tools that expose and measure their internal mathematical reasoning behaviors.

Method: They construct a process-level benchmark organized around a taxonomy of reusable “atomic” mathematical capabilities that underlie agentic behavior. They design tasks spanning planning, action, and feedback in both text-only and multimodal settings. An automated pipeline generates solution trajectories and then uses controlled LLM rewriting to create high-quality, fine-grained annotations of each step in the reasoning process.

Result: Using this benchmark, they show that models that achieve similar end-to-end accuracy on math problems can nonetheless have very different profiles across the atomic agentic capabilities (e.g., they may differ in planning quality, step execution, or use of feedback). This indicates that outcome-only scores mask important differences in internal reasoning behavior.

Conclusion: Process-level, capability-based evaluation is essential for understanding the true mathematical reasoning potential of LLMs as agents. Their benchmark offers a more diagnostic lens than final-answer accuracy and can better guide the design and improvement of next-generation mathematical reasoning agents.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are evolving from performing end-to-end mathematical reasoning to integrating agentic intelligence. However, most existing math benchmarks evaluate only final answers. This outcome-oriented evaluation provides limited diagnostic value for identifying process-level failures or rigorous logic, failing to guide the transformation of LLMs into robust agents. To bridge this gap, we present a process-level benchmark designed to evaluate the inherent agentic mathematical reasoning abilities of LLMs. Our framework aligns problem-solving agentic behaviors with a structured taxonomy of reusable mathematical atomic capabilities. We design a comprehensive suite of planning, action, and feedback tasks across both textual and multimodal contexts, supported by an automated pipeline that synthesizes high-quality trajectories and produces fine-grained annotations via controlled LLM rewriting. Experiments reveal that models with similar end-to-end accuracy can exhibit markedly different agentic capability profiles. This demonstrates that process-level evaluation is crucial for interpreting the true potential of LLMs and guiding the development of next-generation mathematical agents.

</details>


### [189] [GraphMemix: Query-Aware Evidence Forests for Long-Term Multimodal Agent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26983)
*Geng Li,Yuhao Wang,Dong Li,Jianye Hao,Yuxin Peng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GraphMemix is a graph-based long-term memory framework for multimodal agents that builds query-aware evidence forests instead of using global summaries or naive similarity search, improving accuracy and cost-efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing long-term memory systems for multimodal agents either rely on costly, question-agnostic offline summarization of all memories or on simplistic embedding similarity search, which often returns redundant, incomplete, or low-quality context. There is a need for a memory organization method that is query-aware, cost-efficient over the lifecycle, and capable of retrieving complementary evidence that may be low in surface similarity but still relevant.

Method: GraphMemix treats memory organization as a combinatorial optimization problem over a graph. First, it constructs a candidate graph by expanding query-seeded memories using schema-driven and semantic relations to gather potentially relevant context. Second, it defines evidence utility and activation costs that separate direct memory support from relation verification conditioned on anchor nodes, helping to penalize redundancy and conflict. Third, it performs forest optimization to jointly select a subgraph (a forest) that fits within a maximum evidence budget while preserving a reliable relational structure, resulting in a compact, query-specific memory context.

Result: On four long-term multimodal memory benchmarks, GraphMemix yields significant performance gains across multiple underlying foundation models. It achieves better trade-offs between accuracy and lifecycle cost than prior approaches, effectively pushing the Pareto frontier for long-term multimodal memory systems.

Conclusion: Modeling memory retrieval and organization as the construction of a query-relevant evidence forest over a graph enables multimodal agents to avoid expensive global summarization and naive similarity-based retrieval. GraphMemix organizes memory into a structured, query-specific subgraph that captures complementary evidence with controlled cost, leading to more accurate and efficient long-term memory for multimodal agents.

Abstract: Organizing long-term memory for multimodal agents remains challenging because existing methods either suffer from expensive question-agnostic offline summaries or naive embedding similarity matching that introduces incomplete and redundant context. To address these issues, we propose GraphMemix, a combinatorial-optimization graph memory framework that models memory organization as query-aware evidence-forest construction. Specifically, our method consists of three key components:(1) candidate graph construction, which expands multi-view seed memories through schema and semantic relations to acquire query-aware original context; (2) evidence utility and activation costs, which decouples direct memory support from anchor-conditioned relation verification to suppress redundant or conflicting information; and (3) forest optimization, which jointly selects a forest-format memory context under a maximum evidence budget and its reliable relational structure. By organizing memory into a query-relevant subgraph, the method avoids substantial lifecycle cost and recovers low-similarity complementary evidence. Experimental results across four long-term multimodal memory benchmarks demonstrate significant improvements with different foundation models and establish a new Pareto frontier between accuracy and lifecycle cost.

</details>


### [190] [DSA: Evidence-Aware LLM-Agent Orchestration for Multi-Market Stock Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26990)
*Linsen Zhu,Yi Shi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces DSA, an orchestration framework that coordinates LLM agents to perform evidence-aware, multi-market stock research with structured workflows, safety controls, and implementation-level validation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While LLMs can summarize financial information, building a practical stock-research system requires more than summarization: it needs to gather heterogeneous evidence from multiple markets, expose specific data and model capabilities, manage how intermediate opinions are combined, and enforce risk and safety controls on final reports. Existing approaches often lack a rigorous orchestration layer and implementation-level verification of how agents interact and how evidence flows through the system.

Method: The authors design DSA, a modular orchestration framework that decomposes stock research into stages: (1) evidence acquisition from various sources and markets; (2) structured context construction; (3) model-routed analysis where different LLM models/paths are selected; (4) optional role-based and Strategy Skill reasoning; and (5) report generation with selected context and diagnostics. They define two profiles—a default report profile and an agentic profile—that share evidence and routing services but differ in validation and safeguards. In the agentic profile, outputs from core roles are parsed by role-specific parsers, while Strategy Skill outputs go through a signal-eligibility partition and explicit disagreement handling before a final decision agent applies a conservative risk override. The reference implementation covers six regional markets, bundles fifteen Strategy Skills, supports both hosted and local model routes, and is tested via a large manifest of portable offline backend contract tests mapped to contract families representing the LLM-agent architecture.

Result: The reference implementation demonstrates that DSA can support multi-market stock research workflows with multiple Strategy Skills, models, and delivery surfaces. At a frozen software snapshot, 1,457 portable offline backend contract tests all passed, with 596 of these mapped to six core contract families. This provides evidence that the implemented system behaves in accordance with its specified software contracts and architectural design for the tested scenarios.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that DSA provides a practical, evidence-aware orchestration framework for LLM-based stock research, with clear separation of concerns between evidence gathering, reasoning roles, Strategy Skills, and report generation under risk controls. The empirical contribution is not better investment performance, but demonstrated conformance of the implementation to its architectural contracts via comprehensive offline testing. The framework is positioned as an infrastructure and methodology foundation rather than a claim of superior forecasting or returns.

Abstract: Large language models can summarize financial information, but an operational stock-research system must first assemble heterogeneous evidence, expose unavailable data and model capabilities, and control how generated opinions affect a final report. We present DSA, an evidence-aware orchestration framework for multi-market stock research with large language model (LLM) agents. DSA organizes the workflow into evidence acquisition, structured context construction, model-routed analysis, optional role and Strategy Skill reasoning, and report generation with selected context and diagnostics. A default report profile and an optional agentic profile share evidence and model-routing services but use profile-specific output validation and risk safeguards. In the agentic profile, core role outputs are processed by role-specific parsers, whereas Strategy Skill opinions undergo an additional signal-eligibility partition before synthesis; disagreement is supplied explicitly to the decision agent, followed by a conservative risk override. The reference implementation includes six regional market paths, fifteen bundled Strategy Skills, hosted and local model routes, and multiple execution and delivery surfaces. At a frozen software snapshot, a selected manifest of 1,457 portable offline backend contract tests passed; 596 cases were retrospectively mapped to six contract families central to the reported LLM-agent architecture. This evidence establishes implementation conformance for the tested software contracts, not superior report quality, forecasting accuracy, or investment returns.

</details>


### [191] [ASIL: Replacing Screenshot-and-Click with Structured State and Semantic Actions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26991)
*Rui Xie,Lu Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces ASIL, a structured, agent-native alternative to screenshot-and-click control for software-operating code agents, achieving much higher success with far fewer actions and enabling effective training on a variety of applications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing powerful code agents must often operate software via GUI-based screenshot-and-click interfaces, which are inefficient: screenshots provide incomplete state, GUI actions are brittle and semantically shallow, and this mismatch hinders long-horizon planning. There is a need for a more structured, semantically rich, and efficient interface for agents to interact with software applications.

Method: The authors propose ASIL (Agent-Software Interaction Layer), an agent-native interface that exposes software using structured JSON observations and semantic actions that can be executed as code. For each application, they use the deepest possible integration path to surface meaningful operations. They instantiate ASIL across 15 applications and design a benchmark of 300 single-application and 80 multi-application tasks to evaluate performance. They also perform training experiments using small-scale supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and resource-limited on-policy reinforcement learning (RL) on models like Qwen3.5-2B and Qwen3.5-9B.

Result: With ASIL, closed-source models achieve over 80 strict success on both single- and multi-application tasks while using fewer than five actions per task. In contrast, under a repaired runtime and a 50-step screenshot budget, screenshot-and-click agents achieve only 6.6 strict success on single-application and 26.6 on multi-application tasks, which increase to 15.0 and 53.3 on a somewhat easier OSWorld-comparable band. When compared with application-native APIs, ASIL outperforms LibreOffice's UNO API by 28–38 strict points but only matches draw.io's MCP content contract. Training with ASIL data improves Qwen3.5-2B from 58.0 to 72.1 and Qwen3.5-9B from 66.6 to 80.4 via SFT, and further to 74.4 and 82.2 with on-policy RL.

Conclusion: ASIL is a more effective and semantically aligned interface for code agents to operate real-world software than traditional screenshot-and-click control, enabling higher task success with far fewer actions and supporting efficient training. Its structured JSON observations and semantic actions provide a robust abstraction across diverse applications, significantly outperforming GUI control and many application-native APIs, and serving as a strong substrate for both inference-time control and learning.

Abstract: Powerful code agents can execute scripts, call tools, and manage files, yet many important applications remain accessible primarily through graphical user interfaces. We argue that screenshot-and-click is an inefficient interface for software-operating agents: screenshots are state-incomplete, and GUI actions are brittle, semantically weak, and poorly matched to long-horizon planning. We introduce ASIL (Agent-Software Interaction Layer), an agent-native interface that exposes software through structured JSON observations and code-executable semantic actions, realized through the deepest feasible access path for each application. We instantiate ASIL across 15 applications and a benchmark of 300 single-application and 80 multi-application tasks. ASIL reaches above 80 with closed models while executing fewer than five actions per task. Under a repaired runtime and a 50-step screenshot budget, the same tasks yield 6.6 and 26.6 strict success under screenshot-and-click control, rising to 15.0 and 53.3 on an easier OSWorld-comparable band. Against application-native interfaces on matched tasks, ASIL exceeds LibreOffice's UNO API by 28-38 strict points but only matches draw.io's MCP content contract. The structured modality also suits training: small-scale SFT raises Qwen3.5-2B from 58.0 to 72.1 and Qwen3.5-9B from 66.6 to 80.4, and resource-limited on-policy RL further raises them to 74.4 and 82.2.

</details>


### [192] [A Multi-Modal AI Framework for Real-Time Queue Prediction, Management and Optimisation in Intelligent Border Control Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27010)
*Varvara Mama,Eleni Veroni,Nikolaos Kapsalis,Christos D. Nikolopoulos,Anargyros T. Baklezos*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes an AI‑based, real‑time queue prediction and control framework that significantly improves border crossing throughput and waiting times over traditional methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional border control queue management relies largely on static data and simple, rule-based procedures, which cannot handle dynamic, uncertain traffic conditions well. This leads to long queues, higher waiting times, and suboptimal use of staff and infrastructure, especially during demand spikes or atypical conditions. There is a need for a predictive, adaptive system that can integrate multiple heterogeneous data sources and optimize operations proactively rather than reactively.

Method: Introduce a multi-modal AI framework specifically designed for border control. It integrates heterogeneous data sources into a unified representation and uses Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) neural networks to forecast queue lengths and traffic conditions in real time. On top of these predictions, it applies Model Predictive Control (MPC) and scheduling optimization to compute actionable control policies—such as staff allocation or lane configuration—that can be recommended directly to border officers. The system is evaluated using synthetic data that simulates realistic border traffic patterns and uncertainty.

Result: Using synthetic but realistic traffic scenarios, the framework reduces queue prediction error by up to 35% and average waiting time by 30%, while increasing throughput by about 20%, compared with ARIMA-based forecasting and conventional rule-based management strategies.

Conclusion: Combining advanced AI architectures (LSTM-based forecasting) with optimization techniques (MPC and scheduling) enables proactive, adaptive border traffic management. This integrated approach substantially improves prediction accuracy, waiting times, and throughput over traditional methods, indicating its promise as an effective operational tool for modern border control systems.

Abstract: In the present work an efficient border control management procedure is proposed. Compared to operational queue management systems, whose operations are based on mostly static data, the proposed work takes into account dynamic traffic conditions, thus enabling optimal performance, even in cases of uncertainty. To this end, we are proposing a multi-modal Artificial Intelligence (AI) framework, tailored to th needs of border control systems, which enables real-time queue prediction, management, and resource optimization. The novel proposed approach integrates heterogeneous data sources and presents them through a unified representation by employing Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) networks for queue forecasting. Furthermore, it leverages Model Predictive Control (MPC) and scheduling optimization to derive actionable control policies, which in turn can be presented to border control officers. The proposed work has been evaluated using synthetic data simulating realistic traffic. The evaluation results demonstrate that the proposed method reduces queue prediction error by up to 35% and average waiting time by 30%. Accordingly, the average throughput increases by nearly 20%, compared to ARIMA and rule-based methods. The abovementioned results show the effectiveness and efficiency of combining AI architectures with optimization techniques for proactive and adaptive border traffic management.

</details>


### [193] [Omni-Interactive Universal Embedder](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27044)
*Wei-Yao Wang,Kazuya Tateishi,Shuyang Cui,Christian Simon,Takashi Shibuya,Shusuke Takahashi,Yuki Mitsufuji*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces OmniUE, an LLM-based universal embedder that supports omni-interactive querying across text, video, and audio, and shows strong performance gains on multiple benchmarks including a new OmniCHOIR benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current multimodal embedders are moving to LLM-based architectures but largely focus on text-image pairs and mainly support text or simple image interactions. There is a gap in supporting richer modalities (video, audio) and more flexible, user-conditioned interactions such as specifying visual regions or audio segments, within a unified embedding space.

Method: Design OmniUE, an Omni-Interactive Universal Embedder that builds a unified embedding space for text, video, and audio using an omni-LLM with dedicated learnable tokens that extract intermediate-layer representations. Introduce visual and audio segmenters to process fine-grained user interactions like regions of interest and audio spans, aggregate these via the omni-LLM to produce user-conditioned any-to-any embeddings, and support omni-interactive querying. Additionally, create the OmniCHOIR benchmark to evaluate omni-interactive compositional audio retrieval under diverse unimodal and multimodal prompts.

Result: OmniUE achieves state-of-the-art or better performance across several benchmarks: average gains of 10.5% on textual-interactive video benchmarks (MMEB-v2-video), 1.1% on audio tasks (MAEB), 83.7% on visual-interactive benchmarks (SCaR), and 24.1% on the newly introduced omni-interactive OmniCHOIR benchmark, demonstrating strong omni-interactive capability across modalities.

Conclusion: OmniUE demonstrates that a unified omni-modal embedding space combined with omni-interactive querying (including fine-grained region and span conditioning) substantially improves performance across text, video, and audio tasks. The work suggests that jointly advancing omni-modal representation learning and flexible user interactions is a promising path toward truly universal multimodal embedders.

Abstract: Multimodal representation learning has been shifting from traditional two-tower architectures to large language model (LLM)-based embedders due to their strong instruction-following capabilities. Despite this progress, existing approaches primarily focus on language and image modalities, which also remain the dominant modalities for user-conditioned interactions in current embedders. In this paper, we propose the first Omni-Interactive Universal Embedder (OmniUE), which not only learns a unified embedding space across text, video, and audio by leveraging intermediate-layer representations from dedicated learnable tokens, but also supports omni-interactive querying, enabling users to provide inputs in the form of text, visual regions of interest, and audio spans. Within OmniUE, visual and audio segmenters process diverse user interactions and integrate them with an omni-LLM to produce user-conditioned any-to-any embeddings via context aggregation. To evaluate OmniUE's omni-interactive capabilities, we introduce OmniCHOIR, benchmarking models for omni-interactive compositional audio retrieval based on the given text, video, and audio as well as unimodal or multimodal interaction prompts. OmniUE consistently surpasses state-of-the-art baselines across diverse modalities, with average improvements of 10.5% on textual-interactive video benchmarks (MMEB-v2-video), 1.1% on audio tasks (MAEB), 83.7% on visual-interactive benchmarks (SCaR), and 24.1% on our omni-interactive OmniCHOIR benchmark. We believe that jointly advancing omni-modal representation learning and omni-interactive querying paves the way toward universal embedders.

</details>


### [194] [A Contract-Centered Architecture for Scalable and Manageable Agentic Runtimes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27086)
*Yaxiao Liu,Pengbo Liu,Yiwen Liu,Yihua Guan,Zhenghe Hou,Jiaxing Song*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a contract-based runtime architecture and experimental protocol to coordinate enterprise AI deployment, focusing on a falsifiable hypothesis about separating AI capabilities from capacity within cost and control bounds, but presents no implementation or empirical results.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enterprise AI deployment involves complex coordination across many organizational units and technical layers, but current benchmarks emphasize single-agent, single-task performance and say little about how to jointly manage changing AI capabilities, infrastructure capacity, and governed enterprise data. The authors aim to provide a structured way to define responsibilities and make changes safe, measurable, and decidable at the organizational level.

Method: They introduce four responsibility objects—Skill, Harness, Scaffold, and an external data substrate—as shared contracts partitioning ownership and control. They formalize a runtime core A = <S, H, X> (Skill, Harness, Scaffold) with data external to the stack. They articulate a bounded, falsifiable hypothesis P1 about cost-aware capability-capacity separability, define six design conditions as measurable obligations for testability, and propose a cluster-period randomized crossover experiment with specific design features (balanced order, washout, repeated seeds/failure regimes, cluster-aware uncertainty) to test P1 and classify outcomes into four verdict types.

Result: There are no empirical results. The paper’s results are conceptual: the runtime responsibility model (Skill, Harness, Scaffold, external data), the formal hypothesis P1 and its conditions for decidability, and the experimental protocol including verdict categories (supported, falsified, conditional-engineering, inconclusive).

Conclusion: The authors conclude by offering a contract-bounded runtime architecture and a CIO-governed, source-preserving data substrate, together with a falsifiable measurement and experimentation protocol for enterprise AI deployment. They stress that these are design and methodological contributions only, with no completed implementation or measured evidence yet, and position the work as a foundation for future empirical validation in real enterprise environments.

Abstract: Enterprise AI deployment is a coordination problem across business units, application and AI teams, testing, platform engineering, infrastructure, security, operations, and data governance. Use-case benchmarks show whether one agent completes one task, but not how changing capabilities, models, runtime mechanisms, capacity, and enterprise data should be owned, changed, admitted, or evidenced together.
