  We present four responsibility objects as shared organizational contracts: Skill (reusable, versioned capability and workflow asset), Harness (runtime compiler and governor), Scaffold (execution/control boundary and NFR owner), and a stack-external data substrate under independent CIO-governed semantics and telemetry. The runtime core is A = <S, H, X>, with the data substrate outside that stack.
  The central contribution is one bounded, falsifiable hypothesis, P1 (cost-aware capability-capacity separability): within a declared operating region, changing activated capability preserves the capacity-response interaction within a preregistered equivalence margin, while changing compatible Scaffold capacity preserves capability semantics up to a non-inferiority margin, and the required controls stay within a declared enforcement budget. Six design conditions become measured obligations whose coverage, violations, uncertainty, cost, and exclusions determine whether P1 is decidable.
  We propose a cluster-period randomized crossover experiment (balanced order, reset/washout, repeated seeds and failure regimes, cluster-aware uncertainty) with a four-state verdict: supported, falsified, conditional-engineering, or inconclusive. This paper contributes a contract-bounded runtime architecture, a source-preserving data substrate, and a falsifiable measurement protocol. It reports no completed implementation, experiment, dataset, or measured result.

</details>


### [195] [pro-team at LLMs4OL 2026 Tasks Flagship and Reuse: Retrieval-Augmented Generation and Vocabulary-Constrained Filtering for Ontology Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27101)
*Shivam Mishra,Dhannu Ram Meena,Muneendra Ojha,Krishna Pratap Singh,Kuldeep Singh*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They build an offline retrieval-augmented, few-shot prompting pipeline with Qwen2.5-14B to tackle two ontology learning tasks, achieving strong scores for taxonomy-oriented metrics but failing to capture non-taxonomic relations due to a closed, taxonomy-focused relation vocabulary.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ontology learning from text is still difficult even with modern LLMs, which hallucinate terms, output inconsistent structures, and bias toward simple hierarchies instead of richer associative relations. The LLMs4OL 2026 Challenge provides standardized tasks (end-to-end ontology learning and ontology extension/reuse) and evaluation metrics, motivating exploration of practical LLM-based pipelines that mitigate hallucinations, enforce consistency, and improve reuse of existing ontologies.

Method: They design an offline retrieval-augmented few-shot prompting system. First, all-MiniLM-L6-v2 embeddings are used to retrieve the most similar annotated examples (top-5 for Task A, top-2 for Task B) from a demonstration pool. These examples are inserted into prompts to guide Qwen2.5-14B-Instruct in generating ontology structures. A left-truncated windowing strategy ensures task instructions remain in the context even with long prompts. For the ontology extension/reuse task (Task B), they post-process generated triples with deterministic, vocabulary-constrained filtering: triples are kept only if at least one term belongs to the sample’s closed term/type vocabulary, and duplicates of triples already present in the initial ontology are removed.

Result: On Task B (ontology extension/reuse), their method reaches 0.8692 Semantic Graph Similarity, 0.9200 Term-Typing F1, and 0.8540 Taxonomy Discovery F1. On Task A (end-to-end ontology learning), the system achieves 0.7416 Semantic Graph Similarity. Despite these strong taxonomy-related scores, their system does not extract any non-taxonomic relations in Task B.

Conclusion: An offline retrieval-augmented few-shot prompting pipeline with vocabulary-constrained post-processing can yield high-quality, consistent, and ontology-compatible taxonomic structures from text when using LLMs. However, relying on a closed, taxonomy-oriented relation vocabulary severely limits the discovery of non-taxonomic (associative) relations, emphasizing the need for richer relation modeling and possibly more flexible vocabularies or prompting strategies in future work.

Abstract: Ontology learning from text remains challenging despite significant progress in Large Language Models (LLMs), which can hallucinate domain terms, produce inconsistent formats, and favor hierarchical over associative relations. In the LLMs4OL 2026 Challenge, we address both the End-to-End Flagship Task (Task A) and Ontology Extension Reuse Task (Task B) using an offline retrieval-augmented few-shot prompting pipeline. Our system employs Qwen2.5-14B-Instruct with all-MiniLM-L6-v2 for demonstration retrieval, selecting the top-5 examples for Task A and top-2 for Task B. A left-truncated context-windowing strategy preserves task instructions within long prompts. For Task B, generated triples undergo deterministic vocabulary-constrained filtering, retaining triples when at least one endpoint belongs to the sample's closed term/type vocabulary and removing duplicates of the initial ontology. The approach achieves Semantic Graph Similarity of 0.8692, Term-Typing F1 of 0.9200, and Taxonomy Discovery F1 of 0.8540 on Task B, while Task A achieves 0.7416 Semantic Graph Similarity. However, no non-taxonomic relations are extracted, highlighting limitations of closed, taxonomy-oriented relation vocabularies.

</details>


### [196] [LAAF: A Layered Accountability Architecture Framework for LLM Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27102)
*Prachi Chaturvedi,Shahnawaz Ahmad,Ehsan Nowroozi,Muhammad Waqas,George Loukas,Alireza Jolfaei,Lucas Cordeiro,Pierre Dantas*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Systematic review on accountability mechanisms for LLMs in high‑stakes domains, proposing an integrated accountability architecture (LAAF) and mapping it to major AI governance frameworks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly deployed in high‑stakes settings where their fluent yet potentially incorrect outputs can cause real‑world harm, raising urgent questions about who is answerable and how responsibility can be traced and enforced.

Method: A PRISMA‑guided systematic review of literature and regulatory/standards documents (4,512 records screened, 122 primary studies plus 12 key regulatory/standards texts included), synthesised via a sociotechnical accountability model and a four‑layer classification device, then mapped onto major AI governance frameworks.

Result: An integrated sociotechnical model of accountability with five dimensions and four mechanism families (technical controls, human oversight, organisational governance, documentation/traceability), mapped to EU AI Act, NIST AI RMF (incl. GenAI profile), ISO/IEC 42001, and sectoral guidance; identification of four persistent gaps and five unresolved structural tensions in existing instruments.

Conclusion: Existing accountability instruments for LLMs in high‑stakes domains are fragmented and leave critical gaps—especially around human oversight, shared metrics, disciplinary integration, and empirical validation—so the authors propose LAAF, an integrated accountability architecture aligned with cybersecurity guidance, as a synthesized framework that still requires validation in practice.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) operate in hospitals, courtrooms, banks, and public service desks, where fluent, confident outputs are treated as authoritative even when ungrounded or incorrect. When such an output contributes to harm, who is answerable, and through what mechanisms can responsibility be traced, explained, and acted upon? Following PRISMA guidance, five databases were searched from January 2022 to March 2026 against four review questions; of 4,512 records identified, 122 primary studies were included, together with 12 regulatory and standards documents analysed as primary sources. The review consolidates a sociotechnical account of accountability as an actor-forum relation resolved into five dimensions, and synthesises mechanisms across four families: technical controls, human oversight, organisational governance, and documentation and traceability, each with a maturity assessment. The corpus is read through a four-layer classification device spanning provenance, application logic, human oversight, and governance and redress, cross-cut by traceability, role clarity, and continuous monitoring. Both are mapped onto the EU AI Act, whose high-risk obligations have applied since 2 August 2026, the NIST AI RMF with its Generative AI Profile, ISO/IEC 42001, and sectoral guidance in healthcare, consumer finance, education, and the public sector. Four persistent gaps emerge: under-specification of human oversight, absence of shared accountability metrics, disciplinary disconnection, and limited empirical evaluation, alongside five structural tensions that no surveyed instrument resolves. The review closes by consolidating the classification device into an integrated accountability architecture, LAAF, with cybersecurity aligned to the OWASP LLM Top 10 (2025); it is a synthesis of the surveyed evidence rather than a validated artefact.

</details>


### [197] [TransMeme: A Multi-Agent Framework for Cross-Cultural Meme Transcreation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27127)
*Jingyi Zheng,Yule Liu,Zifan Peng,Tianyi Hu,Yuemeng Zhao,Xinhu Zheng,Xinlei He*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a multi-agent framework for cross-cultural meme transcreation that jointly handles cultural adaptation, intent preservation, and multimodal consistency, significantly outperforming baselines in Chinese-English meme adaptation tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Internet memes are widely used for online communication but are highly culture- and language-dependent, creating barriers to understanding across diverse audiences. Simple text translation or rewriting does not capture cultural references, humor, or the interplay between text and image. There is a need for a systematic approach that can adapt memes across cultures while preserving their communicative intent and coherence.

Method: The authors first analyze the task of cross-cultural meme transcreation and identify three core challenges: understanding culture-specific knowledge, preserving intent and tone, and maintaining multimodal (text-image) consistency. They then design a multi-agent framework where specialized agents focus on different subtasks: cultural adaptation, target text rewriting, revision, and conditional visual adjustment. These agents are coordinated and provide feedback to each other, particularly to strengthen target text adaptation and address difficult cases that require deeper cultural or visual intervention. The system is evaluated on bidirectional Chinese-English meme transcreation using both human evaluations and LLM-as-a-Judge comparisons against multiple baselines.

Result: The proposed framework consistently outperforms all baselines in both human and LLM-as-a-Judge evaluations for Chinese-English meme transcreation. In human evaluations, it achieves the best scores on all four evaluation dimensions and shows a 33.1% average improvement over the strongest baseline. In LLM-as-a-Judge assessments, it obtains the highest Top-1 ranking rate at 60%, compared to 26% for the second-best baseline. Component-wise analysis shows that each module contributes to performance gains.

Conclusion: The multi-agent framework is effective for cross-cultural meme transcreation, improving cultural adaptation, intent preservation, and multimodal coherence over existing methods. Remaining challenges are mainly in reconstructing humor and ensuring precise image-text alignment, suggesting that future research should focus on better humor transfer mechanisms and more robust modeling of multimodal relationships rather than just filling cultural knowledge gaps.

Abstract: Internet memes are a pervasive form of multimodal online communication; however, such communication often involves users from diverse linguistic and cultural backgrounds. Therefore, adapting memes across cultures and languages is a central challenge for enabling mutual understanding in online communication. Unlike ordinary translation or standalone text rewriting, cross-cultural meme transcreation must jointly preserve communicative intent, adapt culture-dependent meaning for the target audience, and maintain coherence between text and image. In this work, we first provide an explicit task analysis of cross-cultural meme transcreation and identify three core challenges: culture-specific knowledge understanding, intent and tone preservation, and multimodal consistency. Based on this analysis, we propose a multi-agent framework with specialized agents that are coordinated to address these challenges through cultural adaptation, target text rewriting, revision, and conditional visual adjustment. The framework strengthens target text adaptation with coordinated feedback to handle difficult cases that require deeper cultural or visual intervention. We evaluate the framework on bidirectional Chinese-English meme transcreation using both human evaluation and LLM-as-a-Judge. Our method consistently outperforms all baselines across both evaluation settings. In human evaluation, it achieves the best performance on all four dimensions and delivers a 33.1% average improvement over the strongest baseline, while in LLM-as-a-Judge, it attains the highest Top-1 ranking rate (60% versus 26% for the second-best baseline). Further analysis indicates that each component contributes to the performance. Our error analysis suggests that the remaining bottlenecks lie in humor reconstruction and image-text alignment rather than simple cultural knowledge gaps, pointing to future work on humor transfer.

</details>


### [198] [GRAIN: Bridging Name and Narrative Shifts in Real-World Graph Reasoning through Invariance-Rewarded Agentic RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27142)
*Zike Yuan,Han Zhang,Jianzhi Yan,Le Liu,Cai Ke,Huozhi Zhou,Jian Xie,Jiran Yin,Yukun Cao,Yue Yu,Hui Wang,Ming Liu,Bing Qin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose GRAIN, an RL-trained, single-agent LLM framework that robustly parses text into graph structures using a structure-invariance reward, and introduce GRIT, a benchmark for testing robustness to linguistic shifts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs can perform well on standardized graph tasks but are brittle when node identifiers or task formulations shift in realistic ways. Existing graph tools are invariant to such shifts but require clean structured input, while LLMs extracting topology from noisy text tend to overfit to surface patterns and fail under linguistic variation. Multi-agent approaches to mitigate these problems improve reliability but at the cost of high latency. The paper is motivated by the need for a single-agent, efficient, and robust approach that can reliably map varied natural language descriptions into correct graph structures and reasoning steps, even under distribution shifts.

Method: They introduce GRAIN, a single-agent framework that treats reasoning as a semantic parsing plus tool-execution pipeline. The LLM first parses input text into an intermediate graph structure, then executes deterministic graph tools over this structure. The key technical ingredient is a Structure Invariance Reward used in reinforcement learning: the intermediate graphs extracted by the model are validated against ground-truth topologies, and the reward encourages invariance to superficial textual changes (e.g., node renaming, phrasing shifts). This pushes the model to learn robust text-to-graph mappings instead of memorizing patterns. They also create GRIT, a benchmark specifically designed to test sensitivity to linguistic shifts in graph reasoning tasks.

Result: On the GRIT benchmark and related graph reasoning tasks, GRAIN substantially outperforms multi-agent LLM baselines: it achieves 16.45% higher accuracy while reducing latency by about 24%. It also shows improved structural generalization: compared with supervised fine-tuning (SFT) models, it halves the out-of-distribution performance gap from 15.77% to 7.80%. Additionally, GRAIN maintains robustness and high performance on large-scale graphs that are larger than those seen during training, indicating good scalability beyond the training distribution.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that enforcing structure invariance via RL on intermediate graph representations can make a single LLM agent both more robust and more efficient than multi-agent systems for graph reasoning. GRAIN’s structure-invariance reward leads to better generalization under linguistic and structural shifts, while the GRIT benchmark offers a targeted way to evaluate such robustness. The approach suggests a promising direction for combining LLMs with symbolic tools through learned, topology-aware semantic parsing, especially in settings where latency and real-world variability are critical.

Abstract: Despite their potential in standardized graph tasks, Large Language Models (LLMs) remain brittle to real-world shifts in node identifiers and task formulation. While deterministic graph tools are invariant to such shifts, extracting topological structures from noisy text is highly fragile for LLMs, which often overfit to surface patterns. Moreover, mitigating these parsing failures via multi-agent systems incurs prohibitive latency. To address this, we propose GRAIN, a single-agent framework optimized via reinforcement learning. GRAIN models reasoning as a semantic parsing and tool-execution pipeline, guided by a Structure Invariance Reward. By validating extracted intermediate graphs against ground-truth topologies, this reward forces the LLM to learn robust text-to-structure mappings rather than memorizing linguistic artifacts. We also introduce GRIT, a benchmark evaluating sensitivity to such linguistic shifts. GRAIN outperforms multi-agent baselines by 16.45\% in accuracy with approximately 24\% lower latency. Furthermore, it demonstrates superior structural generalization, halving the out-of-distribution (OOD) gap of SFT models (from 15.77\% to 7.80\%) and maintaining robustness on large-scale graphs beyond the training distribution.

</details>


### [199] [Feature Transformation Enhanced Jacobi Polynomial Graph Filtering for Graph Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27144)
*Xiang Wang,Zhijun Cheng,Zhenyu Meng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose JPGFN, a graph anomaly detection method that combines feature transformation, adaptive Jacobi polynomial graph filtering, and label constraints to better capture fine-grained and frequency-domain information, achieving superior performance on real datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Frequency-domain graph anomaly detection works well but has three key limitations: (1) graph filters are built from static basis functions that cannot adapt to the actual frequency distribution of graph data; (2) node features are treated too coarsely, ignoring the varying importance of each feature dimension and losing fine-grained information; and (3) existing methods make poor use of available node labels, limiting detection performance. The paper aims to overcome these limitations to improve graph anomaly detection in real-world scenarios.

Method: They design JPGFN with three major components: (1) a Feature Separation Transformation Neural Network (FSTNN) that separates node features by dimensions and applies nonlinear transformations, allowing the model to learn fine-grained, dimension-wise feature representations; (2) an adaptive Jacobi polynomial graph filtering module that uses Jacobi polynomials to construct learnable graph filters capable of capturing complex frequency-domain patterns in graph signals; and (3) a node label constraint module that incorporates supervision from node labels to regularize learning and strengthen anomaly detection. The overall architecture jointly learns feature transformations, adaptive spectral filters, and label-informed representations for GAD.

Result: On multiple real-world graph datasets, JPGFN is empirically shown to outperform mainstream graph anomaly detection methods. The experiments demonstrate consistent and significant improvements under standard evaluation metrics, indicating that the combination of feature separation, adaptive Jacobi polynomial filtering, and label constraints leads to more accurate anomaly detection.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that enhancing graph anomaly detection with (i) fine-grained feature transformation via FSTNN, (ii) adaptive Jacobi polynomial-based graph filtering for complex frequency-domain modeling, and (iii) effective use of node labels substantially improves detection performance. The study validates that adaptive frequency-domain filters and label-guided learning are particularly beneficial, suggesting a promising direction for future GAD research integrating advanced spectral filtering and feature modeling.

Abstract: In recent years, graph anomaly detection (GAD) based on frequency-domain filtering have achieved promising results. However, existing approaches still face three major challenges: First, they use static basic function to constructed graph filter which cannot effectively adapt to the frequency-domain distribution of graph data. Second, they fail to adequately consider the importance information of each attribute in the node feature vector, leading to the loss of fine-grained information. Third, they insufficiently utilize node labels for GAD. To address these issues, this paper proposes a novel graph anomaly detection method called JPGFN (Feature Transformation Enhanced Jacobi Polynomial Graph Filtering Network). First, a Feature Separation Transformation Network (FSTNN) is developed to better learn fine-grained node features by feature separation and applying nonlinear transformations to node features across different dimensions. Second, an adaptive Jacobi polynomial graph filtering module is constructed based on Jacobi polynomials to adaptively capture complex frequency-domain features of graph signals. Finally, a node label constraint module is developed to facilitate the use of node labels and enhance the performance of GAD. Experimental results on multiple real-world datasets demonstrate that the proposed method significantly outperforms mainstream approaches.

</details>


### [200] [When Tool Outputs Become Commands: Separating Action Induction from Runtime Authorization in Tool-Augmented LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27146)
*Xiaokun Guo,Zhen Xu,Dongdong Huo,Yanqiu Zhang,Wei Wang,Qinfu Yang,Dongjin Yu,Yu Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces SARA, a framework that separates action suggestion from action execution authorization in tool-using LLM agents to prevent unintended or unsafe real-world side effects while maintaining task performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLM agents increasingly use tools that can perform real-world actions, tool outputs are often treated as both suggestions and executable commands. This conflation means that untrusted tool observations can induce harmful or unintended actions, leading to security and safety risks such as prompt injection or action hijacking. The authors are motivated to design a principled way to distinguish between generating candidate actions and granting them authority to execute, thereby improving robustness against adversarial or unsafe tool outputs.

Method: The authors propose SARA, a runtime architecture that splits the process into two distinct roles: (1) an Action Probe on the Observation side that is context-isolated and focuses on detecting and exposing action-inducing semantics, while recording the provenance (origin) of potential actions across multiple steps; and (2) an execution-side authorization mechanism that only permits actual tool calls when they are supported by the user’s objective and verifiable evidence from prior authorized executions. SARA enforces support at the level of overall goal, the chain of previous executions, and individual tool arguments. It also introduces No-History-Promotion, a constraint that prevents historical mentions of actions from being re-used as implicit authorization, thus stopping the laundering of action origins into execution authority over multi-step interactions. The system is evaluated on AgentDojo and AgentDyn benchmarks, and with multiple agent backbones.

Result: Empirically, SARA achieves a very low attack success rate (ASR), capped at 0.63% across four main evaluation scenarios on AgentDojo and AgentDyn, while preserving competitive task performance relative to baseline agents. Additionally, when applied to various existing agent architectures (agent backbones), SARA consistently reduces ASR, demonstrating its generality and effectiveness at mitigating malicious or unintended actions induced by tool outputs.

Conclusion: Separating action induction from execution authorization, together with explicit action provenance tracking and strict multi-level support checks, provides a robust way to constrain tool-augmented LLM agents against adversarial or unsafe tool outputs. SARA’s No-History-Promotion and provenance-aware authorization mechanisms significantly reduce attack success rates without sacrificing task utility, suggesting that future LLM agent designs should treat observation-derived action proposals and execution authority as distinct, auditable roles in the runtime system.

Abstract: Tool-augmented LLM agents must rely on untrusted runtime Observations to complete open-ended tasks; however, when tool outputs no longer merely provide data but begin to specify concrete actions, they effectively become ``commands'' that can drive real-world side effects beyond user intent. We argue that this risk arises from conflating action induction with execution authorization. To address this distinction, we propose SARA, which treats action induction and execution authorization as distinct runtime roles and separates action provenance from execution authority. On the Observation side, a context-isolated Action Probe exposes action-inducing semantics and persistently records action-origin provenance across steps as a review signal; on the execution side, actual tool calls are authorized only against the user objective and audited evidence from authorized successful executions, while satisfying goal, execution-chain, and argument-level support. To preserve this separation across multi-step execution, SARA applies No-History-Promotion to prevent historical recurrence from laundering action origins into execution authority. Across AgentDojo and AgentDyn, SARA limits ASR to no more than \(0.63\%\) across four primary evaluation settings while maintaining competitive task utility, and consistently reduces ASR across additional Agent backbones.

</details>


### [201] [Thomson: Continual Learning of Frontier Models for SovereignAI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27147)
*Shengzhuang Chen,Jerrod Parker,Yejin Bang,Andrew M. Bean,Nabeel Seedat,Stefan Winzeck,Daniil Glazko,Jannik Zgraggen,Fangyi Yu,Scott Arnott,Dietrich Trautmann,Luca Ciuffreda,Guglielmo Bonifazi,Davide Romano,Bradley Bell,Kirsty Fielding,Daniele Giofrè,Tom Zielund,Ipshita Chatterjee,Sneha Murthy Ghantasala,Manpreet Nanreh,John Scoville,Maciej Sakowicz,Wassim Seifeddine,Lukas Thede,Jonathan Richard Schwarz*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a practical path to frontier-level AI for many institutions by using continual learning on open-weight models, and demonstrates it with a new model, Thomson.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Frontier AI development is dominated by a few highly funded organizations, creating information, economic, and power asymmetries. Public discourse calls for ‘SovereignAI’—the ability of states, companies, or institutions to independently build, deploy, and govern AI—but offers little concrete, near-term guidance for actors with limited resources.

Method: The authors use continual learning on existing open-weight models, leveraging a modern mid- and post-training stack rather than just small-scale fine-tuning, prompt engineering, or tool augmentation on frozen models. They design safeguards at each training stage to balance plasticity and stability, making minimal but high-impact parameter interventions to avoid catastrophic forgetting while improving broad capabilities. This approach is instantiated in a model named Thomson, trained with special emphasis on high-stakes professional tasks.

Result: Using this continual learning framework, the authors obtain performance gains similar to multiple model generations’ worth of improvement, but at substantially lower compute and staffing costs than typically assumed necessary. Thomson achieves competitive performance with contemporary frontier models on agentic tasks, safety benchmarks, legal and tax tasks, multilingual evaluation, and large-scale ‘Deep Research’ tasks. The evaluation exhibits a characteristic π-shaped profile: broad capability gains, including on untargeted skills, with minimal forgetting of prior knowledge.

Conclusion: Continual learning applied to open-weight base models can allow a wide range of institutions, even under constrained budgets, to reach frontier-level performance while retaining ownership of critical parts of the AI stack (models, tools, value alignment, and data privacy). The π-shaped evaluation pattern suggests their training methodology enhances diverse capabilities without the typical trade-off of catastrophic forgetting, making this a viable strategy for realizing practical SovereignAI across many actors.

Abstract: The development of frontier models is commonly perceived to be the exclusive remit of a small number of heavily funded players, creating an information, economic and power asymmetry between developers and the diverse user base of modern AI. Recent public discourse acknowledges this concern, calling for SovereignAI (an organisation's capability to independently build, deploy and govern AI use), but offers little concrete advice on how this can be achieved in the short term under a diversity of funding settings. We argue that frontier performance is achievable by a wide range of institutions through Continual Learning on readily available open-weight models. Unlike limited approaches such as small-scale fine-tuning, prompt engineering, or tool-augmentation of a frozen model, our approach exploits a modern mid- & post-training stack while introducing safeguards that preserve both plasticity and stability at each stage, making the minimal number of high-impact interventions on the parameters. This yields gains comparable to those typically seen across multiple successive model generations, at compute and personnel budgets substantially lower than commonly thought, making ownership of large parts of the SovereignAI stack (model, tool infrastructure, values & data privacy) viable for far more actors. We demonstrate this with Thomson, a general-purpose frontier model trained with an enhanced focus on high-stakes professional work. Thomson performs competitively with recent frontier models across agentic tasks, safety, legal, tax & multilingualism, and large-scale Deep Research. Evaluations show a distinctive $π$-shaped pattern: distinct improvements across a wide range of capabilities, including those not explicitly targeted, while almost completely eliminating the forgetting problem common to narrow domain adaptation.

</details>


### [202] [BPMN4CAI: A BPMN Extension for Modeling Dynamic Conversational AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27149)
*Björn-Lennart Eger,Daniel Rose,Barbara Dinter*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes BPMN4CAI, a BPMN-compliant extension to better model conversational AI in business processes, and validates it via a case study.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard BPMN struggles to adequately represent dynamic, context-sensitive, and adaptive interactions typical of conversational AI systems used in digital business processes. There is a methodological and practical gap in how to model chatbots and virtual assistants within business process models while remaining compliant with BPMN.

Method: The authors follow a Design Science Research methodology to design and develop BPMN4CAI, a BPMN extension. They systematically extend existing BPMN elements and add specialized components tailored to conversational AI characteristics like context handling and adaptive dialogue. They then apply and evaluate this framework via a case study in a real or realistic business process scenario.

Result: The case study shows that BPMN4CAI can be effectively applied to model business processes involving conversational AI. It supports adaptive decision-making, enables robust context management, and ensures more transparent representation of interactions between CAI systems and process participants.

Conclusion: BPMN4CAI closes an important gap in modeling conversational AI within business processes while staying BPMN-compliant. It enhances the expressiveness of process models for CAI by supporting adaptivity, context sensitivity, and transparency, and is demonstrated to be applicable and useful in practice through the case study evaluation.

Abstract: Conversational AI systems, such as chatbots and virtual assistants, are becoming increasingly important to digital business processes. However, the established Business Process Model and Notation (BPMN) standard faces challenges when representing dynamic, context-sensitive interactions. This paper addresses this methodological and practical research gap by developing a standard-compliant BPMN extension (BPMN4CAI). Using Design Science Research methodology, this paper develops an approach that systematically extends existing BPMN elements and incorporates specialized components. The applicability and relevance of the BPMN4CAI framework are demonstrated and evaluated through a case study. The results show that the BPMN4CAI extension facilitates adaptive decision-making processes, robust context management, and transparent interactions for Conversational AI within business processes.

</details>


### [203] [Calibrated Enough to Know, Not Calibrated to Act: Fabricated Evidence Makes LLM Agents Commit to the Unknowable](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27167)
*Pranav Aggarwal*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Study shows LLMs become overconfident and commit to answers on inherently unpredictable questions when those questions are presented in authoritative, professional market-style panels, even when the underlying data is fabricated. A simple supervised fine-tuning intervention can train a decision 'gate' that largely prevents such unwarranted commitments, but this fix is fragile to context and formatting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand when and why large language models make confidently wrong predictions on questions that are provably unpredictable, and to test whether their tendency to act on such questions is driven by actual information or by the authoritative appearance of how information is presented.

Method: The authors run experiments across 12 frontier LLMs, asking them to make directional calls on inherently unpredictable questions (e.g., akin to coin flips) under different presentation conditions: a bare question, a question with genuine market-style panels, and a question with fully fabricated but professional-looking market panels. They measure the rate of 'commitment' to a direction, reported probabilities, and self-assessed knowability. They also train a 3B model via supervised fine-tuning on 540 synthetic unanswerable cases (dice, coins, jars, timers) to create a better 'act/don't-act' decision gate, then test generalization and robustness under different response formats.

Result: Across models, adding professional-looking market panels increases commitment to a directional call from 6.5% to 54.0% on provably unpredictable questions. Fabricated panels yield almost the same commitment as real data (24.5% to 36.8% vs 37.6%), indicating that visual/structural authority, not informational content, drives action. Models still recognize unanswerability when asked directly (labeling questions as irreducible 90% of the time and almost never acting then). A fine-tuned 3B model reduces unwarranted commitments to 0.0% on the original unanswerable tasks and generalizes to three new domains, but the improved gating fails when response formats are rigid and leave no room for explicit reasoning.

Conclusion: LLMs' tendency to give confident answers on unanswerable questions is not due to lack of knowledge or reasoning, but to a failure in the internal decision gate that determines when to act. This gate is highly sensitive to contextual cues like authoritative formatting and can be substantially improved through targeted supervised fine-tuning, yet remains fragile to prompt and format changes. Responsible deployment must therefore address both the trainability and the context-dependence of this act/don't-act mechanism.

Abstract: An LLM agent shown a professional-looking market panel commits to a directional call on a provably unpredictable question far more often than one asked the bare question: across 12 frontier models, commitment rises from 6.5% to 54.0% as evidence is escalated. It commits just as readily when every number on the panel is invented: fabricating the entire display, so nothing the model can see is true except the question itself, still lifts commitment from 24.5% to 36.8%, statistically indistinguishable from the 37.6% produced by genuine market data. What unlocks confident action is not information but the authority of its packaging. The failure is narrow and locatable. Incapacity is not the answer: on matched answerable questions attached to the same panels, the same models answer essentially always, at near-perfect accuracy. Nor is it belief - stated probabilities barely move across the gradient that swings action by 48 points, and score worse than a climatological baseline. Missing judgment isn't it either: asked to classify a question's knowability before acting, models call it irreducible 90% of the time and then commit on just 0.4% of those. The act/don't-act gate is what fails, and the effect is concentrated in a few models rather than universal. Because the gate is separable, it can be trained. Supervised fine-tuning of a 3B model on 540 synthetic cases, predominantly dice, coins, jars and timers, drives commitment to 0.0% on the original cases and transfers to three unseen domains. It does not survive everything: the gate holds exactly when the response format leaves room to reason, and rigid formats that remove that room leave the model confident and wrong on questions it otherwise answers correctly. The gate is trainable and context-fragile, and deployment needs both halves of that sentence.

</details>


### [204] [What Makes Good Agentic Data? An ACE Lens on Data Generation for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27260)
*Xingshan Zeng,Zishan Xu,Boju Zhang,Yuzhou Wu,Lingzhi Wang,Jianghao Lin,Liangyou Li,Yasheng Wang,Lifeng Shang,Xin Jiang,Weinan Zhang,Yong Yu,Qun Liu,Weiwen Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a unified framework for understanding and designing data generated by LLM-based agents, focusing on making this data valid, well-targeted, and diverse rather than just large in quantity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents now learn heavily from data they generate themselves while interacting with environments. However, existing research is fragmented across domains and uses inconsistent evaluation setups, making it hard to see shared principles or systematically improve data generation quality. There is a need for a common language and framework to reason about how to generate interaction data that is actually useful for learning, not just abundant.

Method: The authors introduce a two-level conceptual framework. First, they define agentic data through a factorized representation (E, q, τ, v): environment specification E, task signal q, interaction trajectory τ, and an optional verifier v. They then categorize different data generation paradigms by which component they primarily anchor on and how other components depend on it. Second, they theorize data generation as designing a constrained distribution under the ACE lens: Accuracy, Complexity, and divErsity. Accuracy constrains data to be grounded and consistent; Complexity tunes how hard the data is relative to a target learner configuration; and divErsity manages coverage and redundancy. They survey prior work using this lens, analyzing how it verifies data, shapes difficulty, and broadens behavioral coverage.

Result: The framework exposes common mechanisms across previously siloed agent domains and clarifies the distinctions between generating candidate experiences and verifying/selecting them. The ACE perspective highlights a trend in the literature toward execution-grounded verification, tailoring difficulty to specific learners, and seeking deeper behavioral diversity rather than superficial or purely volumetric diversity. The survey positions many existing methods within this unified space and identifies gaps and emerging mechanisms for agentic data generation.

Conclusion: The authors conclude that the key problem in agentic data generation is not maximizing volume but dynamically allocating valid, informative, and non-redundant experience as both agents and environments change. Their factorized (E, q, τ, v) representation and ACE framework provide a common vocabulary and set of design axes for future work on generating and curating interaction data, with implications for how we scale agents, choose data sources, design training regimes, and implement adaptive learning strategies.

Abstract: LLM agents increasingly rely on generated interaction data to learn how to interact with external environments. Agentic data generation must maintain consistency among environments, tasks, interactions, and success signals while producing experience that is useful rather than merely abundant. Existing work spans many agent domains, but domain-centered organization and heterogeneous evaluation often obscure common generation mechanisms and conflate candidate construction with verification and selection. This work develops a two-level framework for the field. First, we represent agentic data as a common factorized object $(E,q,τ,v)$, comprising an environment specification, task signal, interaction realization, and optional verifier. We organize generation paradigms by their primary anchor and dependency structure. Second, we formulate generation as constrained distribution design through the Accuracy-Complexity-divErsity (ACE) lens. Accuracy establishes the feasible support of grounded and internally consistent data. Within this support, Complexity places learning mass relative to the capability of a declared learner and execution configuration, while divErsity controls coverage and redundancy of data. Using this framework, we explore how prior work verifies generated experience, constructs and calibrates difficulty, and expands behavioral coverage. The literature reveals a shift toward execution-grounded accuracy, learner-relative complexity, and diversity beyond surface variation or dataset size. We further discuss broader directions and emerging trends in agentic data generation through the ACE lens, including their implications for scaling, data sources, training regimes and adaptive learning. Overall, the central challenge is not simply to generate more data, but to continually allocate valid, informative, and non-redundant experience as agents and environments evolve.

</details>


### [205] [Naive Prompt Optimization: Rethinking the Need for Complex Prompt Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27266)
*Yuan Chang,Xiaoqi Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes Naive Prompt Optimization (NPO), a simple, single-lineage prompt optimization method that uses a teacher model’s feedback to iteratively refine prompts, achieving performance comparable to or better than more complex methods like GEPA, with fewer rollouts and strong transfer across similar models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Recursive self-improvement in agentic AI requires efficiently improving autonomous agents across many tasks. Prompt optimization is attractive because it yields gains similar to weight fine-tuning but is cheaper to run and deploy. However, current prompt optimizers are becoming overly complex, so there is a need to test whether much simpler methods can achieve similar gains, leveraging stronger models instead of sophisticated search algorithms.

Method: The authors introduce Naive Prompt Optimization (NPO), a lightweight, linear, single-lineage optimization procedure. A teacher model iteratively revises an existing prompt using rollout feedback from a student model’s performance. The process lacks complex search over many prompt variants and instead follows a simple trajectory of successive revisions guided by the teacher’s reasoning. They empirically compare NPO with other optimizers such as GEPA and GRPO on various tasks, including interactive games, and also test the transfer of optimized prompts across different student models and model families.

Result: NPO achieves performance comparable to or better than GEPA while using fewer rollouts, and this advantage becomes larger when stronger teacher models are used. In interactive games, NPO is generally competitive with GEPA, though GRPO can outperform both on some tasks that are less responsive to prompt optimization. Additionally, prompts optimized via NPO transfer well: when used verbatim with other student models—especially those from the same model family—they deliver similar performance improvements.

Conclusion: Simple, linear prompt optimization via NPO can match or surpass the performance of more sophisticated and complex search-based prompt optimization methods, especially when paired with strong teacher models. This suggests that investing in teacher model quality may be more beneficial than increasing optimizer complexity, and that lightweight prompt optimization techniques can offer practical, generalizable improvements across multiple models and tasks.

Abstract: Efficiently improving autonomous agents across diverse tasks is central to accelerating recursive self-improvement (RSI) in agentic AI, with prompt optimization emerging as a promising approach capable of delivering performance gains comparable to those achieved by fine-tuning model weights, while reducing computational costs in both optimization and serving. However, recent developments increasingly favor unnecessarily complex prompt optimizers. We introduce Naive Prompt Optimization (NPO), a lightweight single-lineage method that iteratively revises prompts using a teacher model with rollout feedback. NPO achieves comparable or better performance than GEPA with fewer rollouts, and its advantage increases with stronger teacher models, suggesting that stronger teacher reasoning can partially substitute for optimizer-side search complexity. In interactive games, NPO remains broadly competitive with GEPA, while GRPO performs better on some tasks less amenable to prompt optimization. We also show that NPO-optimized prompts elicit similar performance improvements when applied verbatim to other student models, especially across models within the same family. Overall, our preliminary results show that simple, linear prompt optimization can rival substantially more sophisticated and complex search procedures.

</details>


### [206] [BrailleBench: Investigating Multi-Criteria Braille Comprehension in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27268)
*Jinghan Zhang,Fengran Mo,Zhiyu Chen,Xiaoyan Han,Kunpeng Liu,Chang-Tien Lu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BrailleBench is a benchmark to evaluate how well large language models understand and generate Braille, revealing a significant gap between regular English performance and Braille accessibility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing large language models are widely used to access knowledge and computational help, but it is unclear whether their capabilities are equally accessible to blind and deafblind users who rely on Braille. Braille, especially its different grades, uses indicators, contractions, and specific digital encodings that may not be handled well by current models. The authors aim to systematically test and quantify how inclusive LLMs are for Braille users and to provide a standardized way to evaluate and improve Braille-related AI capabilities.

Method: The authors construct BrailleBench, a benchmark of 5,570 aligned instances drawn from five datasets covering mathematics, commonsense reasoning, and multi-hop question answering. Each instance is represented in English and in Braille Grades 1 and 2. They design multiple evaluation configurations to separately test: (1) understanding Braille-authored inputs, (2) generating answers in Braille, and (3) performing full end-to-end interactions entirely in Braille. The benchmark is created via a deterministic, expert-reviewed pipeline using a custom Braille Toolkit, explicitly avoiding any LLM-generated data to reduce bias. They then evaluate six representative LLMs on these tasks across the different Braille forms and interaction modes.

Result: Across the six evaluated LLMs, performance in Braille-related tasks lags significantly behind the same tasks in standard print English, indicating a consistent accessibility gap. The models show asymmetric abilities: they are generally better at producing Braille than at understanding Braille, particularly for Grade 2. Grade 2 Braille is notably fragile on the input side, with accuracy degrading more than with Grade 1 when the input is in Braille. When both input and output must be in Braille (fully Braille interactions), model performance declines further, emphasizing the difficulty of complete Braille-based usage.

Conclusion: BrailleBench exposes a systematic and persistent gap between LLM performance in print English and in Braille, showing that current systems are not yet fully accessible for blind and deafblind users. The asymmetry between Braille understanding and generation, and the particular sensitivity of Grade 2 input, highlight concrete technical challenges that future models must address. By releasing BrailleBench and its supporting resources publicly, the authors provide a practical foundation and clear evaluation criteria for developing more inclusive, Braille-capable AI systems.

Abstract: Although Large language models (LLMs) mediate access to knowledge and computational assistance, their capabilities should benefit vulnerable groups in the same way. However, it is unclear whether existing AI systems are inclusive enough for blind and deafblind users to access the same functionality through Braille, whose indicators, contractions, and digital representations introduce distinct requirements for model comprehension. To this end, we introduce BrailleBench, a benchmark for evaluating LLMs in Braille comprehension from different Criteria. BrailleBench aligns 5,570 instances from five datasets, including mathematics, commonsense, and multi-hop question answering across English and Braille Grades 1 and 2. Different configurations are designed to understand whether the systems can comprehend Braille-authored content, express answers in Braille, and complete end-to-end Braille interaction. To ensure the quality and prevent evaluation bias, the benchmark is built through a deterministic, expert-reviewed pipeline via a self-created Braille Toolkit without using any data instances generated by LLMs. We evaluate six representative LLMs from various aspects. The results reveal a persistent gap between print-English capability and Braille accessibility. Braille understanding and expression are asymmetric, where Grade 2 is especially fragile on the input side compared to Grade 1, and fully Braille requests further reduce performance. The experimental observations provide valuable guidance for the development of future Braille AI systems. All related resources in BrailleBench are publicly available for future research.

</details>


### [207] [LLMs Can Design Near-Optimal OR Algorithms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27296)
*Jackie Baek*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper tests if large language models can autonomously design competitive algorithms for classic operations research problems and finds that a strong LLM already matches or beats specialized methods with minimal human guidance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Operations research relies heavily on hand-crafted, problem-specific algorithms that are often complex and time-consuming to design. With the rapid progress of large language models, it is natural to ask whether these models can automatically generate effective algorithms for well-specified OR problems, potentially reducing the need for manual expert effort and accelerating algorithm discovery.

Method: The authors consider three canonical OR domains: inventory control, queueing network control, and assortment optimization. They define two usage regimes for LLMs: (1) instance-level, where the LLM is given a single problem instance and must output a solution; and (2) class-level, where the LLM is given only a description of the problem class and parameter ranges, and must output a general algorithm that maps instance parameters to solutions. Using a single, untuned prompt and access to a Python sandbox with a fixed compute budget, they query several LLMs and benchmark the produced solutions and algorithms against state-of-the-art specialized methods on diverse test instances.

Result: The strongest tested model, referred to as gpt-5.6-sol, performs on par with or better than the best existing specialized approaches on nearly all evaluated instances across the three OR problem classes. This strong performance holds even in the more demanding class-level regime, where the algorithm is produced without seeing the specific evaluation instances in advance. Furthermore, results show marked performance improvements between model generations released less than eight months apart, indicating a rapidly improving capability in algorithm design for these tasks.

Conclusion: For the studied, well-specified OR problems, a single untuned query to a frontier LLM—with access to limited code execution—is already sufficient to generate algorithms that are competitive with, and often superior to, hand-crafted specialized methods. This positions frontier LLMs as a strong empirical baseline for algorithm design in such domains and suggests that future LLM improvements could further transform how algorithms for OR problems are developed.

Abstract: We ask whether large language models (LLMs) can design effective algorithms for well-specified operations research (OR) problems. We study inventory control, queueing network control, and assortment optimization. We evaluate two levels of LLM use: at level 1, the model receives one problem instance and returns a solution for that instance; at level 2, it receives only the problem class description and broad parameter ranges, and returns an algorithm that maps instance parameters to solutions. Human input is minimal: we give one untuned prompt that describes the problem, and the model has access to a Python sandbox tool with a fixed compute budget.
  The strongest model we test, gpt-5.6-sol, matches or outperforms the best existing method on almost all evaluated instances. This holds even at level 2, where the returned algorithm is fixed before seeing the evaluation instances. Performance also improves sharply across models released less than eight months apart, suggesting that this capability is moving quickly. Thus, for the well-specified operations problems we study, a single untuned LLM query can already produce algorithms competitive with specialized methods. These results suggest that frontier LLMs can be a serious empirical baseline for algorithm design in well-specified OR problems.

</details>


### [208] [Verify Smarter, Evolve Further: Efficient Harness Evolution through Behavior-Aware Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27311)
*Jinghan Xu,Yikai Zhang,Aili Chen,Weiyuan Li,Jiaqing Liang,Deqing Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HarnessLens is a framework that automatically improves language-agent harnesses more efficiently by focusing verification on behavior-relevant tasks with attributable evidence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Adapting agent harnesses (which govern how language-model agents use instructions, tools, and runtime components) is expensive because verifying each change requires many costly rollouts. Existing propose-and-verify approaches evaluate every candidate on a fixed, broad task set, which wastes evaluation budget on tasks unrelated to the candidate change and lets aggregate metrics hide specific regressions. A more targeted, budget-aware, and behavior-aware method is needed to evolve harnesses reliably under limited interaction budgets.

Method: HarnessLens is a budget-aware propose-and-verify framework for evolving agent harnesses. It (1) jointly explores both the task space and user-configurable harness components, rather than relying on a fixed evaluation set; (2) derives candidate harness modifications from execution trajectories, using observed agent behavior to propose targeted changes; and (3) selectively verifies each candidate only on tasks that are behavior-relevant, filtered through an attributable-evidence gate that links observed performance changes to specific harness modifications. This design explicitly encodes attribution so that verification focuses on where the candidate is likely to matter, conserving evaluation budget.

Result: Applied to three different agent harnesses and four benchmarks, HarnessLens improves average performance on held-out tasks by 7.6–13.6% while using substantially less evaluation budget than competing baseline methods. These experiments show that the framework can discover better harness configurations more sample-efficiently than standard propose-and-verify approaches.

Conclusion: Behavior-aware verification with explicit attribution to particular harness changes enables more reliable and sample-efficient evolution of language-agent harnesses under constrained interaction budgets. HarnessLens demonstrates that jointly exploring tasks and harness components, and selectively verifying only on behavior-relevant tasks, yields significant performance gains while reducing evaluation cost, suggesting a practical path toward automated, scalable optimization of agent harnesses.

Abstract: Agent harnesses shape how language-model agents use instructions, tools, and runtime components, but adapting these harnesses requires costly verification. Existing propose-and-verify methods typically score every candidate on a fixed task set, wasting rollouts on unrelated behaviors and allowing aggregate scores to obscure specific regressions. We introduce HarnessLens, a budget-aware framework for automated harness evolution. HarnessLens jointly explores the task space and user-configurable components, derives candidate modifications from execution trajectories, and selectively verifies each candidate on behavior-relevant tasks using an attributable-evidence gate. Across three agent harnesses and four benchmarks, HarnessLens improves average held-out performance by 7.6-13.6% while consuming substantially less evaluation budget than competing baselines. These results demonstrate that behavior-aware verification with explicit attribution enables more reliable and sample-efficient harness evolution under constrained interaction budgets. Our code is available at https://github.com/jhxu5214/HarnessLens.

</details>


### [209] [Not All Eval-Awareness Is Equal: Capabilities Framing Predicts Compliance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27340)
*Allison Zhuang,Santiago Aranguri*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper shows that a model’s awareness of being evaluated is not a single thing: different ways the model frames the situation (capabilities vs. safety) lead to very different compliance behaviors, so simply suppressing “eval-awareness” can be misleading for safety.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current safety evaluations often try to reduce a model’s “evaluation awareness” as if it were a single scalar property, under the assumption that this makes evaluations more reliable and less gameable. However, models may interpret being evaluated in qualitatively different ways (e.g., as a test of abilities or as a probe of safety boundaries), which could have very different implications for whether they comply with a query. The authors want to unpack and measure these different framings to understand and improve safety evaluations.

Method: The authors analyze chain-of-thought (CoT) traces from Qwen3-32B on the FORTRESS safety dataset, labeling verbalized evaluation-awareness according to whether it is capabilities-flavored, safety-flavored, both, or neither. They then compare compliance rates across these framings under various steering interventions that target eval-awareness. Additionally, they use a CoT-prefill intervention: they prefill the model’s internal chain-of-thought with eval-awareness-negative content that pushes it toward specific framings, and observe how this affects downstream compliance, to test for a causal link between framing and behavior.

Result: They find that when the model frames the interaction as a capabilities test, it is much more likely to comply with user requests than when it frames it as a safety test, with a +24 to +46 percentage-point compliance gap across all steering conditions. The CoT-prefill experiments support a causal relationship: in 10 of 11 tested prefills, shifting the eval-awareness framing moves compliance in the predicted direction. They also show that aggregate metrics like “eval-awareness suppression rate” can change without affecting the specifically safety-relevant component of eval-awareness, and that the same numerical suppression level can hide very different underlying behavior patterns.

Conclusion: Evaluation awareness is behaviorally heterogeneous: different internal framings of being evaluated (capabilities vs. safety) produce systematically different compliance behaviors. Treating eval-awareness as a single quantity to be globally suppressed is therefore misleading for safety evaluations. Safety pipelines need to distinguish and separately reason about different kinds of eval-awareness; otherwise, interventions that look effective at reducing eval-awareness overall may leave the safety-relevant aspects untouched or even change behavior in undesirable ways.

Abstract: Steering interventions targeting eval-awareness, a model's recognition that it is being tested, are increasingly used in safety evaluation pipelines, where evaluation-awareness is treated as a single quantity to be suppressed. We show that verbalized eval-awareness in chain-of-thought can be identified as capabilities-flavored ("the user is testing my ability to follow instructions"), safety-flavored ("the user is testing my boundaries"), both, or neither: framings that predict compliance very differently. On Qwen3-32B over the FORTRESS dataset, capabilities-framing predicts compliance with a +24 to +46 percentage-point gap over safety-framing across all tested steering conditions. A CoT-prefill intervention on eval-awareness-negative rollouts suggests the link is causal, with 10 of 11 prefills shifting compliance in the predicted direction. Then, eval-awareness is not behaviorally uniform: aggregate suppression rates can move while the safety-relevant component does not, and the same "X% suppression of eval-awareness" can correspond to qualitatively different behavioral outcomes.

</details>


### [210] [Sophistication in GenAI Use: Field Evidence from a Large Firm](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27364)
*Nicholas J. Hallman,Zachary T. Kowaleski,Anu Puvvada,Jaime J. Schmidt*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper analyzes how sophisticated employees are in using generative AI across roles and over time in a large firm.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As generative AI tools spread through organizations, managers lack clear evidence on who uses them effectively, how usage sophistication differs across roles, and whether training improves that sophistication. Understanding these patterns is critical for realizing productivity and strategic benefits from AI investments.

Method: Using proprietary logs from a large firm, the authors analyze 713,564 prompts and associated large language model responses generated by nearly 4,000 back-office employees across 15 functions over eight months in 2025. They construct measures of “sophistication” in genAI use and relate these to employee seniority, functional area, time trends, and exposure to formal AI training.

Result: (1) Senior employees display more sophisticated genAI use, suggesting that domain expertise enhances effective AI use. (2) Sophistication differs sharply across functions and is highest in Strategy, Digital Innovation, and Project Management—roles tied to firmwide strategy and organizational change. (3) There is no meaningful improvement in sophistication over time or persistent gains after formal AI training, implying limited learning or impact from standard training interventions.

Conclusion: Sophisticated genAI use is uneven within the firm: it correlates with seniority and certain strategically oriented roles, but does not automatically improve with experience or typical training. Managers should account for these differences when designing AI adoption strategies and consider new approaches beyond standard training to raise sophistication. The paper also offers operationalizable measures of genAI sophistication that future research can use.

Abstract: We study how sophistication in generative AI (genAI) use varies among the back-office workforce of a large firm. Using proprietary data, we observe 713,564 employee prompts and their corresponding large language model responses from nearly 4,000 back-office employees across 15 functional areas over eight months in 2025. We document three main findings. First, senior employees exhibit more sophisticated genAI use, consistent with domain expertise complementing genAI capabilities. Second, sophistication varies considerably across functions and is highest in Strategy, Digital Innovation, and Project Management, three groups that share a focus on firmwide strategic initiatives and organizational change. Third, we observe neither improvements in sophistication over time nor lasting improvements following formal AI training, suggesting that sophisticated use can be difficult to change. Together, our study provides measures of and insights into sophisticated genAI use that managers can use to improve outcomes and that researchers can use in future research.

</details>


### [211] [CorporateBench: Large-Scale Q&A Benchmarking with Temporal Knowledge Bases](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27391)
*Sil Hamilton,Albert Yu Sun,Oscar J. Romero,Carl-Leander Henneking,David Mimno,Bishan Yang,Igor Labutov*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CorporateBench is a large, human-validated benchmark to test how well LLMs answer questions over realistic, large-scale corporate document collections.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing ways to evaluate LLMs on enterprise document QA are inadequate: real corporate data is private and can’t be shared, and current synthetic datasets are too small and simplistic to reflect real-world scale and complexity. This creates a gap in reliably measuring how well LLMs handle realistic corporate communication and knowledge bases.

Method: The authors build CorporateBench, a multi-task Q&A benchmark based on four synthetic companies of different sizes (12 to 10,000 employees). For each firm, they generate a temporally evolving, logically consistent knowledge base and sample more than 230,000 documents that simulate corporate communications. They design tasks along two axes—information extraction and knowledge base querying—and ensure cross-document logical consistency. They then evaluate five LLMs on these tasks as the corpus size scales up.

Result: CorporateBench corpora exceed 230,000 documents and preserve internal logical consistency across time and documents. When five LLMs are tested, their performance degrades markedly as the size of the input approaches realistic enterprise scales, indicating current models struggle with large, complex corporate corpora.

Conclusion: CorporateBench fills an important gap in the benchmarking landscape by providing a realistic, large-scale, and human-validated benchmark for corporate communication reasoning. It shows that current LLMs perform increasingly poorly at realistic scales, and it offers LLM developers a concrete metric and testbed to improve models for enterprise document QA and knowledge-base reasoning.

Abstract: LLMs are increasingly able to answer complex questions about enterprise-scale document collections. But evaluation is hard: companies don't want to share internal communications, and synthetic datasets have been overly simple. We present CorporateBench (CB), a human-validated multi-task Q&A benchmark whose scale approaches the conditions LLMs encounter in corporate communication networks, with evaluation corpora surpassing 230,000 documents. CB evaluates LLMs across two dimensions (information extraction and knowledge base querying) through four synthetically generated firms ranging from 12 to 10,000 employees. Each corpus is sampled from a temporally evolving knowledge base describing a consistent world, guaranteeing cross-document logical consistency even across hundreds of thousands of documents. We evaluate five LLMs on CB, revealing increasingly poor performance as input size approaches realistic scales. CB provides LLM developers a metric for corporate communication reasoning, filling a crucial gap in the benchmarking ecosystem.

</details>


### [212] [Learning a Continuous Sepsis Severity Score Without Hour-by-Hour Supervision: A Two-Site Retrospective Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27421)
*Kevin Zhu,Ryan Zhang,Baraa Abed,Tilendra Choudhary,Malvern Madondo,Mehak Arora,Yixuan Yang,Alasdair Gent,Aditya Nagori,Omer T. Inan,Krista L. Haines,Patrick Georgoff,Suresh M. Agarwal,Vijay Krishnamoorthy,Tetsu Ohnuma,Mihai V. Podgoreanu,Michael R. Pinsky,Gilles Clermont,Craig M. Coopersmith,Craig S. Jabaley,Rishikesan Kamaleswaran*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They build a new, dynamic sepsis severity index learned from real ICU trajectories that better separates survivors from non‑survivors and tracks response to treatment over time, outperforming old static scores.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing sepsis severity scores (like SOFA) were designed decades ago, use a small set of coarsely binned variables, and are calibrated to outdated patient populations. They also typically treat each timepoint independently instead of considering the whole clinical trajectory, limiting their ability to guide modern, time‑sensitive critical care decisions. There is a need for a continuously updated, data‑driven index that better reflects contemporary practice and patient dynamics.

Method: Retrospective study on two large adult sepsis cohorts from different hospital systems (≈29k and ≈7.7k patients meeting Sepsis‑3). They constructed a new sepsis index using 43 routinely collected variables over the first 72 hours. Instead of assigning outcome labels to each individual timepoint, they used mortality as a trajectory‑level ranking signal and learned to distribute credit non‑uniformly across timesteps, effectively learning which hours were most prognostic. Model performance was evaluated on a fixed 20% test holdout using clinical vignettes and rank‑based metrics (Spearman correlation). Uncertainty was quantified via bootstrap resampling by patient. They assessed discrimination within baseline risk strata and examined both within‑patient and cross‑site correlations, including comparisons to established indices and null controls.

Result: On a 0–10 scale, non‑survivors consistently had higher index scores than survivors by 1.19–1.64 points across all strata of baseline SOFA‑2, lactate, MAP, and creatinine, showing added prognostic separation beyond existing markers. Changes in the index within a patient correlated with changes in key physiological variables, especially lactate (Spearman ρ = 0.39; n = 1,854), with weaker but similar trends for MAP and creatinine. Models trained at different institutions showed substantial cross‑site agreement: between‑site Spearman correlations reached 70–77% of the same‑site ceiling; external within‑patient correlations were 0.54 and 0.59 versus theoretical ceilings of 0.92 and 0.90. The new index correlated with established severity scores, whereas null controls produced near‑zero correlations, supporting that the learned signal is clinically meaningful rather than an artifact.

Conclusion: A trajectory‑based sepsis index derived from routine ICU data can provide hourly prognostic information that discriminates survivors from non‑survivors even within traditional risk strata and tracks physiologic changes in clinically interpretable ways. Its cross‑institutional robustness and alignment with established indices suggest it could serve as a practical decision support tool to complement, rather than replace, clinician judgment in managing sepsis in modern critical care settings.

Abstract: Currently used sepsis severity indices rely on fixed variables and weights established decades ago, which are coarsely discretized and calibrated to a cohort that no longer reflects contemporary critical care. No alternative learned directly from patient trajectories is in routine use. We conducted a retrospective two-cohort study on a total of 29,116 and 7,691 adult patients meeting Sepsis-3 criteria from two hospital systems in Massachusetts and Georgie, respectively.
  We developed a sepsis index using 43 routinely charted variables over a 72-hour treatment window. Unlike previous studies, we use mortality as a treatment-level ranking signal rather than a per-state target, allowing credit to be redistributed non-uniformly across timesteps. Evaluation was done on a permanent 20% test holdout, using clinical vignettes and Spearman correlation. Uncertainty intervals were obtained by bootstrap resampling of whole patients. Under this ranking scheme, non-survivors scored 1.19-1.64 points higher than survivors on a 0-10 scale within all strata of baseline SOFA-2, with similar results stratifying within lactate, mean arterial pressure (MAP), and creatinine. Within-patient change in the index correlated with change in lactate (Spearman rho = 0.39; n = 1,854). Similar, weaker correlations were found for MAP and creatinine. On a cohort level, cross-institutional agreement measured by Spearman correlation between models trained on different sites, were 70-77% of same-site correlation. External within-patient correlations were 0.54 and 0.59 against ceilings of 0.92 and 0.90. Our index also correlated with established indices, while null controls stayed near zero.
  Our index demonstrated hourly prognostic information that meaningfully separates patient outcomes and is consistent with clinical expectation, indicating potential as a decision support tool complementing clinical judgement.

</details>


### [213] [Mechanistic Reaction Prediction via Discrete Flow Matching on Graph-Structured Electron Occupation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27429)
*Nguyen Xuan-Vu,Octavian Susanu,Daniel Armstrong,Philippe Schwaller*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents MAELLE, a mechanistic reaction prediction model that operates directly in electron space using discrete flow matching over electron occupation vectors, achieving competitive and more robust performance than existing methods and recovering chemically interpretable mechanisms and side products.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most ML models for reaction prediction work at the level of molecular graphs or SMILES strings, either generating products de novo or performing heuristic graph edits. These approaches ignore that reactions are fundamentally about electron rearrangements, struggle with mechanistic interpretability, and can be brittle out-of-distribution. The authors want a model that (1) operates on electron movements, (2) yields mechanistically meaningful trajectories without needing step-wise supervision, and (3) is robust to structural and reaction-type distribution shifts.

Method: They model the mapping from reactants to products as a Continuous-time Markov Chain (CTMC) defined over integer-valued electron occupation vectors on all bonding, non-bonding, and hydrogen sites of a molecular graph. They then apply discrete flow matching: they generalize the mixture path construction to discrete electron rearrangements using Optimal Transport, which yields a distribution over intermediate electron configurations (edit moves). Training learns a flow over this electron occupation space that maps reactant to product configurations. This produces sequences of interpretable electron edits, without requiring explicit elementary-step labels.

Result: On the USPTO-480K benchmark, MAELLE attains reaction-prediction performance on par with leading models. In two out-of-distribution tests—one varying structural complexity and another varying reaction type—MAELLE maintains accuracy better than baselines, whose performance deteriorates more. The model’s learned electron flow produces reaction trajectories that align with known mechanistic chemistry and is able to predict plausible side products based on full electron redistribution.

Conclusion: Representing reactions as flows in electron occupation space via discrete flow matching is both accurate and chemically meaningful. MAELLE shows that electron-level modeling can match state-of-the-art graph-based approaches while being more robust out-of-distribution and providing mechanistic interpretability and side-product prediction. This suggests that mechanistic, electron-centric generative frameworks are a promising direction for reaction modeling in computational chemistry.

Abstract: Chemical reactions are fundamentally transformations in electron space, yet most machine learning approaches model them either through \textit{de novo} generation of product molecules or through heuristic graph edits that operate directly on molecular topology.
  We introduce MAELLE (\textbf{M}ech\textbf{A}nistic \textbf{E}dit f\textbf{L}ow-matching on e\textbf{L}ectron r\textbf{E}arrangements), which instead models reactions as discrete flow matching over electron occupation vectors.
  Concretely, we formulate the reactant-to-product mapping as a Continuous-time Markov Chain (CTMC) over the graph-structured integer-valued electron occupation space defined on all bonding, non-bonding, and hydrogen sites.
